## What this system is

A typical modern API is not one server — it is a chain of components. Understanding where time is spent (and where failures can happen) in that chain is the core of backend work.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  A[Client] -->|HTTP 1.1 / 2| B[Load Balancer]
  B --> C[API Gateway]
  C -->|throttle + auth| D[Application Server]
  D -->|cache read| E[(Redis Cache)]
  D -->|cache miss| F[(Database)]
  D -->|async| G[(Message Queue)]
  E --> D
  F --> D
```

## The request, step by step

1. **Client** sends an HTTP request. This may set timeouts, authentication headers, and an `Idempotency-Key`.
2. **Load balancer** picks a healthy instance. Health checks prune unhealthy nodes so traffic never reaches a dead server.
3. **API Gateway** applies cross-cutting rules: authentication, rate limiting, request logging, and request validation.
4. **Application server** executes the controller logic. It checks a cache first; on a hit it skips the database entirely.
5. **Database** answers the query if the cache missed. The result is written back to cache so the next read is fast.
6. **Response** travels back along the same path.

## Components you should know

| Component | Responsibility | Failure mode |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Load balancer | Distribute traffic, health checks | Single point of failure unless configured active/passive |
| API Gateway | Auth, throttling, routing | A misconfigured rule can block all traffic |
| Application server | Business logic, orchestration | Slow downstream calls cause timeouts and cascading load |
| Cache | Serve hot reads fast | Stale data if invalidation is missed |
| Database | Durable source of truth | ACID guarantees, but slow under lock contention |

## Why it matters

Most production incidents are not "the database fell over" — they are latency cascading up the chain until the load balancer marks instances unhealthy and the platform restarts them in a loop. Knowing the chain is the difference between debugging an outage and guessing at it.

## Sequence view

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant C as Client
  participant G as Gateway
  participant A as App Server
  participant R as Redis
  participant D as Database

  C->>G: GET /orders/42
  G->>A: forward (rate limit OK)
  A->>R: GET orders:42
  alt cache hit
    R-->>A: cached order
  else cache miss
    A->>D: SELECT * FROM orders WHERE id=42
    D-->>A: row
    A->>R: SET orders:42 (TTL 300s)
  end
  A-->>G: 200 OK + JSON
  G-->>C: response
```

## Related systems

- [Database indexing](../database-indexing) — why that `WHERE id = 42` is fast at all
- [API gateway caching](../api-gateway-caching) — caching earlier and closer to the user
- [Message queue delivery](../message-queue-delivery) — what happens when the request must not be processed synchronously29:T2536,
## What this system is

**AsyncAPI** is the industry-standard specification for describing **event-driven APIs** — the same role OpenAPI plays for REST. It captures:

- **Channels** (topics, queues, exchanges) and direction (publish/subscribe)
- **Message schemas** (payloads, headers, correlations)
- **Protocol bindings** (Kafka, AMQP, MQTT, WebSocket, HTTP, SNS/SQS, Solace, IBM MQ, etc.)
- **Servers** (broker clusters, connection details, security)
- **Components** (reusable schemas, parameters, traits, correlations)

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Spec[AsyncAPI Document\nYAML/JSON] --> Gen[Code Generators]
  Spec --> Doc[Interactive Docs\nHTML/Markdown]
  Spec --> Gov[Governance\nLinting, CI checks]
  Spec --> Mock[Mock Servers\nTest Consumers]
  Gen --> Prod[Producer SDKs]
  Gen --> Cons[Consumer SDKs]
```

## Why it exists

REST + OpenAPI assumes **request/response**. Modern systems are **event-driven**:

| REST/OpenAPI | AsyncAPI |
|---|---|
| Synchronous, client pulls | Asynchronous, broker pushes |
| One schema per endpoint | Many messages per channel |
| HTTP verbs | Protocol-agnostic (Kafka, AMQP, MQTT, WS, ...) |
| Stateless | Stateful consumers, durable subscriptions |
| `GET /orders` | `orders.created` event on `orders` topic |

AsyncAPI gives you a **single source of truth** for the contract between event producers and consumers — across teams, languages, and brokers.

## Core concepts

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  A[AsyncAPI Document] --> B[info: title, version, contact, license]
  A --> C[servers: broker endpoints + protocol]
  A --> D[channels: topics/queues + messages]
  A --> E[components: schemas, parameters, traits, correlations, securitySchemes]
  A --> F[operations: publish/subscribe + traits]
  A --> G[tags, externalDocs]
```

### Channels

A **channel** is a logical pathway (Kafka topic, RabbitMQ queue, MQTT topic, etc.). Each channel defines:

- `publish` — what a producer may send
- `subscribe` — what a consumer receives
- `parameters` — path/templated parts (e.g., `orders/{region}/{tenant}`)
- `bindings` — protocol-specific metadata (Kafka partition key, MQTT QoS, etc.)

```yaml
channels:
  orders.created:
    description: "Emitted when an order is placed"
    publish:
      operationId: publishOrderCreated
      summary: "Publish new order event"
      message:
        $ref: '#/components/messages/OrderCreated'
    bindings:
      kafka:
        topic: orders.created
        partitions: 12
        replicationFactor: 3
```

### Messages

A **message** defines the payload + headers + correlations:

```yaml
components:
  messages:
    OrderCreated:
      name: OrderCreated
      title: "Order Created Event"
      summary: "Published when a customer places an order"
      contentType: application/json
      payload:
        $ref: '#/components/schemas/Order'
      headers:
        type: object
        properties:
          correlationId:
            type: string
            format: uuid
          causationId:
            type: string
            format: uuid
          traceparent:
            type: string
      bindings:
        kafka:
          key: '{correlationId}'
```

### Schemas

Payloads use **JSON Schema** (draft-07/2019-09 or 2020-12) — same as OpenAPI:

```yaml
components:
  schemas:
    Order:
      type: object
      required: [orderId, customerId, items, totalAmount, currency, placedAt]
      properties:
        orderId:
          type: string
          format: uuid
        customerId:
          type: string
          format: uuid
        items:
          type: array
          items:
            $ref: '#/components/schemas/OrderItem'
        totalAmount:
          type: number
          minimum: 0
        currency:
          type: string
          pattern: '^[A-Z]{3}$'
        placedAt:
          type: string
          format: date-time
```

### Protocol bindings

AsyncAPI is **protocol-agnostic**. Bindings map the abstract channel/message to a concrete protocol:

| Protocol | Binding covers |
|---|---|
| **Kafka** | topic, partitions, replication, key, headers, schema registry |
| **AMQP 0-9-1** | exchange, queue, routingKey, deliveryMode, TTL, dead-letter |
| **MQTT 3/5** | topic, QoS, retain, session expiry, user properties |
| **WebSocket** | subprotocol, headers, ping/pong |
| **HTTP** | webhook POST, CloudEvents mapping |
| **SNS/SQS** | topic ARN, queue URL, FIFO, message group ID |
| **Solace** | topic, queue, TTL, priority, DMQ |
| **IBM MQ** | queue, correlationId, messageId, persistence |

```yaml
servers:
  production:
    url: kafka://kafka.prod.example.com:9092
    protocol: kafka
    description: "Production Kafka cluster"
    security:
      - saslScram: []
    bindings:
      kafka:
        clientId: order-service
        groupId: order-consumer-group
```

### Security

Reuses OpenAPI security schemes + adds protocol-specific:

```yaml
components:
  securitySchemes:
    saslScram:
      type: http
      scheme: bearer
      bearerFormat: SCRAM-SHA-512
    mtls:
      type: http
      scheme: bearer
      description: "Mutual TLS with client certificates"
    apiKey:
      type: apiKey
      in: header
      name: X-API-Key
```

## Tooling ecosystem

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Editor[AsyncAPI Studio\nEditor + Preview] --> Spec
  Spec[.asyncapi.yaml] --> Generator[@asyncapi/generator]
  Generator --> Templates[Built-in + Custom Templates]
  Templates --> Code[Node, Python, Go, Java, Spring, .NET, Kotlin, ...]
  Templates --> Docs[HTML, Markdown, Postman, OpenAPI]
  Templates --> Infra[Docker Compose, K8s, Helm, Terraform]
  Spec --> Lint[@asyncapi/linter]
  Spec --> Diff[@asyncapi/diff]
  Spec --> Mock[@asyncapi/mock]
```

### Key tools

| Tool | Purpose |
|---|---|
| **AsyncAPI Studio** | Browser-based editor with live preview, validation, mock server |
| **@asyncapi/generator** | Code gen: SDKs, server stubs, docs, infra-as-code |
| **@asyncapi/parser** | Parse/validate/normalize documents in Node/TS |
| **@asyncapi/linter** | Style + semantic rules (naming, required fields, breaking changes) |
| **@asyncapi/diff** | Compare two specs, detect breaking/non-breaking changes |
| **@asyncapi/mock** | Run a mock broker from the spec for consumer testing |
| **AsyncAPI CLI** | `asyncapi validate`, `generate`, `diff`, `new`, `preview` |

### Generate a Node.js consumer

```bash
npx @asyncapi/generator \
  -t @asyncapi/nodejs-template \
  -o ./generated \
  ./asyncapi.yaml
```

Produces:
- Typed message interfaces (TypeScript)
- Kafka/AMQP/MQTT client wrappers
- Validation middleware (AJV)
- OpenTelemetry instrumentation hooks

## Versioning strategy

AsyncAPI documents **have a version** (`info.version`), but **event schemas** evolve independently. Best practices:

1. **Schema version in topic** → `orders.v1.created`, `orders.v2.created`
2. **Schema registry** (Confluent, Apicurio, Karapace) — enforce compatibility (BACKWARD, FORWARD, FULL, NONE)
3. **AsyncAPI `components.schemas`** versioned per release; use `x-deprecated` + `deprecation` headers
4. **Consumer-driven contract testing** (Pact, Spring Cloud Contract) — verify consumers before deploying producers

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  V1[orders.v1.created] --> Reg[Schema Registry\nBACKWARD compat]
  V2[orders.v2.created] --> Reg
  Reg --> Prod[Producer deploys v2]
  Prod --> Cons[Consumers migrate\non their schedule]
```

## Governance in CI

```yaml
# .github/workflows/asyncapi.yml
name: AsyncAPI Governance
on: [pull_request]
jobs:
  lint:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: asyncapi/actions/lint@v1
        with:
          files: "asyncapi/**/*.yaml"
  diff:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
        with:
          fetch-depth: 0
      - uses: asyncapi/actions/diff@v1
        with:
          old: "asyncapi/prod.yaml"
          new: "asyncapi/staging.yaml"
      - run: echo "Breaking changes detected" && exit 1
        if: ${{ steps.diff.outputs.breaking == 'true' }}
```

## Tester checklist

- Validate spec: `asyncapi validate asyncapi.yaml` passes
- Lint: naming conventions, required descriptions, no `any` schemas
- Diff against production: no breaking changes without version bump
- Generate consumer SDK → compile + unit test against schema
- Mock server: `asyncapi mock asyncapi.yaml` → run contract tests
- Verify protocol bindings match actual broker config (Kafka partitions, MQTT QoS, etc.)
- Check `correlationId` / `traceparent` propagation in generated code
- Confirm security schemes match deployed auth (SASL, mTLS, API keys)

## What this system is

A **blockchain** is a distributed, append-only ledger of records — *blocks* — chained together by cryptographic hashes. Instead of one trusted database that everyone reads, a blockchain is replicated across many **nodes** that do not trust each other. Each block bundles a set of transactions, a timestamp, and a hash of the *previous* block. That pointer is what binds the chain together: change anything in an old block and its hash no longer matches, invalidating every block built on top of it (see [Secrets, certificates and PKI](../secrets-certificates-and-pki) for the hashing primitives underneath).

Because no single party owns history, nobody can quietly rewrite it. The consequence is a shared state machine that stays consistent without a bank, broker, or cloud account being the referee.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    B1[Block N<br/>tx T1..T3<br/>hash of Block N-1] --> B2[Block N+1<br/>tx T4..T6<br/>hash of Block N]
    B2 --> B3[Block N+2<br/>tx T7..T9<br/>hash of Block N+1]
    B3 --> B4[Block N+3 ...]
```

## What problem it replaces

| Trust model | Without blockchain | With blockchain |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Authoritative data | A central party (bank, app, government) is trusted to store the truth | Truth is the longest hash-linked chain, agreed by the network |
| Auditing | Relies on that party's logs, which they could edit | Every node holds the same append-only history |
| Failure domain | A central outage or compromise takes the system down | Nodes join and leave; the ledger survives as long as replicas exist |
| Settlement | Counterparty risk — I must trust the other side will pay | A transaction only lands if the network agrees it is valid |
| Threat model | Insider can alter records and erase traces | Altering one replica is detected by every other replica |

This buys a specific kind of assurance: **integrity and provenance of records among parties that do not trust each other**, at the cost of throughput, latency, and privacy compared to a normal database. Blockchain is rarely the answer for "I just need a fast database" — that is what [Data warehouse and lakehouse architecture](../data-warehouse-lakehouse-architecture) is for.

## Blocks and hashing

- **Block**: a batch of transactions plus a header. The header includes the previous block hash, a timestamp, a nonce/attested state, and often a Merkle root that commits to the transaction list in one digest.
- **Merkle tree**: transactions are hashed pairwise up to a single root, so a node can prove "this transaction is in this block" by showing only a small hash path, not the whole block.
- **Append-only**: blocks are added by consensus; nobody can insert one in the middle without recomputing every hash after it, which would be detected.
- **Fork**: when two blocks are mined at the same height, the chain briefly splits. Nodes adopt the branch with the most accumulated proof of work or stake — the *longest/heaviest* chain wins and the other branch is orphaned.

## Consensus: how nodes agree

| Mechanism | Idea | Cost | Used by |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Proof of Work (PoW) | Pay real compute to propose a block; the first solver wins | Energy and hardware | Bitcoin, (historically) Ethereum |
| Proof of Stake (PoS) | Lock up tokens as stake; validators are selected and can be slashed for cheating | Capital, not energy | Ethereum after "the Merge" |
| PBFT / leader-based BFT | Nodes vote in rounds; tolerates up to ~1/3 malicious replicas | Small, permissioned networks | Enterprise/government ledgers |
| DAG-based (IOTA, Hedera) | No linear chain; transactions confirm each other as a directed acyclic graph | Fast, no miners | IoT micro-payments, supply chain |

For private networks (permissioned), PBFT-style consensus is usually the right fit because every member is known. For public networks, PoW/PoS are designed to work even when membership is open and adversarial.

## What a node does

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    CLIENT["Client<br/>sends a transaction"] -->|sign + submit| MEMPOOL["Mem pool<br/>transaction waits"]
    MEMPOOL -->|pick transactions| VALIDATOR["Validator / miner<br/>proposes a block"]
    VALIDATOR -->|proposed block| BROADCAST["Full nodes<br/>verify + gossip block"]
    BROADCAST -->|every node stores| LEDGER["Appended to ledger<br/>immutable history"]
```

## When blockchain makes sense

- **Multi-party reconciliation** — several organizations must agree on one shared record and none will let the others hold it (supply chain, trade finance).
- **Provenance and audit trails** — a tamper-evident log is worth more than raw performance.
- **Open, permissionless networks** — anyone can join and no single operator can freeze or censor.
- **Tokenization and settlement** — native digital assets and programmatic payments with an existing trust problem.

Avoid it when the real requirement is confidentiality (a blockchain's history is public), or high throughput, or "we just trust our own database anyway" — in those cases a normal database plus [Change data capture](../change-data-capture) and [Monitoring and observability](../monitoring-and-observability) is better.

## Tester checklist

- Verify immutability: create a chain, tamper with an early block's transaction, and confirm subsequent blocks' hash pointers break.
- Test fork resolution: simulate two conflicting blocks at the same height and confirm the network converges on one chain.
- Confirm a transaction is only final after the required confirmations, not when it first appears in the mempool.
- Check that a malformed or unsigned transaction is rejected by full nodes before it can enter a block.
- Validate node recovery: a node that was offline can resync and reproduce the exact same chain state.
- Exercise consensus edge cases: an empty block, a double-spend attempt, and a block exceeding size/gas limits.

- [Zero Trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — how identity works when there is no central "inside"
- [Data governance and catalog](../data-governance-and-catalog) — contrasting governed central catalogs with decentralized ledgers

- [Smart contracts and decentralized apps](../smart-contracts-and-decentralized-apps) — the programmable layer living on top of the ledger
- [Decentralized identity and verifiable credentials](../decentralized-identity-and-verifiable-credentials) — using the ledger as an issuer of trust, not accounts
- [Data pipelines (ETL/ELT)](../data-pipelines-etl-elt) — how blockchain data is extracted and normalized for analytics
- [Secrets, certificates and PKI](../secrets-certificates-and-pki) — key custody, wallets, and cryptographic identity2b:T19bc,
## What this system is

A **card network** (Visa, Mastercard, and the card schemes) is a message switch, an interchange, and a settlement house in one. Each **card transaction touches four parties** — the cardholder, the merchant, the merchant's **acquirer** bank, and the cardholder's **issuer** bank — and the network stands in the middle of every one of them. The scale the industry quotes ("billions a day") comes from something more interesting than hardware: the network is a *nearly stateless protocol with ruthless budgets*.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    H[Cardholder - POS / e-commerce] --> M[Merchant / POS terminal]
    M --> AQ[Acquirer bank]
    AQ --> N[Network switch - Visa / Mastercard]
    N --> IS[Issuer bank]
    IS --> R[(Risk + account systems)]
    N -.-> CLEAR[Clearing + settlement]
```

## Why fast: the authorization budget

A card payment at the terminal is a **real-time authorization**: the merchant asks "is this good?", and the answer must come back in a second or two — not minutes. The network's job is to move that request from acquirer to issuer, get a decision, and return it. Because the request is small, idempotent by reference, and largely stateless at the switch, the network is built like a high-performance message bus, not a giant database.

| Constraint | Number that drives the design |
| --- | --- |
| Authorization latency budget | ~2 seconds round trip, network included |
| Transaction size | A few hundred bytes per ISO 8583 message |
| Peak rate (global schemes) | Tens of thousands of authorizations per second |
| Failure cost | A workflow-dependent mix of false decline (lost sale) or false approve (fraud) |

## Anatomy of an authorization

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant TERM as "POS terminal"
    participant AQ as "Acquirer"
    participant SW as "Network switch"
    participant IS as "Issuer"
    TERM->>AQ: auth request
    AQ->>SW: BIN lookup
    SW->>IS: forward
    IS-->>SW: approve / decline
    SW-->>TERM: reply to terminal
```

## The switch's design secrets

- **Routing is a lookup, not a query**: the network routes by **BIN/IIN** (the first 6–8 digits of the card number) to the issuer's endpoint. It is a precomputed routing table, updated on an issuer bulletin — no per-transaction database lookup for *where* to send.
- **Stateless in the middle**: the switch does not store the account; it carries the message and returns the answer. State lives at the issuer. Losing a switch node loses nothing recoverable — the design point that makes the network horizontally scalable and cheap to run at any peak.
- **Timeout and fallback**: if an issuer doesn't answer within its SLA, the network may **stand-in authorizes** — accepting within issuer-defined limits — or route via a redundant path. That fallback is how commerce keeps moving even when a bank hiccups.
- **Idempotency and reference numbers**: each authorization carries a unique retrieval reference, so a retried message settles exactly once. This is the same once-only pattern as [Message queue delivery](../message-queue-delivery), pressed into a sub-second protocol.
- **Concurrency without locks**: money is only *reserved* at authorization (a hold) and only *moved* later. Because the switch never touches balances, throughput scales by adding nodes, not by optimizing a single database.

## Volume: clearing and settlement in batch

Authorizations are hot and synchronous; **clearing** (the actual transfer messages) runs in **batches** after the fact. The merchant's terminal captures at end-of-day or per-transaction, the network *nets* and *descends* summaries between each acquirer-issuer pair, and settlement moves the net amounts — exactly the [netting idea from payments clearing](../payments-clearing-and-settlement), applied across thousands of banks at once.

This separation is the single biggest scaling lever: the real-time path is narrow and fast, while the volume path is a batch processing system with reconciliation. Both are necessary; neither can do the other's job.

## How the scale is actually measured

The "million per second" headline is a **peak throughput headline about the whole ecosystem**, not a single request's path. What is genuinely true:

| Layer | Where the volume lands |
| --- | --- |
| Terminal to acquirer | Massive parallelism, country-scale |
| Acquirer to switch | The switch's high-volume ingress |
| Switch to issuer | Fanned out by BIN routing; issued APIs are the real cost |
| The ledger behind it | Held by issuer/acquirer banks, not the network |

For an architect building their own high-volume system, the transferable lessons are: **narrow the hot path, make the middle stateless, route by precomputed keys, reserve-then-settle instead of locking, and put the volume on a batch side-channel.**

## Tester checklist

- Routing: a card with a given BIN reaches its intended issuer, and a BIN-table update takes effect without restarting the switch.
- Timeout: an issuer that exceeds its SLA triggers the configured stand-in or fallback path, and the decision is recorded with the reason.
- Idempotency: re-sending an identical authorization reference is answered identically and does not double-hold funds.
- Latency budget: under synthetic load, a normally-routed authorization completes inside the round-trip SLA.
- Partition tolerance: a switch node failure does not lose in-flight authorizations or corrupt routing state.
- Settlement math: a day of captures nets per acquirer-issuer pair to zero at the aggregate, tieing to the [reconciliation feed](../payments-clearing-and-settlement).
- Decline vs authorize: the fraud/limit decision from the issuer is returned verbatim to the terminal code the merchant's systems parse.

- [Payments, clearing, and settlement](../payments-clearing-and-settlement) — the rails and netting these messages ride
- [E-commerce platform architecture](../ecommerce-platform-architecture) — authorization-vs-capture in the store
- [Core banking systems](../core-banking-systems) — the issuer ledger that holds the cardholder account

## Deep dives

- [Banking risk, fraud, and AML controls](../banking-risk-fraud-and-aml) — the real-time scoring on the issuer's side
- [Message queue delivery](../message-queue-delivery) — once-only, at-least-once delivery at the switch
- [Integration patterns](../integration-patterns) — how acquirers and issuers plug into the scheme
- [Monitoring and observability](../monitoring-and-observability) — latency and throughput telemetry of the authorization path2c:T170a,
## What this system is

A **core banking system** is the bank's double-entry ledger plus the applications that read and write it: account and product definitions, deposits and loans, customer identification, interest, fees, and statutory posting. "Core" means exactly what it sounds like — everything else in the bank (cards, payments, treasury, reporting, CRM) is a satellite that moves money *through* the core or reconciles against it.

The core's job is brutal simplicity under pressure: for every transaction it must know **who has the money, how much, and what the ledger master says after the post**. A bank that cannot state its balances overnight is a bank in regulatory trouble by morning.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    CH[Channels - app, web, ATM, branch] --> G[Gateway / API]
    G --> CORE[Core banking<br/>accounts, products, ledger]
    CORE --> DB[(Account master<br/>+ GL ledger)]
    CORE --> PMT[Payments hub - in/out]
    CORE --> RISK[Risk, fraud, AML checks]
    CORE --> REP[Regulatory + general ledger reporting]
```

## What makes it a ledger, not a CRUD app

| Concept | Meaning in banking |
| --- | --- |
| Double-entry | Every post debits one account and credits another; the ledger always sums to zero |
| Account vs GL | Customer accounts live in subledgers; the finance GL rolls them up — see [ERP finance record-to-report](../erp-finance-and-record-to-report) |
| Posting strategy | Online (real-time, before you reply) vs batch (end-of-day, historically) |
| Debits and credits | Money in is a credit to your account; money out is a debit — two views of the same posting |
| Master files | Reusable product definitions (interest rules, limits) so accounts are just instances |
| Transaction log | Every post is an immutable, numbered entry — the audit trail and the legal record |

The identity of a transaction is the thing architects obsess over: a **transaction reference, a date and time, a value date, an account, an amount, and the balancing post**. Lose any of those and reconciliation breaks.

## The money movement path

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    CUSTOMER["Customer initiates<br/>transfer or payment"] -->|submit| SANITIZE["Validate + check<br/>limits, fraud, AML"]
    SANITIZE -->|passes checks| DEBIT["Debit sender<br/>reserve / hold"]
    DEBIT -->|atomic pair| CREDIT["Credit payee<br/>post, confirm"]
    CREDIT -->|balance to zero| LEDGER["Ledger balancing entry"]
    LEDGER -->|post + confirm| DONE["Settlement + confirmation<br/>to channels and reports"]
```

## Online vs batch — the two computings

Traditionally, debit transactions posted **online** (the balance must drop the instant you pay) and credits were written at the **end of day**, because money arriving overnight needed reconciliation and interest to be computed. Modern cores post credit almost as fast, but the *design split* is still alive:

- **Real-time posting**: funds move at the moment of the transaction; channel sees new balances instantly.
- **Overnight processing**: interest, fees, statement runs, and statutory files happen in a batch window — the notorious "end of day", which is why banks still schedule *maintenance windows* around midnight.

The strategic question for an architect is: **can our ledger post strictly, under high concurrency, while our batch still runs to a hard deadline?** The batch window is usually the bottleneck that forces separate read and write paths.

## When this shows up in architecture

- **Modernization**: replacing a 40-year-old core is a multi-year program; the usual path is strangler — new channel APIs in front, a payments hub beside it, and the legacy core as downstream ledger until the runway closes.
- **Multi-entity**: group banks need a shared or federated ledger per legal entity, with consolidation for group reporting.
- **Digital banking**: the mobile app must *feel* real-time even when the core is herd-batch — ledger reads from a read replica or an operational data store, writes still hit the iron core.

## Tester checklist

- Atomicity: send $100 from A to B with B's account temporarily frozen — confirm the pair either fully posts or fully rolls back, never half-applied.
- Double-entry invariant: after a run of mixed deposits, withdrawals, fees, and reversals, the ledger sums to zero with no unexplained entries.
- Idempotency: re-send an identical transaction reference twice — it must post once and return the same reference.
- Concurrency: two payments emptying the same account cannot make the balance go negative past the agreed limit.
- Value date: a transaction posted today with tomorrow's value date lands on the correct GL day, not the posting day.
- Reversal: reversing a posted entry produces offsetting entries and restores the account and the GL to the pre-transaction state.
- Batch deadline: the overnight run completes within the window even with a padded transaction volume.

- [Message queue delivery](../message-queue-delivery) — how satellite systems receive posting events reliably
- [Data pipelines (ETL/ELT)](../data-pipelines-etl-elt) — moving settlement and ledger data into reporting stores
- [Secrets, certificates and PKI](../secrets-certificates-and-pki) — the keys protecting the money movement paths2d:T948,
## What this system is

Without an index, the database reads every row of a table to answer a `WHERE` clause. With an index, it walks a tree of keys in `O(log n)` steps. This page makes the trade-offs visible.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  subgraph Query["Query planner"]
    Q[SELECT * FROM orders WHERE user_id = 42]
  end

  subgraph WithoutIndex["Without an index"]
    A1[Seq scan] --> A2[Read tables block by block]
    A2 --> A3[Filter every row]
    A3 --> A4[Return 3 rows after reading 1,000,000]
  end

  subgraph WithIndex["With a B-tree index"]
    B1[Index lookup on user_id] --> B2[Find key 42 in tree]
    B2 --> B3[Follow table row pointers]
    B3 --> B4[Read only matching rows]
  end

  Q -->|planner chooses| WithoutIndex
  Q -->|planner chooses| WithIndex
```

## How a B-tree works

- The index is a balanced tree of keys kept **in order**.
- Point lookups follow one root-to-leaf path — roughly `log(n)` reads.
- Range queries (`>=`, `BETWEEN`) walk the leaf nodes, which are linked in a sorted chain.
- Each leaf node stores a pointer to the actual heap (data) row: “the index (not the data) is the book’s table of contents.”

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  R[Root: user_id 20 - 60] --> L1[Node: 20 - 40]
  R --> L2[Node: 41 - 60]
  L1 --> K1[key 42]
  L1 --> K2[key 37]
  L1 --> K3[key 20]
  K1 --> D1[(Row A)]
  K2 --> D2[(Row B)]
  K3 --> D3[(Row C)]
```

## When indexes hurt

| Symptom | Cause |
| --- | --- |
| Slow inserts / updates | Every write updates the index too |
| Huge storage footprint | Index rows copy the key + pointer |
| Planner ignores index | Low selectivity — it would read most rows anyway |
| `NULL` surprises | `NULL` often isn’t in a B-tree index; `WHERE x IS NULL` may scan |

## Things a tester should check

- Does the access path show **index scan** or **seq scan** in the query plan (`EXPLAIN`)?
- Are there duplicate or unused indexes (index usage statistics)?
- Does changing the data volume change query time linearly (missing index) or logarithmically (index present)?

## Related systems

- [API request lifecycle](../api-request-lifecycle) — where indexing makes or breaks endpoint latency
- [LLM RAG pipeline](../llm-rag-pipeline) — why *vector* indexes are a different shape from B-trees
- [Message queue delivery](../message-queue-delivery) — consistent reads behind an eventually consistent queue2e:T19ed,
## What this system is

**Decentralized identity (self-sovereign identity, SSI)** moves identity out of the silo of an account database. Instead of every organization issuing you an account you must log into, you hold **verifiable credentials (VCs)** — digitally signed claims like "is employee of Acme", "over 18", "licensed to drive" — in your own **wallet**, and present them to anyone who needs to verify.

The two core building blocks:

- **Decentralized Identifier (DID)**: a globally unique, resolvable identifier with no central registrar. It looks like `did:example:abc123...` and resolves (via a DID Document) to the public keys used to sign.
- **Verifiable Credential**: a JSON document signed by an *issuer*, given to a *holder*, and presentable to a *verifier*. The [cryptographic signatures](../secrets-certificates-and-pki) (and often a blockchain anchor) make the claim tamper-evident and reputable without a phone call back to headquarters.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    I[Issuer<br/>university, employer, gov] -->|issues signed credential| H[Holder<br/>wallet, self-custody]
    V[Verifier<br/>airport, landlord, API] -->|presents proofs| H
    H -->|presentation<br/>discloses only what is needed| V
    I -.->|publishes DID + status| RT[Registry / chain anchor]
    V -.->|checks signature, revocation| RT
```

## The problem it solves

| Traditional identity | Decentralized identity |
| --- | --- |
| Every service runs its own account table and password reset flow | One wallet holds credentials; services just verify presentations |
| The credential *is* the account — sign in to prove it | The credential is a signed claim — the [signature](../jwt-tokens) proves it |
| Services collect and correlate your data because they must store identity | **Selective disclosure**: present only the fields you need ("is over 18", not my birthday) |
| Anyone with the master record can impersonate you | Only the wallet with the private key can present the credential |
| Identity is bounded by the cloud account of one vendor | DIDs are portable across issuers, wallets, and verifiers |

The design point is *delegation of trust*: the verifier does not need to trust the holder (they might be malicious) or talk to the issuer (who might be offline). It only needs the issuer's public key, which is published once and can be cryptographically verified, possibly anchored in a [blockchain](../blockchain-fundamentals) so the key rotation itself has an auditable history.

## Issuer, holder, verifier

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant ISS as "Issuer"
    participant REG as "Registry"
    participant HOL as "Holder"
    participant VER as "Verifier"
    participant POL as "Trust + attribute policy"
    ISS->>HOL: issue VC
    ISS->>REG: publish DID / revoke
    REG-->>VER: resolve status
    HOL->>VER: present proof
    VER->>POL: match against policy
```

## Key concepts

- **DID method**: each `did:myMethod:` prefix defines how the DID is created, resolved, and updated (e.g., registered on a blockchain, or propagated via a peer's own servers). The method *is* the trust anchor's protocol.
- **DID document**: the resource a DID resolves to — public keys, service endpoints, and authentication material. Rotating a key is updating the document, not re-issuing every credential.
- **ZKP / selective disclosure**: cryptographically prove "this statement about my claim is true" without revealing the underlying data — e.g., prove an age range, not a birth date.
- **Revocation**: revoking a credential means the issuer updates a status registry (often an on-chain accumulator, a message list, or a status list through the [conditional-access-style policy engine](../conditional-access-and-policy-engines)). A verifier must check revocation, or a revoked ("terminated") credential still looks valid.

## When decentralized identity fits

- **Cross-organization onboarding** — hire once, present the same credential everywhere (see [Joiner–Mover–Leaver](../joiner-mover-leaver) for the JML twist).
- **Regulated privacy** — health, finance, and education must minimize data sharing; selective disclosure reduces the surface.
- **Supply chain provenance** — prove a part was certified at each step without revealing the whole contract.
- **Wallets as an integration surface** — abstract away the account sprawl of [SCIM provisioning](../scim-identity-provisioning) by having users carry their own verified attributes.

The hard parts are credential *trust* (which issuers do you accept?) and *revocation speed*. If the ecosystem cannot decide who is a reputable issuer, or if revocation is a day-late cached list, you inherit the very risk you removed.

## Tester checklist

- Verify a VC's signature: tamper with one field and confirm presentation fails by the issuer's published key.
- Test selective disclosure: request "over 18" and confirm the birth date is never transmitted.
- Check revocation: revoke a credential and confirm a verifier that consults the status registry rejects it promptly.
- Validate key rotation: rotate the issuer's DID key and confirm existing credentials still verify against the rotated key.
- Test the wallet boundary: the private key must never leave the wallet, and a UI must be able to request presentation without reading the key.
- Exercise the replay case: can a presented credential be replayed by a different device, and is that controlled by binding presentation to an audience/nonce?
- Confirm interoperability: the DID method, VC format, and status mechanism match what both issuer and verifier announce — no private extensions.
- Check the failure path: an offline registry or unreachable DID must fail closed (deny), not degrade to "trust everyone".

## What this system is

An e-commerce platform is the **commerce loop**: how a shopper on a website becomes an order that ships, gets paid for, and posts to the books. The architecture splits into a **storefront** (fast, read-heavy, customer-facing) and a **back office** (consistent, write-heavy, inventory-and-ledger-facing). The seam between them — and between the platform and the [ERP](../enterprise-resource-planning) — is where e-commerce architectures live or die.

The classic shape is orders-in, inventory-and-cash-out. Most of the interesting engineering is exactly in the step a store demo never shows: **checkout is easy when nothing goes wrong, and everything is about what happens when an order is out of stock, fails payment, or gets returned.**

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    B[Browse / catalog] --> C[Cart]
    C --> K[Checkout + payment]
    K --> O[Order]
    O --> F[Fulfillment / warehouse]
    O --> L[Ledger posting]
    F -->|tracking / delivery| D[Delivered]
    D -.->|returns| R[Returns + refunds]
```

## The commerce loop, step by step

| Stage | System of record | Failure mode to design for |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Browse + search | Catalog / search engine | Data freshness of price and stock |
| Cart | Short-lived session or saved cart | Abandonment, price change mid-session |
| Checkout | Checkout service | The user leaves; the order must not be double-charged |
| Payment | Payment gateway / PSP | 3DS, declined card, authorization expiry |
| Order capture | OMS | Conflicting orders, oversell, address validation |
| Fulfillment | WMS + carriers | Split shipments, backorders, lost parcels |
| Post-sale | Returns / refunds | RMA approval, restock, GL correction |
| Ledger | ERP finance | Reconciliation of gateway settlements and refunds |

## Storefront vs back office

| Concern | Storefront side | Back office side |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Reads | Millions of page views, cached | Few, precise |
| Writes | Cart and orders | Inventory, ledgers, shipments |
| Consistency | Eventually consistent is fine | Strongly consistent is mandatory |
| Latency | Milliseconds matter | Minutes are acceptable |
| Source of truth winner | The catalog cache | The OMS + ERP |

The rule: **never let the storefront read inventory straight from the WMS on every page view**. Cache the promotable view of the catalog and stock (with the stocks levels in the OMS), and reserve inventory at order capture, not at page render.

## Checkout to order to money

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    CHECKOUT["Checkout<br/>address + payment"] -->|authorize| AUTH["Payment intent<br/>authorize"]
    AUTH -->|reserve + confirm| ORDER["Order created<br/>OMS reserves stock"]
    ORDER -->|capture at ship| CAPTURE["Capture<br/>money moves"]
    ORDER -->|fulfill| SHIP["Ship + tracking"]
    CAPTURE -->|settlement feed| ERP["ERP posting<br/>revenue + COGS"]
    SHIP -->|COGS posting| ERP
```

## Payment basics every architect should know

- **Authorization vs capture**: authorize at checkout, capture when you ship. Authorization expires (typically 3–7 days), so delayed shipping needs re-authorization — a classic cause of failed orders.
- **3D Secure**: the bank's step-up that shifts chargeback liability; the conversion hit is real, and it must be a configuration, not an afterthought.
- **Settlement reconciliation**: gateway reports money moved in *batches*; the ERP sees per-order revenue. Every order, fee, refund, and payout needs to tie out line-by-line — this loop is the hardest to automate and the first thing an auditor checks.
- **Refunds**: money sent back is *not* the reverse of a capture; it is a separate movement with its own fees and timings.

## When to build vs buy

Buy the platform when commerce is core but not the differentiator and you want the upgrade path, hosting, and payment connectors handled. Build (or aggressively extend) when your business model depends on custom pricing, selling experiences, or fulfillment logic that no package models — but then you have signed up to own payments security, PSD2/PCA-DSS-adjacent compliance, and every integration. Most realistic answer for the long tail: **a headless storefront on a commerce core**, custom only at the edges you actually differentiate on.

## Tester checklist

- Double-payment: refresh the pay page, re-click pay with a lagging gateway — exactly one charge and one order, never two.
- Stock race: two customers buy the last unit concurrently — one order reserves it, the other backorders cleanly, and the WMS never ships more than it has.
- Auth expiry: an order authorized but not shipped before the authorization expiry re-authenticates instead of silently failing.
- Reconciliation: for a synthetic day of orders, refunds, and fees, the gateway settlement report ties to the ERP GL within tolerance.
- Checkout data: price and stock shown at the cart must be validated at the order level (promo codes, coupons, address validation).
- Returns loop: an approved return reverses inventory and posts the correct GL correction, net of restocking/fees.
- Catalog freshness: changing a price in the catalog reflects in the cache with bounded latency, and the checkout revalidates the price before capture.

- [ERP finance and record-to-report](../erp-finance-and-record-to-report) — how settlement feeds become journal entries30:T1c1c,
## What this system is

The **Risk Management Framework (RMF)** is the process by which a US federal system gets permission to operate: choose a baseline of **NIST SP 800-53 controls**, implement and assess them, document the residual risk, and obtain an **Authority to Operate (ATO)** from an Authorizing Official. **FedRAMP** is the program that applies the same discipline to *cloud services* — one high-fidelity assessment that agencies all reuse instead of each re-auditing the same provider.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    CAT[Categorize<br/>impact level] --> SEL[Select controls<br/>800-53 baseline]
    SEL --> IMP[Implement + document]
    IMP --> ASS[Assess - test the controls]
    ASS --> AUTH[Authorize - ATO decision]
    AUTH --> MON[Monitor - continuous monitoring]
    MON -.->|changes| IMP
```

## Impact levels first, everything else follows

Every control is scoped by the **impact level** of the data — the harm that loss of confidentiality, integrity, or availability would cause:

| Level | Data example | What changes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Low** | Public-ish, unclassified operational data | Smallest control baseline |
| **Moderate** | PII, sensitive operational data | Larger baseline, more documented procedures |
| **High** | High-risk data, law-enforcement sensitive, serious injury/life-focused | Full baseline + extra hardening, often dedicated infrastructure |

In **FedRAMP** those map to **FedRAMP Low, Moderate, High** authorizations; an agency can only run a workload on a cloud service authorized at least as high as the data it will hold. The classification and the authorization *must match*, or the deployment is noncompliant from day one.

## The control families, the real workload

SP 800-53 organizes ~400+ controls into **18 families**. The ones that dominate real assessments:

| Family | What it governs | The controls that eat the most time |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **AC · Access Control** | Who can touch what | Least privilege, separation of duties, session mgmt |
| **IA · Identification & Authentication** | Proving identity | Multi-factor, authenticator mgmt, and the [PIV/CAC](../government-identity-piv-ficam) linkage |
| **AU · Audit & Accountability** | Logging, evidence | Audit records, review/analysis, retention — the proof trail |
| **SC · System & Communications** | Cryptographic protection | Encryption in transit/at rest, boundary protection |
| **SI · System & Information Integrity** | Patching, malware, failure | Continuous monitoring, flaw remediation |
| **CP · Contingency** | Survival | Backup, recovery, failover testing |

Controls are not a checklist you print once — each one is a **system control plus a documented, assessed procedure**, and for cloud the [shared responsibility](../cloud-computing-fundamentals) split decides who supplies what.

## The authorization decision, step by step

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    DATA[Categorize - data impact] -- drives scope --> BASE[Select baseline - 800-53 Low/Mod/High]
    BASE -- tailor --> IMPL[Implement - SPD + procedures]
    IMPL -- evidence --> ASSESS[Assess - independent testing]
    ASSESS -- findings + POA&M --> PACK[Package - POA&M + residual risk]
    PACK -- authorizing official --> ATO[ATO - go or no-go]
```

The **ATO is a business decision, not a test pass**: the Authorizing Official signs on *residual risk* — the findings in the Plan of Action & Milestones (POA&M), the mitigating controls, and the acceptance that the system is good enough given its mission. Systems never wait for "zero findings"; they wait for *informed* acceptance, then run on **continuous monitoring** (CLOUD for cloud: continuous logs, ongoing vulnerability scanning, and periodic reassessment) until the authorization is re-upped or the risk picture changes.

## How FedRAMP changes the cloud play

- **FedRAMP High = one assessment, many agencies.** A cloud service provider (CSP) does the huge assessment once against the full baseline; agencies do a small *inheritance* check and a [cloud landing zone](../cloud-landing-zones) onboarding, then issue their own ATO scoped to the CSP's boundary.
- **Inheritance**, not reinvention: controls owned by the CSP (physical security, platform patching) are *inherited* by the tenant once the CSP is authorized; the tenant still owns app-layer controls. The shared responsibility split is a security-control mapping decision.
- **FedRAMP High vs Moderate** shapes architecture: encryption of data at rest everywhere, FedRAMP-High-authorized regional infrastructure, and — most visibly — it usually *excludes* cheap public-region tenants without additional compensation and the same assurances.

## How it shows up for an architect

- **Before you build**: categorize the data, pick the baseline, and buy cloud only at the right authorization level — then the control set, not the feature list, drives the design.
- **During build**: audit/audit/audit — logging (AU), boundary (SC-7), and authenticators (IA) are where most assessment findings concentrate, so build them in, instrument them, and make evidence exportable.
- **At the end**: the ATO is in the *package* — System Security Plan (SSP), control implementation details, assessment results, POA&M, and the risk acceptance narrative.

## Tester checklist

- Categorization match: confirm the implemented controls and infrastructure at least meet the declared impact level and FedRAMP authorization of the data and cloud service.
- Control-to-evidence: for a sample of controls (one AC, one IA, one AU), the implementation description is backed by a testable, repeatable procedure.
- Boundary completeness (SC-7): only documented interfaces traverse the boundary; no undocumented data egress paths exist in the network design.
- Audit coverage: every system component and every privileged action produces records that are aggregated, retained to policy, and reviewable.
- POA&M closure: each open finding has an owner, a due date, and a compensating control in place pending remediation.
- Inheritance correct: the split of CSP-owned vs tenant-owned controls matches the real deployed architecture, not the marketing boundary diagram.

- [FedRAMP and NIST RMF](../fedramp-nist-rmf) — this process, applied to authorized cloud
- [Zero Trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — the design layer for the access-control controls (AC/IA)
- [Cloud landing zones](../cloud-landing-zones) — the inherited-control boundary the tenant configures

- [Government identity: PIV/CAC and NIST 800-63](../government-identity-piv-ficam) — the required identity backbone behind IA controls
- [Cloud computing fundamentals](../cloud-computing-fundamentals) — shared responsibility as the box the control split lives in
- [Security Operations Center (SOC)](../security-operations-center-soc) — the monitoring side that continuous monitoring leans on
- [Data governance and catalog](../data-governance-and-catalog) — how you prove which data is which impact level in the first place31:T1974,
## What this system is

Government identity is the **identity backbone under every federal control family (IA/AC)**: employees and contractors prove who they are with a **PIV card** (Personal Identity Verification, FIPS 201) or, for the Department of Defense, a **CAC** — a smart card holding a certificate whose private key never leaves the card. The **FICAM** (Federal Identity, Credential, and Access Management) architecture is how those credentials federate across agencies, and **NIST SP 800-63** is the ladder that says *how much* assurance a citizen-facing transaction needs.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    U[User - employee, contractor, citizen] --> C[Credential - PIV / CAC / account]
    C --> P[Identity provider<br/>FICAM trusted flow]
    P --> F[Federation - SAML / OIDC]
    F --> A[Federal applications]
    A --> P
```

## The three reassurance levels (NIST 800-63)

SP 800-63 separates *how sure you are* into three axes. Getting these backwards is the classic federal identity mistake:

| Level | Axis | What it asks | Example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **IAL** | Identity assurance | How strongly was the *person* proven to exist? | (L1) self-asserted, (L2) docs, (L3) biometric-in-person |
| **AAL** | Authenticator assurance | How strongly are they authenticated *now*? | (L1) password, (L2) PLUS a second factor, (L3) hardware crypto |
| **FAL** | Federation assurance | How much trust does the federation pass along? | (L1) minimal, (L2) signed assertions, (L3) identity binding |

The rule that animates everything: **a transaction's risk sets the required level.** Renewing a library card online is IAL1/AAL1; a veteran reading their own medical record or a small business accessing benefits is IAL2/AAL2; a classified-grade authenticator is AAL3 hardware. When someone says "you need 800-63 up-leveled authentication", they mean push AAL, sometimes IAL — and each step costs real user friction and infrastructure.

## The credential lifecycle (the PIV card)

```mermaid
flowchart TB
    R[Identity proofing - in person] --> a{Sponsored<br/>by agency}
    a --> C[Issue - PIV/CAC provisioned]
    C --> U[Use - authentication at every door]
    U --> M[Maintain - renew, update certs]
    M --> L[Revoke - lost, departed, CA fired]
    L --> D[Terminate - card destroyed, access killed]
```

## The architecture pattern

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant U as Employee / contractor - PIV or CAC
    participant I as Identity proofing - FIPS 201 enrollment
    participant CA as Certificate authority - issues PIV certs
    participant P as FICAM identity provider
    participant F as Federation - SAML / OIDC trust chain
    participant A as Agency application
    U->>I: prove identity
    I->>CA: enroll
    CA-->>U: card issued
    U->>P: authenticate
    P->>F: assert identity
    F->>A: SSO session
```

Every hop exists to preserve one property: **proofing happens once, at high assurance; authentication happens everywhere, cheaply, via a federation of trust** — the same principle as [Enterprise SSO](../single-sign-on-sso) applied to multiple independent agencies.

## The pro se / citizen side (eID and login.gov-style)

For citizens there is no employer-issued card, so the trust anchors are different: a **federated login broker** (USAGov login / login.gov) verifies the citizen once at IAL2 (document checks now, biometric where risk demands) and issues a reusable authenticator that agencies accept via federation. The key concepts there:

- **Federation broker, not a database of everything**: it asserts identity + assurance level; each agency still applies its own authorization on top ([RBAC, policy, least privilege](../rbac-and-authorization)).
- **AAL can float**: an agency can accept AAL1 for read-only and demand AAL2 before a write action — keep login cheaper on low-risk reads.
- **Consent & transparency**: the citizen sees exactly what attribute is being shared — government identity is also a [verifiable-credential story](../decentralized-identity-and-verifiable-credentials) in another hat.

## When this shows up in architecture

- **Access control (AC/IA) controls in an ATO**: RMF's IA family basically assumes PIV/CAC for employees and 800-63 levels for citizens — [FedRAMP](../fedramp-nist-rmf) assessments check how cleanly you inherit and consume that.
- **Agency shared services**: FICAM says agencies should consume one shared IdP rather than each building its own credential store.
- **Contractor onboarding**: the same PIV chain covers employees *and* contractors — which for security is why offboarding ([joiner-mover-leaver](../joiner-mover-leaver)) is a compliance event, not just an HR one.

## Tester checklist

- PIV/CAC auth: a PIV card logs in without the private key ever leaving the card (verify by checking no client key extraction is even possible).
- Assurance float: the same citizen is authenticated at AAL1 for read and stepped up to AAL2 before a write/action, and the app honors the declared AAL.
- Federation break: a revoked PIV/citizen account is denied at the application within the revocation SLA across every federated partner.
- Zero proofing reuse: a citizen ID verified at a broker has no hidden path to impersonate a separate real-world identity without going through IAL2 again.
- Evidence of level: audit records show the IAL/AAL/FAL used per transaction so a 800-63 assertion is retraceable to a dated decision.
- Cross-agency SSO: one PIV login reaches multiple agency apps through the federation chain with no re-password prompts.

## Related systems

- [FedRAMP and NIST RMF](../fedramp-nist-rmf) — the compliance frame these IA/AC controls satisfy
- [Identity and access management](../identity-and-access-management) — the enterprise IAM this plugs into
- [Single sign-on (SSO)](../single-sign-on-sso) — the same once-auth-everywhere pattern at agency scale
- [RBAC and authorization](../rbac-and-authorization) — what rides on top of the identity assertion

## Deep dives

- [Secrets, certificates and PKI](../secrets-certificates-and-pki) — the certificate and smart-card mechanics behind PIV
- [Decentralized identity and verifiable credentials](../decentralized-identity-and-verifiable-credentials) — the citizen credential story, modern right
- [Joiner, mover, leaver](../joiner-mover-leaver) — the lifecycle that makes revocation a control, not a chore
- [Zero Trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — identity as the perimeter every federal app assumes32:T193e,
## What this system is

A **grant management system (GMS)** runs the life-cycle of money given by one organization (a federal agency, state, or foundation) to another (a state, university, non-profit, or local government) to carry out a program. Unlike [procurement (source-to-pay)](../procurement-source-to-pay), a grant is *not* a purchase of goods or services — it is the **funding of an outcome**, with the rules for cost allowability, performance, and reporting attached at the moment of award.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    NOFO[Opportunity announced<br/>NOFO / FOA] --> APP[Application + merit review]
    APP --> AW[Award - terms + budget]
    AW --> DD[Drawdown - spending]
    DD --> REP[Performance + financial reporting]
    REP --> CL[Closeout - certify + close]
    CL -.->|lessons| NOFO
```

## The six phases

| Phase | What happens | The artifact that decides it |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **1 · Opportunity** | Agency publishes a funding opportunity (NOFO/FOA) with eligibility, priorities, and evaluation criteria | The announcement is the contract-with-the-applicants |
| **2 · Apply** | Applicants submit a full application: narrative, budget, team, data | The proposal — evaluated against announcement criteria |
| **3 · Review** | Merit review by objective technical reviewers scores against the published criteria | Scores + written critiques, in rank order |
| **4 · Award** | Top-ranked applications get an award with budget, period of performance, and terms & conditions | The award notice — the rules and the money |
| **5 · Operate** | Recipient draws down funds, spends to the budget, reports performance and finances | Progress + financial reports feeding recipient monitoring |
| **6 · Closeout** | Work and spend certified, final reports in, unspent balance returned | The closeout certification |

The defining feature versus a purchase: **nobody "pays" for the performance; the awardee is trusted to spend toward the goal, and the grant is only as good as the monitoring and reporting she can prove.**  That reporting *is* the product of a GMS — not the award record.

## The award-to-drawdown flow

```mermaid
stateDiagram-v2
  [*] --> App: submit
  App: Application - narrative + budget
  App --> Review: submit
  Review: Merit review - scored, evidenced
  Review --> Award: highest ranked
  Award: Award notice - terms + period
  Award --> Draw: approved
  Draw: Drawdown request
  Draw --> Funds: disburse
  Funds: Funds disbursed
  Funds --> Report: account for spend + results
  Report: Progress + financial reports back
  Report --> [*]
```

## The compliance obligations that make GMS distinct

- **Cost allowability** — every dollar must be *allowed* (necessary, reasonable, allocable, consistent): personnel, travel, equipment, indirect. `Unallowable` costs are disallowed at audit.
- **Indirect cost rates** — recipients charge overhead via a negotiated indirect rate; getting the negotiated-rate model wrong is the classic source of year-end disallowances.
- **Drawdown rules** — for the big federal systems, funds are drawn via the Payment Management System (PMS) *as needed*, not paid up-front as a lump sum, and drawdown is tied to actual cash need.
- **Reporting on a calendar** — standard data elements for performance reporting (and the administrative/data standards that gave us the modern tech platforms) mean reports are structured, machine-readable records — a genuine [data-modeling](../data-modeling-star-schemas) problem, not free text.
- **Audit at the top** — recipients are audited, so the GMS must make *evidence traceable*: budget to drawdown to expenditure to report, per award, per period.

## When you'll meet a GMS as an architect

- **Agency side** (the funder): the GMS runs opportunity → review → award → monitoring, publishes notices, and feeds portfolio analytics. It is often *not* an ERP because grants have their own life-cycle and their own [finance/reporting](../erp-finance-and-record-to-report) rules.
- **Recipient side** (university, state government, non-profit): the GMS manages the award book, pre-award budgets, effort allocation, and the drawdown-and-report loop back to the funder. This side lives in the gap between the [HR effort systems](../hr-and-talent-systems) (who works on what) and the general ledger.
- **The seam**: both sides exchange structured award and report data; the best GMS purchases win on *data exchange quality* (standard schema, API, reliable feeds) not on the glamour screens.

## Tester checklist

- Application review integrity: an application is scored only against its announcement's criteria; a score change requires an audit trail with dated reason.
- Award consistency: the award record, the budget, and the notice agree line-for-line, and the budget balances to the total award.
- Drawdown controls: a drawdown in excess of budget or unreleated to cash need is blocked or flagged, and every drawdown is traceable to the award.
- Spend-to-report: month-end financial reports reconcile the budget-to-actual per cost category, and disallowed costs are visible at report time, not audit time.
- Period-of-performance enforcement: spending and reporting outside the award's period (including extensions) are prevented or require an approved amendment.
- Closeout: only awards with all reports in and under-spend resolved can be closed, and the closeout is recorded as the final evidence.

## Related systems

- [Procurement (source-to-pay)](../procurement-source-to-pay) — the sibling workflow for *purchasing* vs *funding*
- [ERP finance and record-to-report](../erp-finance-and-record-to-report) — where award dollars meet the recipient's ledger
- [Enterprise resource planning](../enterprise-resource-planning) — the suite a recipient's pre-award and post-award lives alongside

- [Data governance and catalog](../data-governance-and-catalog) — governed award and reporting reference data
- [Business process modeling and management](../business-process-modeling-and-management) — modeling the NOFO-to-closeout process families
- [System acquisition lifecycle](../system-acquisition-lifecycle) — how an agency itself acquires the GMS
- [Data pipelines (ETL/ELT)](../data-pipelines-etl-elt) — feeding award and report data into shared systems33:T1875,
## What this system is

**Kubernetes (K8s)** is a container-orchestration platform: it takes the containers you built with Docker and runs them reliably at scale — scheduling work onto machines, restarting what fails, scaling what's hot, and connecting the pieces by name. The model has two halves: the **control plane** (the brain: API server, scheduler, controllers) and the **worker nodes** (where containers actually run under a runtime).

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    CP[Control plane - API, scheduler, controllers] --> API[API server]
    API --> ETCD[(etcd - the source of truth)]
    API --> K[Kubelet on each worker node]
    K --> POD1[Pod - app containers]
    K --> POD2[Pod]
    K --> POD3[Pod]
    POD1 --> LB[Service - stable DNS name]
    POD2 --> LB
    POD3 --> LB
```

## Pods and how work is grouped

| Concept | What it is | The mental model |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Pod** | One or more containers sharing a network namespace | The smallest deployable unit — a *sidecar* is a second container in the same pod |
| **Deployment** | The desired-state controller for stateless pods (replicas, rollouts, rollbacks) | "Always keep 3 of these pods up" |
| **Service** | A stable DNS name + load balancer in front of a pod set | Pods die; the service name does not |
| **ConfigMap / Secrets** | Config (and sensitive config) injected as env or volume | Config is data, not a rebuild — see [secrets](../secrets-certificates-and-pki) |
| **Namespace** | Logical partition for teams, environments, or isolation | A cut of the cluster, not a security boundary |

The core promise is **declarative operation**: you write the *desired state* (image, replicas, resource limits, probes) and controllers converge reality toward it. That convergence, not the deployment UI, is the real product — it's how a "Moron wipe" of a node self-heals the workload elsewhere.

## The reconciliation loop, end to end

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant U as Desired state YAML - kubectl apply
    participant A as API server - validates and persists
    participant E as etcd - source of truth
    participant S as Scheduler - places pods on nodes
    participant K as Kubelet - runs and watches pods
    participant H as Ready + serving - through Service

    U->>A: kubectl apply desired state
    A->>E: write
    A->>S: watch for work
    S->>K: bind pod
    K-->>H: reports ready + serving
```

Physically, these reconcile as: controllers keep *current state* equal to *desired state*. If a pod crashes or a node dies, the ReplicaSet control loop notices and spins a replacement. That's the whole trick — and it's why the failures you design Kubernetes for are different: **you now handle pod-restart policies, not process-monitor scripts.**

## When Kubernetes is the right (and wrong) answer

| Choose Kubernetes when | Reach for something simpler when |
| --- | --- |
| Workloads benefit from node pooling, autoscaling, and multi-region placement | A handful of long-running services and heavy ops free for them |
| You already have containers and want self-healing ops | You want a single HTTP service with no rolling/canary needs — see [containers vs plain](../dockerizing-applications) |
| The team has platform skills to own the control plane | Small team — a managed container service with a slick UI is safer |
| Compliance wants reproducible, codeless-image, immutable deploys | You need a super simple stateful datastore with no K8s operator |

The honest advice: **Kubernetes adds an operating system around your containers.** Clusters need a [CI/CD pipeline](../ci-cd-pipelines) to build them, [observability](../monitoring-and-observability) to survive them, and a team that treats the platform as a product. The most common failure on a greenfield Golang or GPT app isn't features — it's adopting K8s before you have (a) verified images, (b) health/liveness probes, and (c) resource requests+limits. Without probes and limits, the scheduler and the autoscaler are flying blind.

## Tester checklist

- Desired-state parity: scale a deployment to N and kill a persistent pod — the controller converges back to N within the SLA, with the replacement reporting Ready.
- Limits are real: a pod exceeding its memory `limits` is restarted (OOMKill) and evicted rather than crashing its node, and CPU `requests` shows in scheduling decisions.
- Probe honesty: a pod whose app is broken but process alive is taken out of the Service (readiness failure) without killing the pod or re-populating it.
- Team continuity: an upgrade of the control plane is rolled (not blown) — the API server stays available and existing workloads are unaffected.
- Secret hygiene: a secret injected via a [Secret object](../secrets-certificates-and-pki) is never visible in pod metadata/env dumps and rotates without a full redeploy path that leaks at any step.
- Security boundary: a namespace compromise doesn't become a cluster compromise — RBAC/NetworkPolicies are enforced as claimed, and a privileged pod is denied where it should be.
- Rollout control: a bad image triggers a rollout that rolls back cleanly to the last-good ReplicaSet, and no traffic loss window is exceeded by the deployment strategy.

- [Dockerizing applications](../dockerizing-applications) — the container images Kubernetes schedules
- [CI/CD pipelines](../ci-cd-pipelines) — how images get built, scanned, and shipped to the cluster
- [Cloud computing fundamentals](../cloud-computing-fundamentals) — the managed-Kubernetes products riding on [AWS](../aws-core-services)/[Azure](../azure-core-services)/[OCI](../oracle-cloud-infrastructure)
- [Monitoring and observability](../monitoring-and-observability) — the telemetry that tells you the cluster is fine

## Deep dives

- [Secrets, certificates and PKI](../secrets-certificates-and-pki) — secrets, service mesh, and TLS in the cluster
- [Service accounts and mTLS / workload identity](../workload-identity-and-mtls) — how pods authenticate to APIs and to each other
- [Database indexing](../database-indexing) — the stateful workloads (databases) that need operators to run well on K8s
- [DevOps delivery loop](../devops-delivery-loop) — the loop that K8s admits and stretches34:Tcf9,
## What this system is

RAG (retrieval-augmented generation) lets an LLM answer questions *about your data* without retraining. Documents are split, embedded as vectors, and searched at query time; only the top matches are stuffed into the prompt. This page diagrams the two halves: **ingestion** and **query**.

## 1. Ingestion — build the searchable index

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  D[Source documents] --> S[Chunker]
  S -->|overlapping chunks| E[Embedding model]
  E --> V[(Vector database)]
  V -->|index: HNSW / IVF| I[ANN index]
```

1. **Chunk**: split documents into logical pieces (~500–1000 tokens) with overlap so meaning isn't severed at boundaries.
2. **Embed**: map each chunk to a fixed-size vector (e.g. 1536 dims); similar meaning → nearby points.
3. **Store**: write vectors into a store like pgvector, Pinecone, or Qdrant.
4. **Index**: approximate nearest-neighbor (ANN) indexes trade a tiny accuracy loss for huge speed on millions of vectors.

## 2. Query — answer with evidence

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant U as User
  participant P as API
  participant E as Embedding model
  participant V as Vector DB
  participant L as LLM

  U->>P: "What is our refund policy?"
  P->>E: embed the question
  P->>V: top-K nearest chunks
  V-->>P: 5 candidate passages + scores
  P->>L: prompt: system rules + passages + question
  L-->>P: grounded answer
  P-->>U: answer + citations
```

## Why the "retrieval" part is where systems die

The model is the easy 50%. Retrieval quality decides everything:

- **Chunk size trade-off**: too small → missing context; too large → diluted relevance.
- **Metadata filters**: `source = support/2024` constrains search and controls the price of retrieval.
- **Re-ranking**: a second, heavier model re-orders the top-N to fix embedding-only misses.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    Q[User question] --> E[Embed question]
    Q -- terms --> K[BM25 keyword search]
    E -- query vector --> V[Vector search - top-K]
    V -- top-K --> HY[Hybrid merge]
    K -- top-K --> HY
    HY -- candidates --> R[Re-ranker]
    R -- 5 passages --> L[LLM - prompt + evidence]
```

## Why hybrid search exists

Embeddings are great at meaning and bad at exact matches (“CVE-2024-1234”). Keyword search is the opposite. **Hybrid retrieval** merges both, then a **re-ranker** picks the final passages — this is the pattern most production systems converge on.

## Tester checklist

- **Grounding**: ask something only in your docs — the answer must cite them, not hallucinate.
- **Blast radius**: inject an adversarial passage into the top-K — does the model follow a competing instruction? (Prompt injection.)
- **Latency budget**: every retrieval stage adds time; measure p95 against your SLO.
- **Version skew**: re-embedding after a doc change must update *all* chunks of that doc (chunk ids → doc ids).

## Related systems

- [LLM agents and workflows](../llm-agents-and-workflows) — how the retrieval result feeds a multi-step agent loop
- [Database indexing](../database-indexing) — vector and B-tree indexes share the same story: no index, no scale
- [Message queue delivery](../message-queue-delivery) — background re-embedding jobs arrive via queues
- [API request lifecycle](../api-request-lifecycle) — the API that fronts this pipeline35:T1a1d,
## What this system is

A **mainframe** (IBM System z is the surviving member of the family) is a massive, purpose-built transactional computer that has quietly run central banking, insurance, airlines, social security, and customs systems for decades. It does not look like a server rack: it is a single, hardened machine with dedicated I/O channels, a hypervisor that runs Linux and z/OS instances side by side, and an operating system — **z/OS** — whose design (address space, data sets, batch, security) predates everything modern by forty years.

Its superpower is **deterministic throughput under heavy, mixed, concurrent transactions**. A mainframe routinely pairs the speed and reliability of a big distributed system with a *single* reconciliation point: one machine, one consistent view, no eventual consistency, no network partition. That is why banking records still live here — and why the fastest mainframe applications outperform distributed clones while the outage history is scandalously good.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    USERS[Online users - CICS transactions] --> ZOS[z/OS - one operating system]
    BATCH[Batch jobs - JCL, night runs] --> ZOS
    ZOS --> DBZ2[Db2 for z/OS - shared data]
    ZOS --> VSAM[VSAM files - legacy data store]
    ZOS --> CICSP[CICS transaction server]
    ZOS --> RACF[Security - RACF, access lists]
```

## The two computings: online and batch

| Mode | Technology | Character |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Online (CICS)** | Transaction server fronting DB2; each request is a short, atomic **transaction** | Sub-second, high concurrency, thousands of transactions per second |
| **Batch (JCL)** | COBOL programs driven by Job Control Language, run at night | Huge file/DB sweeps, end-of-day runs, statements, settlements |
| **Coupling** | z/OS workloads exchange via shared data + messaging (MQ) | Strong consistency between the two modes |

The *batch window* is the existential constraint: banking day ends, the batch must finish before the new day opens. Adding transaction volume shrinks the window; shrinking the window is where modernization pressure comes from — and why mainframe teams are obsessed with **parallel batch, restartability, and checkpoints**.

## The mainframe stack, top to bottom

```mermaid
flowchart TB
    TX["Customers / channels<br/>ATMs, web, cards"] -->|request| CICS["CICS transaction server<br/>maps request to transaction"]
    CICS -->|dispatch| COBOL["COBOL programs<br/>business logic"]
    JCL["Batch - JCL jobs<br/>nightly runs"] -->|night batch| COBOL
    COBOL -->|SQL / I/O| DB2["Db2 / VSAM<br/>the shared data"]
    DB2 -->|z/OS + RACF grant/deny| Z["z/OS + RACF<br/>security + integrity"]
```

The practical truth for an architect: a modern mainframe these days is really a **hybrid z/OS + Linux box** — modern cloud layers (Linux, containers via the hypervisor) sit beside the z/OS core, and the replatforming strategy leans on that split rather than cutting to zero.

## Why it survived — and when it makes sense

| Mainframe strength | What it still buys |
| --- | --- |
| Single consistent ledger | No distributed-systems reconciliation burden for money |
| Massive concurrent I/O | Thousands of transactions per second on one machine |
| Deterministic reliability | Failure rates legacy teams trust and regulators know |
| Data completeness | The whole bank in one Db2/VSAM estate — no dark data shards |

Keep a mainframe when the core is transactionally critical and the cost of moving is higher than the cost of keeping (upgrades, licensed software, skilled staff). Modernize when the *batch window* or the *integration surface* has become the bottleneck that no amount of in-place tuning fixes.

## The strangler, done right

Modernizing a mainframe is a **strangler transformation**: new capabilities live in modern services that talk to the mainframe by API; the legacy transaction stays the source of truth until the day it can be cut over. The pattern:

1. **Publish the core** — expose the CICS/COBOL transactions as APIs (COBOL via CICS Web Services / z/OS Connect), so new front-ends call it not directly but through a service.
2. **Move one capability at a time** — take order fulfillment, or statement generation, off the mainframe into a new service that reads via API and writes back to Db2.
3. **Replicate and compare** — run old and new side by side, compare results, then flip.
4. **Grow the Linux side** — the z/OS core shrinks; the Linux/container partitions and an off-mainframe data replica grow, until the last z/OS workload cuts to the new estate.

The two non-negotiables: **never break the daily batch window during a cutover**, and **keep the single-ledger property** — if you move the ledger off mainframe, you accept the distributed reconciliation you were avoiding all along.

## Tester checklist

- Online atomicity: a CICS transaction must commit or roll back fully — verify an injected failure mid-transaction leaves the Db2 state exactly as before.
- Batch restartability: kill a batch job mid-run; restart must resume from the last checkpoint, not redo the whole window.
- Window discipline: the batch completes inside the fixed night window at padded volume; a new online transaction during batch must not corrupt the shared data.
- Data integrity: Db2/VSAM totals reconcile with downstream (settlements, statements) exactly — one ledger, no drift.
- Security: RACF access is denied/explicitly granted as configured; a request through the API gateway is still subject to RACF rules, not bypassed.
- Strangler parity: old and new services produce identical outputs during parallel run; the flip is reversible.
- Cutover safety: the first day after a capability moves off the mainframe, the batch window still closes on time with the new estate.
