- [Core banking systems](../core-banking-systems) — the domain a bank mainframe actually runs
- [Database indexing](../database-indexing) and [data warehouse and lakehouse architecture](../data-warehouse-lakehouse-architecture) — what Db2/VSAM look like next to modern data stores
- [CI/CD pipelines](../ci-cd-pipelines) — shipping to a modernized hybrid without breaking batch

## Deep dives

- [ERP finance and record-to-report](../erp-finance-and-record-to-report) — the ledger side of mainframe records-to-report
- [Message queue delivery](../message-queue-delivery) — MQ bridging the z/OS core and modern services
- [DevOps delivery loop](../devops-delivery-loop) — bringing delivery discipline to mainframe releases
- [Monitoring and observability](../monitoring-and-observability) — observing a mainframe alongside the distributed estate36:T19f3,
## What this system is

A bank's own ledger is local truth — but money moves between banks through **payment schemes**: shared rulebooks and message networks that let Bank A move value to Bank B on another ledger. The journey has three distinct phases, and confusing them is the classic error in payments architecture:

| Phase | What happens | Risk it bears |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Initiation** | The customer orders a payment; the bank checks funds and sends a message | Rejects, AML, limits |
| **Clearing** | Schemes exchange and match messages, net positions between banks | Message loss, dual processing |
| **Settlement** | Final transfer of value between bank accounts at the central bank (or via nostro accounts) | Settlement risk, liquidity |

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    C[Customer / corporate] --> O[Sender bank]
    O --> CH[Clearing house - message exchange + netting]
    CH --> R1[Recipient bank - credit account]
    O --> S1[Settlement at central bank<br/>debit sender's reserve]
    CH --> S1
    S1 --> S2[Settlement at central bank<br/>credit recipient's reserve]
    R1 --> BW[Beneficiary paid]
```

## The message is the money

Payments run on *messages*, not on moving files of cash. The modern standard is **ISO 20022**: XML messages (`pacs.008` credit transfer, `pacs.002` status, `camt.05X` statements) that carry a real payment and its lifecycle status. Legacy systems used format-per-rail (SWIFT MT10x, SEPA files, domestic files); ISO 20022 unifies them, which is why the industry calls it the language of the future rails. For an architect the practical consequence is: **design your payment domain model around statuses and idempotency keys, not around one wire format.**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant SENDER as "Sender bank"
    participant SCHEME as "Scheme"
    participant CLEAR as "Clearing house"
    participant CB as "Central bank"
    participant BEN as "Beneficiary"
    SENDER->>SENDER: authorize - funds check + AML
    SENDER->>SCHEME: dispatch pacs.008
    SCHEME->>CLEAR: clear
    CLEAR->>CB: net + settle
    CLEAR->>BEN: advise credit
```

## Netting, and why settlement risk is the real enemy

If every payment settled the instant it cleared, a day with 100,000 transfers between the same two banks would move that many individual balances. **Netting** collapses them: each bank nets its per-counterparty position and settles *one* number. This is what makes modern payments volume possible — but it concentrates risk into settlement time:

- **Herstatt / settlement risk**: Bank A pays out on the agreed value date and Bank B fails before paying back. The classical mitigation is **delivery versus payment** (settlement only when the backing transfer is guaranteed) — the same principle as atomic commit in a distributed system.
- **Liquidity**: to net, a bank must fund its account at the central bank; running out is the operational nightmare that real-time gross settlement (RTGS) was invented to support — each payment settles individually against central-bank reserves, immediate and final, at the cost of needing more liquidity.

## The main rails compared

| Rail | Peer | Finality | Typical use |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **RTGS** (Fedwire, TARGET2) | High-value interbank | Real-time, irrevocable | Corporate wires, settlement of other rails |
| **ACH / SEPA** | Batch, low-value | Deferred (next day or two) | Salaries, direct debits, consumer transfers |
| **Instant / RTP** (FedNow, SEPA Instant, UPI) | Real-time, low-value, 24/7 | Immediate | Retail payments, the mobile-app payment |
| **Card schemes** (Visa/MC) | Authorize-then-capture | Clearing after capture | E-commerce and point-of-sale |
| **SWIFT / cross-border** | Correspondent messaging | Two- or three-day correspondent chain | International wires, securities settlement |

The architectural pattern repeats across every rail: **idempotency on the outbound message → status tracking on the inbound status → a reconciliation feed from the clearing house**. Build that pattern once, and adding a new rail is a connector change, not a rearchitecture.

## When this shape shows up

- **Payments hub / enterprise middleware** — one internal service owns outbound schemes, dedupes, and retries, so the core ledger never sends a payment twice.
- **Instant payments**: 24/7 availability, real-time screening, and *the messaging moves at human speed* — a customer aborts a transfer before it "settles" at the speed of thought, so UX and rails must separate *intent* from *irrevocable*.
- **ISO 20022 migration**: the dreaded legacy-to-standard cutover, where the funding challenge is mapping corporate references and address code lists, not the XML.

## Tester checklist

- Idempotency: double-send the same payment request with the same key — exactly one message leaves the bank.
- Status flow: drive a payment through every status (initiated, rejected, cleared, settled, failed, returned) and confirm the internal state march matches the scheme's.
- Rejection path: a customer whose account has insufficient funds is rejected *before* a message is dispatched, with a clean customer-facing status.
- Netting correctness: simulate a day of both-way pairs between two banks and confirm per-counterparty nets and the central-bank settlement sum to zero.
- Reconciliation: the scheme statement (`camt.05X`) ties to internal postings line-by-line, including fees and technical returns.
- Settlement risk: fund the liquidity run solo (one party unfunded) and confirm settlement stalls cleanly, not half-settles.
- Message integrity: one malformed reference or code list value is caught at schema validation and routed to exception handling, not silently dropped.

- [ERP finance and record-to-report](../erp-finance-and-record-to-report) — settlement feeds landing in the GL
- [E-commerce platform architecture](../ecommerce-platform-architecture) — authorization-vs-capture on the card rails
- [Banking risk, fraud, and AML controls](../banking-risk-fraud-and-aml) — the screening every payment passes

- [Message queue delivery](../message-queue-delivery) — the reliable dispatch of outbound payment messages
- [Integration patterns](../integration-patterns) — how scheme connectors plug into the payments hub
- [Monitoring and observability](../monitoring-and-observability) — status and reconciliation monitoring of payment streams
- [Secrets, certificates and PKI](../secrets-certificates-and-pki) — signing and message security on the scheme network37:T176c,
## What this system is

A **quantum computer** computes with **qubits** instead of bits. Where a classical bit is 0 or 1, a qubit is a *superposition* — a probability mixture of 0 and 1 — and two or more qubits can be **entangled**, meaning their states are correlated in ways that have no classical analog. Programs are written as **quantum circuits**: qubits prepared, gates applied, then **measured** back into classical bits.

That is the entire physics, and the hard part is that measurement *destroys the state*: you do not "read" intermediate answers, you run a quantum circuit many times and collect the statistical *distributions* of the outputs. The famous victories (Shor for factoring, Grover for search) exist because those distributions concentrate disproportionately on the right answer with exponentially fewer operations than any classical algorithm.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    Q[Qubits - superposition + entanglement] --> C[Quantum circuit - gates]
    C --> M[Measure - collapse to bits]
    M --> R[Run N times - distribution]
    R --> D[Extract answer statistically]
    Q --> E[Error correction - protects qubits]
```

## Thinking in quantum instead of bits

| Classical bit | Qubit |
| --- | --- |
| Always 0 or 1 | Superposition — a probability over both |
| Many bits = one state of the register | N qubits = 2^N amplitudes in a single state vector |
| Read the answer directly | Measuring collapses the state; you sample the distribution |
| Programs are IF/ELSE | Programs are unitary gates — reversible, no "assignments" |
| Errors are rare and local | Qubits decohere easily; error correction consumes massive overhead |

The mental model for an architect: a quantum program is **not a faster version of a loop** — it is a *different calculus* that only wins on specific problems (factoring, optimization heuristics, quantum chemistry, some simulations). For everything else, a classical box is still better and cheaper.

## The stack today

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    USER["Problem<br/>optimization, chemistry, search"] -->|formulate| ALG["Quantum algorithm<br/>Shor, Grover, VQE, QAOA"]
    ALG -->|circuit| CIR["Circuit compiler<br/>optimizes + maps to hardware"]
    CIR -->|run| HW["Quantum hardware<br/>superconducting / trapped-ion / photonic"]
    CIR -->|map to gate set| CLASS["Classical control + error correction"]
    CLASS -->|compile to gates| HW
    HW -->|qubits measured| OUT["Statistical result<br/>hybrid classical post-processing"]
```

The real architectures are **hybrid**: a classical machine prepares and reads the quantum processor, error-corrects it, and post-processes the samples. Nobody runs a whole business on qubits; the practical pattern today is the quantum processor as an accelerator for a narrow, mathematically hard subroutine called from a classical workflow.

## Where quantum genuinely wins

| Problem domain | Quantum advantage | Classical fallback |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Factoring large integers (Shor) | Exponential speedup — the basis of [post-quantum crypto](../secrets-certificates-and-pki) fears | RSA/ECC breaks as qubit count grows; hence **PQ crypto migration** |
| Unstructured search (Grover) | Quadratic speedup — square root of classical steps | Rarely decides the world; quadratic is no free lunch |
| Quantum chemistry / materials | Natural fit — electrons *are* quantum | Best classical approximations only |
| Combinatorial optimization (QAOA/VQE) | Useful heuristics, but no proven speedup over classical heuristics today | Validates against classical solvers |

The honest scoreboard: **the "quantum advantage" is proven for very few problems, and almost all current machines are narrow, error-prone, and (post-error-correction) slow.** Practical enterprises should plan for *two* realities: keep optimizing classically now, and prepare for the crypto threat (harvest-decrypt: encrypted-now, decrypted-later).

## Tester checklist

- Superposition sanity: a 1-qubit circuit in a balanced superposition returns ~50/50 over many shots, within statistical tolerance — not all 0s or all 1s.
- Gate determinism: the same circuit returns the same distribution when run repeatedly on the same simulator/hardware device within noise budget.
- Measurement collapse: reading a qubit once yields a definite 0 or 1, and re-running masks the state — no "peeking" between gates.
- Error-correction cost: confirm the physical-to-logical qubit overhead and the resulting latency on real hardware versus simulator.
- Hybrid integration: a classical workflow calls the quantum library, parses the returned distribution, and reaches the documented decision path — with a classical fallback when the provider is down.
- Crypto migration planning: inventory which systems depend on RSA/ECC and the harvest-decrypt exposure they carry for the [post-quantum shift](../secrets-certificates-and-pki).
- Noise accounting: results are reported with shot counts and error/readout metrics, and the analysis accounts for those error rates before drawing conclusions.

## Related systems

- [Secrets, certificates and PKI](../secrets-certificates-and-pki) — the crypto stack Shor threatens and the PQ migration that follows
- [Cloud computing fundamentals](../cloud-computing-fundamentals) — where quantum-as-a-service sits in the provider catalog
- [Data warehouse and lakehouse architecture](../data-warehouse-lakehouse-architecture) — where hybrid quantum output lands for analysis

## Deep dives

- [LLM agents and workflows](../llm-agents-and-workflows) — classical AI/optimization that will keep using classical compute for years
- [Monitoring and observability](../monitoring-and-observability) — observing a hybrid classical-quantum pipeline
- [Zero Trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — the security posture that outlives the quantum transition
- [Oracle database ecosystem](../oracle-database-ecosystem) — a reminder that classical data systems remain dominant for decades38:T1306,
## What this system is

**SASE** (Secure Access Service Edge, pronounced "sassy") is the convergence of two previously separate stacks — **SD-WAN** for connectivity and **cloud-native security** — delivered as one cloud service from points of presence (PoPs) distributed around the globe. Gartner coined it in 2019: instead of a WAN that back-hauls all traffic to a data center and applies security there, each branch and remote user connects to the *nearest* PoP, where identity-based policy is enforced once, in a single pass, before routing to SaaS, private, or public internet destinations.

The security half — ZTNA, SWG, CASB, and FWaaS — is sometimes sold on its own as **SSE** (Security Service Edge). SASE = SD-WAN + SSE.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    U[Remote / roaming user] --> POP1[SASE PoP<br/>nearest region]
    B[Branch office] --> POP2[SASE PoP]
    C[Cloud VPC] --> POP2
    POP1 -->|single-pass policy| E[Policy engine<br/>identity, device, risk]
    E -->|ZTNA| PRIV[Private apps / data center]
    E -->|SWG / CASB| SAAS[SaaS apps]
    E -->|FWaaS| INTERNET[Public internet]
```

## The problem SASE replaces

| Classic stack | Effect |
| --- | --- |
| Traffic hairpins to the corporate data center | Remote users and branches travel long, slow paths |
| VPN concentrates access inside the network | Any VPN user can reach the whole "trusted" LAN |
| Security appliances at a central choke point | Not where the traffic actually is anymore |
| Disconnected point products | SWG, CASB, firewall — separate consoles, same gaps |

SASE answers three shifts at once: users everywhere (not in the office), apps in the cloud (not in the DC), and a per-user, per-device world where the old perimeter is gone.

## The four security functions

| Function | Acronym | Role |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Zero Trust Network Access | ZTNA | Per-user, per-app access to private apps — no network-level "inside" |
| Secure Web Gateway | SWG | Filters direct-to-internet web traffic, URL and content policy |
| Cloud Access Security Broker | CASB | Policy and posture on sanctioned SaaS — DLP, shadow-IT discovery |
| Firewall as a Service | FWaaS | Cloud-native firewall and IDS/IPS where the traffic flows |

Identity is the load-bearing wall: SASE policies are **identity + device posture + destination**, evaluated at the PoP nearest the user rather than at a perimeter you no longer control.

## One pass through the PoP

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph Dest["Destinations"]
    SAAS["SaaS<br/>via CASB/SWG"]
    PRIV["Private app<br/>via ZTNA"]
    INT["Public internet<br/>via FWaaS"]
  end
  U["Remote user<br/>+ device posture"] -- "nearest PoP" --> POP["SASE PoP<br/>single-pass engine"]
  POP -- "evaluate identity + policy" --> ID["Identity & posture<br/>SSO + conditional access"]
  ID -- "classified SaaS" --> SAAS
  ID -- "least-privilege app access" --> PRIV
  ID -- "filtered web" --> INT
```

## When SASE makes sense

- **Distributed and remote workforces** — users connect to the nearest PoP instead of a distant VPN concentrator.
- **Cloud-heavy estates** — most traffic is SaaS/public; back-hauling to a DC is pure waste.
- **Branch modernization** — replace router + multiple appliances with one SD-WAN + security edge.
- **Zero Trust rollouts** — ZTNA is the natural on-ramp; identity is already the perimeter.

## Tester checklist

- Verify policy is *identity-based*: a compromised-but-authenticated user still only reaches their allowed apps (ZTNA), not the network.
- Confirm policy is enforced on the **first** pass through the PoP — no split-tunnel escape hatch from SD-WAN to internet bypass.
- Check DLP/classification behavior for unmanaged (BYOD) devices hitting SaaS.
- Test PoP failover: can a session survive a PoP region going down without re-authenticating the user?
- Review the shadow-IT/CASB discovery feed — is it actionable, or just noise?
- Validate that a revoked device loses SASE access promptly (posture is not a binder at login only).

## Related systems

- [Zero Trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — the identity-first model SASE's ZTNA implements
- [SD-WAN and edge connectivity](../sd-wan-and-edge-connectivity) — the connectivity half of the SASE convergence
- [Cloud networking and hybrid connectivity](../cloud-networking-and-hybrid-connectivity) — how branches and PoPs tie into cloud

## Deep dives

- [Conditional access and policy engines](../conditional-access-and-policy-engines) — the per-request decisions SASE applies at the PoP
- [Identity and access management](../identity-and-access-management) — the identity layer every SASE policy hangs off
- [Defense in depth](../defense-in-depth) — layering inside the estate once SASE lets someone in
- [Security Operations Center (SOC)](../security-operations-center-soc) — consuming SASE logs and alerts39:T17f6,
## What this system is

**Smart contracts** are programs deployed onto a blockchain that execute deterministically on every node — code becomes a shared, verifiable public API. A **decentralized application (dApp)** is the combination of a smart contract (the back end, living on-chain) and a normal web front end (off-chain), plus a **wallet** the user uses to sign transactions.

Where a traditional app trusts a server, a dApp trusts *everyone running the contract*. There is no admin account that can edit the ledger's history for a public chain; rules are enforced by the consensus rules described in [Blockchain fundamentals](../blockchain-fundamentals), not by an operator.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    U[User in a browser] --> F[dApp front end<br/>plain HTML/JS]
    F --> W[Wallet<br/>holds private keys]
    W -->|signed transaction| R[RPC endpoint<br/>gateway to chain]
    R --> C[Smart contract<br/>on-chain logic + state]
    C --> E[Blockchain consensus<br/>validates and executes]
```

## What problem it solves

| Classic flow | dApp counterpart |
| --- | --- |
| A server holds a database of balances and a ledger | Balances and state live in the contract's storage, visible to all |
| Back-end logic can be changed or hot-patched at will | Deployed on-chain code is immutable; upgrades are explicit and auditable |
| Users trust the operator not to freeze or censor them | No operator can silently censor a transaction that pays the fee |
| Ownership is "whoever controls the server" | Ownership is "whoever controls the private keys" (self-custody) |

The trade-off is real: the same transparency that removes the operator a trust also removes user privacy (everyone can read all balances) and places strict limits on throughput, so contracts keep only *essential* logic and state on-chain.

## Anatomy of a transaction

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant WAL as "Wallet"
    participant DAPP as "dApp front end"
    participant RPC as "RPC provider"
    participant MEM as "Mempool"
    participant EXEC as "Contract execution"
    participant FINAL as "Confirmed in a block"
    WAL->>DAPP: authorize call
    DAPP->>RPC: submit tx
    RPC->>MEM: queue
    MEM->>EXEC: included in block
    EXEC->>FINAL: committed
```

## Tokens: fungible and non-fungible

| Token type | Example standard | Behavior |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Fungible** | ERC-20 (Ethereum) | Every unit is interchangeable — currency, staking rewards, governance votes |
| **Non-fungible (NFT)** | ERC-721 | Each token is unique — deeds, collectibles, digital art, licenses |
| **Semi-fungible / soulbound** | ERC-1155 / custom | Mixes or non-transferable credentials — the bridge to [decentralized identity](../decentralized-identity-and-verifiable-credentials) |

A token contract is itself just a smart contract keeping a mapping of addresses to balances, with rules like emit an event, transfer, approve, and mint/burn. Any contract or wallet can read those transfers — which is why [Data pipelines (ETL/ELT)](../data-pipelines-etl-elt) indexers exist to normalize on-chain events into analytics tables.

## Wallets and key custody

- **Self-custody**: the wallet holds the private keys; losing them is losing the funds. No "I forgot my password — reset me" path exists.
- **Seed phrase**: keys are derived from a BIP-039 mnemonic; it *is* the account, so it must be handled like [Secrets, certificates and PKI](../secrets-certificates-and-pki) secrets.
- **Hierarchical deterministic (HD) wallets**: one seed derives many addresses, so a dApp can request sub-accounts per session.
- **Contracts as wallets**: multi-sig and smart wallets enforce rules like "need 2 of 3 signers" — moving account policy from a human to code.

## When dApps make sense

- **Open financial primitives** — lending, swaps, stablecoins where transparency and composability beat operator trust.
- **Governance and DAOs** — tokens give votes, and the treasury is a contract, so spending is verifiable by anyone.
- **Protocols that other developers build on** — composability: your contract can call my contract, both serving the same shared state.
- **Self-custody products** — users genuinely control their assets rather than a company's account table.

When in doubt, prefer a normal app: dApps trade performance, privacy, and upgradeability for decentralization. If the "trust third party removed" is a feature you do not actually need, a traditional architecture is cheaper and faster.

## Tester checklist

- Verify the front end never holds keys: signing happens in the wallet, and the dApp only sees the signed payload.
- Test gas/revert paths: confirm the UI reflects a failed transaction (state rollback) instead of silently reporting success.
- Validate access control: call admin-only contract functions as a non-admin and confirm they revert.
- Check reentrancy and integer-overflow behavior with a dedicated security review — these are the classic exploit classes.
- Confirm the UI reads fresh state (post-confirmation) and does not show optimistic stale balances.
- Exercise multi-sig wallets: a transaction authorized by fewer than the quorum must remain pending and unexecutable.
- Verify wallet disconnect/revocation: permissions granted to the dApp are revocable and never persist keys.

## What this system is

The **OWASP Top 10** (2021) is the de-facto checklist of the most common and dangerous web-app weaknesses. It is about *implementation risk* — the flaws that live in code — and complements design-level methods like STRIDE and Zero Trust.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph Ranked["Top 10 (by exploitability + prevalence)"]
    A01[1 · Broken Access Control]
    A02[2 · Cryptographic Failures]
    A03[3 · Injection]
    A04[4 · Insecure Design]
    A05[5 · Security Misconfiguration]
    A06[6 · Vulnerable & Outdated Components]
    A07[7 · Identification & Auth Failures]
    A08[8 · Software & Data-Integrity Failures]
    A09[9 · Security Logging & Monitoring Failures]
    A10[10 · SSRF]
  end
  Ranked -->|feed into| DEF[Defensive checklist: req-by-req & build-time gates]
```

## The ten, distilled

| # | Category | One-line meaning | Classic fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A01 | Broken Access Control | Users reach things they shouldn't | Enforce authz server-side, deny-by-default, no client-side `role` fields |
| A02 | Cryptographic Failures | Crypto used wrong or not at all | TLS everywhere, strong KDFs, no homemade crypto, encrypted secrets at rest |
| A03 | Injection | Untrusted data executed as code | Parameterized queries, strict validation, output encoding |
| A04 | Insecure Design | The *design* has no guardrails | Threat model first, deny-by-default, rate limits, architecture review |
| A05 | Misconfiguration | Defaults, debug modes, open buckets | Config pipelines, security defaults, disable verbose errors |
| A06 | Vulnerable Components | Old libs with known CVEs | Dependency scanning, keep inventory, patch in SLA |
| A07 | ID & Auth Failures | Weak login, session bugs | MFA, strong password rules, lockout, secure session handling |
| A08 | Integrity Failures | Deserialization, tampered pipelines | Signed artifacts, safe deserialization, check integrity |
| A09 | Logging & Monitoring Gaps | Attacks invisible until too late | Structured + complete logs, alerting on anomalies, audit trails |
| A10 | SSRF | Server fetches attacker-chosen URLs | Validate allow-listed destinations, no redirects to internal, metering |

## How the "top" is decided

The list is **data-driven**: it re-ranks by frequency in real scanners, reported incidents, and CTFs — not by academic severity. That's why *Design* and *SSRF* entered in 2021: they were causing outsized damage even at low frequency.

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant S as Source (scanners, bug bounties, CVEs)
  participant O as OWASP
  participant DEV as Development teams
  participant A as Auditors / pentesters

  S->>O: occurrences + exploit data
  O->>O: aggregate & re-rank ~ every 3–4 years
  O->>DEV: Top 10 + ASVS reference
  DEV->>A: verify checklist gates in CI + reviews
  A-->>DEV: findings mapped back to categories
```

## Use it as a *gate*, not a scoreboard

- **Per feature**: at design/implementation, test against the relevant rows (an auth endpoint → A01/A07/A02; an import/export → A08/A03).
- **In CI**: run the automated scans behind it (ZAP, Semgrep) wired to fail the pipeline on Hard/High findings.
- **Interviews & reviews**: if you can't explain *how* you control A01, you have a gap — the Top 10 makes that conversational and specific.
- **It is not a threat model** — it won't tell you a *specific* system's risk profile; pair it with STRIDE for design and Zero Trust for enforcement.

## Tester checklist

- Map your app's endpoints against all ten and note which tax *at least one* control per applicable row.
- Confirm access control is server-enforced: stripping client-side flags must break elevated features.
- Enable verbose errors in staging and confirm the *staging* baseline is not your production config.
- Spot-check the dependency scanner: known-CVE packages must block the build, not just warn.

## Related systems

- [Security standards landscape](../security-standards-landscape) — where OWASP sits among Zero Trust, NIST, ISO 27001, SOC 2
- [Threat modeling with STRIDE](../threat-modeling-stride) — the design-level companion to OWASP
- [Zero Trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — the enforcement layer behind these controls
- [Authentication vs authorization](../authentication-vs-authorization) — A01/A07 in action3b:Tc20,
## What this system is

Threat modeling is not a pentest — it happens **before code** (or before major changes) by asking "if someone attacks us, which of these six generic threats would bite?" STRIDE names the six:

| Letter | Threat | The attacker… | Example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **S** | Spoofing | pretends to be someone else | forging a username / token |
| **T** | Tampering | modifies data or code | changing a transfer amount mid-flight |
| **R** | Repudiation | denies doing something | "I never placed that order" (no audit trail) |
| **I** | Information disclosure | reads data not meant for them | viewing another user's dashboard |
| **D** | Denial of service | makes the system unusable | flooding an endpoint |
| **E** | Elevation of privilege | gains rights they lack | turning a reader into an admin |

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  DF[Draw data-flow diagram] --> EN[Enumerate STRIDE threats<br/>per trust boundary]
  EN --> RK[Rank by likelihood x impact]
  RK --> MI[Mitigate<br/>+ validate with a spike]
  MI --> DO[Document decisions & owners]
  DO -.->|iterate once a quarter| EN
```

## How to run a five-minute pass

1. **Draw it**: components, data stores, trust boundaries (the dashed lines where identity flips — UI vs API vs DB).
2. **For each boundary, ask STRIDE**: at the crossing from the internet → app, S and I and D dominate; at app → DB, T and I dominate.
3. **Score 1–3 each**: likelihood × impact. Fix anything ≥ 6 first — don't gold-plate the rest.
4. **Prove mitigations**: tests that would fail if a threat returned (see checklist).

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  TRUST1[User / Browser<br/>untrusted] -->|over the internet| TRUST2[API layer<br/>partially trusted]
  TRUST2 -->|1 mTLS + ACLs| TRUST3[Database<br/>trusted]
  TRUST1 --> B1{Spoofed token?}
  TRUST2 --> B2{Tampered payload?}
  TRUST2 --> B3{Data leak via over-fetching?}
  B1 --> M1[verify signature + exp]
  B2 --> M2[sign request / validate model]
  B3 --> M3[column-level access control]
```

## Common pitfalls

- **Modeling threats you can't ship** — a mitigation that never ships is worse than none ("documented as risk" ≠ fixed).
- **Forgetting the insider** — the most expensive threats often sit *inside* trust boundaries (shared DB access).
- **OWASP ≠ STRIDE** — OWASP Top 10 is implementation bugs; STRIDE is design. Both belong on the roadmap, at different stages.

## Tester checklist

- Choose the top-ranked threat and write a test that *would have* caught it.
- Verify mitigations exist at the right trust boundary, not one hop downstream.
- Confirm repudiation is covered: audit logs include actor, action, timestamp, and are append-only.
- Re-run the DFD when the design changes — models rot fast.

## Related systems

- [Zero Trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — STRIDE finds what Zero Trust then enforces
- [Session cookies and CSRF](../session-cookies-and-csrf) — a classic STRIDE finding (spoofing via cookie / CSRF noise)
- [API request lifecycle](../api-request-lifecycle) — draw the DFD of the service you're threat-modelling3c:Tff1,
## What this framework is

TOGAF (The Open Group Architecture Framework) answers the question Zachman deliberately avoids: **how do you actually produce architecture?** Its engine is the **ADM** — Architecture Development Method — a staged cycle from vision to overshoot to closure control.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  P[Preliminary<br/>Framework & principles]
  A[Phase A<br/>Architecture Vision]
  B[Phase B<br/>Business Architecture]
  C[Phase C<br/>Info Systems Architecture]
  D[Phase D<br/>Technology Architecture]
  E[Phase E<br/>Opportunities & Solutions]
  F[Phase F<br/>Migration Planning]
  G[Phase G<br/>Implementation Governance]
  H[Phase H<br/>Architecture Change Management]

  P --> A
  A --> B
  B --> C
  C --> D
  D --> E
  E --> F
  F --> G
  G --> H
  H -.->|requirements management| A
```

## What each phase produces

| Phase | Output your organisation can use | Typical audience question |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Preliminary | Principles, tooling choices, architecture repo | "How will we even keep this?" |
| A · Vision | Stakeholder map, value proposition, high-level roadmap | "Why are we doing this?" |
| B · Business | Capability map, value streams, target KPIs | "Which processes improve?" |
| C · Information Systems | Data + application architecture (see Zachman *What/How*) | "Which systems and data change?" |
| D · Technology | Technology reference architecture, platform choices | "Which servers, cloud, tooling?" |
| E · Opportunities | Work packages, project portfolio | "What do we build first?" |
| F · Migration | Transition architectures + a sequenced migration plan | "How do we get there safely?" |
| G · Governance | Architecture compliance reviews, change control | "Is the build still on-spec?" |
| H · Change | Architecture change requests, continuous refinement | "What happens when reality disagrees?" |

## The one thing that makes it "TOGAF"

The ADM is **iterative and requirement-driven**, not a one-shot waterfall. Every arrow from B→H is hybrid with the **Requirements Management** loop in the middle:

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  C1[Discovery] --> R[Requirements Management<br/>continuously re-fed]
  D1[Design] --> R
  G1[Governance] --> R
  R --> SA[Stakeholder acceptance]
```

Two traps kill ADM rollouts in practice:

1. **Phases A–D done with no E–G** — architecture becomes a shelf document nobody implements. TOGAF is explicit: opportunities, migration, and governance are part of the cycle, not afterthoughts.
2. **Method without a repository** — without an architecture repository (the "meta-model" that stores artefacts), every new proposal regenerates the same diagrams by hand.

## How a solution architect works the cycle

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant SA as Solution Architect
  participant Biz as Business Stakeholders
  participant EA as Enterprise Architect
  participant PA as Platform / Ops

  SA->>Biz: Phase A: document vision & concerns
  Biz-->>SA: business goals + constraints
  SA->>EA: align to current baseline architecture
  EA-->>SA: existing standards & reference models
  SA->>SA: Phase C+D: draft target data/technology design
  SA->>PA: feasibility check against platform
  PA-->>SA: gaps, risks, cost input
  SA->>Biz: Phase E: option & opportunity analysis
  SA->>EA: Phase G: compliance review of final design
  EA-->>SA: approve / reject with gaps
```

## Tester-friendly checklist

- Traceability: every Phase C requirement must map to an ADM artefact and back — a governance review will ask for it.
- Baseline-vs-target diffs: the *transition architecture* (Phase F) is what gets tested first, before the target exists.
- Compliance reviews (Phase G) need to run during implementation, not at go-live.

## Related systems

- [The Zachman Framework](../zachman-framework) — the classification grid TOGAF fills cell by cell
- [API request lifecycle](../api-request-lifecycle) — a Phase C artefact traced to running code
- [OAuth 2.0 and OpenID Connect](../oauth2-openid-connect) — a trust-boundary design pattern an architect would put in the repo3d:Tf21,
## What this system is

Classic network security is a **castle**: walls (firewall) around an internal "trusted" LAN, where anything inside is assumed safe. **Zero Trust** inverts that assumption — every request, from anywhere, is untrusted until it proves identity, context, and policy.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  subgraph Castle["Traditional perimeter model"]
    direction LR
    U1[User] --> FW1[Firewall / VPN] --> INSIDE1[(Trusted LAN)]
    INSIDE1 --> S1[(Server)]
    INSIDE1 --> S2[(Database)]
    INSIDE1 --> S3[(Old internal tool)]
  end

  subgraph ZT["Zero Trust model"]
    direction LR
    U2[User] -->|"verify identity + policy"| POL2[Policy engine<br/>continuous authz]
    POL2 -->|"microsegmentation"| S4["App A"]
    POL2 -->|"append network path"| S5["App B"]
    POL2 -->|"never implied trust"| S6["App C (least privilege)"]
  end
```

## The three pillars

1. **Assume breach** — design as if the attacker is already inside the network. Lateral movement is the target to stop.
2. **Identity is the new perimeter** — access is granted per user + device + context, not per subnet. Mutual TLS (mTLS) authenticates both sides.
3. **Enforce every request** — no "trusted once, trusted forever": re-evaluate on each session, each request.

## Key building blocks

| Block | Role |
| --- | --- |
| Identity provider (SSO) | Source of user identity and posture signals |
| Policy engine (PEP/PDP) | Decides every allow/deny per request + context |
| mTLS (service mesh) | Encrypts + authenticates *between services*, not just at the edge |
| Microsegmentation | Logical isolates per workload so a compromise can't hop services |
| Just-in-time access | Privileged access granted for a window, then revoked |

## A request under Zero Trust

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant U as User
  participant EC as Enforcer (edge / proxy)
  participant PE as Policy engine
  participant IDP as Identity provider
  participant S as Backend service

  U->>EC: request (token + device posture)
  EC->>PE: evaluate identity, device, context, risk
  PE->>IDP: verify login + posture
  IDP-->>PE: trusted (or deny)
  PE-->>EC: decision: allow with minimal scope
  EC->>S: mTLS handshake, audited, scoped
  S-->>U: response (limited to allowed actions)
```

## Why teams adopt it

- **Lateral movement dies**: even with VPN access, a user only reaches what policy allows per workload.
- **Blast radius shrinks**: a stolen workstation does not equal "everything".
- **Remote-anywhere becomes safe**: zero network sprawl, zero "just add my home IP".
- **Costs are real**: identity sprawl, per-service policies, and latency from continuous checks — budget for them.

## Tester checklist

- Verify policy is evaluated on *every* request, not cached at login.
- Confirm mTLS actually fails when a service is impersonated.
- Check that a device with a revoked posture signal loses access promptly (not at next login).
- Look for bootstrap holes: is there a path that trusts "internal network" implicitly?

## Related systems

- [Authentication vs authorization](../authentication-vs-authorization) — the per-request authz Zero Trust depends on
- [OAuth 2.0 and OpenID Connect](../oauth2-openid-connect) — identity layer every ZT design plugs into
- [TOGAF and the ADM](../togaf-framework) — where a Zero Trust target architecture lives in the ADM cycle

## Deep dives

- [Identity and access management](../identity-and-access-management) — the identity layer the whole model rests on
- [Conditional access and policy engines](../conditional-access-and-policy-engines) — how every request becomes a policy decision
- [Workload identity and mTLS](../workload-identity-and-mtls) — machines get identities too, east-west traffic locked down
- [Secrets, certificates and PKI](../secrets-certificates-and-pki) — the crypto that makes identities trustworthy3e:Tea3,
## What this system is

Android corporate management is built around one idea: **separation of worlds**. Android's enterprise APIs split a single device into a *personal* side and a *managed* *work profile* side (or a fully-managed single profile for company-owned). Corporate apps, data, config, and wipe live entirely inside the work container — the personal side stays the user's.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Device[Device<br/>factory reset] -->|scan once| Enroll[QR scan / NFC<br/>enrollment token]
  Enroll -->|enroll DPC| EMM[EMM server<br/>DPC installed]
  EMM -->|managed Google Play| Work[Work profile<br/>corporate apps]
  Work -->|hard isolation| Personal[Personal space<br/>user data]
  EMM -->|compliant| Managed[Managed and isolated]
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  E[EMM: Intune / Workspace One / MobileIron] -->|managed Google Play + policy| D[Device]
  D --> W[Work profile<br/>corporate apps + data]
  D --> P[Personal space<br/>user's apps + photos]
  W -->|isolation| P
```

## The three management models

| Model | BYOD (work profile) | COPE (fully managed) | Dedicated (kiosk) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Ownership | User's device | Company-owned | Company-owned |
| Corporate apps | In work profile | Whole device is corporate | Single/multi-app kiosk mode |
| User privacy | Personal side untouched | Personal apps possible but weaker promise | No personal use |
| Wipe | Wipes only work profile | Full factory reset | Full reset |
| Best fit | Employee phones | Sales/field fleets | POS, signage, rugged devices |

## Provisioning a device (no user input needed)

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant Dev as Device (factory reset)
  participant QR as QR / provision network
  participant EMM as EMM server
  participant G as Managed Google Play

  Dev->>QR: scan QR (or boot to hotspot / NFC tap)
  QR-->>Dev: EMM endpoint + enrollment token
  Dev->>EMM: enroll DPC
  EMM->>G: claim device in managed Play
  EMM->>Dev: push work profile + managed config
  Dev->>Dev: corporate apps install into work profile only
```

Provisioning bypasses typing: factory-reset a device, scan a QR code (or tap an NFC tag in Android 10+), and the EMM takes over within minutes. This is how fleets get onboarded in warehouses and clinics without IT hands.

## How the work profile really isolates

- Android runs the personal and work sides as **separate users** under one OS; the work container is encrypted and permission-scoped.
- **Managed config** (per-app JSON-like settings) pushes policies directly into apps via Google Play — no MDM agent inside each app required.
- IT can `wipe the work profile only` on BYOD, leaving photos and personal apps intact — the legal/UX reason employees accept corporate EMM.
- Work apps' **network** can be forced through always-on VPN and DPC-managed certificates; data does not cross profiles without managed-mode paths.

## Tester checklist

- Enroll twice (BYOD work profile + a COPE device) and verify which management capabilities each exposes in the EMM console.
- Confirm work-profile wipe removes corporate apps/data but preserves personal data, and that the device rejoins cleanly.
- Push a managed config to an app and verify it arrives without user action, and that only work-side instances read it.
- Check kiosk/dedicated mode locks correctly: no home button escape, no personal apps, auto-restart on crash.

## Related systems

- [iOS and macOS in the enterprise](../ios-macos-in-the-enterprise) — Apple's supervised-for-corporate counterpart
- [Zero trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — work-profile posture is a condition of access
- [Authentication vs authorization](../authentication-vs-authorization) — work-profile identity gate for corporate apps3f:T9f5,
## What this system is

A cache at the **edge** (CDN or gateway) is the biggest latency win available: a hit never touches your origin. This guide focuses on how cache entries are decided and invalidated — the interactive diagram below is the same flow you can drag around.

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant C as Client
  participant G as API Gateway
  participant E as Edge Cache
  participant O as Origin Server

  C->>G: GET /api/users
  G->>E: lookup cache key
  alt cache hit
    E-->>G: serve cached response
  else cache miss
    E->>O: miss, forward to origin
    O-->>G: 200 + Cache-Control
  end
  G-->>C: serve response to client
```

## The cache decision, in plain words

1. The gateway computes a **cache key** — typically `method + path + normalized query + auth scope`.
2. It looks the key up in the edge cache.
3. **Hit**: serve directly. **Miss**: forward to origin.
4. The origin answers with a `Cache-Control` header that says *how long it may be cached (and by whom)*.
5. On the first request after expiry, the gateway can serve the stale copy while fetching fresh data in the background = **stale-while-revalidate**.

## Headers that matter

| Header | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `Cache-Control: max-age=60` | Cache for 60 s |
| `Cache-Control: public` | Shared caches may store it |
| `Cache-Control: private` | Only the browser may store it — often right for user-specific data |
| `ETag` | Validator: revalidate with `If-None-Match` for a cheap `304` |
| `Vary: Accept-Encoding` | Cache key must include the language/encoding of the response |

## The classic bugs

- **A `private`-only response cached publicly** → user A sees user B's dashboard.
- **Ignoring the cache key for auth** → authenticated and anonymous users sharing entries.
- **Unbounded `max-age` on mutable data** → broken or stale product data until expiry.
- **Missing `Vary`** → a caches compressed and uncompressed variants under the same key.

## Tester checklist

- Inspect the `Cache-Control` header on responses — is `private` data marked private?
- Change a user's profile and confirm the edge does not still serve the old version.
- With `stale-while-revalidate`, confirm the first post-expiry request is still fast (stale served).
- Check `Age` header to see how old a cached response actually is.

## Related systems

- [API request lifecycle](../api-request-lifecycle) — the chain this cache sits inside
- [OAuth 2.0 and OpenID Connect](../oauth2-openid-connect) — why authenticated responses need careful cache keys40:T9b8,
## What this system is

Authentication (**authn**) proves *who* you are. Authorization (**authz**) decides *what you may do*. They are separate stages with separate mechanisms — and the fastest way to leak data is to check one and assume the other happened.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  A[User] -->|1. prove identity<br/>username + password / MFA| B[Authenticate<br/>who are you?]
  B -->|2. identity established| C[Authorize<br/>what may you do?]
  C -->|policy check: role, scope, resource| D{Allowed?}
  D -->|yes| E[Perform action]
  D -->|no| F[403 Forbidden]
```

## The sequence, spelled out

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant U as User
  participant API as API / App
  participant IDP as Identity Provider
  participant POL as Policy Engine

  U->>API: GET /admin/reports
  API->>IDP: authn: validate bearer token
  IDP-->>API: identity = alice (confirmed)
  API->>POL: authz: does alice have role=admin for this scope?
  POL-->>API: allow / deny
  API-->>U: 200 data  or  403 forbidden
```

## Watch the failure modes

| Mistake | Consequence |
| --- | --- |
| Skip authz after authn | Any logged-in user reaches admin endpoints |
| Judge by `isAuthenticated` flag only | No per-resource boundaries |
| Authorize with data the client claims (`?role=admin`) | Tampering grants privileges |
| Same check for read vs write | A read-only user can mutate state |
| Empty `/403` on wrong role | Leaks that the resource exists |

## Common pairings

| Concept | Where it lives |
| --- | --- |
| Authn: passwords, MFA, OAuth tokens | Identity provider, login flow |
| Authz: RBAC, ABAC, scopes, ACLs | Policy engine, middleware, per-resource guards |
| Authn failure | `401 Unauthorized` |
| Authz failure | `403 Forbidden` |

A shorthand many architects use: *authn tells you the **who**, authz tells you the **what** — never skip the second question.*

## Tester checklist

- Verify each endpoint's authz *independently* of authn — log in as a low-privilege user and hit privileged endpoints.
- Check 403 arrives without revealing whether the resource exists.
- Test that scopes / roles are read from the server (token/DB), never from a client-supplied field.

## Related systems

- [OAuth 2.0 and OpenID Connect](../oauth2-openid-connect) — the authn handshake for tokens
- [JWT access tokens](../jwt-tokens) — how identity travels in stateless APIs
- [Zero Trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — authz as a network-wide policy, not a per-app afterthought41:T1044,
## What this system is

AWS is a catalog of services; the art is knowing the ~15 that matter and how they compose. The canonical reference deployment: a **VPC** with public/private subnets across availability zones, a load balancer up front, compute in private subnets, a managed database, and object storage — all gated by **IAM**.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph Pub["Public layer"]
    In[Internet] -- edge --> IGW[Internet gateway]
    IGW -- public route --> ALB[ALB<br/>multi-AZ]
  end
  subgraph App["App layer - private subnets"]
    ECA[EC2 - AZ a<br/>private subnet]
    ECB[EC2 - AZ b<br/>private subnet]
  end
  subgraph Data["Data layer"]
    RDS[(RDS<br/>multi-AZ)]
    S3[(S3 bucket)]
  end
  ALB -- route --> ECA
  ALB -- route --> ECB
  ECA -- SQL --> RDS
  ECB -- SQL --> RDS
  ECA -- assets --> S3
  ECB -- assets --> S3
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  In[Internet] --> IGW[Internet gateway]
  IGW --> ALB[Application load balancer<br/>multi-AZ]
  ALB --> S1[EC2 app server<br/>AZ a - private subnet]
  ALB --> S2[EC2 app server<br/>AZ b - private subnet]
  S1 --> RDS[(RDS database<br/>multi-AZ)]
  S2 --> RDS
  S1 --> S3[(S3 bucket<br/>static + backups)]
  S2 --> S3
```

## The services that matter, in one map

| Category | The workhorses | When to reach for them |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Compute | **EC2**, **Lambda**, ECS/EKS, Fargate | VMs; event-driven/serverless; containers |
| Storage | **S3**, EBS, EFS | Object lake hot+cold; volumes; shared fs |
| Network | **VPC**, Route 53, CloudFront, ALB | Private networks; DNS; CDN |
| Database | **RDS**, DynamoDB, Redshift | Relational/multi-AZ; NoSQL; warehouse |
| Identity | **IAM**, Cognito | Access control; user identity |
| Orchestration | CloudFormation/Terraform | Everything defined as code |

## Serverless vs always-on compute

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  Q[Workload shape?] --> R{Load is...}
  R -->|spiky / event-driven| L[Lambda + SQS/EventBridge<br/>pay per invocation]
  R -->|steady or stateful| E[EC2 / ECS<br/>predictable, cheaper sustained]
  R -->|containerized microservices| K[ECS / EKS - managed k8s]
```

Lambda scales to zero and bills per millisecond — great for APIs, event handlers, glue. EC2/EKS wins when you need predictable sockets, long-lived state, or lowest steady cost. The trigger mapping (S3 events → Lambda, SQS → Lambda) is the glue.

## IAM in five minutes

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  U[User / service / Lambda role] --> Pol[Policy: what action on what resource]
  Pol --> Res[S3 bucket, RDS, API endpoint]
  Policy[Allow s3:GetObject on own bucket prefix]
```

Rules that never break: **identity-based policies** attach to users/roles; **resource-based policies** attach to the thing (bucket policy). A call must be allowed by BOTH where both exist. Least privilege is not a tweak — it's how you avoid "the whole team has Administrator on prod."

## The mistake repeatedly remembered

- Public security group on the database. (Fail the Friday random-scan chat.)
- One IAM user per human with an access key in a notes file — short-lived, role-using credentials, MFA.
- Single-AZ "because it's cheaper" — [fundamentals](../cloud-computing-fundamentals) says lose-one-AZ-without-down-time is the bar for anything important.

## Tester checklist

- Confirm IAM least privilege: the app's role can *only* touch its own S3 prefix, its DB, and outbound to what it needs.
- Delete/stop a Node in the private subnet and verify the ALB health-checks it out and the remaining Node serves.
- Verify an S3 event actually fires to Lambda on upload (object events are the glue in most pipelines).
- Do the cost report drill: right-size the three most expensive resources you found this month.

- [Cloud computing fundamentals](../cloud-computing-fundamentals) — the regions/AZ/model concepts behind these services
- [Azure core services](../azure-core-services) — the identical job with Azure names
- [Cloud networking and hybrid connectivity](../cloud-networking-and-hybrid-connectivity) — VPC peering, transit, and private links
- [API gateway caching](../api-gateway-caching) — the L7 layer in front of your compute42:T1790,
## What this system is

Azure API Management (APIM) is a managed **front-of-house** for APIs: every call to your backend services passes through its gateway, which enforces keys, applies transformation policies, meters usage, and can mock or fail safely. It's the Azure-native answer to "put something in front of our APIs that isn't a bespoke gateway". Three surfaces matter: the **gateway** (runtime), the **publisher portal** (you/control plane), and the **developer portal** (your consumers discover and try your APIs).

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  C[Consumer app] -->|HTTPS| G[APIM gateway]
  G --> P[Policies:<br/>keys, rate limits, transforms]
  P --> B[Backend services<br/>- e.g. Function App / AKS / Logic Apps]
  C -. try + subscribe .-> Dp[Developer portal]
  Op[Publisher portal / CLI / GitHub Actions] -. configure .-> G
```

## The request path

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant C as Client
  participant G as APIM Gateway
  participant P as Policy pipeline
  participant B as Backend

  C->>G: GET /products?x-api-key=…
  G->>G: validate subscription key (or OAuth)
  G->>P: inbound policies - rate limit, quota, transform
  P->>B: forward to backend URL
  B-->>P: response
  P->>P: outbound policies - header strip, encoding
  P-->>C: response to client
```

Every request runs through the **policy pipeline** twice: once *inbound* (auth, limit, transform before hitting your backend) and once *outbound* (normalize responses, strip internal headers, add caching). That's APIM's whole superpower: behavior is added between consumer and backend without touching backend code.

## Tiers and where to put it

| Tier | Positioning | Fits |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Developer | Testing, low SLA, no SLA uptime guarantee | Dev/QA only |
| Basic | Entry production, cheaper, limited features | Small APIs, low traffic |
| Standard | Production default, SLA, more policies | Most production workloads |
| Premium | VNet injection, multi-region, >10k calls/sec | Enterprise, regulatory, DR |

APIM is typically deployed **in front of** your other PaaS (Functions, App Service, AKS, Logic Apps) — acting as the single entry point, so those backends can be locked down to accept traffic only from the gateway. Putting it *behind* your own network (Premium + VNet) is how APIM becomes an internal API router too.

## Identity: keys AND OAuth

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph Keys["Subscription keys"]
    K1[Ocp-Apim-Subscription-Key header]
    K2["Identify the caller - app, not user"]
    K3["Fine for server-to-server burst control"]
  end
  subgraph OAuth["OAuth 2.0 / JWT"]
    O1[validate-jwt policy]
    O2["Identify the human or service principal"]
    O3[Claims: roles, audiences]
  end
  C[Requests] --> Keys
  C --> OAuth
```

The three layers of APIM identity most teams need:

- **Subscription keys** prove *who's calling* (which registered app), used for metering/quotas — but they are **not** security: they travel in plaintext headers and are shared. Never rely on them as an authz boundary.
- **`validate-jwt`** policy checks the JWT a caller presents: issuer, audience, expiry, and claims. This is where *your* identity provider's tokens actually gate access.
- **Client certificates / mTLS** for machine-to-machine when keys are too weak — APIM can require the caller's cert on the connection.

## The policy DSL

Policies are declarative XML that read like no-code middleware:

```xml
<inbound>
  <rate-limit calls="100" renewal-period="60" />
  <validate-jwt header-name="Authorization" failed-validation-httpcode="401">
    <required-claims>
      <claim name="aud" match="all">
        <value>https://api.myapp.com</value>
      </claim>
    </required-claims>
  </validate-jwt>
</inbound>
```

The patterns that matter in production: **rate-limit & quota** (protect the backend), **validate-jwt** (identity), **set-backend-service** (route to a different backend per environment), **mock** (return canned responses so the frontend can build while the backend catches up), and **cors** (or better, keep CORS off and front it properly).

## Key gotchas

- **Keys ≠ authz.** Treat subscription keys as throttling/metering, never as a security perimeter.
- **The gateway adds latency** — a few ms, but every request pays it; benchmark with APIM in and out of the path.
- **Default response caching just caches**: set proper `Cache-Control` and policy `cache-store` to benefit, or you cache stale mutable data.
- **Don't let every backend call route through APIM** for high-throughput internal chatter — use it as the *front door*, not the plumbing.
- **Policy errors silently 500** unless you add error handling: put `retry`, `on-error` sections so transient backend failures have a story.

## Tester checklist

- **Auth matrix**: no key, wrong key, expired/malformed JWT, wrong audience — verify each gets the intended status (401/403/429), not a leak to the backend.
- **Rate limit**: burst past the limit and confirm 429s + headers; confirm quota resets on the defined window.
- **Transform fidelity**: a header-transform policy on outbound actually strips internal headers (server, internal IPs).
- **Backend lockdown**: call the backend URL directly (bypassing APIM) and confirm it rejects with no key.
- **Latency budget**: measure p95 through APIM vs direct to confirm the front-door tax is within your SLA.

- [API request lifecycle](../api-request-lifecycle) — the full journey this gateway sits at the mouth of
- [API gateway caching](../api-gateway-caching) — the caching half APIM's gateway policy implements
- [Azure core services](../azure-core-services) — APIM alongside App Service, Functions, Event Hubs
- [Serverless computing and functions](../serverless-computing-and-functions) — the common backend APIM fronts (Function Apps)
- [OAuth 2.0 and OpenID Connect](../oauth2-openid-connect) — the tokens `validate-jwt` parses
- [Threat modeling STRIDE](../threat-modeling-stride) — why you still front a gateway with real OWASP hygiene43:T12d8,
## What this system is

Azure's most distinctive ideas are **resource management** (every resource lives in a resource group inside a subscription, under a management hierarchy) and **PaaS first** (App Service, Functions, and managed SQL are the default path, with VMs as the escape hatch). The analogous reference deployment to the AWS one:

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph Pub["Public layer"]
    In[Internet] -- edge --> GW[Application gateway<br/>multi-zone]
  end
  subgraph App["App layer"]
    AV1[App Service - zone 1]
    AV2[App Service - zone 2]
  end
  subgraph Data["Data layer"]
    ASQL[(Azure SQL<br/>HA + geo-replica)]
    STG[(Blob storage<br/>static + backups)]
  end
  GW -- route --> AV1
  GW -- route --> AV2
  AV1 -- SQL --> ASQL
  AV2 -- SQL --> ASQL
  AV1 -- assets --> STG
  AV2 -- assets --> STG
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  In[Internet] --> GW[Application gateway<br/>multi-zone]
  GW --> AV1[App Service / VM<br/>zone 1]
  GW --> AV2[App Service / VM<br/>zone 2]
  AV1 --> SQL[(Azure SQL Database<br/>HA + geo-replica)]
  AV2 --> SQL
  AV1 --> ST[(Blob storage)]
  AV2 --> ST
```

## The management tree

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  Root[Microsoft Entra ID tenant]
  Root --> MG1[Management group - prod]
  Root --> MG2[Management group - non-prod]
  MG1 --> Sub1[Subscription - prod apps]
  MG1 --> Sub2[Subscription - prod data]
  Sub1 --> RG1[(Resource group: app-rg)]
  RG1 --> R1[App Service - web tier]
  RG1 --> R2[Function app - background]
  Sub2 --> RG2[(Resource group: data-rg)]
  RG2 --> R3[Azure SQL Database]
  RG2 --> R4[Storage account]
```

RBAC policies, budgets, and guardrails are inherited **top-down** from management groups → subscriptions → resource groups. Put sensitive data in its own subscription so a mis-wired policy can't silently cross the boundary.

## The services that matter, in one map

| Category | The workhorses | When to reach for them |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Compute | **VMs**, **App Service**, **Functions**, AKS | VMs; web apps; serverless; containers |
| Storage | **Blob storage**, Azure Files, disks | Object store; shared fs; volume |
| Network | **VNet**, Azure Load Balancer/App Gw, DNS | Private networks; entry points |
| Database | **Azure SQL**, Cosmos DB, PostgreSQL Flex | Relational; NoSQL; managed alternatives |
| Identity | **Microsoft Entra ID** (formerly AAD) | Users, service principals, device identity |
| Govern | Policy, budgets, RBAC | Guardrails + cost control |

## PaaS staging: the slot model

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  AppSvc[App Service] --> Slot1[Staging slot]
  AppSvc --> Slot2[Production slot]
  Slot1 -->|warm-up + swap| Slot2
```

App Service slots let you deploy to a **staging slot**, warm it, then `swap` it into production atomically — the simple, built-in answer to zero-downtime releases. AKS/Linux are the escape hatches when slots stop fitting.

## RBAC and identity: two different knobs

| | Azure RBAC | Entra ID roles |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Grants | Access to *resources* (read/write a storage account) | Tenant/admin powers (run identity, reset passwords) |
| Examples | Contributor on app-rg | Global admin, app admin |
| Mindset | Least privilege per resource | Only the few people with tenant-wide power |

A recurring incident source: someone with Entra-tier "Azure AD admin" authority — sure, it's not "resource access," but it can reset the identity of everything. Keep the two separate in your head.

## Governance knobs that stop incidents before they start

- **Policy**: enforce "production storage must encrypt + have network isolation" as *policy*, not a preference.
- **Budgets + alerts**: get woken when cost deviates, not at the month-end invoice.
- **Management groups**: a clear prod vs non-prod split is the cheapest firewall you'll ever buy.
- **Deletion locks** on resources you'd cry over.

## Tester checklist

- Swap a slot mid-traffic and confirm zero dropped requests and instant rollback by swapping back.
- Verify RBAC cascade: escalate into the data subscription from the app subscription should be *blocked by inheritance*, not by a friendlier rule.
- Break a region dependency (stop zonal resources) and confirm the HA pair (SQL geo-replica, second zone) takes over.
- Run a budget report: which top-3 resources tripled? Investigate right-sizing before you pay another invoice.

## Related systems

- [Cloud computing fundamentals](../cloud-computing-fundamentals) — the regions/AZ/model concepts behind these services
- [AWS core services](../aws-core-services) — the identical job with AWS names
- [Cloud networking and hybrid connectivity](../cloud-networking-and-hybrid-connectivity) — VNet peering, ExpressRoute, and VPN gateways
- [Identity and access management](../identity-and-access-management) — Entra ID is the identity layer this rides on44:T1cfc,
## What this system is

Azure's integration story is a toolkit, not a product: **Service Bus** (enterprise messaging), **Event Grid** (event routing between Azure services), **Event Hubs** (high-throughput stream ingestion), **Logic Apps** (no/low-code orchestration glue), plus **API Management** as the front door. There is no "right" single service — each solves a different delivery-semantics problem, and real systems use several at once.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Src[SaaS / line-of-business apps] -->|events| EG[Event Grid]
  Src -->|rare ever| A[Azure functions]
  A -->|messages| SB[Service Bus]
  Comp[Other Azure services] -->|telemetry / events| EH[Event Hubs]
  LOG[Logic Apps - orchestration] --> SB
  LOG --> EG
  APIM[API Management] --> Functions[Functions / App Service]
```

## The four services, and when each wins

| Service | Delivery model | Best for |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Service Bus** | Ordered queues + topics, at-least-once, TTL, DLQ | Enterprise work queues, durable decoupling, competing consumers |
| **Event Grid** | Pulses events to subscribers, auto-retry, dead-letter | Connecting Azure resources ("blob created → trigger function"), fan-out |
| **Event Hubs** | Massive throughput, partition-keyed, no per-message ack | Telemetry, click-streams, anything with very high event volume |
| **Logic Apps** | Orchestration engine (low-code) | Workflows across systems, human approvals, no-code glue |

The selector rule: **a system that must process a message reliably later = Service Bus; an event Azure itself should react to = Event Grid; a firehose of telemetry = Event Hubs; a multi-step workflow = Logic Apps.** They compose — it's common to receive on Service Bus, route decisions through Logic Apps, and publish results onto Event Grid.

## Service Bus: the durable work horse

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant P as Producer
  participant Q as Service Bus queue
  participant C1 as Consumer 1
  participant C2 as Consumer 2
  participant DLQ as Dead-letter queue

  P->>Q: send message (+ properties, TTL)
  Q->>C1: "lock" the message
  Q->>C2: competing consumer - one gets it
  C1-->>Q: ack (complete) OR
  C1-->>Q: abandon (redeliver) OR
  C1-->>Q: dead-letter (poison)
  Q->>DLQ: messages past max-delivery / TTL
```

- **Queues & topics**: a queue has one logical consumer pool (competing consumers share work); a topic fans out to many subscriptions, each with its own durable copy.
- **Locking** gives exactly-once-ish *processing*: a consumer gets an exclusive lock, must `complete()` within the lock duration, or the message is retried — never duplicated-in-flight, but at-least-once overall, so handlers stay idempotent.
- **Settle one way**: `complete` (done), `abandon` (redeliver), `defer` (park to peek later), or **dead-letter** for poison messages — the DLQ is where bad payloads go instead of poisoning the queue forever.
- **Ordering caveat**: queues/topics give ordering only per the `session`/partition key you set; safest for meaningful order is a **sessionful** queue where each session id is processed serially.

## Event Grid: the reactive router

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph Sources
    S1[Blob storage - new blob]
    S2[Container registry - image push]
    S3[Resource group events]
    S4[Custom topics - your apps]
  end
  S1 --> EG[Event Grid topic]
  S2 --> EG
  S3 --> EG
  S4 --> EG
  EG --> D1[Function - process]
  EG --> D2[Logic App - workflow]
  EG --> D3[Service Bus - re-queue]
  EG --> H[Event Hubs - land in stream]
```

The defining trait of Event Grid: it **routes, not stores**. Events are small facts ("a blob appeared"), delivered to matching subscribers with retries and a dead-letter sink, but there's no per-message-audit log like Service Bus. It's the fastest path between Azure resources — trivial to wire `blobCreated → Functions` with no code in the middle.

## Event Hubs: the firehose

Event Hubs is a **streaming ingestion bus**: it mirrors Kafka's partition/offset model so that high-volume producers land events quickly and consumers (Functions, Spark, AKS) read at their own pace. Order is guaranteed per `PartitionKey`. It is not for workload queues (no per-message ack like Service Bus) and not for routing (that's Event Grid) — it's the place *before* processing, where millions of events/sec arrive and get persisted briefly.

## Logic Apps: orchestration without app code

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  T[Trigger - HTTP / schedule / Service Bus] --> C[Condition on the payload]
  C -- yes --> A[Call an API - e.g. ERP, CRM]
  A --> H[Human approval - the workflow pauses]
  H -- approved --> N[Notify + write result]
  H -- rejected --> R[Handle rejection path]
  C -- no --> S[Skip / log]
```

Logic Apps is **control flow, not compute**: it sequences steps (calls, approvals, transforms), persists state, handles retries, and shows you the whole workflow visually. When a process is "send invoice → get finance approval → update ERP → email the submitter", that's a Logic App — because encoding it in a service would be glue, not value.

## Putting it together: a reference shape

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  User[User action] --> APIM[API Management - auth, rate limit]
  APIM --> FA[Function App - validate + enqueue]
  FA -->|message| SB[Service Bus - durable queue]
  SB --> W[Worker - Function - process]
  W -->|outcome event| EG[Event Grid]
  EG --> Notify[Notify service]
  EG --> LOGC[Logic App - follow-up workflow]
```

For most integration-first estates the shape is remarkably consistent: **APIM at the front, Service Bus for durable work, Functions as workers, Event Grid to fan results out, Event Hubs only when there's a true firehose, Logic Apps for the human-step workflows.**

## Tester checklist

- **Delivery semantics drill**: kill a consumer mid-message; verify the message is redelivered (at-least-once) and that the handler's idempotency dedupes the duplicate.
- **Poison message**: send a bad payload repeatedly; confirm it dead-letters, and that the DLQ is inspected and drained on a schedule.
- **Session/order**: with a sessionful queue, insert out-of-order and verify each session id processes serially.
- **Fan-out**: publish one topic message; confirm every subscription received its own durable copy.
- **Event Grid retry**: fail a subscriber endpoint and verify retries + dead-letter sink, and check `deliveryCount`/diagnostics logs.
- **Lock expiry**: set a short lock, process slowly, and confirm `complete()` on an expired lock surfaces as a duplicate/retry the consumer must tolerate.

## Related systems

- [API gateway caching](../api-gateway-caching) and [Azure API Management (APIM)](../azure-api-management-apim) — the front door the integration stack sits behind
- [Message queue delivery](../message-queue-delivery) — the pattern Service Bus implements
- [Pub/Sub and event streaming](../pub-sub-event-streaming) — the model Event Grid and Event Hubs both embody
- [Serverless computing and functions](../serverless-computing-and-functions) — the workers that consume from Service Bus and Event Grid
- [Integration patterns](../integration-patterns) — the five families; every Azure service maps to one
- [File transfer and object storage](../file-transfer-and-object-storage) — Service Bus's file-based rival for legacy exchange
- [API request lifecycle](../api-request-lifecycle) — where APIM fits in the full chain45:Tf23,
## What this system is

A backend framework turns "here is an HTTP request" into "here is a behaved response" in a predictable way: **middleware** runs first, a **router** picks a handler, the **controller/service** does the work, and the response goes back out — with error handling, auth, and logging as layers in between.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Req[HTTP request] --> MW[Middleware chain<br/>logging, auth, body parsing]
  MW --> RT[Router matches path + method]
  RT --> CT[Controller / handler]
  CT --> SV[Service layer - business logic]
  SV --> DB[(Database / external API)]
  DB --> SV
  SV --> CT
  CT -->|JSON / HTML| Res[Response]
  MW -->|error handler| Res
```

## A request through the framework

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Req[HTTP request] -->|enter chain| MW[Middleware<br/>auth, logs, parse]
  MW -->|pass through| Router[Router<br/>method + path]
  Router -->|invoke handler| Svc[Controller to service]
  Svc -->|async IO| DB[(Database / IO)]
  DB -->|build payload| Res[Serialized response]
```

## Framework vs library

| | Library (Express, Fastify, Flask) | Framework (NestJS, Spring Boot, Rails, Django, ASP.NET Core) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Who calls whom | Your code calls it per route | It provides a lifecycle; it calls you |
| Structure | You decide folders/patterns | Conventions + scaffolding baked in |
| Boilerplate | Minimal at first | More, but consistent across teams |
| The trade-off | Freedom, drift between devs | Discipline, harder to bend |

The classic first-project algorithm: **few endpoints + a small team → library; a product with many engineers → framework** — so the architecture isn't reinvented per feature.

## The framework map

| Framework | Language | Shape | Stands out for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Express / Fastify | Node.js | Minimal, middleware | APIs, microservices, start-ups of any size |
| NestJS | Node.js (TS) | Opinionated, DI modules | Structure, decorators, testability |
| FastAPI | Python | Async, type-driven | Fast iteration, auto OpenAPI docs |
| Django / Rails | Python / Ruby | Batteries, MVC, admin | CRUD products fast; conventions |
| Spring Boot | Java/Kotlin | Heavyweight, enterprise | Large orgs, strong typing, DI |
| ASP.NET Core | C# | High-perf, structured | Windows orgs, high throughput |

## Sync vs async (where the bugs live)

- **Thread-per-request** (Spring/Rails with blocking DB drivers): each request holds a thread. Fine until your pool fills under a slow DB — then requests queue.
- **Event-loop/async** (Node, Python `asyncio`, Kotlin coroutines): few threads multiplex many requests. Great for IO-bound work; dangerous if a handler blocks the loop with CPU work or a sync call.
- The bug that bites either way: **blocking the wrong model** — a CPU loop on an async loop, or seconds-long work on a single thread pool.

## Tester checklist

- Verify the full pipeline fires for one request: middleware ran in order, route matched, service called once, response shaped right (inspect logs/body).
- Confirm a middleware abort short-circuits the whole chain (auth rejection must not reach the controller or DB).
- Test error handling: an exception in the service must produce the framework's standardized error body, not a raw stack trace.
- Probe the blocking failure: hammer an endpoint that calls a slow service and confirm throughput degrades predictably (that's the async/latency signature).

## Related systems

- [API request lifecycle](../api-request-lifecycle) — the end-to-end path this framework lives inside
- [Frontend frameworks and rendering](../frontend-frameworks-and-rendering) — the client that consumes these responses
- [Webhook integrations](../webhook-integrations) — inbound push traffic your framework must handle idempotently
- [Message queue delivery](../message-queue-delivery) — offloading work the request can't wait for46:T16a5,
## What this system is

Banks must not only transact — they must **know their customer is who they claim, their money is not laundered, and their losses are survivable**. That is the risk and compliance layer: a set of checks that sit *alongside* the [core ledger](../core-banking-systems) and [payment rails](../payments-clearing-and-settlement), screening risk, fraud, and money-laundering on every transaction, and reporting the results to regulators.

It is genuinely a second system behind the account ledger: while account balance is the primary record, the *risk posture* of a customer, a transaction, and the bank itself is what gets examined under audit.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    TX[Transaction arrives] --> FRAUD[Fraud detection - real-time scoring]
    FRAUD --> F[Case review when flagged]
    FRAUD --> KYC[KYC / AML checks - errors, sanctions]
    KYC --> R[Reporting - STR, SAR, regulatory filings]
    FRAUD --> RISK[Risk limits - credit, exposure, liquidity]
    RISK --> TX2[Approved / declined / referred]
```

## The three control layers

| Control | Question it answers | Latency required |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Credit & market risk** | Is the exposure, the counterparty, or the market move beyond appetite? | Near-real-time, plus batch |
| **Fraud detection** | Is this *the rightful owner* making this transaction from this device/path? | Milliseconds (real-time) |
| **AML / KYC** | Is this customer/sender/beneficiary sanctioned, PEP, or patterned like laundering? | Seconds on transaction, broader on onboarding |

The trap is mixing them: a **credit** decision ("can we afford this counterparty") is different from a **fraud** decision ("is this really the customer"), and conning them together either rejects good business or lets bad through. Architects should keep the data model *one funnel with several scorers*, not one scary "risk flag".

## Real-time fraud, step by step

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    TX["Transaction event"] -->|raw| ENRICH["Enrich<br/>device, geo, velocity, patterns"]
    ENRICH -->|features| MODEL["ML + rules engine<br/>score the action"]
    MODEL -->|pass| LOW["Low risk - auto pass"]
    MODEL -->|friction| MID["Medium - step-up<br/>2FA, re-verification"]
    MODEL -->|deny| HIGH["High - block + review"]
```

The design tension is **customer friction vs false positives**. A step-up challenge (OTP, biometric, or re-login) stops fraud cheaply and is reversible; a hard decline is not. Good fraud systems are calibrated to push *uncertainty* toward step-up rather than blanket decline, and the calibration is a business decision owned jointly with the risk team.

## KYC and AML: the know-your-customer loop

- **Onboarding**: identity verification (document + liveness), PEP/sanctions screening, and risk-rating at account opening.
- **Ongoing monitoring**: transaction monitoring looks for structuring, rapid movement, and inconsistent profiles; sanctions lists are refreshed continuously.
- **Reporting**: suspicions become **STR/SAR** (suspicious transaction/activity reports) — a regulator-required, confidential filing that *must not* tip off the customer.
- **The net effect**: an AML program is a *filter that produces regulated outputs*, so the boring engineering — reliable feeds, dedupe, auditable history, and case-management — matters more than any single scorer.

## Risk appetite: the bank's own threshold

Risk controls are only meaningful against an explicit appetite: limits per customer, per sector, per counterparty, and per product. The architecture must let treasury/credit **set limits in one place** and have every channel and every payment rail read them — the same refusal should show in the API, the branch, and the wire room. Disjointed limit tables are how a bank discovers its exposure was really double what any one system reported.

## Tester checklist

- Fraud threshold: craft the same transaction from warm, stepped-up, and anomalous contexts — each routes to the intended outcome (auto-pass, step-up, block).
- Friction rule: a medium-risk event triggers step-up exactly once per session, not a retry loop that abandons the customer.
- AML screening: a counterparty on a sanctions list is blocked at onboarding *and* at payment time, and the block generates the required case record.
- Limited exposure: a customer limit raised in treasury is reflected in the wire, card, and API paths within the SLA, not per-system lazily.
- STR handling: a suspicious transaction produces a report that is fully audited and *never* leaks to the customer-facing view.
- Idempotency of monitoring: replays of a transaction feed do not double-count the monitoring signal or generate duplicate cases.
- Calibration evidence: every pass/step-up/deny decision can be re-derived from its features for the regulator, with an auditable model version.

- [Core banking systems](../core-banking-systems) — the ledger every control wraps around
- [Payments, clearing, and settlement](../payments-clearing-and-settlement) — the rails the screening sits on
- [Identity and access management](../identity-and-access-management) — the customer identity basis of KYC
- [Zero Trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — the same identity-and-risk posture applied to the bank's own systems
