- [Data pipelines (ETL/ELT)](../data-pipelines-etl-elt) — feeding raw transactions to the scorers and regulators
- [Monitoring and observability](../monitoring-and-observability) — watching false-positive rates and case backlogs
- [Data governance and catalog](../data-governance-and-catalog) — governed reference data for sanctions and rates
- [Secrets, certificates and PKI](../secrets-certificates-and-pki) — the cryptographic custody protecting the control layer47:T179a,
## What this system is

A **business process** is the sequence of activities, decisions, and hand-offs that turns an input into an outcome the business cares about — *a lead becomes a signed order*, *a goods receipt becomes a payment*. **Business process management (BPM)** is the discipline of making those processes *visible, measured, and improvable*: you cannot improve a process you cannot see.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  M[Model the process] --> A[Automate or enable]
  A --> R[Run - events + records]
  R --> S[Measure - KPIs]
  S --> I[Improve - redesign]
  I --> M
```

A process is **not** the software that supports it. The process is the shared understanding of *who does what, when, and what if*; the software (a CRM, an ERP, an approval engine) is one possible mechanism for carrying it out. When the process and the system disagree, you have a live process bug.

## The anatomy of a process

Every process can be decomposed into a few load-bearing elements:

| Element | Question it answers | Failure mode |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Activity** | What gets done? | Activity with no named owner |
| **Event** | What starts/stops it? | Process waiting on an event nobody publishes |
| **Decision** | Which branch do we take? | Rules hidden in someone's head |
| **Actor / role** | Who does it? | Hand-off to a role that has no inbox |
| **SLA / deadline** | How long is acceptable? | Deadline with no escalation |
| **Data / artifact** | What passes between steps? | "We always knew it" — but no system knows it |

The **flow** is the skeleton; the **decision rules** are the organs; the **escalation** is the immune system. A process modeled without deadlines and escalations is a diagram, not a process.

## Process decomposition

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  L1[Level 1 - value chain<br/>e.g. order-to-cash] --> L2a[Level 2 - process<br/>e.g. credit check]
  L1 --> L2b[Level 2 - process<br/>e.g. warehouse dispatch]
  L2a --> L3a[Level 3 - sub-process / task<br/>e.g. run credit report]
  L2a --> L3b[Level 3 - decision<br/>e.g. approve / reject]
  L3b --> L4[Level 4 - system step<br/>e.g. ERP approval workflow]
```

Decomposition answers a practical question: **who owns which level?** Executives own the value chain, process owners own level 2, teams own tasks, and system owners own the automated steps. The classic failure is modeling everything at one level — too abstract for the implementer, too granular for the decision-maker.

## The process lifecycle

1. **Model** — draw the current (as-is) process in BPMN, with swimlanes per role and explicit decisions. If a step has no swimlane, it has no owner.
2. **Automate or enable** — decide what the system does (rules, approvals, notifications) versus what a human must still judge. Over-automating a human-judgment step is how you build a bad workflow.
3. **Run** — the process executes; every step leaves a *process record* (who did it, when, how long) so the process is audit-able.
4. **Measure** — KPIs: cycle time, hand-off count, first-pass yield, wait time between steps. *The process record is the source of the KPI.*
5. **Improve** — the measured pain points get redesigned; the new process is modeled, automated, and run again.

## Where the process meets the system

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Event[Business event - e.g. order placed] -->|triggers| Rules[Decision rules + policy]
  Rules -->|automate the routine| System[Workflow engine / ERP]
  Rules -->|route the judgement| Human[Human task - approvals, judgment]
  System -->|step executed| Record[Process record - audit trail]
  Human -->|decision logged| Record
  Record -->|aggregate| KPI[KPIs - cycle time, yield]
```

The integration pattern to teach: **the business event starts the process, the rules decide the route, the system automates the routine steps, the humans handle the judgment, and every step lands in a process record.** The record is what turns "we think it works" into "we measured it."

## Business Process Model and Notation (BPMN)

BPMN is the drawing language of processes: **events** (circles), **activities** (rounded rectangles), **gateways** (diamonds), **swimlanes** (who does what). Its superpower is precision — a BPMN diagram can be so unambiguous that an engine can *execute* it directly (BPMN is both diagram and code). The pitfalls are the same as any modeling:

- Model **decisions as gateways with named conditions**, not as arrows with vibes.
- Model **exceptions explicitly** — "what if the vendor rejects?" is a normal path, not an edge case.
- Keep the diagram **executable-or-explainable**: if an engine would get stuck, a human will too.

## Tester checklist

- Swimlane audit: walk the as-is process and confirm every activity sits in a named role's lane with a named owner.
- Exception drill: run the process with the rejection / error path (vendor rejects, customer cancels) and confirm the exception route exists and escalates.
- KPI provenance: pick one measured KPI and trace it back to specific fields in the process record — it must be computable, not asserted.
- Decision honesty: check that every gateway condition is explicit enough that a new analyst and the workflow engine would both pick the same branch.
- Hand-off latency: measure wait time between two swimlanes — the place most processes actually lose their time.

## Related systems

- [Software development lifecycle (SDLC)](../software-development-lifecycle) — where process requirements become software, phase by phase
- [System acquisition lifecycle](../system-acquisition-lifecycle) — the build-vs-buy gate that decides whether SDLC or procurement follows
- [ERP systems](../enterprise-resource-planning) — the system that carries many core processes (order-to-cash, procure-to-pay)
- [Integration patterns](../integration-patterns) — how the events that start and advance processes move between systems
- [ITIL change and release management](../itil-change-and-release-management) — the change-process that governs process software, itself a process48:Te95,
## What this system is

Change data capture (CDC) turns the **database's own write-ahead log** into an event stream. Instead of the application remembering to emit events on every write (and getting it wrong when it forgets), a CDC connector reads the binary log / WAL and publishes each committed change. Debezium is the reference open-source implementation; every cloud vendor has a managed cousin.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  App[Application] -->|INSERT / UPDATE| DB[Source DB<br/>transaction log]
  DB -->|read WAL| CDC[CDC connector<br/>Debezium / cloud]
  CDC -->|publish change| T[Events topic<br/>db.customers]
  T -->|consume → re-index| S[Search index]
  T -->|consume → load| WH[Data lake / warehouse]
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  App[Application] -->|INSERT / UPDATE / DELETE| DB[(Source DB<br/>postgres / mysql)]
  DB -->|transaction log / binlog / WAL| CDC[CDC connector<br/>Debezium / cloud CDC]
  CDC --> T[events topic<br/>db.customers]
  T --> S[Search index]
  T --> C[Cache warm-up]
  T --> L[Data lake / warehouse]
  T --> N[Notifications]
```

## How a change becomes an event

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant App as Application
  participant DB as PostgreSQL
  participant CDC as Debezium
  participant T as Kafka topic

  App->>DB: UPDATE customers SET city='Oslo' WHERE id=7
  DB->>DB: write row image + LSN to WAL
  CDC->>DB: rewind binlog from last LSN
  DB-->>CDC: change record (id, before{Oslo}, after{Oslo})
  CDC->>T: publish "U" record, partition by PK
  S->>T: consume and re-index customer 7
```

A single **Kafka Connect** pipeline can fan out one database change to a search index, a cache, a warehouse, and downstream systems — no dual-write, no missing events, no application-level publish.

## Outbox vs CDC

| | Dual-write (app emits event) | Outbox pattern | CDC |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| How events get created | App publishes after write | App writes event row in the *same* DB transaction; relay publishes | Connector reads the DB log |
| Consistency risk | Event and write can split (crash between) | Atomic — safe | Atomic — safe |
| Requires app changes | Requires it at every write | One insert per event | **None** |
| Catalog of changes | Only what you chose to emit | Only what you chose to emit | **Everything**, including schema events |
| Best for | Thin events, custom semantics | Reliable business events | Bulk sync, search/cache/analytics targets |

## Where CDC hurts

- **Schema changes** become events (`ALTER TABLE` is in the log) — consumers wired to the old shape break until they migrate.
- **Initial snapshot + continuous** mode is where bugs hide: the boundary between "backfill everything" and "only-delts" is easy to get wrong (miss rows, or duplicate).
- Order is per-table (by PK in the log); deleting is represented by a "before, no after" tombstone.
- **DDL-heavy or high-write databases** produce enormous logs; you are paying to stream every write, even ones nobody consumes.

## Tester checklist

- Restart the connector mid-stream and confirm exactly-to-exactly-connection (no missing, no duplicated rows) via LSN/offset replay.
- Suspend the source DB for hours — confirm lag is bounded and recoverable rather than data loss.
- Verify tombstones (hard deletes) propagate as deletes to the target, not as stale copies.
- Test `ALTER TABLE` exposure: consumers must tolerate or visibly fail, not silently corrupt.

## Related systems

- [Pub/Sub and event streaming](../pub-sub-event-streaming) — where CDC's events land
- [Message queue delivery](../message-queue-delivery) — delivery semantics the downstream queue enforces
- [Integration patterns](../integration-patterns) — where CDC sits in the integration landscape49:T11a2,
## What this system is

**CI** (continuous integration) runs automated checks on every commit — lint, test, build — and the **CD** (continuous delivery/deployment) half packages the result into an **artifact** and promotes it through environments until it reaches production. The output is not "a deploy"; it's a repeatable, auditable path.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Commit[Commit / PR] -->|trigger| CI[CI: lint + unit]
  CI -->|gate: green| Build[Build image / bundle]
  Build -->|push versioned| Art[(Artifact registry - immutable SHA)]
  Art -->|promote| Staging[Staging env + smoke]
  Staging -->|gate: approve| Prod[Production]
  Prod -->|error rate| Rollback[Rollback on alarm]
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Commit[Commit / PR] --> Lint[Lint + unit tests]
  Lint --> Build[Build image/bundle]
  Build --> A[(Artifact registry<br/>versioned + immutable)]
  A --> E2E[Integration + e2e tests]
  E2E -->|gate: tests green| Staging[Deploy to staging]
  Staging -->|gate: manual approve or smoke| Prod[Deploy to production]
  Prod --> Post[Post-deploy checks + rollback on alarm]
  Post --> Rollback[Automatic rollback]
```

## The stages that never change (even when tools do)

| Stage | Purpose | Typical checks |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Commit/PR | Fast feedback on the change | Lint, unit tests, type checks |
| Build | Encapsulate the app as one artifact | Build image, SBOM |
| Test | Prove it behaves | Integration, e2e, security scan |
| Package/Promote | Make the artifact official | Book that exact SHA, not "latest" |
| Deploy(staging) | Safe rehearsal | Env parity, smoke tests |
| Deploy(prod) | The real thing, controlled way | Canary/blue-green, rollback trigger |

The rule to hold onto: **you promote build artifacts, never recompile**. If prod rebuilds from source, you're not releasing the tested thing.

## Environment as a "gate"

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  A[Artifact v1.4.2] --> Dev[Dev env - do anything]
  Dev --> Stg[Staging - mirror prod config + secrets]
  Stg -->|manual approval or automated smoke| Prod[Prod - least privilege, audited]
  Prod -->|alarm / error rate| RB[Rollback to v1.4.1]
```

Environments are *promotion steps*, not copies: staging should run on the same config shape and secrets as production, or the gate proves nothing.

## Deployment strategies compared

| Strategy | How it releases | Downtime | Risk profile |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Recreate** | Stop old, start new | Downtime | Simplest, riskiest for live |
| **Rolling** | Replace nodes gradually | None | Single version alive at any moment, mixed briefly |
| **Blue-green** | New stack beside old, switch traffic | None (a cutover) | Instant rollback by switching back |
| **Canary** | Send small % to new, increase if healthy | None | Catch problems on real traffic, earlier |
| **Feature-flag release** | Ship code dark, toggle in prod | None | Decouple deploy from release |

The mature answer is usually **canary or blue-green for infra changes, plus feature flags for product changes** — "the code is in prod" and "users see it" become two different events.

## What makes a pipeline trustworthy (security too)

- **Secrets only via the pipeline's secret store** — never in the build step's logs or schema files.
- **Signed/sbommed artifacts**: pin checksums, so prod deploys exactly the tested bytes.
- **Every deploy is a git commit + tag**: you can name who/what/when by looking at the repo, not guessing.
- **Manual gates where context matters, automation elsewhere**: let humans click for prod, machines do the rest.

## Tester checklist

- Corrupt a build artifact and confirm promotion blocks it (integrity fails at staging, never a prod surprise).
- Verify staging truly mirrors prod: same secret keys, same env vars, same region/scale shape.
- Trigger an alarm on the canary wave and confirm rollback fires to the previous artifact within your MTTR.
- Prove *any* change to the pipeline definition is itself reviewed and versioned in git.

## Related systems

- [Git and version control](../git-and-version-control) — the commits and PRs that trigger all of this
- [DevOps delivery loop](../devops-delivery-loop) — the wider loop this pipeline sits inside
- [Jira and agile delivery](../jira-and-agile-delivery) — where the work tickets for each stage live
- [AWS core services](../aws-core-services) — where many pipelines land (ECR, CodeBuild, Pipeline)
- [Dockerizing applications](../dockerizing-applications) — the image that pipelines build, scan, and promote4a:T1089,
## What this system is

The cloud is a managed utility: you rent compute, storage, and network at *region* scale instead of owning data centers. The core mental model — **regions + availability zones (AZs) + service models** — is identical across AWS and Azure even when the names differ.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  App[Your app<br/>deployed across AZs] -- compute --> AZA[Availability zone A]
  App -- compute --> AZB[Availability zone B]
  AZA -- primary writes --> DB1[(Managed DB - primary)]
  AZB -- replica reads --> DB2[(Managed DB - standby)]
  DB2 -- failover --> DB1
  DB1 -- "RPO/RTO met" --> OK[Keeps serving<br/>one zone fails]
```

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  subgraph Region["Region: us-east-1 / East US"]
    direction LR
    AZ1[Availability zone A<br/>separate building]
    AZ2[Availability zone B<br/>separate building]
    AZ3[Availability zone C<br/>separate building]
  end
  App[Your app - deployed across 2+ AZs] --> AZ1
  App --> AZ2
```

A **region** is a geographic cluster of 2-4+ **availability zones** — independent power, cooling, and fibre. A true cloud deployment tolerates losing one whole AZ and keeps serving. That is the single biggest resilience idea in cloud.

## The three service models

| | IaaS | PaaS | SaaS |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| You manage | App + some config | App | Nothing but usage |
| AWS | EC2, VPC | RDS, Lambda, ECS | S3? no — SaaS is like Office 365 |
| Azure | VMs, VNet | App Service, Functions | SaaS SKUs you consume |
| Best for | Legacy lift, full control | Ship apps fast, minimal ops | Just-use it |

Your job as a Cloud Engineer is mostly **choosing the model per workload**: databases on IaaS means patching MySQL yourself; on PaaS you get patching, backups, and failover for free — and pay accordingly.

## Shared responsibility model

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  subgraph Provider["Cloud provider (AWS / Azure)"]
    A[Physical security, hardware, network]
    B[Hypervisor, host patching, managed services]
  end
  subgraph You["Customer"]
    C[Your data, access policies, applications]
    D[Runtime config on IaaS, IAM, encryption keys]
  end
```

The boundary moves depending on the service: with **IaaS** you own OS-to-top; with **PaaS/SaaS** the provider owns almost everything below your code — but *you always* own your data, your IAM, and your key material. "The cloud encrypted it" is never a substitute for your own encryption decisions.

## Designing for failure

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  L1[Load balancer across AZs] --> V1[App in AZ A]
  L1 --> V2[App in AZ B]
  DB[(Managed DB<br/>multi-AZ replica)]
  DB -->|failover| DB2[(Standby AZ C)]
  B2[(Backup policy<br/>RPO/RTO defined)]
```

Three numbers shape every design: **RPO** (how much data you can lose) and **RTO** (how fast you must be back) and **cost** (what you'll pay for both). Multi-AZ + automated failover shrinks RPO/RTO; backups are the safety net when both fail.

## Cost levers (the art of not overspending)

- **Right-size then autoscale**: most cloud bill statements are runaway idle VMs.
- **Reserved/commitment plans** for steady load, **spot** for batch/fault-tolerant work.
- **Storage tiers**: hot/cold/archive — put the audit logs in archive, not hot S3/Blob.
- **Serverless** (Lambda/Functions) is often the cheapest for spiky workloads and the most expensive for sustained steady load.

## Tester checklist

- Kill/stop one AZ's instances (in a staging account) and verify the app re-serves from the remaining AZs within your RTO.
- Restore from a backup into a scratch region and confirm RPO is at most your stated window.
- Verify IAM/RBAC is least-privilege: no cloud-admin on daily users, no giant ALLOW on read buckets.
- Watch a month of cost report: which two line items most exceed the forecast? Right-size them.

## Related systems

- [AWS core services](../aws-core-services) — the same fundamentals with AWS names
- [Azure core services](../azure-core-services) — the same fundamentals with Azure names
- [Cloud networking and hybrid connectivity](../cloud-networking-and-hybrid-connectivity) — how regions talk to each other and your office
- [Zero Trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — cloud identity and posture at scale4b:T101c,
## What this system is

A **landing zone** is the scaffolding below your cloud: the account/subscription structure, shared identity, central networking hub, and governance guardrails that every new team consumes instead of rebuilding. Its job is to make *"deploy something new in the cloud"* a safe, fast, repetitive action — not a blank check.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  Root[Organization / root] --> Mgmt[Management account]
  Root --> Security[Security account]
  Root --> Infra[Network account]
  Root --> Prod[Prod workload accounts]
  Root --> NonProd[Non-prod workload accounts]
  Mgmt -->|guardrails pushed down| Prod
  Mgmt -->|guardrails pushed down| NonProd
  Infra -->|shared VPC/VNet + egress| Prod
  Infra -->|shared VPC/VNet + egress| NonProd
```

## The pattern in one picture

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  A[Team: we need a workload] --> B[Pipeline asks for a new account/subscription]
  B --> C{Pre-built scaffolding applies}
  C --> D[Identity: SSO + least-privilege roles]
  C --> E[Network: landing zone VPC/VNet + peering]
  C --> F[Policy: encryption, budget, tagging]
  C --> G[Monitoring: logging + alert baseline]
  D --> H[Workload runs compliant from day one]
  E --> H
  F --> H
  G --> H
```

## The core building blocks

| Layer | What it provides | Without it |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Account/subscription structure | Separation between prod and non-prod, per-business-unit | One shared account with everything in it |
| Identity foundation | SSO, sign-on roles, break-glass, role per workload | Shared root keys, admin-role-everything |
| Network hub | Shared transit VPC/VNet, egress, private endpoints, on-prem links | Every team re-creates IP space, VPNs multiply |
| Guardrails | Policy (encryption, compliance), budgets, tagging | Config drift you discover in audits |
| Observability baseline | Central logging/audit trail, alert channels | Teams fly blind, incidents are archaeology |

## The identity and network seams

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph Seams["Workload seams"]
    ID["Identity:<br/>SSO + RBAC"]
    NW["Network hub:<br/>VPC / VNet"]
    GR["Policy + budgets + tagging"]
  end
  Root[Organization root] -- structure --> W[Workload accounts / subscriptions]
  W -- "least privilege" --> ID
  W -- "peering / private links" --> NW
  W -- "enforced on deploy" --> GR
  GR -- "audit-clean baseline" --> OK[Compliant from day one]
```

## Rules of the game

- **Guardrails beat documentation.** Policy turning a wrong config into a deploy failure beats a 40-page "cloud conventions" doc that nobody reads.
- **Every workload is its own isolated unit.** A future incident in one account can't pivot through the landing zone into the rest (the same logic as [micro-segmentation](../zero-trust-architecture)).
- **The landing zone is a product with an owner**, continuously versioned — not a one-time setup you did and forgot.
- **Break-glass stays boring.** Emergency access exists, sealed, rotated, and tested — used only when the normal path fails.

## Tester checklist

- Create a fresh workload account/subscription from the pipeline and verify it inherits policy, encryption, budget, and monitoring *before* any resource exists.
- Try to bypass: can a lone dev create a resource with no owner tag, or an unencrypted bucket, or an external VPN? The guardrails must block, not warn.
- Verify identity: the new account's only admin path is SSO + MFA; there is no long-lived root key in use.
- Break-glass drill: emergency admin credentials work, alert on use, and can be rotated/revoked afterwards.

## Related systems

- [AWS core services](../aws-core-services) — the service-level detail the landing zone organizes
- [Azure core services](../azure-core-services) — management groups, subscriptions, and policy
- [Cloud computing fundamentals](../cloud-computing-fundamentals) — the region/AZ reality the landing zone runs on
- [Cloud networking and hybrid connectivity](../cloud-networking-and-hybrid-connectivity) — the shared network hub pattern
- [Disaster recovery and backup](../disaster-recovery-and-backup) — what the landing zone must prepare for4c:T1105,
## What this system is

Cloud networks are software. A **VPC** (AWS) or **VNet** (Azure) defines an isolated IP space; you split it into subnets, decide what goes public vs private, connect regions by **peering/transit**, and reach your office through **VPN or private dedicated links**. The skill is trading connectivity against exposure.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph OnPrem["Corporate"]
    LAN[Corporate LAN]
  end
  subgraph Cloud["Cloud network"]
    LINK[VPN / DX / ExpressRoute]
    HUB[Hub VPC/VNet<br/>transit]
    SPOKE_A[Spoke: app]
    SPOKE_D[Spoke: data]
    EP[Private endpoint<br/>no public IP]
  end
  LAN -- "site link" --> LINK
  LINK -- "encrypted tunnel" --> HUB
  HUB -- "spoke route" --> SPOKE_A
  HUB -- "spoke route" --> SPOKE_D
  SPOKE_A -- "in-VPC only" --> EP
  SPOKE_D -- "in-VPC only" --> EP
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph OnPrem["Your office / data center"]
    O1[Corporate LAN]
    O2[VPN/edge device]
  end
  subgraph CloudA["AWS region"]
    V1[VPC hub - shared services]
    P1[spoke VPC - app]
    P2[spoke VPC - data]
    E1[Private endpoint / VPC endpoint]
  end
  O1 --> O2 -->|IPsec or Dedicated Connect - DX / ExpressRoute| V1
  V1 --> P1
  V1 --> P2
  P1 --> E1
  P2 --> E1
```

## How VPCs talk to each other (and to you)

| Way to connect VPC/VNet | What it gives | Watch-outs |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Peering** | Two networks, direct private IP | No transitive hops - peering is point-to-point (VPC) |
| **Transit gateway / hub** | A hub routes to many spokes | Hub becomes a SPOF - rightsize + protect it |
| **Private endpoint / VPC endpoint** | Reach a service by private IP without traversing the internet | Endpoint per service; watch costs |
| **VPN gateway / tunnel** | Site-to-site encrypted over internet | Best-effort throughput, does not beat dedicated |
| **Dedicated connect** (DX / ExpressRoute) | Private, stable, high-throughput to provider | Slow to provision, dedicated cost |

Rule of thumb: **hub-and-spoke with a transit layer**, private endpoints for sensitive services, and public internet only for what must be public. The add lecture: peerings don't cross regions silently and peering is not transitive — if you have many VPCs, use the hub, don't chain peerings.

## Private endpoint vs "just a security group"

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph PublicPath["Public - SG / NSG permit list"]
    A1[App] --> R1[Elastic IP / LB]
    R1 --> DB1[(Database public route)]
  end
  subgraph PrivatePath["Private - endpoint only"]
    A2[App in VPC] --> E2[Private endpoint IP in-VPC]
    E2 --> DB2[(Database - no public IP at all)]
  end
```

A security group/NSG *filtering* a public-facing DB is still on the public internet — one mis-rule and it leaks. A **private endpoint** takes the resource entirely off the public surface: only in-network traffic can reach it. Prefer endpoints for anything holding data.

## Security basics that differ per platform

| | AWS | Azure |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Network filter | Security group (stateful, per-ENI) + NACL (stateless, per-subnet) | NSG (stateful, per-subnet/NIC) + Azure Firewall |
| Private service | VPC/VPC endpoint | Private Link |
| Site-to-site | VPN Gateway, Transit Gateway + VPN | VPN Gateway, vWAN |
| Dedicated | Direct Connect | ExpressRoute |

The stateful/stateless difference is the classic test question: **SGs stateful** (reply traffic auto-allowed), **NACLs stateless** (you author both directions).

## Tester checklist

- Verify peering/transit has no accidental transitive path (A → hub → B must not let A reach B unless intended).
- Scanner proves a private-endpoint database has *no* public IP assigned anywhere.
- Cut the dedicated link and confirm VPN failover resumes within your RTO (dual-path designs should be tested, not hoped).
- Check that egress from spoke VPCs is bounded (via NAT/egress filtering), not "any destination OK."

## Related systems

- [Network routing and BGP](../network-routing-and-bgp) — what underpins routing across the underlay
- [SD-WAN and edge connectivity](../sd-wan-and-edge-connectivity) — how the office side reaches these tunnels
- [Cloud computing fundamentals](../cloud-computing-fundamentals) — regions and AZs behind every VPC
- [Zero Trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — reducing trust per subnet and endpoint, not per network4d:T1785,
## What this system is

A Configuration Management Database (CMDB) is a **database of configuration items (CIs)** — servers, applications, services, licenses, network devices, databases, people-owned assets — plus the **relationships** between them, plus the attributes that matter (owner, version, environment, patch status). It is not a fancy inventory: a spreadsheet of assets is a list, and a CMDB is a *model*. The difference is the relationship graph — "this application runs on that server which connects to that database" — because that graph is what incident, change, and dependency analysis all query.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph Assets["Configuration items (CIs)"]
    S1[Web server - v2.1]
    S2[App server - v1.8]
    DB[(Database - v19c)]
  end
  subgraph Model["Relationships = the value"]
    R1[S1 -.runs on.-> S2]
    R2[S2 -.connects to.-> DB]
    R3[App -.owned by.-> Team]
  end
```

## CIs, relationships, attributes

| Concept | Example | Why it matters |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **CI** | `web-prod-01`, `ERP application v11` | The atomic unit you reason about |
| **Attribute** | owner, env, version, patch level | What you filter and report on |
| **Relationship** | `app runs-on server`, `server connects-to db` | The graph that makes incidents diagnosable |
| **Status** | Installed, retired, in-prod | Keeps the model honest about what's real |

The useful CMDB is the one where the **relationship graph is current enough that "what breaks if this server dies?" and "who owns this CI?"** get a trustworthy answer. That trust is the whole ballgame: a CMDB that nobody believes gets abandoned, and an abandoned CMDB is worse than none because it lies with authority.

## The trust cycle (why CMDBs fail)

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  A[Data enters<br/>via discovery + humans] --> B[People trust it]
  B --> C[People keep it current<br/>- feed back changes]
  C --> A
  D[Data goes stale] --> E[People stop trusting]
  E --> F[People stop feeding it]
  F --> D
```

Every CMDB project is a bet on which cycle you'll be on in a year. The technical half is easy (discovery tools, adapters, a schema). The cultural half is decisive: if consuming a CI record costs less effort than guessing, the CMDB lives; if a stale record ever burns someone, it dies. The practices that keep the good cycle spinning:

- **Ownership as a first-class attribute** — every CI names a human; stale records get chased to their owner, not silently cleaned.
- **Discovery feeds the model automatically** — cloud, VMs, agents, network scans update CIs without a human typing them.
- **Consumers close the loop** — incident, change, and capacity teams *query* the CMDB as their default first move, which creates the feedback that keeps it honest.
- **A CI is retired the day it's retired** — the model must reflect reality or it decays from day one.

## CMDB vs asset management vs inventory

| Store | Tracks | The question it answers |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Hardware/asset register** | Purchased things, ownership, lifecycle, cost | "What do we own and who's paying for it?" |
| **Inventory/software list** | Install counts, licenses | "What software runs and are we compliant?" |
| **CMDB** | CIs *and their relationships* | "What runs on what, and who does it affect?" |

They share data and often live in the same tool, but their purpose differs: the asset register is about money and custody, the CMDB is about **impact**. "Which users does this database support?" is a CMDB question; "how many Macs do we have?" is an asset register question. Conflating them is the most common reason a "CMDB implementation" ships an inventory that nobody consults during an incident.

## Discovery and the source of truth

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph Sources
    Cl[Cloud APIs - AWS/Azure/OCI]
    A[Agents - installed]
    N[Network scan - SNMP]
    CM[Manual / import - CSV, spreadsheets]
  end
  Cl --> D[Discovery + reconciliation]
  A --> D
  N --> D
  CM --> D
  D --> CMDB[(CMDB - golden record)]
  CMDB --> E[Enterprise service management]
```

The single-source-of-truth question is where CMDB projects overreach. The healthy answer is **federation with one reconciled layer**: discovery tools feed each asset type, humans own the exceptions, and the CMDB publishes the agreed record to whoever consumes it. The unhealthy answer is "we will model every AWS resource, VM, and network interface manually" — that's how a CMDB rots in a quarter.

## Tester checklist

- **The impact question**: pick a database CI, ask "which applications and users would be affected if it failed" — confirm the relationship graph returns the right set.
- **Staleness drill**: retire a server in reality, keep it in the CMDB, and confirm either discovery removes it or the owner gets paged within the SLA you set.
- **Trust test**: compare CMDB CI versions against reality (agents/cloud API) for a sample and measure drift — under a few percent is healthy, double digits is dying.
- **Ownership check**: every CI in a sample must resolve to a named human owner, not "IT".
- **Discovery fidelity**: create a new VM in the cloud and time how long it takes to appear as a CI with the right relationships.

## Related systems

- [ITIL incident and problem management](../itil-incident-and-problem-management) — the incident desk queries the CI graph to diagnose impact
- [ITIL change and release management](../itil-change-and-release-management) — change records must update CIs or the model goes stale
- [Monitoring and observability](../monitoring-and-observability) — telemetry attaches to CIs, tying alerts to the impact graph
- [Cloud computing fundamentals](../cloud-computing-fundamentals) — the elastic resource soup that discovery must keep up with
- [Cloud landing zones](../cloud-landing-zones) — tagging and organization that make cloud discovery sane
- [Business process modeling and management](../business-process-modeling-and-management) — how the process view maps onto the CI view4e:T16f5,
## What this system is

Computer vision turns pixels into decisions. A camera produces frames; your system must locate objects (**detection**), describe them (**classification**), track them across time (**tracking**), and often do it fast and close to the source. The model is the star, but four things decide whether a vision pipeline survives production: **data plumbing**, **preprocessing**, **model-serving**, and **the hardware** you run it on.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  S[Camera / source] -->|frames| A[Acquire]
  A -->|decode + stabilize| P[Preprocess]
  P -->|resize + normalize| M[Model inference]
  M -->|boxes + classes + scores| R[Post-process]
  R -->|filter + dedupe| D[Decide & act]
  D -->|annotations| S1[(Event store)]
```

## Ingestion: streams, not files

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant C as Camera
  participant P as Stream hub
  participant W as Worker pool
  participant S as Storage
  C->>P: continuous frames (RTSP/WebRTC)
  P->>W: frames to workers
  W-->>P: {object, confidence, location}
  W->>S: annotated frame (sampled)
```

- **Frame rate vs value**: 30 fps of raw video burns bandwidth and GPU for little gain. Most pipelines sample — 1–5 fps for people counting, event-triggered capture for alerts.
- **Stream middleware** (Kafka, Kinesis, MQTT) buffers and fans frames out so a slow model never forces a dropped camera.
- **Sampled persistence**: store all detections (small JSON), store a *fraction* of full frames (they're huge and rarely needed later).

## Preprocessing: small, cheap, and consistent

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  I[Raw frame - e.g. 3840x2160] --> D[Decode to RGB]
  D --> R[Resize to model input - e.g. 640x640]
  R --> N[Normalize + color space]
  N --> P[Pack into tensor]
  P --> M[Inference]
```

Three rules that matter more than any model trick:

- **Match training preprocessing exactly** — resize method, mean normalization, channel order (BGR vs RGB) all quietly destroy accuracy if mismatched.
- **Do it before the model, off the critical path** — decode/resize with hardware acceleration (GPU/DSP, or codecs), never in Python loops.
- **Resize is a trade, not a detail** — smaller input = faster, but small objects disappear. Match input resolution to the smallest object you must detect.

## Detection vs classification

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph Detect["Object detection"]
    D1[Frame] --> D2[Bounding boxes<br/>+ class + confidence]
  end
  subgraph Classify["Classification"]
    C1[Single image] --> C2[One label<br/>e.g. defect / no defect]
  end
  subgraph Seg["Segmentation"]
    S1[Image] --> S2[Every pixel gets a class]
  end
```

Pick the cheapest tool that answers the question:

- **Classification** answers *"which one?"* — a single image in, one label out. Cheapest, fastest, best accuracy per compute.
- **Detection** answers *"where and what?"* — boxes, needed when objects are among clutter or multiple.
- **Segmentation** answers *"exactly which pixels?"* — expensive; use only when pixel-level boundaries matter (medical imaging, autonomous driving).

## Model serving: the latency stack

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant A as Application
  participant S as Served model (Triton / OpenVINO / TensorRT)
  participant G as GPU pool
  A->>S: batch of frames
  S->>G: batched inference
  G-->>S: boxes + scores
  S-->>A: structured results
```

- **Batch, don't stream one-by-one**: GPUs are throughput machines; group frames (even 4–8) to amortize fixed overhead.
- **Serve next to the data**: edge deployment (Jetson, edge GPU appliance in the factory/office) beats round-tripping frames to cloud — the latency and bandwidth math usually decides it: cloud for batch, edge for real-time.
- **Post-process off the model**: NMS (non-max suppression) to collapse overlapping boxes, confidence thresholds, and class filtering run cheaply in CPU code — don't pay GPU for them.

## The hardware reality

| Where it runs | Typical stack | When |
| --- | --- | --- |
| On-device / edge | CPU + DSP/NPU, INT8 quantized model | Real-time, privacy, offline |
| On-prem GPU | TensorRT/Optimized engine box | Sustained high fps, no cloud egress |
| Cloud | GPU instances, auto-scaled | Bursty, batch analysis, elastic cost |

Quantization (FP32 → INT8) is the classic lever: ~4× throughput for a small accuracy dip. Combined with pruning and distillation it is how models the size of a small LLM end up running on a $50 camera module.

## Tester checklist

- **Data mismatch**: feed preprocessed frames and raw frames to the same model assert accuracy is the same — preprocessing must not be the silent accuracy killer.
- **Corner cases**: occluded objects, low light, mirrored images, and frames with the same object partially cut off at frame edges.
- **Determinism**: two runs over identical inputs should produce identical outputs (watch floating-point variance across batch sizes).
- **Latency SLO**: measure end-to-end p95 under load, not the model's reported single-inference time — queueing and batching dominate.
- **Drift**: the live distribution (lighting, angles, season) drifts from training data; sample new detections periodically and compare confidence distributions.

## Related systems

- [LLM agents and workflows](../llm-agents-and-workflows) — vision models feed a multimodal agent the same way RAG feeds it text
- [LLM RAG pipeline](../llm-rag-pipeline) — vector search on image embeddings uses the same retrieval stack
- [Data pipelines ETL/ELT](../data-pipelines-etl-elt) — vision annotations land in the same warehouse as every other signal
- [Kubernetes and container orchestration](../kubernetes-and-container-orchestration) — how GPU inference pods get scheduled and scaled
- [Data warehouse and lakehouse architecture](../data-warehouse-lakehouse-architecture) — where detection events become reporting tables4f:Td9c,
## What this system is

A Zero Trust architecture executes decisions through a small engine: a **policy enforcement point (PEP)** intercepts every request, and a **policy decision point (PDP)** answers *allow, deny, or step-up* — by combining user, device, location, risk, and data signals on every request, not at login.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  U[User / machine] -->|request| PEP[Policy enforcement point<br/>proxy / gateway / SDK]
  PEP -->|evaluate| PDP[Policy decision point]
  PDP --> S1[Identity signal]
  PDP --> S2[Device posture]
  PDP --> S3[Location / network]
  PDP --> S4[Risk / anomaly scoring]
  S1 --> PDP
  S2 --> PDP
  S3 --> PDP
  S4 --> PDP
  PDP -->|allow / deny / step-up| PEP
  PEP -->|allow + minimal scope| App[Backend app]
```

## The signals a PDP weighs

| Signal | What it proves | Can be spoofed? |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Identity (tokens + MFA) | "Who" | Requires the credential — the core |
| Device posture (endpoint compliance) | "From a healthy device" | Only if the endpoint is compromised |
| Location / IP / network | "From where" | Yes — VPN, proxies |
| Anomaly / risk score (velocity, geo, peer) | "Behaves like the user" | Indirect — heuristic, not proof |
| Trusted app / capability | "From a known appliance" | The app's own auth |

The art is **weighting**: a risk signal alone never *grants*; it only *raises or lowers* the bar. "Logged in from an unusual country + new device + admin role" is not access — it's a step-up prompt or a block.

## Per-request vs continuous evaluation

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  A[Session token issued] --> B{Per-request: PDP check<br/>every request}
  B -->|pass| C[Allow with minimal scope]
  B -->|fail live signal| D[Step-up or kill session]
  D --> E[Re-auth with higher bar]
```

- **Per-request**: stateless checks on each call (cheap, catches token/lacency changes).
- **Continuous/risk-adaptive**: background telemetry watches for changes (device just got quarantined, token used from a second IP) and downgrades live.

Where these go wrong: caching the decision at login ("trusted once, trusted forever") — which is exactly the old perimeter habit ZT is supposed to kill.

## Just-in-time and privilege elevation

Privileged access is the crown jewel. **JIT / PAM** makes admin rights *temporary and requested*: a user asks, a grant appears for a window, then it evaporates. Never "admin by default." The principle extends to service accounts too — short-lived workload credentials ([workload identity](../workload-identity-and-mtls), [PKI](../secrets-certificates-and-pki)) are the machine analog.

## Tester checklist

- Trigger a live signal change mid-session (quarantine the device) and confirm the next request is denied, *not* the next login.
- Verify step-up actually re-authenticates: reaching a sensitive scope from an abnormal context requires MFA again.
- Check that a *risk-only* signal can never authorize — only block or escalate.
- Time-box a JIT grant and confirm it expires on schedule with auditing of who requested it.

## Related systems

- [Zero Trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — the umbrella architecture this engine powers
- [Identity and access management](../identity-and-access-management) — where the identity signal originates
- [Workload identity and mTLS](../workload-identity-and-mtls) — policy for machine-to-machine calls too
- [OAuth 2.0 and OpenID Connect](../oauth2-openid-connect) — the token plumbing policies run on50:T1a96,
## What this system is

Every compute decision comes down to two mismatches. **CPU vs GPU**: a CPU is a few very fast, generalist cores built for latency; a GPU is thousands of small, specialized cores built for throughput. **ARM vs x86**: two instruction-set architectures (ISAs) that have converged in performance while diverging in economics. Neither is "better" — each wins a different job, and choosing wrong is the most expensive mistake in cloud bills and ML latency.

## CPU vs GPU: latency vs throughput

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph CPU["CPU - few, fast, generalist"]
    c1[8-64 cores]
    c2[~3-5 GHz] --> c3[Branch prediction<br/>cache hierarchies]
  end
  subgraph GPU["GPU - many, small, specialized"]
    g1[thousands of cores]
    g2[Lower clock - ~1-2 GHz] --> g3[Massive parallelism<br/>SIMT lanes]
  end
  W[Workload] --> Q{Shape?}
  Q -- few, sequential, branchy --> CPU
  Q -- many, parallel, uniform --> GPU
```

- **CPU** is optimized for **latency**: complex control flow, big caches, deep pipelines, strong single-thread performance. It runs your web server, your database queries, anything unpredictable.
- **GPU** is optimized for **throughput**: thousands of cores execute the same instruction on different data in lockstep (SIMT). Great where the same math runs on millions of points — graphics, matrix multiply, vector search, training batches.
- The GPU is **not fast at one thing**, it is fast at *many at once*. A single serial dependency chain runs slower on a GPU than on a CPU.

## The SIMT model in practice

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  Op[One instruction] --> B1[Data element 1] --> ALU[ALU lane]
  Op --> B2[Data element 2] --> ALU
  Op --> B3[Data element 3] --> ALU
  Op --> B4["Data element N (...)"] --> ALU
  ALU --> W1[Write results in parallel]
```

Matrix a×b where both are 1024×1024 = ~1 billion independent multiply-adds. A CPU core does them mostly one at a time; a GPU spreads them across cores so the whole thing finishes in the time a CPU takes for a few thousand. That's the entire story of why ML training lives on GPUs: **the work is embarrassingly parallel, so throughput wins**.

But the same shape helps you find GPU-bound *workloads* that aren't ML: image/video encoding, cryptographic hashing at scale, string/JSON processing, and **vector search** all parallelize.

## Where GPUs are a trap

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Lockstep[Divergent branches] -->|"if value == null - half the threads idle"| Waste[Lanes wasted]
  Transfers[CPU-GPU transfers] -->|"copying data across PCIe"| Waste2[Latency hidden]
  Serial[Long serial chains] -->|"next step needs previous result"| Waste3[GPU idles]
```

Remember the classic exchange-rate failure: **data movement outweighs the math**. Copying 10 MB across PCIe (or network for cloud GPUs) can cost more than the compute would take on CPU. Cheap workloads + expensive transfers = GPU-accelerated *slowness*.

## ARM vs x86: the ISA split

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  subgraph x86["x86 - Intel / AMD"]
    a1[Complex instructions - CISC]
    a2[Backward compatible since 1978]
    a3[High single-thread per-core]
    a4[Decode-heavy - more power per work]
  end
  subgraph ARM["ARM - Apple / AWS Graviton / Ampere"]
    b1[Simple instructions - RISC]
    b2[Licensable - many vendors]
    b3[Many, efficient cores]
    b4[Lower power per work - better perf/W]
  end
```

- **x86** (Intel, AMD) is **CISC**: complex instructions, massive backward compatibility, extremely strong single-core. It remains the default for high-clock latency-bound work.
- **ARM** is **RISC**: simpler fixed-width instructions, and — crucially — **anyone can build one**. Apple (M-series), AWS (Graviton), Ampere, and Qualcomm all ship ARM because the ISA license is buyable.
- What flipped ARM from "phone chip" to "data center chip" is the **perf-per-watt equation**: Graviton and M-series deliver comparable throughput to x86 at a fraction of the power and cost.

## The real decision: perf-per-watt and total cost

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  W[Workload] --> Q{What dominates?}
  Q -- latency / single-thread --> X[High-clock x86]
  Q -- "throughput / parallel" --> G[GPU for math, ARM for scale-out]
  Q -- "sustained load, scale-out" --> A[ARM - Graviton / Ampere]
  Q -- "training / large vectors" --> G
```

- **Power density is now the ceiling.** Data centers sell watts, not cores. A chip that does the same work for half the power lets you run ~2× the workload in the same rack — this is why cloud providers push ARM instance families so hard.
- **Total-cost math**: ARM instances (e.g. AWS Graviton) routinely come out 20-40% cheaper for the same throughput on scale-out workloads — API servers, caches, web tiers, CI fleets.
- **x86 still wins where latency is the SLA and software is tied to x86 details**: some commercial databases and legacy binaries ship x86-only, making the "cheaper" ARM option a migration project.

## Choosing compute shape by workload

| Workload | Shape | Best silicon |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Web API, gateway, app tier | Latency, spiky, serial | ARM (scale-out) or x86 high-clock |
| Database OLTP | Latency, branchy | x86 high-clock / high cache; ARM where tuned |
| Batch / ETL / CI | Throughput, parallel | ARM many-core or GPU for math-heavy |
| ML training | Parallel math | GPU |
| ML inference | Mix — batch = GPU, real-time = CPU/edge | GPU for batch, quantized on CPU/NPU for edge |
| Vector search / encode | Parallel | GPU or ARM, measure |
| Legacy commercial software | Often x86-locked | x86 |

## Tester checklist

- **Benchmark the real mix, not a microbenchmark**: an ARM vs x86 decision measured on one synthetically parallel test lies — run your actual request mix and total-cost over a week.
- **Check power, not just speed**: compare perf-per-watt, since rack power and cooling cap your actual scale.
- **Software lock-in test**: run your observability, commercial DB, and security agents on the candidate ARM image before committing — dwarves hide in middleware.
- **GPU sanity check**: profile your kernel's PCIe transfer time vs compute time; if transfers dominate, the GPU is a liability.
- **Cost per result**: for ML workloads, measure cost per 1k inferences/train-epoch, not instance-hour price.

- [Cloud computing fundamentals](../cloud-computing-fundamentals) — regions, AZs, and the service model your chosen silicon runs as
- [Serverless computing and functions](../serverless-computing-and-functions) — where the platform hides the CPU/GPU decision from you
- [Computer vision pipelines](../computer-vision-pipelines) — GPUs and NPUs in real-time inference systems
- [Kubernetes and container orchestration](../kubernetes-and-container-orchestration) — how heterogeneous CPU/GPU/ARM nodes get scheduled51:Tcd3,
## What this system is

A CRM is the **front office**: customer-facing teams start and transact here. It owns the customer's whole journey — marketing touch, lead, opportunity, quote, order, case, and renewal — before (and after) the ERP ever sees a transaction. Where the ERP is the *system of record for money*, the CRM is the *system of record for relationships*.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Lead[Lead] --> Qual[Qualify] --> Opp[Opportunity] --> Quote[Quote] --> Win[Closed won] --> Order[Order into ERP] --> Service[Service + renewals]
```

## The core objects

| Object | Owner | State it tracks | ERP handoff |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Lead / prospect | Marketing + sales | New → qualified | Converted to account+contact |
| Account / contact | Sales ops | Hierarchy, territory | Customer master |
| Opportunity | Sales | Stage, amount, probability | None until won |
| Quote | Sales | Draft → accepted | Order / contract |
| Order | Sales or ERP | Confirmed → shipped | The real handoff |
| Case / ticket | Service | Open → resolved | Field-service or warranty |

The dirty secret of CRM architecture: **a CRM without an ERP handoff is a list of wishes**. The quote-to-order moment is where the relationship system must hand an unambiguous, priced, legal order to the money system — and that handoff is most of the integration work.

## Sales pipeline, the funnel

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  L[Leads in] --> Q1[Qualified - meetings happen]
  Q1 --> P[Proposal / quote]
  P --> W{Win or lose}
  W -->|win| O[Closed won - handoff to ERP]
  W -->|lose| L2[Closed lost - keep for future]
  O --> S[Service: cases, renewals, upsell]
```

Funnel analytics is where the CRM earns its keep: **conversion rates between stages** tell you where the funnel leaks. The stages are configuration; the *metrics* are the system.

## The handoff, done right

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Lead["Lead"] -->|qualify| Opp["Opportunity"]
  Opp -->|configure - price| Quote["Quote (CPQ)"]
  Quote -->|accepted| Won["Closed won"]
  Won -->|order + contract| ERP["ERP order"]
  ERP -->|fulfill + onboard| Serv["Service + renewal"]
```

## Tester checklist

- Quote-to-order: a quote accepted in CRM must create exactly one order in the ERP with matching line items, prices, and currency — no double entry.
- Field-level match: the customer the CRM is "in love with" must be the same customer the ERP bills (account mapping, not a name string).
- Funnel truth: stages must match the sales process, and a win/lose reason must be enforced — not free text.
- Role-scoped data: sales reps see only their territory, and the service desk sees the full history without editing it.

- [ERP systems](../enterprise-resource-planning) — where the won deal becomes money
- [Webhook integrations](../webhook-integrations) — how CRM events ping everything downstream
- [Message queue delivery](../message-queue-delivery) — decoupling CRM events from API consumers at scale
- [API request lifecycle](../api-request-lifecycle) — the request path behind each CRM integration
- [OAuth grant types](../oauth-grant-types) — the auth mechanics behind CRM API access
