## What this system is

Data governance is the **answer to "whose data is this, what does it mean, who may use it, and is it true?"** — enforced by a data catalog (inventory + metadata), ownership (every data set has a human answerable for it), access rules (who may see what), and quality checks (does the data match its claims). Without it, every analytics team independently builds "the real revenue" and nobody agrees on what the number means.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  P[Data producers<br/>systems + pipelines] -->|register| C[Data catalog<br/>metadata + lineage]
  C -->|assign steward| O[Owners<br/>one answerable human per set]
  C -->|expose rules| A[Access control<br/>roles + policies]
  C -->|monitor| Q[Quality checks<br/>freshness + accuracy]
  A -->|grant| CON[Consumers<br/>BI, ML, reporting]
  Q -->|trust| CON
  CON -->|discover + feedback| C
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  P[Producer] -->|register + document| C[Catalog]
  C --> O[Owner / steward assigned]
  C --> A[Access policies per role]
  C --> Q[Quality + freshness monitors]
  O --> CON[Consumer trusts + uses]
  A --> CON
  Q --> CON
```

## The four pillars

| Pillar | The question it answers | What it looks like in practice |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Catalog** | What data exists, where, and what does it mean? | Searchable inventory: tables, columns, descriptions, tags, owner |
| **Ownership** | Who is answerable for this data? | A named owner/steward per data set, reviewable and on-call for it |
| **Access** | Who may read/modify it, and why? | Role-based permissions, PII classification, approval flows |
| **Quality** | Is it current, complete, and correct? | Freshness, row-count, and validity checks with alerts |

## Ownership: one human per data set

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  DS[Data set: sales_orders] --> O1[Owner: the business lead]
  DS --> O2[Steward: the data engineer who knows the pipes]
  DS --> O3[Consumer: BI team]
  O2 --> Q[Owns: accuracy, freshness, definition of each metric]
```

"Governance" collapses into a single enforceable rule: **every data set has one named owner**, and that owner signs off on its definition, quality, and who may access it. The failure mode to avoid is an **orphan data set** — created three years ago by a team that left, trusted by nobody, cached everywhere. Orphan data is how "the real number" starts having five versions.

## The catalog is the nervous system

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  C[Catalog entry] --> M1[Metadata: owner, description, tags, PII flags]
  C --> M2[Lineage: upstream sources → this table → downstream reports]
  C --> M3[Quality: last refreshed, row count, check status]
  C --> M4[Access: who has what, pending requests]
  D[Discover: analysts search the catalog before building] --> C
```

The catalog turns "where is the customer data?" from a tribal-knowledge question into a search. **Lineage** (this table comes from `orders` via pipeline `p1`, feeds report `r2`) is what makes impact assessment possible: "if we change this column, what breaks?" Without lineage, every schema change is a roll of the dice against every report nobody can find.

## Access and classification: the security spine

- **Classify first, gate second**: mark PII (personal data), sensitive, and public at the catalog level; access rules and masking attach to the classification, not to ad-hoc roles.
- **Row/column-level security** for the sensitive columns: a consumer can read `customers` but *not* `customers.email` — masking at the catalog/engine layer, not "just don't select it."
- **The least-privilege audit**: the recurring finding is a BI group with a warehouse-wide "analyst" role that quietly reads PII they don't need. Access follows a *purpose*, granted on request, reviewed.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  R[Request access] --> C{Is the purpose approved?}
  C -->|yes| G[Grant scoped role]
  C -->|no| D[Deny + record]
  G --> M{Need to see PII?}
  M -->|no| MASK[Masked view]
  M -->|yes| PII[Explicit, justified, logged]
```

## Quality checks: the trust contract

| Check | Catches |
| --- | --- |
| Freshness | "Last loaded 3 days ago" — the report is stale and nobody knows |
| Row-count / volume | Pipeline silently stopped or partially loaded |
| Null/validity rate | A column that's suddenly 80% null is a broken transform |
| Duplicate-key check | Double-counted fact rows |
| Definition agreement | Two dashboards disagreeing on the same metric |

**One definition per metric** is the governance hill to die on: `revenue` is either defined (same SQL, same grain, same timezone, certified in the catalog) or it isn't a metric at all. The technical trick that makes this stick is **metrics as governed artifacts** (a shared metrics/SQL layer), not copied formula snippets in each dashboard.

## Tester checklist

- Pick a data set in the catalog and verify it has: an owner, a description, quality checks, and lineage to both its source and its consumers. If any are missing, that's the governance gap.
- Try to access a PII column with a role that has no justification: it must be masked or denied, not simply "not mentioned."
- Find two dashboards that show the "same" metric and confirm they read the *same* certified definition (or surface the disagreement).
- Kill a source pipeline and confirm the freshness monitor alerts within the SLA — not after someone asks why the report is wrong.
- Verify lineage impact: identify what downstream reports would break if a column is renamed, and confirm the catalog tells you that list before the change.

## Related systems

- [Data warehouse and lakehouse architecture](../data-warehouse-lakehouse-architecture) — the systems governance governs
- [Data pipelines (ETL/ELT)](../data-pipelines-etl-elt) — the pipes whose health the quality checks watch
- [RBAC and authorization](../rbac-and-authorization) — the access model behind the catalog's grants
- [Data modeling and star schemas](../data-modeling-star-schemas) — the models that need a certified owner53:T1790,
## What this system is

Data modeling is deciding **how to shape data before you store it**. The data architect's core move is recognizing that the *same* business facts need two different models: a **transactional model** (normalized, write-optimized, for the apps) and an **analytical model** (denormalized, read-optimized, for reporting). The star schema is the analytical model's default shape — and misunderstanding why it's a star is where most data teams lose their way.

```mermaid
erDiagram
    "Operational DB - 3NF, write-optimized" ||--o{ "Model the same facts for analysis" : "extract + transform"
    "Model the same facts for analysis" ||--o{ "Fact table - measures and foreign keys" : "facts"
    "Fact table - measures and foreign keys" ||--o{ "Dimension date" : "date_key"
    "Fact table - measures and foreign keys" ||--o{ "Dimension customer" : "customer_key"
    "Fact table - measures and foreign keys" ||--o{ "Dimension product" : "product_key"
    "Dimension date" ||--o{ "BI queries - filter and group by dims" : "by month"
    "Dimension customer" ||--o{ "BI queries - filter and group by dims" : "by region"
    "Dimension product" ||--o{ "BI queries - filter and group by dims" : "by SKU"
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  OPS[(Operational 3NF)] -->|ETL/ELT| FACT[fact_sales]
  FACT -->|date_key| D1[dim_date]
  FACT -->|customer_key| D2[dim_customer]
  FACT -->|product_key| D3[dim_product]
  D1 --> BI[BI: revenue by month]
  D2 --> BI
  D3 --> BI
```

## The two models, side by side

| | Transactional (OLTP) | Analytical (star schema) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Model | Normalized (3NF), minimal duplication | Denormalized: one fact + joined dimensions |
| Optimized for | Fast writes, consistent single records | Fast reads, big scans, filtering by dimension |
| Shape | Many small related tables | A few wide fact tables + dimension tables |
| Example | `orders`, `order_lines`, `customers`, `products` all separate | `fact_sales(amount, qty, date_key, cust_key, prod_key)` |
| Driven by | Apps, transactions | BI, reports, analytics |

A star schema is simply a **fact table in the middle** (the measurements: `amount`, `quantity`) surrounded by **dimension tables** (the descriptors: date, customer, product). Every dimension links to the fact by a surrogate key. It's a star because lots of small dimension tables point *at* one central fact.

## Facts vs dimensions: the two kinds of table

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  F[fact table] --> FM[Measures: amount, qty, duration<br/>these are the numbers you sum/avg]
  F --> FK[Foreign keys to every dimension<br/>the descriptors of each event]
  D[dimension table] --> DL[Label + attributes: name, region, category]
  D --> SK[Surrogate key - stable, not the source id]
```

- **Fact tables** hold *measures* (numbers you aggregate) and *foreign keys* into dimensions. They grow huge; queries filter and group by dimension attributes, then sum the measures.
- **Dimension tables** hold *descriptive attributes* (labels you slice by). The **surrogate key** is key: it decouples the warehouse from source-system IDs that get reused or changed.
- **Degenerate dimensions** live in the fact when a descriptor has no attributes of its own (e.g. an order number — it's a dimension with no extra columns, so keep it as a column, not a joined table).

## Granularity: the decision everything hangs on

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  Q[What does one row in the fact mean?] --> O1[One row per order line]
  Q --> O2[One row per order]
  Q --> O3[One row per day per product]
  Q --> O4[One row per event - finest]
  G[Granularity decided - <br/>you can aggregate, never split]
```

The granularity of a fact table ("each row = one order line") is fixed when you build it. You can **always aggregate up** and lose detail — you can *never* split one order line back into two rows later. Choosing granularity too coarse ("sales by day") makes "hourly by region" impossible later. Coarse granularity is the classic irreversible modeling mistake.

## Normalization trade-offs, in the architect's chair

| Choice | Wins | Costs |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 3NF operational schema | Consistent updates, no duplication, app-friendly | Slow analytical queries, many joins |
| Star/denormalized dimensions | Fast reads, obvious query paths, BI-friendly | Load-time complexity, joins in load, dimension maintenance |
| **Slowly changing dimensions (SCD)** | History preserved ("old address" still answers "what was it then") | More rows, type-1 vs type-2 trade-offs |

**SCD type-2** (keep a current row + a history row keyed by effective dates) is the standard answer to "customers change their region — do old reports rewrite history?" The answer is no: preserve the dimension version at event time. SCD type-1 (overwrite) is for attributes only useful in the present.

## Tester checklist

- Query the star and confirm the shape: any report reads *one* fact and *joins* the dimensions it needs — no dimension-dimension joins.
- Force a granularity trap: aggregate the finest fact up to "per month" and confirm it's exact; then try to split it back and confirm the data is gone for good.
- Change a dimension attribute (a customer's region) and verify type-2 history: the report for last month still shows the old region, the current view the new one.
- Confirm surrogate keys are stable: a source id reused/reassigned must not corrupt the fact (that's what the surrogate key prevents).
- Check for fan-out/duplication: one order with two line items must not double-count in the fact if your grain is per-order-line.

## Related systems

- [Data warehouse and lakehouse architecture](../data-warehouse-lakehouse-architecture) — the system the star schema lives in
- [Data pipelines (ETL/ELT)](../data-pipelines-etl-elt) — how facts and dimensions get built from source data
- [Database indexing](../database-indexing) — the indexes the query planner uses on facts
- [Data governance and catalog](../data-governance-and-catalog) — who owns and certifies these models54:T17a1,
## What this system is

A data pipeline moves data from source systems into analytical storage — reliably, repeatedly, and at the right freshness. The two recurring choices are **batch vs streaming** and **where you transform** (ETL: transform before loading into the warehouse; ELT: load raw, transform inside the warehouse). Every pipeline debate in a room with data engineers is really one of these two choices.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  SRC[Source<br/>database / API / files] -->|change feed / query| EXT[Extract<br/>CDC / query / pull]
  EXT -->|batch job| PIPE[Pipeline orchestrator<br/>DAG of tasks]
  PIPE -->|stream events| LOAD[Load broker<br/>message queue / sink]
  LOAD -->|land raw| LAKE[Raw layer<br/>bronze / landing]
  LAKE -->|transform to gold| OUT[Refined tables<br/>gold for analytics]
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  S[Source] -->|CDC / query| E[Extract]
  E -->|batch| O[Orchestrator - DAG]
  E -->|stream| Q[(Message queue / stream)]
  O --> L[Load to raw layer]
  Q --> L
  L --> T[Transform to refined tables]
  T --> BI[BI / analytics / ML]
```

## Batch vs streaming in one table

| | Batch | Streaming |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Freshness | Hours-to-daily, on a schedule | Seconds-to-minutes, continuous |
| Volume shape | Big chunks, aligned windows | Continuous small events |
| Complexity | Simpler, retryable, well-understood | Stateful, exactly-once machinery |
| The right question | "Is yesterday's data good enough?" | "Does the report need to move in real time?" |

The architect's default is **batch**, upgraded to streaming only when a *business need* demands freshness — because every streaming pipeline adds state, ordering, and exactly-once complexity that batch just doesn't have. "We need real time" is a functional requirement, not a quality badge.

## ETL vs ELT: where does the transform live?

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  ETL[ETL - transform before load<br/>older warehouse era] --> L1[Load transformed, clean data]
  ELT[ELT - load raw first<br/>modern cloud warehouse] --> L2[Land raw cheaply, transform in-warehouse]
  L2 --> S1[Powerful engines: ray of cloud compute]
  L2 --> S2[Raw preserved for reprocessing]
```

| | ETL | ELT |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Extract | Same | Same (get the raw data out) |
| Transform | Before the load, in the pipeline tool | After the load, **inside the warehouse** (SQL) |
| Why it shifted | Warehouses were costly → clean before you pay to store | Cloud warehouses made compute cheap → store raw, transform on demand |
| Strength | Load only clean, curated output | Raw data preserved, transforms in one SQL engine, reprocess anytime |

The modern answer is usually **ELT**: land raw (bronze) in the lake/warehouse cheaply, then build refined views (gold) in SQL. It preserves the source of truth and defers modeling until you know what you need — transformation becomes a query, not a one-way trip.

## The pipeline's three contract rules

Apache Airflow-style **DAGs** (`extract → validate → transform → load → notify`) give retries, scheduling, and lineage for free. Three rules keep them honest:

1. **Idempotency** — re-running a job must give the *same* result, not double-count. Keys (natural keys or load IDs) plus full-refresh-or-upsert semantics are the guarantee. A pipeline that doubles rows when re-run is a pipeline that lies.
2. **Backfills** — at some point raw data arrives late or a transform changes. The golden-layer views must be *recomputable* from raw (that's why ELT stores raw), so a backfill = re-run transforms, not re-fetch sources.
3. **Observability** — a pipeline is only "healthy" if row counts, freshness, and schema are checked on every run. Alerts fire on "this table didn't update for 24 h", not just on job failure.

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant O as Orchestrator
  participant T as Transform job
  participant W as Warehouse

  O->>T: run task (idempotent)
  T->>W: declare transaction
  T->>W: write rows keyed by load id
  T->>W: validate: count = expected
  W-->>T: commit (re-run = same result)
  T-->>O: success + metrics
```

## Source extraction: CDC above full dumps

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  OLT[OLTP source] --> CDC[Change data capture<br/>log-based, only changes]
  CDC --> KW[(Kafka / stream)]
  KW --> PIPE[Pipeline]
```

For frequent, fresh loads the extract is **CDC (change data capture)** reading the source's transaction log (`WAL`) — you capture *changes*, not whole tables. Full-table dumps scale badly and hammer the source. But CDC is front-loaded complexity (ordering, duplicates, schema drift), so the right call for a small low-churn table is still a simple periodic full refresh — pick the minimal mechanical comedy that meets the freshness SLA.

## Tester checklist

- **Idempotency test**: run a pipeline twice and confirm row counts and aggregates are identical (no double counting).
- **Backfill test**: change the golden transform, re-run it from raw for a past time window, and confirm the time-travelable historical numbers change — not by appending.
- **Freshness guard**: pause the source feed, and confirm the alerting flags the stale table within the SLA window, not after a human notices.
- **Schema drift**: rename a source column and confirm the pipeline either transforms or *fails loudly* with validation, never loads corruption silently.
- **Exactly-once (streaming)**: deliberately restart a consumer mid-batch and verify no event is lost or duplicated end to end.

## Related systems

- [Data modeling and star schemas](../data-modeling-star-schemas) — what the golden layer's tables look like
- [Data warehouse and lakehouse architecture](../data-warehouse-lakehouse-architecture) — the destination layers pipelines feed
- [Change data capture](../change-data-capture) — the streaming extraction in front of pipelines
- [Monitoring and observability](../monitoring-and-observability) — the metrics pipelines need to stay trustworthy
- [Pub/sub event streaming](../pub-sub-event-streaming) — the queue middlemen events ride through55:T15a4,
## What this system is

Where does analytical data *live*? Three architectures answer differently. A **warehouse** curates data into a governed, queryable store. A **data lake** keeps raw files at low cost, schema optional. A **lakehouse** tries to merge them: lake storage cost, warehouse governance and SQL on top. The data architect's job is to pick the right home per data set — not to have one religious position.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  SRC[Source systems<br/>OLTP apps, files] -->|pipeline| ING[Ingest - batch + stream]
  ING -->|land raw| LAKE[Data lake<br/>raw files, cheap]
  ING -->|curated load| WH[Warehouse<br/>curated tables]
  ING -->|ACID table| LH[Lakehouse<br/>lake + ACID tables]
  LAKE -->|govern later| LH
  WH -->|SQL| BI[BI / ML / reports]
  LH -->|SQL + files| BI
```

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  S[Sources] --> I[Ingest]
  I --> LAKE[(Lake - raw files, cheap, no schema)]
  I --> WH[(Warehouse - curated, schema-on-write, governed)]
  LAKE --> LH[(Lakehouse - ACID tables on lake storage)]
  WH --> BI[BI + ML]
  LH --> BI
```

## Warehouse vs lake vs lakehouse

| | Data warehouse | Data lake | Lakehouse |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Storage | Optimized columnar (proprietary or open) | Object storage — files | Object storage + open table format |
| Schema | Schema-on-write, enforced | Schema-on-read, optional | Schema enforced on ACID tables |
| Governance | Strong: ACID, roles, lineage | Weak by default (a swamp without rules) | Mature via table formats (Delta/Iceberg/Hudi) |
| Best for | Curated, trusted reporting | Raw/exploratory, ML, cheap retention | Both: raw + refined, one copy |
| Cost for big data | Expensive to store everything | Cheap to store everything | Cheap storage, compute on query |

The one-paragraph summary: **warehouses win on trust, lakes win on cost, lakehouses try to take both.** In practice most orgs run *both*: a lake holds raw and semi-raw data cheaply, and a warehouse/lakehouse curates the governed views people actually query.

## The lakehouse mechanism: open table formats

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  F1[Data files - Parquet] --> T[Table format metadata - Delta/Iceberg/Hudi]
  F2[More files - versioned] --> T
  T --> Q{Queries see a snapshot?}
  Q -->|yes| OK[ACID: readers see consistent state,<br/>time travel to old versions]
  Q -->|no| WRONG[Files half-written - analytics corrupt]
```

A lakehouse is not "SQL on files" (that's a lake with extra steps and fragile consistency). The table format (Delta Lake, Iceberg, Hudi) adds **ACID transactions over object storage**: writers commit atomically, readers see a consistent snapshot, and you can **time-travel** to previous versions. That's what turns cheap lake storage into something you can trust for a report.

## What belongs where

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  Q{Data set: refined, frequent, governed?} -->|yes| WH[Warehouse / lakehouse - curated]
  Q -->|no - raw, transient, exploratory?| LAKE[Lake - land it, figure it out later]
  Q -->|both: cheap raw + governed refined?| BOTH[Lakehouse - one copy, layers on top]
```

- **Warehouse-first**: canonical business reporting, finance, anything you answer for in an audit — needs ACID, roles, and lineage *now*.
- **Lake-first**: raw clickstreams, sensor dumps, ML feature exploration — storing *everything* curated would cost too much and nobody knows the schema yet.
- **Lakehouse pattern**: land raw in the lake, then define refined governed tables (via the table format) for the queries that matter — keeping one copy of the data instead of duplicating it into a warehouse.
- **The universal law**: never let analysts query production OLTP directly; everything analytical flows through curated copies.

## The failure modes a data architect owns

- The **data swamp**: a lake without governance is a lake where nobody trusts the data — the fix is catalogs, owners, and at least a bronze/silver/gold (raw/refined/aggregated) layer naming.
- **Two sources of truth**: a warehouse and a lake storing the same fact differently, reconciled by nobody — pick per-data-set ownership and a single definition per metric.
- **Schema drift**: source columns change and the pipeline breaks silently; the lakehouse's schema-evolution rules and validation are what catch it.
- **Buying only one answer**: choosing lakehouse and storing *nothing* governed, or warehouse and paying to store gigabytes nobody queries.

## Tester checklist

- Verify ACID: read the table while a load is mid-write — readers must see either the old or the new snapshot, never a half-written middle.
- Enable time travel ability: query the table as of yesterday and confirm the row counts match that version exactly.
- Check governance: a member of the BI group can *not* read the raw lake's sensitive columns unless granted; readers see a defined view, not the files.
- Confirm pipeline schema drift detection: a renamed source column either transforms or explicitly fails the layer, not silently corrupts.
- Cost test: query the same report from warehouse vs lakehouse and compare latency and scan cost per run — know what you're paying for curation.

## Related systems

- [Data modeling and star schemas](../data-modeling-star-schemas) — the shapes these curated layers store
- [Data pipelines (ETL/ELT)](../data-pipelines-etl-elt) — how data reaches each layer
- [Data governance and catalog](../data-governance-and-catalog) — the owners, lineage, and access rules
- [Change data capture](../change-data-capture) — the streaming in path for warehouse loads56:T1336,
## What this system is

Defense in depth is the strategy of building **many independent layers of control** so that defeating one does not defeat the system — the military idea of successive rings around the objective. One firewall, one password, or one antivirus is a *point defense*; defense in depth assumes any single control will eventually fail and therefore never lets a single failure equal total compromise.

Each layer should also be *diverse* — different vendor, different mechanism, different owner — because identical layers fail for the same reason.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    P["Identity & policy"] --- N["Perimeter & network"]
    N --- H["Host & endpoint"]
    H --- A["Application"]
    A --- D["Data"]
    D --- P
```

An attacker entering at the network layer still has to defeat host, application, and data controls before the mission-critical asset — which is why each layer gets its own detection, not just prevention.

## The layers

| Layer | Examples | What it stops |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Physical | Guards, locks, security zones, hardware security modules | Physical tampering and theft |
| Perimeter & network | Firewalls, segmentation, VPN/ZTA gateways, IDS/IPS, DNS filtering | Blind scanning and lateral spread |
| Identity & access | SSO + MFA, conditional access, least privilege, JML | Credential abuse and insider access |
| Host & endpoint | EDR, hardening, patch management, disk encryption, application allowlists | Malware execution and persistence |
| Application | Input validation, WAF, secure code review, secrets scanning, rate limits | Injection and app-level exploits |
| Data | Encryption at rest/in transit, tokenization, backup, DLP, access reviews | Stolen or exfiltrated data |

The layers aren't a checklist to all boil down to tools — **people and process** are controls too (security awareness, incident response, change management), and they often fail *first*.

## Parallel chains, not one fence

Controls in depth are usually arranged in parallel so a failure in one chain doesn't drop the whole defense:

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  ATT[Attacker] -->|attempt| PER[Network edge<br/>FW + IDS/IPS]
  PER -->|network path| HST[Host<br/>EDR + hardening]
  PER -->|bypasses?| IDN[Identity<br/>SSO + MFA]
  HST -->|local access| APP[Application<br/>WAF + authz]
  IDN -->|identity check| APP
  APP -->|authorized only| DAT[Data<br/>encryption + ACLs]
```

## Defense in depth + Zero Trust

Defense in depth and [Zero Trust](../zero-trust-architecture) are complementary, not competing:

- **Zero Trust** answers *who and under what conditions* gets access, per request.
- **Defense in depth** answers *what happens when access is somehow granted anyway* — the blast radius inside the network, host, and data.

In practice: Zero Trust shrinks the perimeter to the identity; defense in depth makes sure a compromised identity still can't reach the crown jewels silently.

## Principles that make it work

1. **Assume failure** — design for the control that *will* be evaded, and detect it.
2. **Diversity of mechanisms** — don't stack five logins and call it depth; vary vendors, protocols, and owners.
3. **Detection for every prevention** — logging, alerting, and response at each layer, feeding a [SOC](../security-operations-center-soc).
4. **Fail safe, deny by default** — when a control errors, it should deny, not allow.
5. **Crown-jewel focus** — deepest layers protect the data that, if exfiltrated, ends the company.

## Tester checklist

- For each layer, enumerate what it prevents *and* what it lets through — verify the next layer catches the overflow.
- Confirm controls have *different* failure modes: same vendor signature-based detection at two layers is one layer.
- Check that a control failure trends toward *deny* (fail closed), not allow.
- Verify detection layers actually produce usable alerts and that response is exercised (tabletop a breach).
- Look for the classic gap: a control on paper but not enforced in the critical path (e.g., encryption configured but app plaintexts to another internal service).

## Related systems

- [Zero Trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — the identity-focused perimeter that pairs with layered depth
- [Threat modeling with STRIDE](../threat-modeling-stride) — find the threats each layer must counter
- [OWASP Top 10](../owasp-top-10) — the application layer's threat roster

## Deep dives

- [Security Operations Center (SOC)](../security-operations-center-soc) — where layer detections converge
- [Identity and access management](../identity-and-access-management) — the identity layer used by every other layer
- [Secrets, certificates and PKI](../secrets-certificates-and-pki) — the crypto that authenticates the chain
- [Cloud networking and hybrid connectivity](../cloud-networking-and-hybrid-connectivity) — network segmentation across the estate57:Te9d,
## What this system is

DevOps is not a tool — it's the **feedback loop**: an outcome is wanted (ticket), a change is made (git), proven (pipeline), shipped (release), observed (metrics/alerts), and the loop closes when the next ticket reflects what actually happened. Each guide in this module is one stage of the loop.

```mermaid
stateDiagram-v2
  [*] --> Ticket
  Ticket: Ticket - user needs X
  Ticket --> Commit: the why
  Commit: Commit + PR
  Commit --> Pipeline: build + test
  Pipeline: Pipeline - lint, test, deploy
  Pipeline --> Release: ship
  Release: Release to prod
  Release --> Observe: is-it-fine
  Observe: Observe - metrics and alerts
  Observe --> Feedback: lessons
  Feedback: Feedback - next requirement
  Feedback --> Ticket: loop closes
  Feedback --> [*]
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  T[Ticket: user needs X] --> C[Commit + PR]
  C --> P[Pipeline: lint, test, build, deploy]
  P --> R[Release to prod]
  R --> O[Observe: logs, metrics, alerts]
  O --> F[Feedback: what broke / what improved]
  F --> T
```

## Who touches what, and where it lives

| Artifact | Owned by | Lives in | The job |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Requirement | Product/architect | Jira | The *why* |
| Change | Dev | git branch + PR | The *what* |
| Build | Pipeline | Artifact registry | The *proof* |
| Runtime truth | DevOps/SRE | Observability stack | The *is-it-fine* |
| Feedback | Everyone | Next ticket | The *loop* |

The discipline is **immutability of each link**: the ticket says why, the commit says what shipped, the artifact matches the tested code, and the alarm links back to the deploy. Break any link and incidents become archaeology.

## Deploy vs release, observability as a habit

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  DEP["Deploy (code in prod)"] --> REL["Release (users see it)"]
  REL --> OBS[Observe - dashboard + alerts + logs]
  OBS -->|anomaly| ROL[Rollback fast / feature-flag OFF]
  OBS -->|healthy| NEXT[Next ticket learns it]
```

The strongest teams separate **deploy** (dark, wrapped in features flags) from **release** (turning it on) — then observability, not speculation, drives the next step.

## Metrics that tell the truth

| Metric | Means | Trend that's good |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Lead time | From commit to production | Shrinking |
| Deploy frequency | How often you ship | Growing (safely!) |
| Change fail rate | % of changes that break prod | Low and stable |
| MTTR | Mean time to restore | Shrinking |
| Escaped defects | Bugs reaching users | Falling |

Watch the **co-variances** — deploy frequency up *and* fail rate up is a curve to notice, not a badge. The DORA metrics are the standard vocabulary here.

## Tester checklist

- Pick one release and walk the full chain in reverse: alarm → deploy SHA → artifact → build job → committing PR → ticket. Any dead link is a gap to fix.
- Confirm a prod rollback is a *practiced* flow: someone has done a canary-off/blue-green flip recently, not just "we could."
- Check the observability story behind every alert: each alarm has a runbook that names the ticket/library that produced it.
- Report the four DORA numbers for one month from your actual systems — where is the worst coefficient?

## Related systems

- [Git and version control](../git-and-version-control) — the change-making stage of the loop
- [CI/CD pipelines](../ci-cd-pipelines) — the proving-plus-shipping stage
- [Jira and agile delivery](../jira-and-agile-delivery) — the intent and feedback sides
- [ITIL change and release management](../itil-change-and-release-management) — the governance layer that decides when the loop may touch production
- [API gateway caching](../api-gateway-caching) — observability and caching at the edge of runtime truth58:T15b8,
## What this system is

DHCP (Dynamic Host Configuration Protocol) automates the boring-but-critical job of turning a blank network interface into a configured host: IP address, subnet mask, default gateway, DNS servers, NTP, domain suffix. A fleet of devices, printers, and phones that changes daily would be unmanageable if every address had to be typed by hand — DHCP leases each device an address and *reclaims* it when it's done.

It works over UDP: clients broadcast on **port 67/68**. No initiator address yet, so the client's first messages are broadcast `255.255.255.255` — the "election" that only the DHCP server wins.

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant C as Client - no IP yet
  participant R as L2 broadcast switch / relay agent
  participant S as DHCP server - scope + lease pool

  C->>R: DHCP DISCOVER - broadcast
  R->>S: forwarded via relay agent or on-link
  S-->>C: DHCP OFFER, REQUEST, ACK
  Note over C: configured - IP + gateway + DNS
```

## The DORA conversation

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant C as Client
  participant R as Relay agent (optional)
  participant S as DHCP server

  C->>R: DHCP DISCOVER (broadcast)
  R->>S: forward with giaddr (relay's address)
  S->>R: DHCP OFFER (offered IP + options)
  R->>C: broadcast OFFER on the segment
  C->>R: DHCP REQUEST (I choose this offer)
  R->>S: forward to the server
  S->>R: DHCP ACK (confirmed, lease starts)
  R->>C: ACK delivered to the client
```

That's **DORA**: **D**iscover, **O**ffer, **R**equest, **A**ck. It's four messages in two exchanges (broadcast round-trip for offer, then explicit request+ack for the chosen lease). Modern cases collapse things: when a client reconnects before its lease expires, it can go straight to `REQUEST` (renewal at T1/T2 timer thresholds) — no full DORA needed.

## Scopes, pools, leases, reservations

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  S[DHCP scope: 10.20.0.0/24] --> P[Pool 10.20.0.100 - 10.20.0.200]
  P --> L[Lease: given IP + options, for 8h]
  S --> R[Reservation: printer MAC → 10.20.0.50 fixed]
  L --> R
```

| Concept | What it is | Gotcha |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Scope** | Boundary of addresses a server manages (a subnet) | Define via the network's subnet mask, not a guess |
| **Pool** | Range of addresses the server may assign | Overlapping pools → duplicate IPs across servers |
| **Lease** | Address + options valid for a duration | Too-short leases churn DNS; too-long stalls IP-pool reuse |
| **Reservation** | MAC → fixed IP binding | `binding clients` only works per MAC; printers & servers use these |
| **Exclusion** | Addresses the server won't assign (fixed hosts must live here) | Without exclusions the pool eats your static stats |

## Options that make an address actually useful

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  S[DHCP ACK] --> O1[Option 1: subnet mask]
  S --> O2[Option 3: default gateway]
  S --> O3[Option 6: DNS servers]
  S --> O4[Option 15: domain suffix]
  S --> O5[Option 51: lease time]
```

An IP alone is useless — the **options** are the real payload. The server is authoritative for the whole config: gateway, DNS, NTP, and even the domain search suffix. That's why "I can ping the server but DNS is broken" often turns out to be "the DHCP scope points at the wrong DNS server."

## DHCP relay: one server, many segments

A DHCP server answers broadcasts on its own subnet — but offices typically have many VLANs. The **relay agent** (often the router/switch, or `ip helper-address` on Cisco / `dhcp-relay` on Linux) catches the broadcast, stamps its own address in `giaddr`, and forwards the request **unicast** to the server. The server uses `giaddr` to pick which scope to answer from — that's why a relayed client's IP comes from the *right* subnet without any client-side config.

## Security and stability

- **DHCP starvation**: exhaust the pool with fake `DISCOVER`s so legitimate devices get nothing. Guard with DHCP snooping (trust switch ports only) and pool monitoring.
- **Rogue DHCP**: a rogue server offering `10.10.10.1` as gateway gets all traffic. Prevent with DHCP snooping; detect by watching for unexpected servers.
- **Lease hygiene**: clients renew at T1 (50% of lease) and T2 (87.5%); a client that vanishes keeps its IP until the lease lapses. After a network redesign, flush stale leases (`dhcpd lease` files / IPAM cleanup).
- **Across clouds**: in AWS/Azure, DHCP is invisible — the VPC hands each NIC an address from the VNet/VPC CIDR and your "DHCP options set" replaces options 3/6/15 (gateway is implicit, DNS is the VPC resolver, domain is the option set).

## Tester checklist

- Change the scope's gateway or DNS option and confirm a renewed client picks it up immediately (`ipconfig /renew`, `dhclient -r && dhclient`).
- Verify reservations: reboot a printer and check it still owns its fixed IP, while other devices around it get pool addresses.
- Confirm lease timers behave: set a 2-minute lease, note the T1 renewal traffic, and confirm the server reclaims the address after expiry (`release` then no renewal).
- Test relayed subnets: get a device in VLAN 400 and confirm it received a VLAN-400-scope address with the correct gateway (proves `giaddr` works).
- Check for rogue DHCP: sniff for unanswered `OFFER`s from unknown servers.

## Related systems

- [OSI seven-layer model](../osi-seven-layer-model) — DHCP lives at the boundary of layers 2/3
- [TCP/IP and TLS](../tcp-ip-and-tls) — the addressing DHCP configures
- [Network routing and BGP](../network-routing-and-bgp) — the gateways and subnets DHCP hands out59:Tff2,
## What this system is

**Backups** recover *loss* (a deleted table, a bad deploy). **Disaster recovery (DR)** recovers *re-availability* (a whole region, an entire service) — automation plus a plan that turns "the primary is gone" into "traffic keeps serving." Every DR architecture is a trade-off between cost and the two numbers that matter: **RPO** (how much data you can lose) and **RTO** (how long you can be down).

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Data[Data loss tolerance] --> RPO[RPO - minutes / hours / days]
  Outage[Recovery speed needed] --> RTO[RTO - seconds / minutes / hours]
  RPO --> Strategy[Choose DR strategy]
  RTO --> Strategy
  Strategy --> Cost[Cost it honestly - every level up the ladder is more expensive]
```

## The four strategies on the ladder

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph Cheap["Cheap to run"]
    A[Backup + restore] -->|hours| B[Pilot light - small always-on core]
  end
  subgraph Expensive["Expensive to run"]
    subgraph Warm["Pilot light + scaled replicas"]
      C[Warm standby - runs, not serving]
      D[Multi-site active-active]
    end
  end
  B --> C
  C --> D
```

| Strategy | What exists on standby | RTO/RPO picture | Cost |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Backup + restore | Only backups/snapshots | RTO hours, RPO depends on backup cadence | Lowest |
| Pilot light | Tiniest core (DB replica, load balancer) running | RTO ~1hr, RPO minutes | Low |
| Warm standby | Full copy running, not serving | RTO tens of minutes, RPO near-zero | Medium |
| Multi-site active-active | Both sites serving live, traffic split | RTO ~seconds (failover), RPO zero | Highest |

The classic error is skipping the ladder: jumping to "active-active!" as a slogan, then discovering no one knows the runbook and nobody tested the network cutover.

## The DR flow (failover, practiced)

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Primary[Primary region] -->|cadenced backup| Backup[Backups - immutable]
  Primary -->|sync / async replication| Replica[Replica / standby region]
  Replica -->|promote on outage| Failover[Failover decision]
  Backup -->|restore path| Failover
  Runbook[Runbook + regular drills] -->|verified steps| Failover
  Failover -->|validated in drill| Serve[RTO met - serving resumes]
```

## The truths that make a DR plan survive contact

1. **Replication lag is data loss.** "Async replication to the second region" means an outage loses the last N minutes — if that's unacceptable, you need sync, and sync costs latency on every write.
2. **RPO is not "backup schedule".** Snapshots protect *damage* (ransomware, bad deploy) — a restore from t-minus-3-hours is a loss of 3 hours. Different tools.
3. **The plan is a lie until it's tested.** An untested failover runbook is a screenplay. Draft and tabletop it, then execute real drills (kill a region in staging first).
4. **Backups belong off-path of the attackers.** Immutable/air-gapped, versioned, tested-for-restore — because the common destroy path is *inside* the compromised account.
5. **Return is the second half.** Failover without a tested *failback* plan keeps you "temporarily in the new region" for years.

## Tester checklist

- Run a restore test: pick a random week-old backup and restore it into a scratch environment — it must actually work, not just "look complete."
- Chaos drill: in staging, cut the primary region and run the runbook with a timer — stopwatch your RTO, measure the real RPO.
- Verify backup immutability: a compromised admin account cannot delete snapshots/backup copies.
- Practice failback: return traffic to the primary after recovery, within the same expectations as the forward failover.

## Related systems

- [Cloud computing fundamentals](../cloud-computing-fundamentals) — regions, AZs, and the RPO/RTO concepts this builds on
- [Cloud landing zones](../cloud-landing-zones) — the foundation DR plans attach to
- [CI/CD pipelines](../ci-cd-pipelines) — deployments and rollbacks operate on the same RPO/RTO clock
- [Monitoring and observability](../monitoring-and-observability) — the telemetry that tells you failover happened5a:Tf5c,
## What this system is

DNS is the phone book that turns `api.example.com` into an IP so TCP/IP can route your bytes. Load balancing is the traffic cop in front of many servers that answers "which healthy server handles this request?" They work together: DNS points to one or more LB VIPs, the LB spreads load across a pool.

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant B as Browser
  participant R as "Recursive resolver"
  participant A as "Authoritative DNS - example.com zone"
  participant LB as "Load balancer VIP"
  participant P as "Healthy server pool"

  B->>R: A? api.example.com
  R->>A: root -> .com -> zone
  A-->>R: A 203.0.113.10 TTL
  R-->>B: answer
  B->>LB: connect VIP
  LB->>P: L7 health check
```

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  Browser[Browser] -->|query api.example.com| R[Recursive resolver]
  R -->|root → .com → example.com NS| A[Authoritative nameservers]
  R -->|answer: 203.0.113.10 + TTL| Browser
  Browser -->|connect 203.0.113.10| LB[Load balancer VIPs]
  LB --> S1[App server 1 + health]
  LB --> S2[App server 2 + health]
  LB --> S3[App server 3 + health]
```

## DNS resolution, step by step

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant C as Client
  participant Res as Recursive resolver (ISP/local)
  participant Root as Root (. )
  participant Com as .com zone
  participant Auth as auth DNS for example.com

  C->>Res: A? api.example.com
  Res->>Root: A? api.example.com
  Root-->>Res: refer to .com server
  Res->>Com: A? api.example.com
  Com-->>Res: refer to example.com authoritative
  Res->>Auth: A? api.example.com
  Auth-->>Res: A 203.0.113.10, TTL 60
  Res->>C: answer + TTL 60
  Note over C,Res: every hop caches per TTL — the "propagation delay" is your TTL
```

Practical rules: **TTL** is your blast radius for changes. Lower it to 60 *before* a migration, not during. `CNAME` aliases follow — but a root domain can't be a CNAME (use a provider alias). Never let DNS *round-robin* multiple LB pools without validating: it's oblivious to health.

## L4 vs L7 load balancing

| | L4 (TCP/UDP-based) | L7 (application-aware) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Looks at | IP + port (e.g. tcp/443) | HTTP request — host, path, cookie, header |
| Routing | Per connection | Per request: `/v1` → pool A, `api.` → pool B |
| Features | Fast, protocol-agnostic | TLS termination, retries, rate limit, auth, WAF |
| Health probe | TCP connect / SYN | HTTP status checks, full transaction probes |
| Unit of failover | Connection | Request |

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  In[Internet] --> LB7[L7 LB<br/>TLS term + routing]
  LB7 --> P1[Pool /v1 · API servers]
  LB7 --> P2[Pool /v2 · canary]
  LB7 --> P3[Static · CDN]
```

## What health checks really guarantee

A LB is only as smart as its health probe. An *up* service that is actually stuck (thread pool exhausted, DB down) still passes a TCP probe — probe **depth** must match risk: L7 HTTP 200 with a timeout, spread on slow pools, and a **drain** before shutdown so in-flight requests finish (gracefully transition the node to unhealthy without killing live connections).

## Tester checklist

- Change a record with a short TTL and confirm caches expire on schedule (`dig @resolver name +tcd` honestly, all resolvers are their own universe).
- Force a node unhealthy and confirm the LB removes it and re-routes new requests (and drains in-flight on the real thing).
- Check TLS termination is where you think: LB-terminated means the LB sees plaintext and must be inside your trust boundary.
- Verify DNS failover paths for your VIPs themselves (multiple A/AAAA, or DNS-based Geo-DNS) — DNS is a dependency, not a given.

## Related systems

- [TCP/IP and TLS](../tcp-ip-and-tls) — the transport beneath an LB connection
- [API request lifecycle](../api-request-lifecycle) — how a request travels after the LB
- [API gateway caching](../api-gateway-caching) — L7 logic layering (gateways sit in front of LBs or in place of them)5b:T197c,
## What this system is

**Dockerizing** an application is packaging it — code, runtime, libraries, config — into a single **image**, then running that image as a **container** with a stable, repeatable contract: *the same image, the same behavior, everywhere it runs.* The container is a process, not a VM; it shares the host kernel but gets its own filesystem, network, and PID space.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  S[Source code] --> D[Dockerfile]
  D --> B[Build - layered image]
  B --> R[Registry - push + tag]
  R --> H[Host - pull + run]
  H --> C[Container - isolated process]
```

The promise is *reproducibility*, and it comes with discipline: a Dockerfile that only works on one engineer's machine has failed at the first step.

## From Dockerfile to image

A **Dockerfile** is a recipe: each instruction produces a **layer**, layers are cached, and the final image is a stack of layers. Build order is the whole game — put expensive, rarely-changing steps *early* so later edits only rebuild what changed.

```dockerfile
FROM node:20-alpine AS builder
WORKDIR /app
COPY package.json package-lock.json .
RUN npm ci
COPY . .
RUN npm run build

FROM node:20-alpine
WORKDIR /app
COPY --from=builder /app/node_modules ./node_modules
COPY --from=builder /app/.next ./.next
COPY --from=builder /app/package.json .
EXPOSE 3000
USER node
CMD ["npm", "start"]
```

The **multi-stage** pattern above is the architectural heart of dockerizing: the first stage carries the build toolchain, the final stage ships only the runtime. Dependencies install once in `builder`; the runtime stage stays small, and small images are fast to pull and surface fewer attack targets.

## The build context and the cache

- **Build context** — the set of files the build can COPY. Keep it minimal (`.dockerignore` for `node_modules`, `.git`, caches); a context sent to a remote builder drags every byte across the network.
- **Layer cache** — unchanged layers are reused. Order `COPY package*.json` *before* `COPY .` so dependency installs don't re-run on every code edit.
- **Tag discipline** — `latest` is a moving target that lies about what you're running. Tag with content or commit-derived IDs (`app:abc123`), and make the running stack reference a pinned tag you can interrogate.

## What happens when `docker run` fires

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Image[Image from registry] -->|bridge network| Network[Network - get an IP + ports]
  Image -->|mount layers| FS[Filesystem - layer stack + volume]
  Network -->|ports mapped| Start[Start processes from cmd]
  FS -->|ready| Start
  Start -->|readiness probe| Health[Healthcheck + logging]
  Health -->|signal + drain| Stop[Graceful stop + cleanup]
```

Three things matter about runtime behavior:

1. **Networking** — containers get their own network namespace. `EXPOSE` documents a port; `-p host:container` actually maps it. Local traffic can be slow to reveal shared-network differences, so test with host→container→container paths.
2. **Storage** — the writable layer vanishes when the container dies. Sticky data (databases, uploads) lives in **volumes**; **bind mounts** are for development. A stateless app with no volumes is the ideal: replace boxes, not corpses.
3. **Signals and PID 1** — the container manager sends SIGTERM and expects the main process to exit cleanly. A classic bug: the main process isn't PID 1, signals hit a shell, and "graceful shutdown" becomes a hard kill. Run your app as the actual entrypoint process.

## Compose: the multi-service stack

**Docker Compose** is the developer-facing orchestrator: one YAML declares *services, networks, volumes, environment* for a whole stack (app + database + cache + queue). It is not production Kubernetes — it is the fastest honest model of how your services actually talk.

```yaml
services:
  web:
    build: .
    ports: ["3000:3000"]
    environment:
      DATABASE_URL: postgres://app:app@db:5432/app
    depends_on:
      db:
        condition: service_healthy
  db:
    image: postgres:16
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD-SHELL", "pg_isready -U app"]
      interval: "5s"
```

Sections here are load-bearing: `depends_on` with a **healthcheck condition** replaces blind sleep-fast-forward guessing, and putting `DATABASE_URL` in compose makes the environment explicit (and points clearly at where the production version lives — that is, in secrets, not in this file).

## The security knife-edge

- **Run as a non-root user** — the official images default to root; the `USER node` line in the Dockerfile is not decoration.
- **Slim base images** — Alpine-style bases shrink attack surface and pull time; the trade-off is musl vs glibc quirks for native dependencies. Verify, don't assume.
- **Pin base images** — `FROM node:20-alpine` floats. Pin a digest or an exact tag to make builds reproducible and upstream changes explicit.
- **Never bake secrets** — environment secrets are inputs to the container at run time, injected via [secrets management](../secrets-certificates-and-pki), never `ENV`-hardcoded or COPY'd into an image layer. Once a secret is in a layer, it is in history forever.
- **Scan the image** — add container scanning to the pipeline ([CI/CD](../ci-cd-pipelines)) so known CVEs in the base image block the build rather than surfacing in production.

## Tester checklist

- Build twice, change one source file, rebuild: the expensive layer should not re-run (cache hit) and the final image must be byte-same for a given tag.
- Run the container with no host port and confirm inter-container traffic uses the Compose service name, not `localhost`.
- Kill the runtime signal gracefully: send SIGTERM, confirm the app drains requests and the container exits 0 (no force-kill).
- Prove statelessness: `docker rm` + re-create from the image, verify behavior is identical and data survived only in the declared volume.
- Scan the final image for the base-image CVE list before promoting it to production.

## Related systems

- [CI/CD pipelines](../ci-cd-pipelines) — the pipeline that builds, scans, and promotes images to the registry
- [Linux servers in the enterprise](../linux-servers-in-the-enterprise) — the host OS, kernel features, and distro choices containers run on
- [Secrets, certificates and PKI](../secrets-certificates-and-pki) — where runtime secrets and TLS for containers come from
- [Cloud computing fundamentals](../cloud-computing-fundamentals) — pushing the same image into serverless, managed ECS/EKS, or AKS dependencies5c:T250c,
## What this system is

Endpoint security is the set of layers that protects **every device that touches your data**: laptops, desktops, phones, tablets, and the servers that are also endpoints. It is not one product — it is a stack: enrollment and identity, OS hardening, antivirus/EDR, patch and update rings, full-disk encryption, policy and compliance, and the management plane that pushes all of it. In the [zero trust](../zero-trust-architecture) world, the endpoint is the new perimeter: **a device is not trusted because it's "inside" — it's trusted because it can prove identity, posture, and compliance on every request.**

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  subgraph Endpoint["The endpoint - any device"]
    ID[Identity -<br/>enrolled user + device cert]
    OS[OS hardening -<br/>config baseline]
    AV[EDR / AV -<br/>detect + respond]
    CRYPTO[Encryption -<br/>BitLocker / FileVault / full disk]
    PATCH[Patch -<br/>update rings + compliance]
  end
  ID --> Policy{Posture check}
  OS --> Policy
  AV --> Policy
  CRYPTO --> Policy
  PATCH --> Policy
  Policy -->|compliant?| Access[Access to apps + data]
  Policy -->|not compliant| Block[Quarantine / limited access]
```

## The endpoint security stack

| Layer | What it does | The archetypal products |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Enrollment & identity** | Onboard the device, bind it to a user, issue a device identity | Microsoft Entra join, Intune, Jamf, Workspace ONE |
| **OS hardening** | A compliance baseline: settings, firewall, restrictions, no admin | Security baselines (Windows/Linux/macOS), CIS Benchmarks |
| **EDR / AV** | Detect and respond to malware and suspicious behavior | Defender for Endpoint, CrowdStrike, SentinelOne |
| **Encryption** | Full-disk and removable-media encryption, tamper protection | BitLocker, FileVault, LUKS |
| **Patch & update rings** | Controlled rollout of OS/app/security updates | Windows Autopatch, patch rings, WSUS/MECM |
| **Policy & compliance** | What a device must prove before accessing anything | Conditional Access, compliance policies, posture checks |
| **Management plane** | The single pane that enrolls, configures, and reports | Intune, MECM, Jamf, MDM + EMM |

The mental model: **the management plane defines the truth, and every endpoint continuously proves it holds.** A device that falls out of compliance (missing patches, AV disabled, disk unencrypted) loses access until it fixes itself — that loop is the whole architecture.

## The request path: endpoint → app

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant E as Endpoint (managed device)
  participant M as Management plane (Intune / MDM)
  participant P as Policy engine (Conditional Access)
  participant A as App / data

  E->>M: enroll - register device + user identity
  M-->>E: push config baseline + EDR + encryption policy
  E->>P: request access - present device cert + posture proof
  P->>P: check compliance (patch, AV, encryption, policy)
  alt compliant
    P-->>E: grant access
    E->>A: work - EDR watches behavior
  else not compliant
    P-->>E: block / limited access + remediation
  end
```

The key architectural fact: **the endpoint request is not trusted until the policy engine verifies posture.** The device doesn't "get on the network"; it gets access *conditionally*, per app, per request — which is why endpoint security and [identity & access management](../identity-and-access-management) are the same conversation.

## The layers that carry most of the value

- **Enrollment is the front door.** An unmanaged device is an untrusted device. Enrollment binds hardware to identity (device cert / attestation), so later posture checks have something to measure against. The failure is shadow-IT: devices using corporate data that were never enrolled, so every other layer has no hook.
- **EDR is detection + response, not just antivirus.** Classic AV matches known signatures; EDR adds behavioral detection on the endpoint, collects telemetry, and lets the [SOC](../security-operations-center-soc) respond remotely (isolate a host, kill a process, hunt laterally). The architecture question is coverage: an EDR only protects devices that run it and report — gaps in enrollment or agent health are the real holes.
- **Full-disk encryption is the lost-laptop layer.** BitLocker/FileVault mean a stolen device is a brick, not a data breach. It fails silently when keys are escrowed in a place the attacker can also reach, or when the drive isn't actually encrypted (a fleet of "BitLocker enabled" devices with TPM keys surrendered on request).
- **Patch rings are a risk calculation.** Patching is the #1 hygiene layer (most breaches are known vulnerabilities), but rolling updates everywhere at once is how you break the fleet. Rings: canary → broad → all, with health gates between, so a bad patch never lands everywhere.
- **Policy/compliance is the enforcement arm.** Conditional Access / compliance policies decide access based on posture in real time. This is what makes "device not compliant → no data" real instead of aspirational.

## Hardening: the baseline matters more than any single tool

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  B[CIS / vendor security baseline] --> D[Apply via management plane]
  D --> C[Continuous compliance check]
  C -->|pass| OK[Trusted device]
  C -->|fail| REM[Remediate automatically]
  REM --> C
```

A hardening baseline (CIS Benchmarks, vendor baselines) is the *configuration contract* every other layer assumes: local admin removed, firewall on, autoruns restricted, telemetry enabled. It's applied by the management plane and continuously checked — drift is found and fixed, not assumed. The honest priority: **no admin rights, prompt patching, and disk encryption prevent more incidents than an exotic EDR config.**

## Managing the fleet without breaking it

| Practice | Why | The failure mode it prevents |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Patch rings + health gates** | A bad update is caught in canary before fleet | "The update broke everyone" |
| **Gradual policy rollout** | Policy bugs surface on a subset | "The policy locked out half the org" |
| **Self-service remediation** | Users fix posture without a ticket | Devices stuck non-compliant for weeks |
| **Agent/EDR health monitoring** | Know when a device loses coverage | "We thought it was protected" |
| **Retire/remove old OS versions** | Unsupported OS = unpatchable holes | Ancient Windows/Android in the estate |
| **Separation of duties** | Security team ≠ device admin permissions | One credential pwns the whole fleet |

## The failure modes an architect owns

- **Enrollment gaps**: the estate's devices were never all enrolled; every layer silently excludes them, so coverage is a fiction printed on the dashboard.
- **Posture theater**: compliance checks that only confirm *something* was installed, not that it's *running and current* (AV "enabled" but definitions three months old).
- **Local admin everywhere**: users (or malware via users) run with admin rights, defeating the hardening baseline.
- **Encryption with escrowed keys**: keys recoverable by the same actor who stole the laptop.
- **Patch lag on the crown jewels**: servers and critical machines patched last "because stability," while attackers target exactly them.
- **Agent health blindness**: no telemetry that the EDR/agent is reporting, so the SOC finds out a host went dark only during an incident.

## Tester checklist

- **Enrollment coverage**: query the management plane — every active corporate device must be enrolled, enrolled with a real user, and none show as unknown/unmanaged.
- **Posture gate**: disable AV on a test device and confirm access to data is actually blocked (or degraded) by the policy engine — not just logged.
- **Encryption reality**: sample devices and confirm full-disk encryption is genuinely on, keys are escrowed safely, and a lost-drive test yields no readable data.
- **Patch ring**: push a deliberate test patch to canary, verify it clears the health gate, then confirm broad rollout; verify an unhealthy canary *halts* promotion.
- **Remediation loop**: make a device non-compliant and confirm the user sees a remediation prompt and access is restored only after it fixes itself.
- **EDR response**: run a benign detection (test alert), confirm the [SOC](../security-operations-center-soc) sees the telemetry and can isolate the host remotely.
- **Local admin audit**: confirm the admin-rights baseline is enforced fleet-wide and exceptions are named and reviewed.

## Related systems

- [Zero trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — the model where every endpoint must prove posture to get access
- [Identity and access management](../identity-and-access-management) — the user identity the device binds to
- [Security operations center (SOC)](../security-operations-center-soc) — where EDR telemetry and response actually land
- [Security standards landscape](../security-standards-landscape) — the frameworks (CIS, NIST) the baselines come from
- [Virtualization and hypervisors](../virtualization-and-hypervisors) — where endpoint security meets the host layer
- [Windows in the enterprise](../windows-in-the-enterprise) and [Linux servers in the enterprise](../linux-servers-in-the-enterprise) — fleet-management cousins for specific OSes
- [Android in the enterprise](../android-in-the-enterprise) and [iOS/macOS in the enterprise](../ios-macos-in-the-enterprise) — mobile endpoints in the same posture loop5d:T15ba,
## What this system is

**Record-to-report (R2R)** is the ERP chain that turns business events into financial statements. Every operational post — a sale, a purchase, a shipment, a payment, a return — eventually becomes a **journal entry** against the **general ledger (GL)**. Finance modules live downstream of everything: [order-to-cash](../enterprise-resource-planning) feeds revenue, source-to-pay feeds liabilities, and inventory movements feed COGS (cost of goods sold).

The GL is deliberately simple: accounts, debits and credits, a period (month), and a ledger. The sophistication is in what *writes* to it. When an architect says "ERP finance", they mostly mean the plumbing that keeps every upstream module's postings balanced and reconcilable.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    OT[Order-to-cash<br/>revenue + AR] --> GL[General ledger]
    SP[Source-to-pay<br/>liabilities + AP] --> GL
    PM[Plan-to-produce<br/>inventory + COGS] --> GL
    HR[Hire-to-retire<br/>payroll + accruals] --> GL
    GL --> P[Period close<br/>adjustments + consolidation]
    P --> R[Financial reports<br/>P&L, balance sheet, cash flow]
```

## The double-entry discipline

Every posting is a **balanced** set of debits and credits, grouped by account, department, project, and profit center. A single goods receipt touches several dims, so the finance module's job is to keep the *slices* (accounting view) consistent with the *totals* (the ledger view). Where this breaks is the eternal arguments about **valuation**:

| Method | What it costs when | Surprise |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Standard costing | A planned unit cost; differences record as variances | Variances pile up when prices drift |
| Moving average | Every receipt re-averages the unit cost | Fine until an audit wants exact layers |
| FIFO / LIFO | Costs attach to specific receipt layers | Order of layers becomes the tax shape |

The choice matters to the GL but is mostly invisible to operations — except at **period close**, when the variance accounts must be cleared and explained.

## A whole chain landing in one entry

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Event["Business event<br/>ship goods / pay invoice"] -->|debit side| Dr["Dr: COGS / AP<br/>Dr: asset or expense"]
  Event -->|credit side| Cr["Cr: inventory / AR<br/>Cr: revenue / liability"]
  Dr -->|sum to zero| GL["GL posting<br/>balancing, dated, referenced"]
  Cr -->|sum to zero| GL
  GL -->|post| Book["Ledger + subledgers<br/>AR, AP, assets"]
  Book -->|close + report| Rep["Period close + reports"]
```

## Period close, the monthly ritual

Close has four jobs, in this order — and each one either passes or blocks the month:

1. **Reconcile subledgers to GL** — AR and AP balances match the top-level accounts; mismatches halt the close.
2. **Run valuations and adjustments** — inventory valuation, accruals, depreciation, currency revaluation.
3. **Freeze and post** — lock prior periods; post only to the open period.
4. **Report and consolidate** — P&L, balance sheet, cash flow, for each entity and the group.

The metric that matters is **time-to-close**: a modern ERP posts continuously so the close is confirmation, not catch-up. If your close still needs a week of spreadsheets, the subledgers and the GL have silently diverged all month.

## The reconciliation truth

The GL is the last word for reporting, and it will only ever be as accurate as the feeds that reach it. The classic failure is the **settlement loop**: the payment gateway clears money in batches, the [e-commerce platform](../ecommerce-platform-architecture) records orders, and the ERP posts revenue — and the three never tie out line-by-line. The finance architecture that survives an audit automates three-way matching on *every* posting, not just the big ones.

## Tester checklist

- Balancing: introduce a malformed feed (an unbalanced or missing dimension posting) and confirm it is rejected, never split silently into the GL.
- Subledger to GL: run the reconciliation for a month with known AP/AR transactions — zero unexplained difference.
- Valuation switch: recalculate COGS under standard and moving-average for the same inventory, and confirm the GL variance accounts carry the difference explainably.
- Close order: attempt to post to a closed period and confirm it is blocked; post an adjustment to the open period and confirm the prior period's reports are unchanged.
- Feed integrity: guarantee even delivery of every source-systems feed (retry, dedupe, idempotency) so a re-sent posting cannot double-count.
- Report drill-down: every report figure bisects to journal entries, source documents, and original events.

- [Procurement (source-to-pay)](../procurement-source-to-pay) — the AP side of the ledger
- [Data warehouse and lakehouse architecture](../data-warehouse-lakehouse-architecture) — analytics on top of the ledger

- [Integration patterns](../integration-patterns) — the reliable feeds into the finance modules
