- [Data governance and catalog](../data-governance-and-catalog) — the chart of accounts as governed reference data5e:T11a1,
## What this system is

An ERP is not a single app — it is a **suite of modules** (finance, procurement, manufacturing, inventory, HR) that share **one database and one master-data model**. The value proposition is brutally simple: one record of truth for the whole company, so the sales order the front office sees is the same sales order finance invoices and the warehouse ships. The price is equally simple: you configure within a package's boundaries instead of building your own.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  MO[Master data - customers, vendors, materials, GL accounts] --> F[Finance]
  MO --> S[Supply chain]
  MO --> M[Manufacturing]
  MO --> P[Procurement]
  MO --> H[Human resources]
```

## The process chains that define ERP

Every module exists to serve one of five end-to-end chains. When someone says "ERP", they mean these five:

| Chain | Starts with | Ends with | Touches |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Order-to-cash** | Customer order | Cash in the bank | Sales, inventory, logistics, AR, GL |
| **Source-to-pay** | Purchase requisition | Supplier paid | Procurement, receiving, AP, GL |
| **Plan-to-produce** | Demand forecast | Finished goods in stock | Supply planning, manufacturing, inventory |
| **Record-to-report** | Business event | Financial statements | All modules, GL, reporting |
| **Hire-to-retire** | Job requisition | Ex-employee offboarded | HR, payroll, identity |

## Order-to-cash, the canonical chain

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  SO[Sales order] --> AC[Availability check]
  AC --> DL[Delivery / shipping]
  DL --> IN[Invoice to customer]
  IN --> AR[Accounts receivable]
  AR --> GL[General ledger posting]
```

Each arrow is a **state change on shared documents** (order status, delivery status, invoice status). An ERP forces those states to agree; a mess of point-to-point systems lets each step drift. This is why the ERP wins on process integrity and loses on agility — changing the chain means changing the whole suite.

## Config vs code

| Decision | In an ERP | In custom code |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Business rules | Configuration tables, workflows | If/else in a service |
| Changing a rule | Config change, retest the impact | Code change, redeploy |
| The famous trap | Config drift nobody can explain | Customizations that block upgrades |
| Best posture | Tune in-app config, keep custom code minimal and isolated | Only for true differentiators |

The upgrade problem is where architectures are decided: **every customization becomes an upgrade tax**. The architects' rule is *configure, then extend via APIs — don't modify the package*.

## The happy path, step by step

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  SO["Sales order"] -->|validate + ATP| ATP["ATP check"]
  ATP -->|fulfill| Del["Delivery"]
  Del -->|invoice| Inv["Invoice + GL"]
  Inv -->|settle AR| Cash["Payment received"]
```

## Tester checklist

- Chain integrity: create an order, fulfill, invoice, and pay — every downstream document must pick up the upstream state automatically (no manual re-keying).
- Master-data consistency: an address change on the customer master must propagate everywhere it is referenced, without duplicates.
- Kit/multi-location: a single order split across warehouses must reconcile to one invoice and one GL posting.
- Upgrade exposure: confirm no custom code bypasses standard validation posts (fields the suite expects).

## Related systems

- [Procurement (source-to-pay)](../procurement-source-to-pay) — the buying half of the same suite
- [Warehouse management systems](../warehouse-management-systems) — where ERP inventory becomes physical movement
- [Supply chain management](../supply-chain-management) — the planning layer above the ERP execution core
- [HR systems](../hr-and-talent-systems) — the people module and its downstream feeds
- [CRM systems](../crm-systems) — the front office that hands off to order-to-cash
- [Housing management systems](../housing-management-systems) — asset-side property management outside the suite
- [Tenancy management systems](../tenancy-management-systems) — the rental cash stream that posts to finance
- [Integration patterns](../integration-patterns) — how the suite talks to everything outside it
- [Data warehouse and lakehouse architecture](../data-warehouse-lakehouse-architecture) — where record-to-report data goes to be analyzed
- [Business process modeling and management](../business-process-modeling-and-management) — the order-to-cash and procure-to-pay processes this suite executes5f:Ted0,
## What this system is

Integrating with files is the least glamorous but most durable pattern in architecture: banks, ERPs, and legacy systems trade data as files. This page compares the traditional **drop folder** and the modern **object store + event** designs.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  PTNR[External partner] -->|upload file| BUCK[Object storage<br/>incoming prefix]
  BUCK -->|ObjectCreated| Q[Queue: file-ready event]
  Q -->|consume| IMP[Importer: verify checksum]
  IMP -->|archive + record| RC[Move to processed/ prefix]
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph Classic["Classic SFTP"]
    A1[External partner] -->|upload file| B1[SFTP drop folder]
    B1 --> C1[Poll for new files]
    C1 --> D1[Parse + import]
  end

  subgraph Modern["Object storage + events"]
    A2[Producer] -->|upload to bucket prefix| B2[(Object storage)]
    B2 -->|event notification| C2[Queue / function]
    C2 --> D2[Parse + import]
  end
```

## The options, compared

| | SFTP / FTP | Shared network drive | Object storage (S3, GCS) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Push or pull | Partner pushes | Both | Push + event fell; pull allowed |
| Preset | Server, keys | VPN + mount | Managed, REST API, fine-grained IAM |
| Trigger | Poll the folder | Poll | Native event notifications (S3→SNS/SQS, GCS→Pub/Sub) |
| Scale | Moderate | Small | Massive, versioned, cheap |
| Big files | Fine | Fine | Multi-part uploads, range reads |

## The event-driven modern flow

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant P as Bank / partner
  participant B as Bucket
  participant Q as Queue
  participant W as Importer

  P->>B: upload statement-2026-08.csv (public? no - presigned / IAM)
  B-->>Q: event: ObjectCreated
  W->>Q: consume file-ready event
  W->>B: download object (checksum verify)
  W->>W: parse + import rows
  W->>B: write receipt + move to processed/ prefix
```

## The rules that keep file integrations honest

1. **Name files with immutable IDs + version/date** — nobody should overwrite a file that is mid-import.
2. **Ship checksums** (`sha256sum` file, or object checksum) and verify after download.
3. **Encrypt at rest, and ideally PGP-encrypt the file payload** for regulated data — storage encryption should not be the only barrier.
4. **Treat the folder as a claim-check (outbox)** — the file is the event; the event that *says* a file exists is a hint, not the source of truth. Always confirm the object exists before importing (at-least-once again).
5. **Move, don't delete** — archive to a `processed/` prefix so drift is auditable.

## The classic failure: "I don't see my file"

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  A[File uploaded to wrong prefix] --> X1[No event fired]
  B[File name collides - overwrite] --> X2[Partial import of mixed data]
  C[Polling poll misses during outage] --> X3[Gap never caught up]
  D[Tar file, not csv] --> X4[Import crashes, no retry]
  E[Checksum mismatch undetected] --> X5[Silent data corruption]
```

## Tester checklist

- Drop a corrupt/oversized/unexpected-format file and confirm the pipeline fails loudly into a DLQ, not silently.
- Replay by re-uploading the same name — must not import twice (dedupe by content hash).
- Test the event gap: delete the event but leave the object — the importer must still find the file (no data lose).
- Verify permissions end-to-end: the importer can only read its bucket/prefix, partners cannot.

## Related systems

- [Message queue delivery](../message-queue-delivery) — the queue the modern flow sits on
- [Change data capture](../change-data-capture) — streaming rows instead of moving files
- [Pub/Sub and event streaming](../pub-sub-event-streaming) — event notifications at scale
- [ERP systems](../enterprise-resource-planning) — the classic file-trading partner (bank statements, POs)60:Tdb4,
## What this system is

A "website framework" is really a bundle of two decisions: **which UI library** (React, Vue, Svelte, Angular) and **where the HTML is produced** (client, server, at build time). The second decision dominates your metrics — first byte, searchability, interactivity — so it comes first.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  U[User request] --> D{Where is HTML produced?}
  D -->|browser only| CSR[CSR / SPA - empty shell + JS]
  D -->|server per request| SSR[SSR - full HTML from server]
  D -->|at build time| SSG[SSG - prebuilt HTML files]
  D -->|hybrid| ISR[ISR / islands - mix on demand]
```

## The rendering models, compared

| | CSR / SPA | SSR | SSG | Islands |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| HTML arrives | Empty shell | Full page | Full page, eager | Full page with lazy areas |
| First paint | After JS loads | Fast (server) | Fast (static) | Fast, then hydrate lazily |
| SEO / no-JS | Weak | Good | Best | Good |
| Dynamic data | Best (fetch on client) | Server fetch per request | Needs rebuild/revalidation | Hybrid |
| Framework fit | Anything, SPA-style | Next/Nuxt/SvelteKit | Static sites, docs, blogs | Astro, islands architecture |

The trap is memorizing "SSR = slow": the real question is **time-to-interactive**. A CSR SPA can *paint* fast but needs a JS round-trip to *work*; SSR trades server work for control and renders text for crawlers.

## The framework map

| | React | Vue | Svelte | Angular |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Paradigm | Components + hooks | Components + reactive refs | Compile-to-JS, no runtime diffing | Opinionated framework (DI, RxJS) |
| Ecosystem | Largest, hardest to choose | Balanced, batteries | Growing, fast and light | Batteries-included, strict |
| Learning curve | Steep (meta-frameworks) | Gentle | Gentle | Steep (concepts first) |
| Best for | Teams hiring React devs today | Quick, maintainable SPAs | Small bundles, animated UIs | Enterprise teams wanting structure |

In practice the library barely decides the page-speed story — the **meta-framework** does (Next.js/Nuxt/SvelteKit/Astro). Choose the framework for your team's skills; choose the rendering model for your traffic.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  B[Browser request] -->|first paint, SSR/SSG| HTML[Server / build<br/>HTML]
  B -->|load shell, SPA| Bundle[JS bundle from CDN]
  HTML -->|reuse DOM| H[Hydration<br/>attach handlers]
  Bundle -->|until JS parses| H
  H -->|time-to-interactive| Ready[Interactive page]
```

## Tester checklist

- Measure time-to-first-byte vs time-to-interactive separately — the SPA-vs-SSR argument is decided by both, not one.
- Verify SSR pages render with JS disabled in the crawler profile (SEO) and compare to the SPA story.
- Check hydration failures: a mismatched client/server render throws console errors — the suite's runtime-health check catches them.
- Prove a metrics claim: run Lighthouse/Web Vitals on the same page under CSR, SSR, and SSG builds.

## Related systems

- [Full-stack frameworks](../fullstack-frameworks) — the meta-frameworks that glue these choices together
- [Server-side rendering internals](../server-side-rendering-internals) — what actually happens inside the SSR pipeline
- [API request lifecycle](../api-request-lifecycle) — what the client asks of the server
- [Session cookies and CSRF](../session-cookies-and-csrf) — the state your hydration and routes depend on
- [Mobile application frameworks](../mobile-application-frameworks) — the same skills, different canvas61:Te44,
## What this system is

A full-stack framework (Next.js/Nuxt/SvelteKit/Remix) closes the loop between the two halves of a web app: **one codebase** where the server renders the page (fetching its own data) and the client hydrates it — so you never write a separate REST layer, fetch-from-the-client bootstrap, and a duplicated loading state by hand. The alternative it competes against is **SPA + separate API**.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Req[Request] --> Server[Server component / loader]
  Server --> D[(Database / API)]
  D --> Server
  Server --> HTML[Server-rendered HTML with data]
  HTML --> Client[Client hydration]
  Client --> Action[Server actions - mutations]
  Action --> Server
```

## The mental model that replaces REST glue

| | SPA + separate API | Full-stack framework |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Data for the page | Client fetches after mount (loading spinners) | Server fetches during render (no empty shell) |
| Duplicated logic | Fetch/state code on both sides | Server renders, client hydrates, same framework |
| Ops | Independent deploy of API and SPA | One app; page speed tightly coupled |
| Mutations | POST to an endpoint, refetch/optimistic update | Server actions get called, forms revalidate |
| Type sharing | Manual DTOs/Typescript types | Framework pulls types from the same data layer |

That last row is the real trade — a full-stack framework **couples** your frontend release to its data layer. Useful, but you've lost the "API as a product" boundary that a split team (or a public API) needs.

## Server components vs client components

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  subgraph Server["Server component - runs on server"]
    A[SQL/data access]
    B[Secrets, or consumers don't see]
    C[Render text, layout, static parts]
  end
  subgraph Client["Client component - 'use client'"]
    D[Event handlers, state]
    E[browser APIs, effects]
    F[Interactive widgets - hydrated]
  end
  Server --> Client
  Client --> Browser[Browser]
```

**Server components** fetch and render on the server (data stays off the wire, secrets stay server-side). **Client components** opt-in interactivity. The discipline is default-to-server, escape-hatch-to-client — refuse that split and you rebuild the SPA accidental complexity you were escaping.

## The request → happy path

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Req[Request] -->|load + fetch| SC[Server component<br/>fetch data]
  SC -->|SSR render| HTML[Rendered HTML + RSC payload]
  HTML -->|reuse DOM| H[Client hydration]
  H -->|time-to-interactive| Ready[Interactive page]
```

## Tester checklist

- Verify the page renders server-side without JS (crawler/no-JS profile) — server components must not require client fetch to paint.
- Confirm server-only code (env secrets, DB) never leaks into the client bundle — grep the shipped JS for a canary secret.
- Check data mutation: a form submission via server action changes server state and the page reflects it after a route refresh.
- Watch cold-start latency: server work per request shifts caching and load-balancers (SSR cost vs static pre-render).

## Related systems

- [Frontend frameworks and rendering](../frontend-frameworks-and-rendering) — the rendering models behind the scene
- [Server-side rendering internals](../server-side-rendering-internals) — routing, render, stream, and hydration in detail
- [Backend web frameworks](../backend-web-frameworks) — what the server half implicitly replaces
- [API request lifecycle](../api-request-lifecycle) — the request path when you do split the API out
- [Session cookies and CSRF](../session-cookies-and-csrf) — server-side state for your server actions62:Td75,
## What this system is

Git is the shared memory of your codebase: every change is a **commit** (snapshot + message + author), changes happen on **branches**, and they come together through **merges or pull requests**. Done well, history is a legible story with a release at each chapter. Done badly, it's a tangle of force-pushes and "fixed" commits.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Branch[feature/checkout-ui] -->|push| PR[Pull request]
  PR -->|required checks| Review[CI + review - protected branch]
  Review -->|squash merge| Main[main - always deployable]
  Main -->|release| Tag[Release tag - v1.4.0]
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  F1[feature/checkout-ui] -->|PR + review| M[main]
  F2[bugfix/118-timeout] -->|PR + review| M
  M -->|tag v1.4.0| R[(Release branch / tag)]
  R -->|hotfix from tag| M
```

## The development flow

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  A[Issue in tracker] --> B[Branch from main]
  B --> C[Work in small commits]
  C --> D[Open pull request]
  D --> E{CI + review}
  E -->|green + approved| F[Squash-merge to main]
  E -->|fail| C
  F --> G[Deploy via pipeline]
  G --> H[Monitor + feedback]
```

**Protect main**: no direct pushes, required reviews, required CI. `main` should always be *deployable* — anything else turns releases into archaeology.

## Merge vs rebase vs squash

| Operation | Shape of history | When to use |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Merge** | Adds a merge commit, preserves branch history | Long-lived branches, preserving context |
| **Rebase** | Replays your commits on top, linear history | Cleaning a local branch before PR; avoid on shared/pub branches |
| **Squash merge** | One commit summarizes the whole branch | Most PRs — clean main, single blame point |

Rule that prevents grief: **never rebase a shared branch** (rewrites history others built on; force-push tears down clones and CI). Squash-merge on main, rebase your own local work.

## Things that actually go wrong

- **Force-push accidents**: only allow force-push on your own unpushed work; protect-main blocks it everywhere else.
- **Revert ≠ undo**: `git revert` creates a new commit and is safe on shared history; `git reset`/`reset --hard` rewrites history and is the foot-gun.
- **Large/secret files in history**: deleting a file now does not remove it from history — `.gitignore`, BFG/filter-repo, secret scanning, and rotate secrets.
- **Merge conflicts are feedback**: they say two changes overlapped; the fix is domain understanding, not `git checkout --theirs`.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  subgraph Good["Commit messages that behave"]
    A[Short subject + why not just what]
    B[Reference issue: JIRA-118 fix timeout]
    C[One logical change per commit]
  end
```

## Tester checklist

- Rebase a branch onto a drifted main and confirm re-runs of CI pass before the PR (not after merge).
- Verify upstream `main` protection: a direct push must fail; only PRs with passing checks land.
- Try the dangerous path with a backup: `git reset --hard` gone wrong — can you still recover the commit? (reflog!)
- Check history hygiene: log scan for secrets and large blobs as part of the pipeline, not a reaction.

## Related systems

- [CI/CD pipelines](../ci-cd-pipelines) — what consumes the commits your branches produce
- [Jira and agile delivery](../jira-and-agile-delivery) — where the issues these branches reference live
- [DevOps delivery loop](../devops-delivery-loop) — the full ticket-to-production loop63:T265f,
## What this system is

Google Cloud (GCP) is a catalog of services; the art is knowing the ~15 that matter and how they compose. Its identity differs from the other clouds in three ways: **Kubernetes-first** (GKE was Google's own creation, so containers are native), **serverless-grade managed services** (Cloud Run, BigQuery, Spanner were built by the company that runs them at Google scale), and **data/ML as a first-class story** (BigQuery, Dataflow, Vertex AI are its strongest draw). The canonical reference deployment: a **VPC** with public/private subnets across regions, a load balancer up front, compute on GKE or Cloud Run in private subnets, managed databases, object storage, and everything gated by **IAM**.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  In[Internet] --> LB[Global load balancer<br/>regional backends]
  LB --> CR[Cloud Run - container<br/>scale to zero]
  LB --> GKE[GKE - Kubernetes<br/>cluster]
  CR --> SQL[(Cloud SQL<br/>regional HA)]
  GKE --> SQL
  CR --> GCS[(Cloud Storage - bucket)]
  GKE --> GCS
  Pub[Pub/Sub topic] --> CF[Cloud Functions -<br/>event handler]
  CF --> SQL
```

## The services that matter, in one map

| Category | The workhorses | When to reach for them |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Compute | **Compute Engine**, **GKE**, **Cloud Run**, Cloud Functions | VMs; Kubernetes (native); serverless containers; event functions |
| Storage | **Cloud Storage**, Persistent Disk, Filestore | Object storage hot+cold; volumes; shared fs |
| Network | **VPC**, Cloud Load Balancing, Cloud CDN, Cloud DNS | Private networks; global LB; edge caching; DNS |
| Database | **Cloud SQL**, **Spanner**, **BigQuery**, Firestore | Relational HA; planetary-scale relational; warehouse; NoSQL |
| Streaming | **Pub/Sub**, Dataflow | Async messages; event streams |
| Identity | **Cloud IAM**, Identity-Aware Proxy (IAP), Workforce pools | Access control; user identity |
| Orchestration | Terraform, Cloud Deployment Manager | Everything defined as code |

## The key architecture: serverless-first compute

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  Q[How to run this workload?] --> R{Shape?}
  R -->|container, autoscaled, scale-to-zero| RUN[Cloud Run -<br/>your default for services]
  R -->|event function| FNS[Cloud Functions -<br/>glue, small handlers]
  R -->|Kubernetes needed| GKE[GKE -<br/>native k8s, full control]
  R -->|stateful / very custom| VM[Compute Engine -<br/>VMs]
```

GCP's default posture is **Cloud Run first**: a container that scales to zero, autoscales, gives you a URL and canary versions, and bills only while serving. It's the "containers without Kubernetes" story. Reach for **GKE** when you need k8s-native features (stateful sets, node pools, specialized scheduling, GPU/TPU workloads), and **Cloud Functions** for event glue. Compute Engine is the escape hatch for anything that needs a plain VM. The decision, condensed: **container? Cloud Run. Needs k8s? GKE. Tiny event? Cloud Functions. Everything else? Compute Engine.**

## GKE: Kubernetes as a native service

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  subgraph Cluster["GKE cluster"]
    subgraph Control["Control plane (managed)"]
      API[API server -<br/>fully managed]
    end
    subgraph Nodes["Node pools"]
      N1[Autoscaled node pool]
      N2[GPU / TPU pool]
    end
  end
  API --> N1
  API --> N2
```

GKE is Google's own Kubernetes as a managed service: the control plane is Google-managed (you never patch the API server), and node pools autoscale. The k8s fundamentals — deployments, services, probes, scheduling, scaling — all apply ([Kubernetes and container orchestration](../kubernetes-and-container-orchestration) is the mechanics). The GCP-specific decisions: **which node pool** (standard VMs vs Spot vs GPU/TPU), **regional vs zonal cluster**, and **Autopilot vs Standard** (Autopilot = fully managed nodes and less tuning; Standard = you own node pools).

## The data crown jewels

| Service | What it is | When it wins |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Cloud SQL** | Managed MySQL/Postgres/SQL Server with HA + backups | Your default relational database, single-region needs |
| **Spanner** | Horizontally scalable relational with external consistency | Need *global* relational: multi-region writes, no leader election problem |
| **BigQuery** | Serverless columnar data warehouse | The [warehouse](../data-warehouse-lakehouse-architecture) for big analytics; no servers, pay per query |
| **Firestore** | Managed NoSQL document store, realtime sync | Mobile/web realtime apps, game state, IoT |
| **Cloud Storage** | Object storage with classes (hot/cold/archive) | The universal "files and artifacts" layer |

The pattern that marks a GCP estate: **BigQuery as the analytics center**. Because it's serverless and fast, it becomes the destination for events (via Pub/Sub → Dataflow or BigQuery Storage Write), replacing a self-hosted warehouse. The common trap is *choosing Spanner when Cloud SQL suffices* — Spanner's power costs real money and operational nuance, and single-region relational apps are usually fine on Cloud SQL.

## Pub/Sub and the async spine

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Prod[Producer - app / function / GKE] -->|publish| TOPIC[Pub/Sub topic]
  TOPIC -->|push / pull| SUB[Subscription]
  SUB --> C1[Cloud Run / Functions consumer]
  SUB --> C2[Dataflow / BigQuery sink]
```

Pub/Sub is Google's managed [pub/sub messaging](../pub-sub-event-streaming): topics, subscriptions with push/pull delivery, exactly-once-ish via message IDs plus at-least-once semantics, and ordering per ordering key. It's the spine that decouples GCP services (storage events → Cloud Functions, streaming data → Dataflow → BigQuery) and its serverless nature means consumers scale without you provisioning brokers.

## IAM and Identity-Aware Proxy

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  U[User / service account] --> Role[IAM role -<br/>what action on what resource]
  Role --> Res[Cloud Run service,<br/>bucket, BigQuery dataset]
  Ext[End-user - via Google / external identity] --> IAP[Identity-Aware Proxy -<br/>checks identity before reach]
  IAP --> App[Your app - no VPN needed]
```

- **Cloud IAM** is role-based: roles (owner/editor/viewer, plus fine-grained roles like `roles/run.invoker`) attach to principals (users, groups, **service accounts**) and grant actions on resources. Service accounts are the "workload identity" of GCP — every compute instance/Cloud Run revision runs as one. The rules are the same least-privilege discipline as everywhere: roles scoped as tight as possible, no "editor on everything."
- **Identity-Aware Proxy (IAP)** is GCP's zero-trust application gate: it checks identity + context *before* traffic reaches your app, so internal apps don't need a VPN or public exposure. It's the GCP-native [zero trust](../zero-trust-architecture) move — put IAP in front of private apps and lock the rest of the network down.

## The mistake repeatedly remembered

- **Public exposure of the database** — a Cloud SQL instance with an external IP and open auth, discovered by a scanner in a day.
- **Default-to-VM** — reaching for Compute Engine when Cloud Run would do; you pay for an always-on machine to run a service that idles 90% of the time.
- **Spanner "because it scales"** — planetary-scale relational that a single-region Cloud SQL would serve at a fraction of the cost and complexity.
- **Wide IAM** — a service account with `roles/editor` "so it's not annoying," leaking the blast radius across every project.
- **One region, no HA story** — everything in a single zone/region "for simplicity," violating the [cloud fundamentals](../cloud-computing-fundamentals) bar.
- **Pub/Sub duplicate blindness** — consumers that don't handle at-least-once, so a retried message creates a duplicate downstream.

## Tester checklist

- **IAM least privilege**: confirm the app's service account can only reach its own bucket prefix, its DB, and what it needs — no editor/owner anywhere.
- **Cloud Run scale-to-zero**: idle a service and confirm it scales to zero (no running instance, no billing), then a request warms it back up.
- **GKE node-pool autonomy**: drain/cordon a node and confirm the cluster re-schedules pods to a healthy pool without downtime.
- **Load balancer failover**: stop the primary backend region and confirm the global LB routes to the secondary with no client-visible outage.
- **Pub/Sub delivery**: publish a message, kill a consumer mid-delivery, and confirm redelivery (at-least-once) and that your consumer dedupes.
- **BigQuery cost guard**: run a query over a large table and confirm the bytes-scanned estimate and cost controls (no unbounded scans).
- **IAP gate**: attempt to reach a private app without IAP identity and confirm it's blocked before your app even sees the request.

- [Cloud computing fundamentals](../cloud-computing-fundamentals) — the regions/Zones/availability model behind these services
- [AWS core services](../aws-core-services) and [Azure core services](../azure-core-services) — the identical job with different names (map the vocabulary: VPC/VNet/GCE, S3/Blob/GCS, RDS/SQL/Cloud SQL)
- [Kubernetes and container orchestration](../kubernetes-and-container-orchestration) — the mechanics GKE packages as a service
- [Pub/Sub and event streaming](../pub-sub-event-streaming) — the model Cloud Pub/Sub implements
- [Data warehouse and lakehouse architecture](../data-warehouse-lakehouse-architecture) — where BigQuery sits
- [Data pipelines (ETL/ELT)](../data-pipelines-etl-elt) — the pipelines Dataflow runs into BigQuery
- [Cloud networking and hybrid connectivity](../cloud-networking-and-hybrid-connectivity) — VPC peering, interconnect, and private links
- [Horizontal vs vertical scaling](../horizontal-vs-vertical-scaling) — the scaling math behind autoscaled node pools and Cloud Run64:T15e8,
## What this system is

GraphQL is a **query language for your API** plus a runtime. Instead of many endpoints each returning a fixed shape, you have one endpoint (`POST /graphql`) where the client **declares exactly which fields it wants**, nested to any depth. The server answers with a matching shape. The deal: huge frontend flexibility and one round-trip — bought with graph traversal costs, cache complexity, and a resolver layer you control.

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant C as Client
  participant G as GraphQL server
  participant DB as Database(s)
  participant S as "Other services (REST / gRPC)"

  C->>G: POST /graphql (query declaration)
  G->>DB: resolve fields
  G->>S: batch / DataLoader
  S-->>G: results
  G-->>C: JSON matching requested shape
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  C[Client] -->|query user name and orders| G[GraphQL runtime]
  G -->|schema type-checking| V{Valid?}
  V -->|no| E1[Errors - no execution]
  V -->|yes| R[Resolve field by field]
  R --> D[Databases / services]
  R --> O[Return exactly the requested shape]
```

## The schema is the contract

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Q[type Query] --> QF[fields: user, orders, search]
  M[type Mutation] --> MF[fields: createOrder, updateUser]
  T[type User] --> TF[fields: id, name, orders, email]
  ON[type Order] --> NF[fields: id, total, status, placedAt]
```

Unlike REST, where the contract is implied by endpoints + docs, GraphQL has a **machine-readable schema** (`type User { ... }`). That schema gives you: introspection (clients discover the API), validation (a query that mentions a non-existent field fails *before* execution), and tooling (autocomplete, codegen, documentation).

- **`Query`** — the reads, **one entry point** for fetching.
- **`Mutation`** — the writes. All requests ride the same single endpoint.
- **Resolvers** — functions that answer "where does this field's value come from?", each field resolved independently. That's where your performance either shines or catches fire.

## Queries, and the graph-traversal cost

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant C as Client
  participant G as GraphQL server
  participant DB as Database
  participant Svc as Sub-services

  C->>G: query user id 1, orders, order lines
  G->>DB: fetch user row
  G->>DB: fetch orders where user=1
  G->>Svc: resolve product titles (N calls?)
  Svc-->>G: product metadata
  G->>C: assembled exact shape
```

The query asks for nested data **in one round-trip** — great for chatty mobile apps. But the server must resolve every requested field, and a naive resolver does **N+1**: fetch the user, then one query per order, then one per line item. The fix is **batching** (DataLoader: coalesce a query's identical field loads into one call) and **depth/query-size limits** so a client can't ask for a bomb-shaped query (`orders { lines { orders { lines { ... } } } }` tens of levels deep).

## Strengths and the price

| | GraphQL | REST |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Shape of response | Client controls — no under/over-fetching | Fixed per endpoint |
| Round-trips | One query, nested in one call | Multiple calls for related data |
| Contract | Schema + introspection, typed | Docs + conventions |
| Caching | Hard — POST endpoint, varies by query | Natural via HTTP caching (`Cache-Control`, `ETag`) |
| Server cost | Resolver traversal, N+1 trap | Predictable per-route work |
| Learning/simplicity | Bigger server surface | Simple, boring, universal |

The honest summary: GraphQL shines when **clients differ wildly** (mobile, web, third parties), when **related data is fetched together**, and when you value a typed contract. It costs you with **cache complexity** — every query is a distinct POST body, so HTTP caching doesn't help like REST; you need normalized client caches (Apollo), `persisted queries`, or `GET`-style caching for it.

## Pagination and mutations: the two design questions

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  P1[offset/limit - simple, unstable] --> PAG[Pagination]
  P2[cursor-based - stable, modern] --> PAG
  P3[relay-style connections] --> PAG
  PAG --> P4[Edge + node model: metadata about each item]
```

- **Cursor pagination** is the GraphQL default for lists (stable across inserts, like REST's modern answer). **Relay Connections** formalize it with `edges { cursor node }` + `pageInfo { hasNextPage }`.
- **Mutations** return the mutated object (and often changed related state) so the client can update its cache in one shot — avoiding a follow-up query.

## Tester checklist

- Send a query with a non-existent field: it must fail validation *before* execution (schema is enforced).
- Test depth/query-cost limits: a runaway nested query should be rejected by your size guard before it reaches resolvers.
- Add a DataLoader and re-run an N+1 query: confirm the database request count drops from `1 + N` to a small constant (watch the DB query log).
- Verify error handling: partial resolvers failing should produce an `errors` array in the response, not a failed HTTP request — and know which is which.
- Compare payload sizes: request the same data via GraphQL vs REST and confirm you're actually fetching less (that's the contract's point).

## Related systems

- [RESTful API design](../restful-api-design) — the other mental model for the same job
- [API request lifecycle](../api-request-lifecycle) — the chain GraphQL runs through
- [Authentication vs authorization](../authentication-vs-authorization) — how identity gates the schema's fields
- [OAuth 2.0 and OpenID Connect](../oauth2-openid-connect) — how clients authorize against GraphQL APIs65:T2bdb,
## What this system is

Scaling is the answer to "we have more work than one machine can do," and there are exactly two directions to grow in. **Vertical scaling (scale up)** makes the machine bigger: more CPU, more RAM, faster disks. **Horizontal scaling (scale out)** adds more machines. Nothing else — auto-scaling, replicas, sharding, partitioning — is a separate option; it's an implementation of one of these two axes. The art is that the two axes are *not interchangeable*: they buy different properties, fail differently, and constrain your architecture differently.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph Vertical["Scale up - one bigger box"]
    V1[Server<br/>2 vCPU / 4 GB]
    V2[Server<br/>16 vCPU / 64 GB]
  end
  subgraph Horizontal["Scale out - more boxes"]
    H1[Server A]
    H2[Server B]
    H3[Server C]
  end
  V1 --> V2
  H1 --> LB[Load balancer]
  H2 --> LB
  H3 --> LB
```

## The two axes

| Axis | What you do | What it buys | Where it typically breaks |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Vertical** | Bigger machine (vCPU, RAM, disk, GPU) | Simpler ops: one box, one app | Hardware limit: you can't buy bigger; a single point of failure; downtime for resize |
| **Horizontal** | More machines behind a balancer | Elasticity: add/remove as load changes; survive a node dying | State: each node must be interchangeable, so shared state must live elsewhere |

The one-sentence summary: **vertical is the easy win until it stops being possible; horizontal is the durable win but only if your app is built to allow it.** Almost every scaling story is "scale vertically first because it's free, then be forced horizontal because horizontal requires architecture."

## Vertical scaling: when it's the right answer

```mermaid
flowchart TD
  Q{One instance today?} -->|yes| V1{Have headroom on the machine?}
  V1 -->|yes| UP[Resize - cheapest, simplest]
  V1 -->|no| V2{Is the load mostly CPU/RAM bounded?}
  V2 -->|yes, fits one box| UP
  V2 -->|no - know it'll grow past any box| HOR[Plan horizontal now]
```

Vertical is the pragmatic first move in real life: it takes minutes, needs no code change, and fixes 80% of "it's slow" for a while. It is the *correct* long-term answer specifically when:

- **The workload is itself single-threaded or single-aware** — a Postgres service that needs one big shared buffer, an OLTP database, a monolithic app with in-memory session state.
- **The data can't be partitioned** — a queue with strict global ordering, or an analytic query that must see the whole dataset (this is why some databases stay vertical: horizontal = distributed = consensus and joins across nodes).
- **The environment caps you** — you run on hardware you own, in a colo, or under licensing/legacy constraints.
- **The load is finite and predictable** — a report that runs for an hour on Friday; you size one machine for peak and ignore elasticity.

The costs: vertical resizing usually means **downtime** to reboot into the bigger shape, a **hard ceiling** (the cloud's largest shape, the vendor's biggest box), and **one point of failure** — when that machine dies, everything dies. It also tends to be *more expensive* than horizontal per unit of capacity once you pass a few nodes.

## Horizontal scaling: the laws you must satisfy

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  U[Users] --> LB[Load balancer<br/>round robin / least conn]
  LB --> W1[Worker 1 - stateless]
  LB --> W2[Worker 2 - stateless]
  LB --> W3[Worker 3 - stateless]
  W1 --> S[(Shared state<br/>DB / cache / queue)]
  W2 --> S
  W3 --> S
```

Horizontal scaling is a *contract* your application must sign, and it has three clauses:

1. **Statelessness**: any request can land on any node. Whatever a node needed to remember lives outside it — in a shared database, cache, object store, or queue. If each request is independent and the app doesn't care which node runs it, you can add a node and instantly absorb more load.
2. **A shared-state home**: the "memory" moves elsewhere (DB, Redis, Kafka, blob storage). That store — not your app — becomes the real scaling burden. Horizontal scale-out usually just moves the bottleneck onto the database.
3. **A distribution layer**: something decides which node handles which request — a load balancer for services, a partition/partition-key for queues and event streams, a shard key for data. If distribution is wrong (all traffic to one node, all messages to one partition), you have N nodes but one node's worth of capacity.

The payoff is real: **elasticity** (scale down at night, up at peak), **failure isolation** (one node dies, others keep serving), and **no hard ceiling**. The price is real too: you now run a distributed system, and distributed systems fail in new ways (partial failures, retries, idempotency, ordering caveats) even though they look like a simple cluster.

## Stateless vs stateful: the axis that decides for you

| Workload | Natural axis | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Web tier, API server, worker that is fed by a queue | **Horizontal** — trivially | Pure compute, zero shared memory, just add pods |
| OLTP database (single logical DB) | **Vertical first**, then read replicas / sharding | Horizontal requires splitting truth or accepting distributed consistency |
| Message queue with strict global order | **Vertical** (or single-partition) | One partition = one node processes serially |
| Event stream with partition keys | **Horizontal** — per partition | Each partition is independent; scale by adding partitions |
| In-memory cache | **Horizontal** with proper key sharding | A cache that must hold the whole dataset fits one big node better |

The mistake to avoid is assuming the horizontal label applies uniformly: **your stateless web tier can scale to a hundred copies, but the database behind it still scales vertical-first.** "Horizontal scaling" of a system almost always means: web/worker tiers scale out freely, and the data tier gets a harder, slower scaling story of its own.

## Scaling the data tier: replicas, shards, partitions

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  subgraph Replicas["Read replicas - scale READs"]
    W[R/W primary] --> R1[Read replica]
    W --> R2[Read replica]
  end
  subgraph Shards["Sharding - scale WRITEs + reads by key"]
    S1[Shard 0 - users 0-50k]
    S2[Shard 1 - users 50k-100k]
    S3[Shard 2 - users 100k+]
  end
```

- **Read replicas** scale *reads* (queries) by fanning them to copies — but writes still go to one primary. Read-heavy systems get enormous horizontal benefit this way with little architecture change (async replication may lag, so "read your own write" needs care).
- **Sharding** splits data across nodes by a key (user_id, tenant, region), scaling both reads *and* writes. The costs appear immediately: joins now cross servers, transactions become per-shard, and a hot key (one celebrity account) can unbalance a shard. Sharding is the "last resort that works" for data — do everything elastic first, shard only when you've proven the key distribution.
- **Queues and event streams** scale horizontally via partitions: each partition is an independent ordered lane, and throughput = partitions × per-partition rate. Ordering is preserved only within a partition — global ordering is the one thing horizontal cannot give you.

## Auto-scaling: vertical and horizontal automation

- **Horizontal auto-scaling** (the default in K8s, cloud VMAS, serverless): add/remove instances based on a metric (CPU, queue depth, request rate). It's the *elastic* promise of the cloud — you pay for what you need — but it only helps if your app is horizontally scalable, and it lags reality: your metric reflects the load *of a minute ago*, so sharp spikes outrun it unless you scale on leading indicators (queue depth, ingress rate) rather than trailing ones (CPU).
- **Vertical auto-scaling** exists too, but is slower and usually needs a restart to apply, which is why it's rarer — a DB that can grow its RAM limits in place is the nicer case, but don't count on zero-downtime for arbitrary workloads.

The rule: **automate horizontal scaling on your stateless tiers aggressively; treat data-tier scaling as a manual, deliberate, high-consequence step.**

## The decision, condensed

```mermaid
flowchart TD
  Start[Work per unit exceeds one node?] --> S1{Is state shared?}
  S1 -->|no - stateless| H[Scale horizontally - the easy win]
  S1 -->|yes - stateful| S2{Can the state be partitioned/ replicated?}
  S2 -->|read-heavy| REP[Read replicas]
  S2 -->|key is clean| SHARD[Shard by key]
  S2 -->|no - one logical whole| V[Scale vertically - buy the biggest box]
```

| Situation | Reach for |
| --- | --- |
| Web/API/worker tier getting hot | Horizontal (stateless), auto-scale |
| A single OLTP database nearing limits | Vertical first; add read replicas for reads; shard only as last resort |
| Queue or event stream behind on throughput | Horizontal, more partitions — but lose global ordering |
| Workload that fits one big machine, predictable peak | Vertical, size for peak |
| Cache that must hold the entire dataset | Vertical (or horizontal with a good hash ring) |

## Tester checklist

- **Vertical resize**: change the machine/compute shape and confirm the new resources are actually seen by the app (ulimit, heap, conn pool) and workloads benefit — a resize that doesn't change the app's configured limits buys nothing.
- **Horizontal add/remove**: hot-add a node and confirm new traffic actually flows to it (session stickiness off, health check passing) and it drains cleanly when removed.
- **Statelessness probe**: bounce one node mid-traffic and confirm zero requests fail — if requests in flight to that node fail, you have in-memory state that violates the contract.
- **Shared-state validity**: after scale-out, confirm the DB/cache/queue the nodes now share is real — faked by per-node caches that went gold after the first request.
- **Distribution check**: with N nodes, confirm load is actually spread with no hot node drawing all traffic (or one hot partition starving the rest).
- **Auto-scale reality**: induce a spike and confirm instances add fast enough (leading-indicator scaling) and remove after the load drops, without thrash.

- [Cloud computing fundamentals](../cloud-computing-fundamentals) — the resource vocabulary vertical/horizontal both operate on
- [DNS and load balancing](../dns-and-load-balancing) — the distribution layer horizontal scaling depends on
- [Kubernetes and container orchestration](../kubernetes-and-container-orchestration) — where horizontal scaling becomes a platform feature
- [Message queue delivery](../message-queue-delivery) — the queue that gives you horizontal workers with ordering caveats
- [Pub/Sub and event streaming](../pub-sub-event-streaming) — partition-based horizontal throughput for events
- [Database indexing](../database-indexing) — the data-tier levers before you resort to sharding
- [Data warehouse and lakehouse architecture](../data-warehouse-lakehouse-architecture) — where the analytic side scales (compute, not writes)
- [Monitoring and observability](../monitoring-and-observability) — the metrics that drive safe auto-scaling66:T105c,
## What this system is

A housing management system (property management, PMS) runs the **asset side** of residential real estate: the buildings and units you own or manage, their physical state, who is responsible, and the maintenance and inspections that keep them rentable. Where the [tenancy management system](../tenancy-management-systems) owns *who rents what*, the housing system owns *what the building is*. The two are joined at the unit — the master object both sides operate on.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  P[Portfolio] --> B[Building / development]
  B --> U[Unit - the core object]
  U --> O[Occupancy state]
  U --> M[Maintenance + inspections]
  U --> L[Linked tenancy records]
```

## The object model

| Object | Holds | Owned by | Failure mode |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Portfolio / estate | Grouping of buildings | Asset owner | Reporting across the wrong scope |
| Building / development | Address, blocks, common areas | Property team | Unit → building links drift |
| **Unit** | Number, type, area, condition, features | Housing system | The integration hub both sides trust |
| Unit status | Let / vacant / maintenance / hold | Housing system | Stale status blocks new lettings |
| Work order | Job, trade, status, cost | Maintenance | Vendor never completed but status says closed |
| Inspection record | Condition, photos, date | Inspections | Photos lost, disputes lose |

The **unit** is the record of truth: tenancy, rent, maintenance, and inspections all hang off it. If the housing system and the tenancy system disagree about a unit's status, that disagreement is a live booking bug.

## The unit lifecycle

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  AC[Acquire / build] --> IN[Setup - config, features, spec]
  IN --> AV[Available to let]
  AV --> OC[Occupied - under tenancy]
  OC --> MT[In maintenance / hold]
  MT --> AV
  OC --> RE[Returned to portfolio / sold]
```

The unit's **status is a state machine with rules**: you cannot let a unit that is in maintenance, and you cannot move a tenancy in before the prior one has signed out. Most integration bugs in property software are *status races* — two systems updating the same unit's state without a single owner.

## The maintenance pipeline

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  R[Issue raised - tenant or inspection] --> A[Assess + prioritise]
  A --> V[Assign vendor / internal trade]
  V --> W[Work order + cost approval]
  W --> D[Done - photos + sign-off]
  D --> B[Billing - to owner, tenant, or insurance]
```

Maintenance is where housing management spends money, so the costing and approval trail matters: who authorised the work, at what estimate, against which budget. The auditability of this chain decides whether a dispute becomes an argument or a report.

## The unit → occupancy path

```mermaid
stateDiagram-v2
  [*] --> Unit: setup
  Unit: Unit setup
  Unit --> Available: ready
  Available: Available to let
  Available --> Tenancy: lease signed
  Tenancy: Tenancy created
  Tenancy --> Occ: move-in
  Occ: Occupied
  Occ --> Maint: life in service
  Maint: Maintenance + rent
  Maint --> [*]
```

## Tester checklist

- Status integrity: force a conflict (unit shows occupied in tenancy system, vacant in housing) — the reconcile must surface it, not silently accept.
- Let-gating: a unit in maintenance must not be lettable; a let attempt must be blocked at the source.
- Maintenance audit trail: every work order must carry approval, estimate, actual cost, and completion sign-off — a closed-but-incomplete order must trip a flag.
- Inspections: photos and notes must be bound to unit and date, surviving a re-let of the same unit.
- Billing split: work charged to owner vs tenant vs insurance must post to the right account (check the finance link).

## Related systems

- [Tenancy management systems](../tenancy-management-systems) — the other half that joins at the unit
- [ERP systems](../enterprise-resource-planning) — the finance and asset-register backbone
- [Message queue delivery](../message-queue-delivery) — decoupling unit-status events from downstream lettings
- [Data governance and catalog](../data-governance-and-catalog) — PII in tenant-linked inspection notes67:T1184,
## What this system is

HR systems cover the second-most-sensitive master data a company owns — **people**. The core is the HRIS/HCM: the authoritative employee record (hire date, org, job, comp, location). Around it orbit payroll, benefits, time & attendance, recruiting (ATS), and learning. Below it sits the integration that matters most operationally: **every HR event fanning out to identity, access, and finance**.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  H[Hire event] --> R[HRIS - employee record]
  R --> P[Payroll]
  R --> B[Benefits + time]
  R --> I[Identity provisioning]
  R --> F[Finance - cost center postings]
```

## The hire-to-retire chain

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  J[Job requisition] --> Recruit[Recruiting - ATS]
  Recruit --> Offer[Hire - HRIS record]
  Offer --> Provision[Provision identity + accounts]
  Provision --> Move[Transfers and changes over time]
  Move --> Term[Termination / offboarding]
  Term --> Deprovision[Deprovision identity + access]
  Term --> Pay[Final pay + compliance records]
```

The chain is an **event bus with states**: the terminal states matter as much as the happy path. A leaver whose identity is never deprovisioned is the classic insider-threat hole. This guide deliberately pairs with [joiner-mover-leaver](../joiner-mover-leaver) — that page owns the access half.

## The HR systems landscape

| System | Owns | Master data | Common failure mode |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| HRIS / HCM | Authoritative employee record, org, jobs | Name, ID, hire/term dates, org, comp, location | Corrections never propagate downstream |
| Payroll | Pay runs, taxes, banking | Pay rates, bank details, tax jurisdiction | Wrong file released on cutoff day |
| ATS (talent) | Candidates, requisitions, interviews | Candidate + applicant trackers | Duplicate candidate records |
| Benefits / time | Enrollment, leave, attendance | Plan elections, time balances | Missed yearly open-enrollment sync |
| Learning / performance | Courses, goals, reviews | Course IDs, reviewer links | Orphaned learning plans |

## The field of derivatives

Almost every downstream system is a **derived copy** of the HRIS record: identity systems, single sign-on, directories, finance cost-center postings, benefits carriers, learning platforms. The HRIS is the source of truth; everything else subscribes. Two integration truths follow:

- **Identity provisioning is correctness, not convenience.** Provisioners (SCIM) turn HR events into directory/SSO/application access mechanically. Verifiable either way: the new hire can sign in before the first day, or the mystery of why they can't is traced here first.
- **Payroll is the highest-consequence batch.** A single run touches thousands of bank movements and statutory filings. Nobody "fixes it in the next release" without regulatory impact.

## The hire → first paycheck path

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Hire["Hire event"] -->|create| HRIS["HRIS record"]
  HRIS -->|SCIM provision| ID["Identity + directory"]
  ID -->|first login| SSO["Single sign-on"]
  HRIS -->|payroll setup| Pay["Payroll setup"]
  Pay -->|run payroll| Bank["Bank + compliance"]
  SSO -->|access| Ready["Productive employee"]
  Bank -->|pay| Ready
```

## Tester checklist

- Joiner: with only an HR event dated in the future, confirm the identity record exists and is *disabled* until the effective date, and the correct SSO/app grants activate on it.
- Mover: a transfer changing cost center must post to finance with the new org, not silently keep the old one.
- Leaver: termination date passes → accounts deprovisioned, sessions revoked, licenses reclaimed, payroll closed out.
- Payroll cutoff: a change arriving after the payroll batch snapshot must NOT mutate that already-closed run.
- Data quality: a corrected SSN/name in HRIS must flow to every derivative with no stale duplicate.

## Related systems

- [Joiner-mover-leaver](../joiner-mover-leaver) — the access lifecycle that HR events trigger
- [SCIM identity provisioning](../scim-identity-provisioning) — the wire protocol that propagates HR to identity
- [Single sign-on (SSO)](../single-sign-on-sso) — the session layer the employee logs into
- [Privileged identity management](../privileged-identity-management) — elevated access for HR admins themselves
- [ERP systems](../enterprise-resource-planning) — hire-to-retire is one of the five ERP chains
- [Data governance and catalog](../data-governance-and-catalog) — HR data is PII-heavy and heavily governed68:T15ab,
## What this system is

HTTP authentication is the **`Authorization` request header** (plus the `WWW-Authenticate` response header that tells you *how* to authenticate). The header carries a **scheme** and scheme-specific credentials: `Basic`, `Bearer`, `Digest`, or a custom scheme. It is the oldest, simplest layer of "who may access this endpoint" — and the one most often misused.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  CL[Client] -->|attach header| RQ[GET /api with Authorization header]
  RQ -->|dispatch| CH{Server parses scheme}
  CH -->|valid| OK[200 - allowed]
  CH -->|missing or invalid| DENY[401 + WWW-Authenticate]
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  C[Client] -->|Authorization: Basic dXNlcjpwYXNz| S[Server]
  S --> V{Decode + verify?}
  V -->|yes| OK[200 OK]
  V -->|no / absent| CH[401 + WWW-Authenticate: Basic realm=...]
```

## Basic auth, honestly explained

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  U[username:password] --> B1[base64 encode] --> H1[dXNlcjpwYXNz]
  H1 --> H2[Authorization: Basic dXNlcjpwYXNz]
  H2 --> S[Server decodes and compares]
```

Basic auth is one line: **`Authorization: Basic <base64(username:password)>`**. The credentials are `base64` encoded, **not encrypted** — anyone who sees the header sees the password (base64 is trivially reversible). Three rules decide whether it's acceptable:

1. **Only over TLS** — over plain HTTP, the header *is* the password broadcast to the network.
2. **Only for low-stakes or system-to-system** credentials — a shared API key with narrow scope, never a human's master password.
3. **Never against a database of user passwords** — compare against a hash (and rate-limit to survive brute force).

The classic failure: "it's behind HTTPS so base64 is fine" — then an operator pulls the header into a log, a proxy, or an error message, and the credential leaks in plaintext where the TLS never protected it.

## The `WWW-Authenticate` dance

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant C as Client
  participant S as Server

  C->>S: GET /private (no auth)
  S-->>C: 401 + WWW-Authenticate: Basic realm="Admin"
  C->>S: GET /private + Authorization: Basic ...
  S-->>C: 200 OK (or 403 if not allowed)
```

When auth is missing/invalid, the server answers **`401`** with a **`WWW-Authenticate`** header naming the scheme (and often a `realm` describing what's protected). The client then *retries with credentials*. That's why you'll see two requests in the network tab for a Basic-protected page.

## Basic vs Digest vs Bearer vs API keys

| Scheme | How it works | Use it for | Danger |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Basic** | `base64(user:pass)` | TLS-only, low-stakes, simple | Credential in every header; replayable if leaked |
| **Digest** | Hash of user:pass + nonce | Legacy, still-plaintext apps | Weak hash; rarely worth it today |
| **Bearer** | An opaque token from an issuer | Tokens you already hold (OAuth/JWT) | Whoever holds the token is you — no password involved |
| **API key** | A shared secret, often `X-API-Key` | System-to-system, scoped | Same leak problem as Basic; also logged |
| **mTLS** | Certificate on the connection | Machine-to-machine, high-trust | Complex to roll out; strongest tie to the wire |

**Bearer and API keys are the modern defaults** for machine APIs precisely because they don't carry a *password* — a leaked token is revocable and (ideally) short-lived, whereas a leaked Basic pair is the user's real login. The recurring refrain in every auth review: "this is a credential, treat it like one."

## Reading the header: what a tester looks for

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  H[Authorization header seen?] --> A{Over HTTPS only?}
  A -->|no| B[FAIL - credential in cleartext]
  A -->|yes| C{What scheme?}
  C -->|Basic| D[Is it base64 user-pass? rate-limited? non-human?]
  C -->|Bearer / API key| E[Expiry? revocation? scope limited?]
  C -->|something custom| F[Is the scheme well-defined and not invented crypto?]
```

- Is the header **ever logged** (proxy, gateway, app logs)? One `req.headers` dumped to logs = credential leak.
- Does the server check **over** the header only, or also **caches** a response keyed by the credential (a cached 200 shared across users)?
- Is there **rate limiting / lockout** on failures? A Basic endpoint without it is a brute-force treadmill.
- Are credentials **revocable and rotated**? A static API key from 2019 with access to prod is an incident waiting.

## Tester checklist

- Confirm every Basic/Bearer API you expose **refuses** plaintext HTTP (redirect to HTTPS, or reject entirely) — the header must never travel unencrypted.
- Attempt auth with the base64 decoded and sent wrong: expect `401` with `WWW-Authenticate`, not `200` or `403`-with-data.
- Check that failed attempts are rate-limited/locked and that logs do **not** contain the full `Authorization` header.
- Verify a leaked-token scenario end to end: revoke a key/token and confirm in-flight and new requests fail (not just "stale cache").
- Distinguish 401 vs 403: unauthenticated = 401 + challenge; authenticated-but-forbidden = 403. Mixing them up hides bugs and leaks resource existence.

## Related systems

- [OAuth grant types](../oauth-grant-types) — where Bearer tokens come from instead of Basic credentials
- [OAuth 2.0 and OpenID Connect](../oauth2-openid-connect) — the full token lifecycle Basic has nothing to do with
- [Authentication vs authorization](../authentication-vs-authorization) — authn (this) vs authz (the next step)
- [JWT tokens](../jwt-tokens) — what a modern bearer token often contains69:Tff1,
## What this system is

Zero Trust starts with identity. IAM is the layer that answers permanently *"who is this?"* and *"what may they do?"* — for humans, service accounts, and workloads. If identity is wrong, every access decision built on top of it is wrong too. The four jobs of IAM: **prove** (sign-up/sign-in), **attribute** (roles/groups/claims), **issue** (tokens), and **revoke** (when access must end).

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  U[User] -->|credentials / MFA| IdP[Identity provider<br/>directory + SSO]
  IdP -->|assert identity| SSO[Single sign-on broker]
  SSO --> App1[App A via OIDC/SAML]
  SSO --> App2[App B via OIDC/SAML]
  IdP -->|provision / deprovision| Dir[(Directory store<br/>Entra ID / Okta / corporate AD)]
```

## The sign-in flow behind "one password"

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant U as User
  participant App as App A
  participant IdP as Corporate IdP
  participant MFA as MFA / FIDO2

  U->>App: wants to open reusable App A
  App->>IdP: redirect + OIDC/SAML request
  IdP->>U: login page
  U->>MFA: passkey / code / number match
  MFA-->>IdP: proof of second factor
  IdP->>App: grant token + user claims
  App->>IdP: validate token signature
  App-->>U: session established
```

Federation means the *corporate* IdP owns the proof; each app trusts the same source. One revoke at the IdP immediately kills access everywhere — the property every ZT design depends on.

## Lifecycle: where identity actually goes wrong

| Phase | What you do | Typical failure |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Provision | Auto-create account on hire, from HR feed | Manual, slow, delayed |
| Use | Groups from management/HR drive access | Groups drift, un-reviewed |
| Change | Role change re-scopes groups | Old rights linger to "not break anything" |
| Revoke | Offboard: disable IdP + kill sessions + deprovision | "Disabled but still licensed"; orphan service accounts |

The **orphaned account** is the quiet disaster: a service account with admin rights nobody monitors. Lifecycle automation and periodic access reviews are the counter-measures.

## RBAC vs ABAC (the authorization models)

| | RBAC (roles) | ABAC (attributes) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Decides by | "You are an admin" | attributes: user, device, time, location, data tag |
| Pros | Simple, human-readable | Granular, context-sensitive |
| Cons | Role explosion as you grow | Policy sprawl if not disciplined |
| Fit | Small estates, clear job families | Zero Trust / conditional access at scale |

Most enterprises run both: RBAC for coarse grants, ABAC/conditional access to narrow *when* they apply.

## Tester checklist

- Offboard a user: account disabled, sessions killed, and all app entitlements gone — verify *actual* revoke, not just "disabled".
- Verify SSO reaches every app: no app quietly keeps its own credential store (shadow identity).
- Replay an old JWT after revocation and confirm it's rejected (short token TTL + revocation callback).
- Walk a role change: new group grants, old group removed — then review that nothing re-attaches via nested groups.

## Related systems

- [Zero Trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — the umbrella every IAM decision plugs into
- [Conditional access and policy engines](../conditional-access-and-policy-engines) — turning identity attributes into allow/deny
- [OAuth 2.0 and OpenID Connect](../oauth2-openid-connect) — the tokens and flows IdPs speak
- [Workload identity and mTLS](../workload-identity-and-mtls) — the machine side of identity

## Deep dives

- [Single sign-on (SSO)](../single-sign-on-sso) — the login half of identity
- [Role-based access control (RBAC)](../rbac-and-authorization) — the authorization model that assigns what you may touch
- [Privileged identity management (PIM)](../privileged-identity-management) — no standing admin, just-in-time privilege
- [SCIM identity provisioning](../scim-identity-provisioning) — the provisioning half: accounts arrive and leave automatically
- [Joiner-mover-leaver (JML)](../joiner-mover-leaver) — the lifecycle events that drive it all6a:T1c0d,
## What this system is

An incident response (IR) playbook is a **prepared, rehearsed runbook** for a specific class of bad day: ransomware, phishing, credential compromise, data breach, DDoS, insider threat. Its purpose is to remove *thinking under panic* — when the page fires, people execute a known script instead of improvising. Unlike an alert or a ticket, a playbook is **scenario-shaped**, has **owners per step**, and ends with a **lessons-learned loop**. The IR team's real product is not "response" — it's the book of playbooks.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  DET[Detect] --> TRI[Initial triage]
  TRI -->|confirmed incident?| ESC[Declare + escalate]
  TRI -->|false positive| CLOSE[Close as no-incident]
  ESC --> CON[Contain - slow the bleeding]
  CON --> ERA[Eradicate - remove the cause]
  ERA --> REC[Recover - restore verified service]
  REC --> LES[Lessons learned - update the playbook]
  LES --> DET
```

## The phase model (whatever framework you use)

| Phase | The question | What happens | Typical owner |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Preparation** | What will we do before? | Playbooks, tools, access, comms channels, contacts | Security leadership |
| **Detection & triage** | Is this real and how bad? | Verify the alert, assess scope, assign a severity/pager | SOC / on-call |
| **Containment** | How do we stop the spread *now*? | Isolate hosts, kill access, revoke creds, cut egress | IR team + network |
| **Eradication** | What is the root cause to remove? | Remove malware, close the path, patch, change secrets | Security engineering |
| **Recovery** | When is it safe to come back? | Restore from verified back-ups, re-enable services, validate | Service owners |
| **Lessons learned** | What changes before the next one? | Root-cause review, playbook edits, training, budgets | The whole team |

The mental model: **detect is cheap to perform and expensive to get wrong; contain is the hour that saves the estate.** Most of a playbook's value sits in triage (who decides) and containment (the first concrete actions), because those run while everything is still on fire.

## A concrete playbook skeleton (credential compromise)

```mermaid
flowchart TD
  A[Cred leak / token theft detected] --> B{Scope: user, app, or admin?}
  B -->|user| C[Revoke user sessions + force password reset]
  B -->|app / token| D[Rotate app credentials + invalidate tokens]
  B -->|admin / privileged| E[Crime-scene: isolate host, kill sessions]
  C --> F[Audit recent sign-ins + unusual actions]
  D --> F
  E --> F
  F --> G{Any evidence of wider breach?}
  G -->|yes| H[Declare elevated incident - engage full IR]
  G -->|no| I[Contain closed - monitor + document]
```

Good playbooks share a skeleton even when the scenario differs:

1. **Trigger conditions** — exactly which alerts/tickets start this playbook.
2. **Initial actions (5–15 min)** — revoke, isolate, kill, reset: the steps that buy time.
3. **Decision gates** — branching logic ("if admin → different path") with named deciders.
4. **Escalation tree** — who gets engaged at which severity, with a page tree not a guessing game.
5. **Evidence preservation** — what to collect and where, before anyone "fixes" the evidence away.
6. **Verification + handoff** — how everyone knows containment actually worked and who's running recovery.
7. **Retrospective hooks** — where lessons feed back (and the playbook gets edited for next time).

## Why playbooks fail in the real world

| Failure | The real reason | The fix |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Written, not rehearsed** | A playbook nobody has ever run in a drill | Tabletop exercises and fire drills on a schedule |
| **No named owners** | "Security team" is a meeting, not a person | Every step gets a named owner + a back-up |
| **Tools aren't provisioned** | The playbook says "revoke" but the revoke tool isn't accessible to on-call | DRY-RUN the access: can the on-call person actually do the step? |
| **Stale playbooks** | Systems changed, playbook didn't | Version inside the [CMDB](../cmdb-and-configuration-management) and re-validate on infra change |
| **Communications gap** | Ransomware raced the playbook; nobody told the business | A comms tree and template messages as part of the playbook |
| **Recovery without verification** | Service "restored" from backups that were also encrypted/laggy | Verified restores (test the backup, not just believe the copy) |

The deepest rule: **a playbook is a living document, not a deliverable.** If it's not edited after every incident and exercised between them, it's shelfware with a security branding.

## The post-incident loop (where teams actually improve)

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  INC[Incident closed] --> R[Retrospective]
  R --> FB[Findings: people / process / tech]
  FB --> PLAY[Update the playbook]
  PLAY --> DRILL[Drill / tabletop]
  DRILL --> PREP[Shorter time-to-contain next time]
```

The retrospective must separate blame from cause, then turn findings into **three concrete edits**: the playbook (steps), the tooling (access/automation gaps), and the training (who wasn't ready). An incident response capability that doesn't measure time-to-contain across incidents isn't learning — it's just documenting.

## Tester checklist

- **Trigger fidelity**: raise the exact alert a playbook starts on and confirm the playbook's trigger conditions match reality (no "we don't use that alert anymore").
- **5-minute test**: hand a fresh on-call person the playbook and the tools, and time them to the *first concrete containment action* — it must be under the target, unaided.
- **Decision-gate walk**: simulate both branches of a gate (user vs admin credential hit) and confirm each path resolves with its named decider, not an abandoned thread.
- **Tool reality check**: for each step claiming "revoke/reset/isolate," confirm the on-call role actually has that permission in production — the playbook must match the [RBAC](../rbac-and-authorization) reality.
- **Backup verification**: restores in the playbook come from backups that have been *test-restored* to a known-good point, not merely "scheduled."
- **Playbook CX after an incident**: after a drill or real incident, the retrospective produced a measurable edit (step, tool, or owner) — not just a meeting's minutes.

## Related systems

- [Security operations center (SOC)](../security-operations-center-soc) — where detection runs and where playbook execution is orchestrated
- [Endpoint security architecture](../endpoint-security-architecture) — the containment layer (isolate host, kill process, revoke) that playbooks trigger
- [Zero trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — the posture model that makes containment "revoke once, everywhere"
- [Threat modeling (STRIDE)](../threat-modeling-stride) — where the scenarios a playbook must cover come from
- [DevOps delivery loop](../devops-delivery-loop) and [ITIL incident management](../itil-incident-and-problem-management) — the sibling operations loops playbooks share a rhythm with
- [CMDB and configuration management](../cmdb-and-configuration-management) — where playbook owners and affected CIs get versioned6b:Ted1,
## What this system is

Every integration in your estate is one of a small family of patterns. Gregor Hohpe's "Enterprise Integration Patterns" named the classical four; event streaming and webhooks modernized them. When an integration frustrates you ("why is nothing happening?", "did the file arrive?"), the root cause is almost always a pattern-property mismatch — you built a file-transfer guarantor that needs messaging's delivery semantics.

## The five families

| Pattern | Coupling | Delivery semantics | Latency | Classic use |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **File transfer** | Loose (files) | At-least-once by poll+checksum | Batch (minutes-hours) | ERP/legacy exchange, bank statements |
| **Shared database** | Tightest (schema shared) | In DB, locks copied | Real-time-ish | Internal monoliths, reporting |
| **RPC / streaming RPC** | Tight (interface shared) | At-most-once; caller handles the rest | Lowest | Service APIs, gRPC, sync calls |
| **Messaging** | Loose (payload contract) | At-least-once + retries + DLQ | Milliseconds | Work queues, fan-out |
| **Shared event stream** | Very loose (schema-as-weak-contract) | At-least-once + replay | Milliseconds-Batch | Pub/sub, CDC, event sourcing |

## How to choose

```mermaid
flowchart TD
  A[Need to connect two systems?] --> B{Who drives it?}
  B -->|Human scheduled batch| C[File transfer / SFTP]
  B -->|System triggers in real time| D{Are both sides alive?}
  D -->|Yes, both online| E{RPC / webhooks}
  D -->|No / need durability| F{Messaging}
  F --> G{One consumer or many?}
  G -->|One| H[Work queue / message]
  G -->|Many| I[Pub-sub / topic]
  B -->|Keep DBs in sync automatically| J[Change data capture]
```

## The two axes that explain most failures

- **Synchronous vs asynchronous**: RPC/webhooks are sync — the caller *waits* and must handle failure itself. Messaging and CDC are async — nothing waits, but "eventually" means retries, ordering caveats, and idempotency.
- **Push vs pull**: webhooks push (source owns delivery, hard to retrofit), files and queues pull or poll. If a receiver is offline for hours, only pull/queue patterns survive without data loss.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph Sync["Synchronous (caller waits)"]
    A[RPC / gRPC]
    B[Rest API]
    C[Webhook]
  end
  subgraph Async["Asynchronous (eventually)"]
    D[Message queue]
    E[Pub/Sub topic]
    F[File drop folder]
    G[CDC stream]
  end
```

## Trade-off cheat sheet

| I want… | Pattern to reach for | Guarantee I must accept |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Immediate response, simple ops | RPC/webhook | Duplicates possible, caller owns retries |
| Durable, exactly-one-ish work | Message queue + idempotency | At-least-once, ordering per key |
| Many consumers see everything | Pub/Sub | Ordering per partition, replay responsibility |
| No app changes | CDC | Schema events, log footprint |
| Auditable, human-greppable | File transfer | Batch latency, folder drift |

## Related systems

- [Message queue delivery](../message-queue-delivery) — the messaging work-horse pattern
- [Pub/Sub and event streaming](../pub-sub-event-streaming) — the many-consumer family member
- [Webhook integrations](../webhook-integrations) — the synchronous push pattern
- [File transfer and object storage](../file-transfer-and-object-storage) — the classic, modernized
- [ERP systems](../enterprise-resource-planning) — the suite these patterns connect the estate to
- [Procurement (source-to-pay)](../procurement-source-to-pay) — an ERP-adjacent chain that needs them
- [Change data capture](../change-data-capture) — the no-application-changes pattern
- [Business process modeling and management](../business-process-modeling-and-management) — how the events these patterns carry drive business processes6c:Te46,
## What this system is

Apple devices in a corporation are managed remotely through **MDM** (mobile device management) once they're enrolled via **Apple Business Manager (ABM)**. Enrollment happens at first boot — before the user ever signs in — so the device is corporate-owned, **supervised**, and policy-controlled from day one. This is the Apple answer to Windows' domain join.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Vert[Device purchased / registered] -->|serial added| ABM[Apple Business Manager]
  ABM -->|automated enrollment via DEP| MDM[MDM: Intune / Jamf]
  MDM -->|supervision + config profiles| D[Device]
  D -->|inventory + compliance| MDM
  MDM -->|approve/distribute| App[Apps via App Store / VPP]
  App --> D
```

## How enrollment actually happens

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant D as Device (out-of-the-box)
  participant ABM as Apple Business Manager
  participant MDM as MDM server
  participant U as User

  D->>ABM: activation at boot, sends serial
  ABM-->>MDM: "this device is managed — handshake"
  MDM->>D: install MDM profile → device is supervised
  MDM->>U: accept portal, sign in with work identity
  MDM->>D: push config profiles (Wi-Fi, VPN, restrictions)
  MDM->>D: approve + push apps, VPP licenses
  Note over MDM,D: no user bypass - supervision locks enrollment, remote wipe always available
```

Key actors:
1. **ABM** — the identity catalog of corporate Apple devices (and volume app licensing). Without ABM, the device belongs to the user, not the company.
2. **MDM server** — Intune/Jamf/Workspace One command the device via Apple's DEP/MDM protocol.
3. **Supervised** — the elevated state Apple gives *corporate-claimed* devices: allows config profiles like "always on VPN", deep restrictions, and App Store-only app installs that cannot be overridden.

## Supervised vs personal devices

| | Personal device (BYOD) | Supervised/ABM corporate |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Belongs to | The user | The company |
| Config depth | Limited (work profile-ish controls) | Full: restrictions, payloads, app lockdown |
| App install | User freedom | Only approved, MDM-approved apps |
| Wipe | User's consent | Full remote wipe at any time |
| Umbrella | light touch | Heavy, always-on controls |

## Why "App Store-only" is the interesting security lever

On a supervised device, the MDM can forbid **everything except the App Store** — killing sideloaded/signed-app attack surface and rogue marketplaces in one boolean. Combined with **managed Apple IDs** (no personal identity dependency), FileVault (macOS full-disk encryption), and hardware-backed [SEP / Secure Enclave](../zero-trust-architecture), the device behaves like a thin corporate client.

## Tester checklist

- Enroll a fresh supervised device via DEP automation and verify the user cannot unpair/enroll-away (supervision holds).
- Confirm a config profile lands (Wi-Fi/VPN) within the check-in window, and that violating restrictions is blocked, not just warned.
- Verify VPP app licensing of a flagged app reaches exactly the assigned devices — license count matches device count.
- Wipe drill: confirm remote wipe/`Find My`-style outstanding claim works against a lost device within policy SLAs.

## Related systems

- [Android in the enterprise](../android-in-the-enterprise) — the counterpart work-profile model
- [Windows in the enterprise](../windows-in-the-enterprise) — same lifecycle under a different management stack
- [Zero trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — supervised + compliant posture gates access
- [Authentication vs authorization](../authentication-vs-authorization) — managed Apple IDs as identity6d:T19fc,
## What this system is

Change enablement is the governance layer that lets an organisation **modify a service without breaking it**. It decides *who may authorise what*, when, and with how much ceremony, then release and deployment management carries the approved change into production. The question every change answers: *what risk do we absorb, and who signs for it?*

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  R[Change request] --> A[Assess - impact + risk]
  A --> C[CAB or pre-authorised]
  C --> W[Dispatch - schedule + backout]
  W --> B[Build + test]
  B --> D[Deploy to production]
  D --> P[Post-implementation review]
```

A change is **not** the same as a release. The change is the *decision and the plan* (what changes, with what risk, on whose authority); the release is the *assembly* (a build that bundles one or more approved changes); the deployment is the *act* of putting that release into a live environment. Governance lives on the change; mechanics live on the release.

## Change types: three paths, three weights

| Type | Authority | Ceremony | Use for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Standard** | Pre-approved (in the catalogue) | None per change — it's already authorised | Password resets, adding a user to a group, routine patching runs |
| **Normal** | Change Advisory Board (CAB) | Assess, approve, schedule, review | Service or infra changes with real blast radius |
| **Emergency** | Emergency CAB (ECAB), or after-the-fact if time-critical | Compressed or retroactive | Fixing a P1 outage, critical security patch, urgent data recovery |
