Standard changes are the design goal: **if a change is routine, you shouldn't need a meeting every time** — pre-authorise once and let it flow. Normal changes are where the risk conversation happens. Emergency changes exist to be broken by design; the discipline is the mandatory post-implementation review, where an emergency that becomes a habit is diagnosed.

## The change lifecycle

1. **Request** — a ticket with description, rationale, owner, and target environment. Vague requests get bounced before the CAB ever sees them.
2. **Assess** — impact and probability, what could break, and — critically — the **backout plan**: how you get back to the previous state.
3. **Authorise** — the CAB (or the pre-authorised standard path) signs off with conditions. The keyword in the minutes is always "approved **with** the named mitigations".
4. **Dispatch** — schedule into a change window with a start, an end, and an **owner of the window**; late overruns are failures.
5. **Build + test** — the release is assembled (via [CI/CD pipelines](../ci-cd-pipelines)) and verified against the plan.
6. **Deploy** — the change leaves the CAB's hands; the deployment team executes with freeze discipline.
7. **Review** — post-implementation review confirms what actually happened, what deviated, and whether the change page learned anything for the next one.

## The CAB: who signs for risk

A Change Advisory Board is **not a rubber stamp and not a bottleneck** — it's the calibrated, delegated decision point. It works best when it's small and relevant: a service owner, the team whose environment is changing, an ops or infra lead, a security or architecture rep when the risk says so, and the change manager as facilitator. Its output is a decision the record can point to: date, attendees, conditions, and the named accountable owner. Emergencies get the ECAB — or documented after-the-fact authorisation when the window cannot wait.

```mermaid
stateDiagram-v2
  [*] --> Request
  Request: Change request
  Request --> Assess: ticket
  Assess: Assess risk + backout
  Assess --> CAB: change record
  CAB: CAB / pre-authorised
  CAB --> Build: approved - build
  CAB --> Deploy: approved - load
  Build: Build + test release
  Build --> Deploy: release ready
  Deploy: Deploy in change window
  Deploy --> Review: verify + learn
  Review: Post-implementation review
  Review --> [*]
```

## The service catalog and service requests

**Service catalog** is the menu of what IT actually sells — the user-visible catalog (email, VPN, CRM) mapped to the technical catalog that delivers it (Exchange, gateway, the CRM app). Every catalog item should state its price in business terms: *availability, capacity, continuity, security*.

**Service requests** (a new laptop, an account unlock, a software install) are the **standard-change** pathway made visible to users — pre-authorised, no CAB, fulfilled against SLAs. The catalog and the request catalogue are the same lever: what you're willing to *promise* and what you're willing to *do quickly without a meeting*.

## SLAs, OLAs, and UCs — making promises real

The service-level stack is the contract ladder between "what we promise" and "who actually has to deliver it":

| Layer | Between | Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **SLA** | Service provider and customer | 99.9% availability; P1 response in 15 minutes |
| **OLA** | Two internal teams | Networking resolves tickets within 4 hours of escalation |
| **UC** | Provider and external supplier | Vendor patches the firewall within 3 business days |

Tracing this ladder is how "the SA promised 99.9%" becomes a real, auditable, assigned obligation — and it's exactly where most SLA brokenness hides: the OLA and UC limbs of the chain were never written down.

## Tester checklist

- Take an approved normal change and confirm the backout plan is concrete: who to call, what command, how long to revert, and a test for "we are back".
- Verify the standard-change catalogue actually covers the routine things people do, and that a standard change never needs a CAB meeting.
- Drill an emergency change: trigger it during the outage, confirm the retro-authorisation got recorded and a post-implementation review is mandatory, not optional.
- Audit change windows: does the deployment record show the window's real start and end, and who was accountable for the overrun?
- Walk an SLA to its UC: pick one public promise and confirm an OLA + UC exist that would actually be breached first.

## Related systems

- [ITIL incident and problem management](../itil-incident-and-problem-management) — where change fixes land after root cause, and where war-room changes originate
- [CI/CD pipelines](../ci-cd-pipelines) — the build/test/release mechanics behind step 5 of the lifecycle
- [DevOps delivery loop](../devops-delivery-loop) — the cadence that wants standard changes automated, not CAB'd
- [Monitoring and observability](../monitoring-and-observability) — the alerting baseline that post-implementation review borrows to verify a change is clean6e:T16eb,
## What this system is

ITIL splits operations into two parallel tracks. **Incident management** restores service after an unplanned interruption — speed is the whole game. **Problem management** attacks the *unknown root cause* behind one or many incidents — permanence is the whole game. Every incident gets restored; only the repeat or unexplained ones earn a problem record and the root-cause work that follows.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  I[Incident - restore now] --> P[Prioritise<br/>impact + urgency]
  P --> R[Resolve + verify]
  R --> C[Close + review]
  P -.->|repeats / unexplained| PR[Problem record]
  PR --> RC[Root cause analysis]
  RC --> KE[Known error + workaround]
  KE --> FX[Fix via change management]
```

The two tracks hand off deliberately: an incident can be closed as "workaround applied" while the problem underneath stays open. That separation is what lets you restore fast *and* stay honest that a real fix is still owed.

## The incident lifecycle

1. **Detect** — a monitoring alarm, a user report, or a ticket from a linked team. Detection latency is the first source of lost time.
2. **Log + categorise** — every incident gets a record; categorisation decides *which team* owns it.
3. **Prioritise** — impact (how many users / services are hurt) × urgency (how fast it hurts) → priority and SLA clock.
4. **Diagnose** — find the firing component and the likely cause; escalate when the diagnosis stalls or the SLA clock runs hot.
5. **Resolve + restore** — apply a fix *or a workaround*; the goal is restoration, not permafix. Verify the service actually recovered.
6. **Close + review** — the incident is closed only when the user confirms and monitoring confirms. A review decides whether a problem record is owed.

## The priority matrix

| Priority | Impact | Urgency | Example | SLA target |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **P1 - Critical** | Whole service down | Immediate | Payments processing fails | Minutes to respond, <4h restore |
| **P2 - High** | Major feature down | Fast | Checkout broken for all users | 15min respond, same-day |
| **P3 - Medium** | Workaround exists | Normal | One region slow, others fine | Hours, ticket tracked |
| **P4 - Low** | Cosmetic | Low | Wrong label in UI | Best effort, queue |

Priority drives where the SLA clock runs and who gets woken. The classic failure is a P3 that nobody re-grades into a P1 when usage spikes — **re-prioritisation is a first-class step**, not an afterthought.

## Escalation: functional and hierarchical

- **Functional escalation** — the record moves to a *different team* that is better equipped (network, database, vendor) because the diagnosis crossed a boundary.
- **Hierarchical escalation** — up the management chain because the SLA is at risk, resources are contested, or the user-facing impact demands an exec to unblock.

The rule: escalate to **enable**, not to comfort. Each step should add a decision-maker or a skilled responder, and the incident record has to travel with the handoff.

## The incident-to-problem pipeline

```mermaid
stateDiagram-v2
  [*] --> DetectionLogging
  DetectionLogging: Detection + logging
  DetectionLogging --> CategorisePrioritise: logged
  CategorisePrioritise: Categorise + prioritise
  CategorisePrioritise --> Escalate: no diagnosis / SLA at risk
  CategorisePrioritise --> DiagnoseResolve: owned + worked
  Escalate: Escalate - SLA at risk
  Escalate --> DiagnoseResolve: extra muscle
  DiagnoseResolve: Diagnose + resolve
  DiagnoseResolve --> RestoreVerify: fix or workaround
  RestoreVerify: Restore + verify
  RestoreVerify --> CloseReview: verified
  RestoreVerify --> ProblemRecord: repeat / unknown cause
  CloseReview: Close + review
  ProblemRecord: Problem record - find root cause
  CloseReview --> [*]
  ProblemRecord --> [*]
```

## Problem management: the cure loop

1. **Problem record** — opened from a major incident, a flood of similar incidents, or a proactive trend scan.
2. **Root cause analysis** — the technique (5 whys, fishbone, log forensics) matters less than actually *reaching* a cause you can point at.
3. **Known error + workaround** — the name ITIL gives a diagnosed cause that still has no fix: document it and drop it in the knowledge base so the next incident resolves in minutes, not hours.
4. **Fix via change** — the actual repair goes through [change and release management](../itil-change-and-release-management) so it lands with the same governance as any other production change.

The output that compounds is the knowledge base: every ticket is a candidate for a runbook entry, and every known error buys the incident desk time later.

## Tester checklist

- Run a P1 drill: alarm → incident → first response inside the SLA target, with the record surviving the handoff.
- Forced a repeat-alarm scenario (same alert twice in a day) and confirm the second incident auto-links to the existing problem record.
- Verify a workaround-closed incident correctly leaves its problem record open and re-flagged at review.
- Re-grade test: change an incident from P3 to P1 and confirm the SLA clock and escalation path actually re-arm.
- Audit escalation: pick a closed major incident and confirm every handoff preserved the timeline and added a named owner.

## Related systems

- [ITIL change and release management](../itil-change-and-release-management) — where problem fixes and incident war-rooms get approved change records
- [Monitoring and observability](../monitoring-and-observability) — the alerting layer that provides most incident detection
- [Security operations center (SOC)](../security-operations-center-soc) — the sibling pipeline for *attacks*, sharing telemetry with the outage side
- [DevOps delivery loop](../devops-delivery-loop) — closing the loop from incident feedback back into the delivery pipeline6f:Tecd,
## What this system is

Jira (and its Atlassian siblings) is the **issue tracker** that records *what must change and why*, from epics down to tasks, and tracks each through a state machine: who owns it, whether it's in progress, whether it's verified. Its superpower is **traceability**: every commit, PR, and test points back at a ticket, so "shipped but forgotten" becomes rare.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  E[Epic: New checkout flow] --> S1[Story 1: guest checkout]
  E --> S2[Story 2: payment validation]
  S1 --> T1[Task: API endpoint]
  S1 --> T2[Task: UI form]
  S1 --> B1[Bug: empty-cart crash]
```

## The issue types (know what each is for)

| Type | Level | When you create one |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Epic** | Container | A big initiative spanning releases (a checkout rewrite) |
| **Story** | Vertical slice | A user-visible outcome with acceptance criteria |
| **Task** | Work item | Engineer work that isn't a user story (CI setup) |
| **Bug** | Defect | Something that should work but doesn't |
| **Sub-task** | Sibling | Breaking a story into trackable pieces |

The trap is inventing issue types every sprint — stick to Epic/Story/Task/Bug and let fields (priority, labels, sprints) carry the nuance.

## The workflow states (a state machine you control)

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  TD[To Do] --> IP[In Progress]
  IP --> CR[In Review]
  CR --> IP
  CR --> RF[Done - code merged]
  RF --> V[Verified on prod]
  V --> C[Closed]
  TD --> CN[Blocked]
  CN --> TD
```

- **In Review** means "reviewer owns it now" — move it when you *send* it, not when it's approved.
- **Done vs Closed**: `Done - code merged` is not `Verified` — a truthful team distinguishes shipped from *confirmed-in-prod*.
- **Blocked** is a first-class state with a visible cause, or it silently rots.

## Scrum vs Kanban boards

| | Scrum | Kanban |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Sprints | Fixed timeboxes (1-2 weeks) | Continuous flow, no timebox |
| Planning | Sprint goal + committed scope | Pull-based, WIP limits |
| Metrics | Velocity, burndown | Cycle time, throughput, WIP |
| Fits | Predictable product iterations | Ops/support streams (tickets, incidents) |

Both are delivery *lenses* over the same issue types — the choice is about flow, not about which tool.

## Definition of Done is the quality contract

A story is Done when its **acceptance criteria pass**, code is merged with review, CI builds and tests green, and it's **deployed and verified**. Anything less is "in progress wearing a disguise." The `verified` state exists precisely to catch stories marked Done that never got a second look in prod.

## Tester checklist

- Take a Story whose acceptance criteria exist and confirm every criterion maps to a test that actually ran (not a "should be ok").
- Follow a Bug end-to-end: reproduce → in progress → in review → verified on the fix branch vs prod.
- Audit stale states: how many issues sit in `Done - code merged` but never reach `Verified`? (that's your leak)
- Confirm the code↔ticket link both ways: the commit references the ticket, and the ticket links the PR/commit.

## Related systems

- [Git and version control](../git-and-version-control) — the commits that reference these tickets
- [CI/CD pipelines](../ci-cd-pipelines) — where "done" moves from code to prod
- [DevOps delivery loop](../devops-delivery-loop) — ticket to production to feedback, closed loop
- [Integration patterns](../integration-patterns) — Jira also integrates via its own APIs and webhooks
- [ITIL incident and problem management](../itil-incident-and-problem-management) — the ops/support stream where tickets, incidents, and problems get governed
- [Software development lifecycle (SDLC)](../software-development-lifecycle) — the phase gates the issue types map onto70:T103c,
## What this system is

**JML** (Joiner / Mover / Leaver) is the operating model for identity: three kinds of life-change events, each with a defined set of access consequences. Joining grants access, moving rescopes it, leaving revokes it. Done manually, these are the source of ghost accounts and admin sprawl; done as an event-driven pipeline, they become invisible and reliable.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  HR["HR system"] -->|join / move / leave| Store["Identity store"]
  Store -->|revoke sessions| SSO["SSO sessions<br/>killed on leave"]
  Store -->|sync accounts| SCIM["SCIM apps<br/>provision + deprovision"]
  Store -->|strip privilege| PIM["PIM privileges<br/>deactivated"]
  PIM -->|every change logged| Audit["Auditable state"]
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  HR[(HR system)] -->|join event| ID[Identity store]
  HR -->|move event| ID
  HR -->|leave event| ID
  ID -->|provision| Apps[Apps - via SCIM]
  ID -->|rescale| Apps
  ID -->|deprovision| Apps
  ID -->|strip privilege| PIM[Privileged access]
  ID -->|suspend + kill sessions| SSO[Every SSO session]
```

## The three phases

| Phase | Trigger | The access move | Failure if skipped |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Joiner** | First day in HR | Accounts created in the right groups, right roles | Manual "set-up" tickets, late access, shadow IDs |
| **Mover** | Role/team change | Old groups removed, new groups added, privilege rescoped | Accumulated stale rights ("they used to…") |
| **Leaver** | Termination date | Sessions killed, accounts suspended/deleted, privilege stripped, license reclaimed | Orphan accounts = the #1 audit finding |

Each is a *state change in HR* that triggers a chain: HR → identity store → [SCIM](../scim-identity-provisioning) provisioning → [SSO](../single-sign-on-sso) session revocation → [PIM](../privileged-identity-management) privilege deactivation.

## The leaver chain, end to end

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant H as HR - term date
  participant I as Identity store
  participant S as SSO / IdP
  participant A as Apps (SCIM)

  H->>I: termination event with exact date/time
  I->>S: disable accounts + revoke sessions
  I->>A: deprovision / suspend via SCIM
  I->>I: PIM grants deactivated for leaver
  Note over A: device access revoked by MDM as a parallel chain
```

Notice the **date on the termination event**: identity should act *at* the boundary, not at "whenever someone remembers."

## Why JML is really an access-governance feature

- **Access reviews**: with JML wired up, reviews become *"does the current state match the events we processed?"* instead of "document everything in a spreadsheet."
- **Orphan detection**: accounts that never got a termination event are the canary — a monitoring query for "active past 90 days + no supervisor" is your safety net.
- **Separation of duties**: a lemma of JML is that moves don't *add* — a promotion shouldn't keep the leaver-tier rights of the old job unless that is a deliberate decision ([RBAC](../rbac-and-authorization)).

## Tester checklist

- Join drill: a new-hire event with a future start date must create accounts, roles, and app memberships exactly at start (not a day early).
- Move drill: change a user's team and confirm old group access *removes*, new *adds* — and the intersection is re-reviewed.
- Leaver drill with a timer: at 17:00 termination, by 17:00:30 all SSO sessions are dead and SCIM accounts suspended.
- Anti-pattern check: is there *any* manual "give this user access right now" path that by-passes the event chain? (Kill it.)

## Related systems

- [Identity and access management](../identity-and-access-management) — the IAM layer JML orchestrates
- [SCIM identity provisioning](../scim-identity-provisioning) — the transport that executes JML events
- [Single sign-on (SSO)](../single-sign-on-sso) — session revocation on leave
- [Privileged identity management](../privileged-identity-management) — deactivating privileged access on exit
- [Role-based access control](../rbac-and-authorization) — the roles JML adds and strips
- [HR systems](../hr-and-talent-systems) — the HR events that trigger the JML chain71:Tf70,
## What this system is

A **JWT** (JSON Web Token) is a signed, self-contained statement like *"this is user 42, role admin, valid until noon"*. Because it is signed, an API can trust the token's claims **without asking anyone** — that statelessness is both the benefit and the danger.

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant C as Client
  participant A as Auth server
  participant API as Protected API - stateless verify

  C->>A: login + MFA
  A-->>C: issue signed token - header.payload.signature
  C->>API: Bearer header with JWT
  API->>API: verify signature + exp only
  API-->>C: response
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  A[header<br/>alg: HS256] --> B[payload<br/>sub: 42, role: admin, exp: ...]
  B --> C["signature<br/>HMAC(header.payload, secret)"]
  C --> D[token: header.payload.signature]
```

## The three parts

| Part | Contents | Purpose |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Header | `alg`, `typ` | Says which algorithm signed it |
| Payload | claims: `sub`, `exp`, `iss`, `aud`, `role`, `scope` | The identity + permissions |
| Signature | digest over `header.payload` with the secret | Integrity + authenticity |

The whole token is `base64url(header) + "." + base64url(payload) + "." + base64url(signature)` — remember: **payload is readable by anyone** (it's only encoded, not encrypted). Never put secrets in a JWT.

## How verification (should) work

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant C as Client
  participant API as API
  participant IDP as Identity Provider

  C->>IDP: login
  IDP-->>C: access_token (JWT)
  C->>API: GET /me  Authorization: Bearer <token>
  API->>API: 1. split token, 2. verify signature w/ secret,<br/>3. check exp + aud, 4. read claims
  API-->>C: 200 (no DB lookup needed)
```

The API proves the token was minted by the IDP by checking the signature with a **shared secret** (HMAC) or the IDP's **public key** (RSA/EC). Once it trusts the signature, it trusts the claims — unless it needs to check revocation.

## The stateless-vs-stateful trade-off

| | Stateless JWT | Session (stateful) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Where identity lives | Inside the token | In the server's session store |
| Server lookup on each request | None (fast, scalable) | Yes (Redis / DB hit) |
| Logout / revoke | Hard — must wait for `exp` or keep a blocklist | Instant — delete the session |
| Info exposure | Payload readable if token leaks | Nothing in the token |

If you need instant revocation (logout, account ban), you'll add a **token blocklist/denylist** or keep short-lived tokens with refresh tokens (see OAuth/OIDC guide).

## The attacks that keep failing teams

- **`alg: none`** — never accept unsigned tokens; pin the algorithm server-side.
- **Algorithm confusion** — `RS256`-signed tokens replayed as `HS256` will "verify" *only* if the API wrongly uses the public key as the HMAC secret.
- **Weak secret brute force** — a guessable HMAC secret is a signed-anything pass.
- **Stale `exp`** — check expiry, always, on every endpoint.
- **`aud`/`iss` not checked** — tokens minted for another app or issued by a different IDP are accepted.
- **Token in a URL query string** — leaks into logs and browser history; keep tokens in memory / short-lived, never in `localStorage` unless fully understood.

## Tester checklist

- Try the `alg=none` variant and a tampered payload with the same signature — both must be rejected.
- Verify that `exp`, `aud`, and `iss` checks actually fire (craft tokens with bad values).
- Confirm tokens are never logged, not even in API gateway request logs.
- Check the refresh-token rotation: a used refresh token must not be reusable.

## Related systems

- [OAuth 2.0 and OpenID Connect](../oauth2-openid-connect) — where JWTs are minted and delivered
- [Authentication vs authorization](../authentication-vs-authorization) — verifying `who` is only the first question
- [Session cookies and CSRF](../session-cookies-and-csrf) — the stateful alternative72:Td1d,
## What this system is

Linux powers nearly everything a corporation runs that isn't on a desktop — API servers, databases, CI runners, network appliances, cloud instances. "Managing Linux" at scale isn't SSHing into hosts; it's making hosts **interchangeable** so a crashed one disappears and a fresh one looks identical.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  G[Golden image / IaC] --> C[Configuration as code<br/>Ansible/Terraform/Puppet]
  C --> H1[Server 01]
  C --> H2[Server 02]
  C --> H3[Server 03]
  H1 --> P[Patch pipeline]
  H2 --> P
  H3 --> P
  P --> I[(Monitoring / inventory)]
```

## The two mental models

| Old way (pets) | New way (cattle / immutable) |
| --- | --- |
| SSH in, fix it live | Never patch a live server; build a new image |
| Drift over months | Config is code, auditable in git, reproduced everywhere |
| Hand-written playbooks | Idempotent, versioned, tested in CI |
| Servers have names & moods | Servers have roles and are disposable |

Most enterprises run a mix: stateful databases still demand care, stateless tiers are freely rotated. The rule that holds everywhere: **the source of truth is code, not a shell session.**

## The pieces that matter

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  subgraph Build["Build"]
    A[Distro + minimal base] --> B[Golden image<br/>packer/containers]
  end
  subgraph Run["Run"]
    C[systemd: service units, restarts, journald logs]
    D[Config split: secrets from vault, not in files]
    E[Hardening: CIS baseline, selinux/apparmor, minimal packages]
  end
  subgraph Keep["Keep current"]
    F[Patch rings: canary → broad → prod]
    G[Unattended security updates by policy, reboot coordination]
  end
  B --> C
  C --> D
  D --> E
  E --> F
  F --> G
```

## The patch cycle (the drumbeat that never stops)

- Patch **rings** mirror Windows Autopatch: a canary host gets the update first; only on success does it roll to broad. A bad kernel in one ring doesn't take down production.
- **Secrets** never ship in images or repos — pulled at boot from a vault/secret manager ([identity best practice](../authentication-vs-authorization)) with short-lived credentials.
- **Reboots** are planned, drained, and verified — orchestrated, never chaotic.
- Vulnerability triage is a *risk* calculation, not an all-or-nothing: you patch what's reachable first.

## Tester checklist

- Rebuild a host from the golden image and diff it against peers — drift should be ~zero or a documented exception.
- Kill a stateless node mid-traffic and confirm orchestration reconstitutes it without user-visible impact.
- Roll a deliberately bad package to the canary ring and verify the ring's monitors catch it *before* broad rollout.
- Verify a fresh host cannot boot with secrets available in its filesystem (secrets come from the vault only).

## Related systems

- [Windows in the enterprise](../windows-in-the-enterprise) — same fleet principles, different OS
- [Zero trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — hosts are identities with short-lived credentials
- [Authentication vs authorization](../authentication-vs-authorization) — every service gets a service identity
- [API request lifecycle](../api-request-lifecycle) — what the servers are actually serving
- [Dockerizing applications](../dockerizing-applications) — the container runtime that turns a host into a service platform73:T1534,
## What this system is

A single LLM call is a function: prompt in, text out. An **AI workflow** is a system that decides *which* call to make next. The deciding force is usually the model itself — it outputs a structured **tool call**, your code executes the tool, feeds the result back, and repeats until the model emits a final answer. That loop is the agent pattern. The alternative is a **workflow DAG** where you, not the model, decide the order of steps. Almost every production system is a mix: hard-coded structure where steps are predictable, agent loop where the path is not.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  O[Model] --> S{Needs a tool?}
  S -->|yes - emit tool call| A[Execute tool in your code]
  A --> R[Append result as observation]
  R --> O
  S -->|no - final answer| Out[Answer to user]
```

## Agent loop vs workflow DAG

| | Workflow (DAG) | Agent (open loop) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Who picks the next step | You, at design time | The model, at runtime |
| Predictability | High — fixed pipeline | Low — model can go anywhere |
| Debuggability | Deterministic, replayable | Needs session logging + eval |
| Cost | Bounded, visible | Unbounded unless capped |
| Fits | Classify → extract → answer | Explore, plan, and use many tools |
| Failure mode | Rigid — breaks on unseen inputs | Drift — hallucinated or repeated tool calls |

The rule of thumb: **fix the loop only as much as the problem needs**. A workflow for jobs with a known shape; an agent when the shape is unknown until the model sees the input. Teams that default to "full agent" for everything inherit unbounded cost and unreplayable bugs.

## Tools: the model's arms

Tool calling is JSON in, JSON out: the model returns `{ "tool": "search_docs", "args": {...} }`, your code validates the arguments against the actual function's schema, executes **your** trusted code, and appends the result. Three design rules matter more than prompt wording:

- **Schemas do the work** — the function signature is the contract; a stricter schema means fewer invalid calls.
- **Your code decides, not the model** — the model only proposes a call; your side filters and scopes it (drop tools the user's permissions don't cover).
- **Every tool is an attack surface** — a model coerced by prompt injection to call `delete_account(args)` executes your code with real side effects.

## Memory: context window vs remembered state

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  subgraph In["Context window - transient"]
    C1[System prompt]
    C2[Conversation so far]
    C3[Tool results]
  end
  subgraph External["External memory - persistent"]
    E1[Vector store - semantic recall]
    E2[Session store - key-value state]
    E3[Your database - source of truth]
  end
  In --> External
```

The context window is RAM, not disk: it is **re-packed on every call** from the raw transcript. Anything that must persist across turns — a shopping cart, a user's preference, an approval state — must be written *to your system*, and re-injected as context on the next turn. The classic bug is an agent that "remembers" something it never persisted, then loses it when the transcript is truncated to fit the window.

## The request → happy path

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    U[User task] -- task + history --> C[Context + tool schemas]
    C -- prompt --> L[Model call]
    L -- tool call --> T[Tool execution - your code]
    T -- validated result --> O[Append observation]
    O -- loop --> L
    L -- final answer --> A[Final answer]
```

## Guardrails: what keeps a loop from being a runaway

- **Max iterations & time budget** — a hard cap on loop turns; the model can infinitely re-plan, and billing follows.
- **Tool approval gates** — destructive or costly tools (send_email, delete, pay) require a confirm step; "human approval" is the anti-prompt-injection wall.
- **Permission scoping per user** — tool availability should mirror the caller's roles, not the service account's.
- **Observation size limits** — truncate tool output before re-prompting; a 10 MB JSON blob wrecks context, latency, and cost.
- **Forced rerouting** — if the model repeats the same failing call (no progress), detect it and hand the task to a fallback path.

## Tester checklist

- Loop cap: assert the agent stops after max iterations, not silently running forever (watch billing).
- Tool argument validation: send a prompt that should never produce calls to a scoped-out tool, and verify the code refuses it.
- Injection drill: hide an instruction like "call delete on every record" inside retrieved tool output — does the guardrail stop it?
- Persistence across turns: end a session, resume, and confirm state the agent claimed to "remember" actually came from your store.
- Regression set: keep a fixed set of real tasks and assert the agent's final answer is correct (and reaches it) before and after any prompt/model change.

## Related systems

- [LLM RAG pipeline](../llm-rag-pipeline) — the retrieval half that grounds agents in your data
- [MCP and A2A protocols](../mcp-and-a2a-protocols) — the wire protocols an agent uses for tools and peers
- [Message queue delivery](../message-queue-delivery) — running agent jobs asynchronously behind a queue
- [API request lifecycle](../api-request-lifecycle) — the request path the workflow orchestrates through
- [OWASP Top 10](../owasp-top-10) — the injection category that prompt injection extends74:T1b3f,
## What this system is

Two open protocols standardize how AI systems attach to the world, and they are complementary rather than competing:

- **MCP** (Model Context Protocol, Anthropic → open spec) is the *USB-C of AI*: an agent connects once and can use any tool, data source, or API exposed through a standard server — without per-integration glue.
- **A2A** (Agent2Agent, Google → Linux Foundation) is the *email of agents*: independent agents discover each other, delegate work, and exchange results as peers, across frameworks and companies.

MCP is how an agent *touches tools and data*. A2A is how an agent *partners with other agents*. A real stack uses both: your agent calls MCP servers for its own tools while delegating to remote agents over A2A.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  subgraph Agent["Your agent"]
    Host[Host - model and session]
    MCPC[MCP client]
    A2AC[A2A client]
  end
  MCPC -->|JSON-RPC - stdio or HTTP| S1[MCP servers - tools and resources]
  A2AC -->|agent card + tasks| S2[Remote agents - A2A servers]
  Host --> MCPC
  Host --> A2AC
```

## MCP: agent-to-tool

Three roles: the **host** (your AI application — it owns the model and the conversation), **clients** (one per server connection), and **servers** (each exposes **tools** for actions, **resources** for data, and **prompts** for reusable templates). The data layer is **JSON-RPC 2.0**; the transport is **stdio** for local processes or **streamable HTTP** (Server-Sent Events for streaming) for remote servers, authenticated with OAuth.

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant H as Host
  participant C as MCP client
  participant S as MCP server
  H->>C: start session
  C->>S: initialize with capabilities
  S-->>C: supported capabilities
  C->>S: tools/list
  S-->>C: tool schemas
  C->>S: tools/call
  S-->>C: result
  C-->>H: context for the model
```

Security is built into the shape: the **host is the enforcement point**. Capability negotiation decides what each side supports; servers are isolated from each other and cannot read the full conversation. This is also where the well-known failure modes live — every tool is an attack surface, so the host must gate calls with consent and permission checks, not the model.

## A2A: agent-to-agent

A2A treats agents as opaque peers. Four concepts do the work:

| Concept | What it is |
| --- | --- |
| **Agent Card** | A JSON discovery document at `/.well-known/agent-card.json`: identity, description, skills, endpoint, and auth requirements |
| **Task** | A stateful unit of work: `submitted → working → input-required → completed / failed / canceled` |
| **Message** | One turn of a conversation, carrying **parts** (text, file, or structured data) |
| **Artifact** | A deliverable produced by a task, also composed of parts, streamable incrementally |

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant A as A2A client agent
  participant R as A2A remote agent
  A->>R: GET /.well-known/agent-card.json
  R-->>A: capabilities and endpoint
  A->>R: send message with task
  R-->>A: task submitted
  R-->>A: working with streaming updates
  R-->>A: artifact updates
  R-->>A: task completed
```

`input-required` is the mechanism for a remote agent to ask for clarification mid-task, which is what makes long-running delegation interactive. Tasks can be polled, subscribed to over SSE, or pushed to a webhook when they outlive a connection. The primary binding is **JSON-RPC 2.0 over HTTPS**, with gRPC and REST as alternates — deliberately boring plumbing.

## The two layers, compared

| | MCP | A2A |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Relationship | Client-server: an agent *uses* a server | Peer: agents *delegate* to agents |
| Unit of work | Tool call / resource read | Task with a lifecycle |
| Discovery | Capability negotiation during initialize | Agent Card at a well-known URL |
| Transport | stdio, streamable HTTP | JSON-RPC over HTTPS, gRPC, SSE, webhooks |
| Authentication | OAuth for remote HTTP | OAuth 2.0 |
| Data exchanged | Tool arguments and structured results | Messages and artifacts with typed parts |
| Trust | Servers stay isolated from the conversation | Remote agents are untrusted peers |

If MCP is "how an agent uses a capability", A2A is "how agents partner on a task". An MCP server is reachable *inside* an agent's world; an A2A remote lives outside it, behind an agent card.

## How they compose

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    U[User task] -- task --> AG[Your agent - host + model]
    AG -- context --> MC[MCP client]
    MC -- tool calls --> MS[MCP servers - tools and data]
    AG -- delegate --> AC[A2A client]
    AC -- task + artifacts --> RS[Remote agent - card + tasks]
```

The rule of thumb: **expose capabilities as MCP servers, front agent pairs with A2A cards.** A support agent might use MCP against the ticket system internally, then delegate an order-status lookup over A2A to a billing agent on the other side of a company boundary.

## Cross-boundary security notes

- **MCP**: the host gates every tool call — a compromised or prompt-injected server must not reach tools the user didn't consent to. Verify tool schemas before they influence execution.
- **A2A**: remote agents are *untrusted peers*. Validate the agent card, authenticate with OAuth, and route delegated work — especially anything that cascades back into your MCP tools — through the user's permission scope, not a broad service account.
- **Supply chain**: capability metadata (tool definitions, agent cards) is attacker-influenceable; treat changes as config and review them.

## Tester checklist

- **MCP flow**: stand up a mock server and verify `initialize` → `tools/list` → `tools/call`, including streaming over streamable HTTP.
- **Isolation**: confirm one MCP server cannot read another server's context or the full conversation.
- **A2A lifecycle**: exercise `submitted → working → completed` plus `failed` and `canceled`, and confirm terminal states end any stream.
- **Resilience**: kill a task mid-run and reconnect — `get`/subscription must recover state without losing artifacts.
- **input-required**: a remote agent asking for clarification mid-task must surface to the user and resume the same task.
- **Auth**: reject calls to remote MCP/HTTP endpoints and A2A remotes without valid credentials.
- **Cross-layer scope**: a task delegated over A2A must not let the remote agent invoke MCP tools beyond the requesting user's permissions.

## Related systems

- [LLM agents and workflows](../llm-agents-and-workflows) — the agent loops these protocols wire up
- [LLM RAG pipeline](../llm-rag-pipeline) — a retrieval engine commonly exposed as an MCP resource
- [OAuth 2.0 and OpenID Connect](../oauth2-openid-connect) — the auth layer both protocols lean on
- [Workload identity and mTLS](../workload-identity-and-mtls) — machine-to-machine trust for remote servers
- [API request lifecycle](../api-request-lifecycle) — the JSON-RPC/HTTP machinery underneath75:Tb1b,
## What this system is

When an API must not process work in the request-response cycle (sending emails, resizing images, running AI jobs), it writes a **message** to a queue and returns immediately. A **worker** later picks the message up. The price of decoupling is a new failure mode: messages are delivered at least once, so **your handler must be idempotent**.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  API[API request] -->|persist first| DB[Database<br/>write + idempotency key]
  API -->|enqueue| Q[Message queue]
  Q -->|at-least-once| W[Worker<br/>consumer group]
  W -->|idempotent handler| DONE[Side effect<br/>dedupe → ack]
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  A[API writes to DB] --> B[(Database)]
  A -->|enqueue| Q[(Message Queue)]
  Q -->|consume| W[Worker]
  W -->|process + side effects| S[(Email / Image / AI job)]
  W -->|ack| Q
  B --> A
```

## Why “at least once”

Most brokers (SQS, RabbitMQ, Kafka) guarantee at-least-once, not exactly-once:

1. Worker reads message and starts processing a side effect (email sent).
2. Worker crashes before sending the **ack**.
3. Broker sees no ack, assumes failure, and redelivers — or the consumer group rebalances.
4. Same message is processed again → the email is sent twice.

```mermaid
flowchart TD
  M1[Message published] --> W1[Worker A reads]
  W1 -->|side effect: email sent| W1C[Worker A crashes before ACK]
  W1C --> Q2[Broker redelivers]
  Q2 --> W2[Worker B reads again]
  W2 -->|processes same id| SEND[Duplicate side effect!]
```

## How to stay safe

| Technique | What it does |
| --- | --- |
| **Idempotency key** | A stable header like `order-42`; the handler stores processed keys and skips repeats |
| **Outbox pattern** | API writes the business row *and* the message in the same DB transaction; a relay publishes rows from the outbox table — no dual-write race |
| **Dead letter queue (DLQ)** | Messages that fail after N retries move aside for inspection instead of looping forever |

## Tester checklist

- Replay a message manually — the second run must be a no-op.
- Kill a worker mid-handling and confirm redelivery + no partial side effects.
- Verify the DLQ buries poison messages instead of blocking the queue.
- Check ordering guarantees: are you relying on exactly-once ordering you were never promised?

## Related systems

- [API request lifecycle](../api-request-lifecycle) — the request that *writes* the message
- [LLM RAG pipeline](../llm-rag-pipeline) — AI jobs routed through queues for async inference
- [Pub/Sub and event streaming](../pub-sub-event-streaming) — the fan-out sibling of competing-consumer queues
- [Integration patterns](../integration-patterns) — where messaging fits among the five integration families
- [Webhook integrations](../webhook-integrations) — receivers that deliver to an HTTP callback instead of a queue76:Tdaa,
## What this system is

One large frontend, one team, one big release. **Micro-frontends** split the UI into independently owned apps (checkout, catalog, accounts) composed into a single page at **runtime** — each with its own repo, deploy, and release cadence. The benefit is organizational (autonomy), not technical: the compounding cost is versioning, shared state, and cross-app consistency.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Shell[App shell<br/>navigation + layout] --> C[Remote: checkout module]
  Shell --> Cat[Remote: catalog module]
  Shell --> A[Remote: accounts module]
  C --> R[Each team ships independently]
  Cat --> R
  A --> R
```

## Build-time vs runtime composition

| | Build-time composition | Runtime (module federation) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| When | Each app is npm-built into the shell | Remote apps are fetched at runtime (shared runtime) |
| Version skew | One version, one release | Each remote flows on its own release |
| Failure blast | A bad dep blocks the whole build | A broken remote can degrade a region of the page |
| Rollback | One atomic deploy | Per-remote rollback, coordination required |
| Best for | Team boundaries with shared release | Large orgs with genuinely independent teams |

Module federation (Webpack/Rspack, Vite plugins) is the current runtime tooling: the shell `remote()`-imports modules and **shares** libraries (React, router) so duplicates don't balloon the bundle.

## How a page is composed

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Shell[App shell] -->|bootstrap| Nav[Navigation + layout]
  Nav -->|lazy mount| Checkout[Checkout remote module]
  Nav -->|lazy mount| Catalog[Catalog remote module]
  Checkout -->|its own release| Page[Composed page]
  Catalog -->|its own release| Page
```

## The three agreements that keep it from collapsing

1. **Shared runtime contract**: which React/router versions, how remotes are exposed, and how events/navigation are typed — agreed once, enforced by the shell.
2. **State ownership**: no global store written by many remotes. Each module owns its state; cross-module data goes through the shell's event bus or an API, never shared mutable objects.
3. **Design tokens, not ad-hoc CSS**: styles flow from a shared token/CDN source so visual drift against each other stays low without a monorepo.

## When it's right (and when it isn't)

- **Right**: >3 teams on one product, genuine release independence needed, a real shared shell is maintained.
- **Wrong**: a two-person team "for flexibility" — you pay federating cost with no autonomy win. If the smoke-test becomes *"did all remotes load today?",* reconsider; you turned a feature app into a micro-SPOf.

## Tester checklist

- Load every route with one remote deliberately down — the page must fail graceful (that module degrades), not produce a blank shell.
- Verify version-mix works: a new checkout-release alongside an old catalog-release on the same shell.
- Measure bundle share: confirm shared modules (React/core) are fetched once, not duplicated per remote.
- Cross-module navigation must hold state and deep-links across remote boundaries.

## Related systems

- [Frontend frameworks and rendering](../frontend-frameworks-and-rendering) — the frameworks the shell and remotes are built with
- [CI/CD pipelines](../ci-cd-pipelines) — each remote ships through its own pipeline
- [Cloud networking and hybrid connectivity](../cloud-networking-and-hybrid-connectivity) — routing/versioning problems that grow with every added frontier77:T2900,
## What this system is

Microsoft Entra ID (formerly Azure Active Directory) is Microsoft's **cloud identity provider**: the directory of users, devices, and apps behind Microsoft 365, Azure, and thousands of SaaS apps. It is the identity backbone of most modern Windows/Office estates, and the mental model that unlocks it is **tenant**: every organization gets an isolated tenant (a directory of identities + the policies that govern them), and everything else — apps, SaaS, devices, external collaborators — hangs off that one boundary. "Entra ID architecture" is mostly the *art of deciding what a tenant is* and how identities flow across trust boundaries.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  subgraph Tenant["Your tenant (the trust boundary)"]
    U[Internal users]
    D[Devices - enrolled]
    A1[Apps - OIDC / SAML / WS-Fed]
    SAAS[Connected SaaS - 2000+ gallery apps]
    POL[Conditional Access + policies]
    RB[RBAC - roles and scopes]
  end
  subgraph External["External identities"]
    B2B[Guest users - B2B]
    B2C[Consumer identities - B2C tenant]
    FED[Federated orgs - cross-tenant]
  end
  External --> Tenant
```

## The core objects that make the directory

| Object | What it is | The failure if ignored |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Tenant** | One isolated directory (a `*.onmicrosoft.com` domain), the security boundary | Two orgs merged into one tenant = one boundary confusion |
| **User** | Directory principal with identity + attributes + entitlements | Guest/vendor users dumped into the same object model as employees |
| **Group** | Member container used for assignment | Assigning apps directly to users instead of groups = unmanageable sprawl |
| **Service principal / enterprise app** | The *instance* of an app in your tenant (the "who" that gets permissions) | Confusing the app registration (the definition) with the principal (the grants) |
| **Device identity** | Enrolled devices with their own identity for [zero trust](../zero-trust-architecture) | Devices trusted by account only — posture checks have nothing to attach to |
| **App registration** | The app's metadata: client ID, redirects, API scopes | Client secrets committed to repos; wrong redirect URIs |

The three that trip everyone: **tenant** (the boundary), **app registration vs service principal** (definition vs instance), and **group-based assignment** (the only thing that scales). Get those three right and most Entra architecture is sound.

## The identity lifecycle: join, access, leave

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant HR as HR / source of truth
  participant E as Entra ID
  participant Apps as Apps + SaaS
  participant P as Conditional Access

  HR->>E: provision user (joiner)
  E->>Apps: assign via group + provision (SCIM)
  E->>P: device enrolls, posture established
  P->>Apps: grant sign-in when compliant
  HR->>E: offboard (leaver)
  E->>E: disable, revoke sessions, block sign-in
  E->>Apps: deprovision access (SCIM)
```

Users are born from an HR/source-of-truth, assigned to groups, provisioned into SaaS via [SCIM](../scim-identity-provisioning), and on leaving are disabled, session-revoked, and deprovisioned. The loop is the same as [joiner-mover-leaver](../joiner-mover-leaver) everywhere; Entra's twist is that **Conditional Access evaluates posture at sign-in**, so the directory is also an enforcement point, not just a list.

## External identities: B2B, B2C, and cross-tenant

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph Ext["External identity - in YOUR tenant"]
    G[Guest user<br/>- has an account elsewhere]
    GC[Guest account -<br/>a shadow object in your directory]
  end
  G -->|invited / redempt| GC
  GC -->|sign-in via their own IdP| Apps[Your apps]
  B2C[Consumer tenant<br/>- separate directory for public users] --> C[Customer-facing apps]
```

- **B2B collaboration** is for *business collaborators*: you invite an external user (who keeps their home identity) into your tenant as a **guest**. Entra stores a shadow guest object, but sign-in happens at the guest's own IdP — you never manage their password. Guests then get app access like anyone, scoped through Conditional Access. This is the model for vendors, partners, and contractors.
- **B2C** (Azure AD B2C) is the opposite: a *separate consumer tenant* where you create and own end-user identities for your public/customer-facing apps (social sign-in, self-service registration). It is **not** the same tenant as your employees — that split is deliberate and mandatory for governance.
- **Cross-tenant access** is when two orgs federate or trust each other's tenants (M365 shared channels, guest sync via [SCIM](../scim-identity-provisioning)); Entra now manages this with cross-tenant access settings and an external-collaboration policy that whitelists/blacklists which tenants can be invited.

The B2B vs B2C decision in one line: **B2B = "your vendor can see our SharePoint," B2C = "our customers have accounts in our app."** Mixing consumer and employee identity in one directory is the classic governance disaster — which is exactly why B2C is its own tenant type.

## Tenant topology: the decision that defines the architecture

```mermaid
flowchart TD
  Q[One tenant or many?] --> C{Regulatory / legal separation required?}
  C -->|yes - hard isolation| M[Multiple tenants with<br/>cross-tenant rules]
  C -->|no| S{Single operational unit?}
  S -->|yes| ONE[Single tenant - default, simplest]
  S -->|no - brands / subsidiaries| SEG[Segmented tenants -<br/>cost of cross-tenant sharing]
```

| Decision | Choose when | The price |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Single tenant** | One org, one governance model, default for most | Everything shares one boundary; guest bloat if partners pile in |
| **Multiple tenants** | Regulatory isolation, subsidiaries, B2C vs employee | Cross-tenant sharing (guests, channels, sync) is real ongoing cost |
| **B2C as its own tenant** | Any consumer-facing app | Must consciously keep employee and consumer identity apart |

The architecture mistake to avoid: **letting tenant topology grow by accident.** Every "just invite them into our tenant" adds a guest; every "can we sync their staff?" adds a cross-tenant rule; before long the boundary is ambiguous. Decide the topology deliberately, apply an external-collaboration policy, and treat tenant boundaries as the [defense-in-depth](../defense-in-depth) perimeter they are.

## Conditional Access: the enforcement engine

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Sign[Sign-in attempt] --> CA{Every policy evaluated}
  CA -->|block / require MFA / require compliant device| P[Allowed only if posture + context OK]
  CA -->|session controls| SS[Session - app session / sign-in frequency]
```

Conditional Access is where Entra becomes zero-trust enforcement: *every* sign-in is evaluated against policies that combine who (user/group), what (app), where (location), and *device posture*. The signature policies: **require MFA for admins**, **require a compliant device**, **block legacy authentication**, **location-based risk**, and **sign-in frequency/session controls**. It is the mechanism that makes "trusted device + MFA + right location" a requirement instead of a wish, and it's why [endpoint security](../endpoint-security-architecture) and Entra are one system, not two.

## The failure modes an architect owns

- **Tenant sprawl**: a tenant per pilot, per team, per "we might need it" — cross-tenant sharing becomes ungovernable.
- **Mixing B2B, B2C, and employees**: guests and customers and staff in one directory with one policy surface — the governance disaster B2C's separate tenant exists to prevent.
- **Assigning apps directly to users**: no groups, so every app is a user-by-user list; onboarding/offboarding means touching hundreds of rows.
- **No Conditional Access**: sign-in works from any device, any location, no MFA-for-admins — the estate runs on trust and passwords.
- **Legacy auth left on**: protocols that bypass MFA (Basic auth, IMAP/SMTP) still enabled, silently defeating every policy.
- **App registration/principal confusion**: a developer's app registered with broad delegated permissions that outlive its purpose; scopes never trimmed.
- **Guest bloat**: external collaborators invited into the employee tenant with no lifecycle policy, no expiry, no review.

## Tester checklist

- **Guest lifecycle**: invite an external user, confirm their sign-in happens at their own IdP, that they can reach only what's assigned, and that removing them from the group immediately blocks access.
- **B2B vs B2C boundary**: confirm consumer identities live in a separate B2C tenant and can never resolve into the employee directory.
- **Conditional Access realness**: craft a sign-in from a disallowed location/device and confirm the policy *blocks or requires MFA*, not merely logs.
- **MFA for admins**: a global-admin sign-in without MFA must be impossible in test — the policy applies even from a "trusted" device.
- **SCIM provisioning**: change a user's group membership and confirm the downstream SaaS app's entitlements sync within the SLA; on offboarding, deprovisioning lands.
- **Tenant topology audit**: list all tenants, guests, and cross-tenant rules and confirm each is deliberate and covered by policy — no accidental boundaries.
- **App principal hygiene**: enumerate service principal permissions and confirm none exceed their current purpose (least privilege, scopes trimmed).

## Related systems

- [Identity and access management (IAM)](../identity-and-access-management) — the discipline Entra ID implements for Microsoft estates
- [Single sign-on (SSO)](../single-sign-on-sso) — the OIDC/SAML mechanics Entra brokers for apps
- [OAuth 2.0 and OpenID Connect](../oauth2-openid-connect) — the token flow Entra's app registrations and API scopes use
- [Conditional access and policy engines](../conditional-access-and-policy-engines) — the policy engine Entra runs at sign-in
- [Zero trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — the model where device posture gates access
- [SCIM identity provisioning](../scim-identity-provisioning) — how users sync out to SaaS apps
- [Endpoint security architecture](../endpoint-security-architecture) — the device compliance Entra's Conditional Access reads
- [Privileged identity management](../privileged-identity-management) — the privileged-access arm of the same directory
- [Microsoft Graph API](../microsoft-graph-api) — the programmatic interface to everything in the tenant78:T16fe,
## What this system is

Microsoft Graph is the **single API for Microsoft 365**: instead of a separate API for Exchange, OneDrive, Teams, SharePoint, Entra ID, and Intune, everything hangs off one service rooted at `https://graph.microsoft.com`. Company data — users, mail, files, chats, devices, groups — is a graph of **entities** with **relationships** you traverse with REST verbs (or GraphQL-style select lists).

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  App[Your app<br/>Graph SDK] -->|get token| Auth[OAuth 2.0 / MSAL<br/>delegated or app-only]
  Auth -->|Bearer token| G[Microsoft Graph<br/>graph.microsoft.com]
  G -->|delegated| Users[Users / Groups<br/>Entra ID]
  G -->|delegated| Mail[Mail / Calendar<br/>Exchange Online]
  G -->|app-only| Files[OneDrive / SharePoint]
  G -->|app-only| Teams[Teams / Chats]
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  App[Your app] -->|Authorization header: Bearer token| G[Microsoft Graph]
  G --> U[Users, Groups]
  G --> M[Mail, Calendar]
  G --> D[Drive items]
  G --> T[Teams, Chats]
  G --> K[Devices - Intune]
```

## The model: entities and relationships

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  U[user] -->|memberOf| G1[group - member/owner]
  U -->|manager| U2[user]
  U -->|messages| M1[message]
  U -->|drive| D1[drive]
  G1 -->|has| D2[drive - SharePoint site]
  G1 -->|joinable via| T1[team]

```

The Graph is a **true graph**: navigate relationships in either direction (`GET /me`, `GET /me/directReports`, `GET /groups/{id}/members`). Key entry points:

| Resource | How you reach it |
| --- | --- |
| Me / users | `GET /me`, `GET /users/{id}`, `GET /users?$filter=...` |
| Groups | `GET /groups`, `GET /groups/{id}/members` |
| Mail | `GET /me/messages?$top=10`, `POST /me/sendMail` |
| Calendar | `GET /me/events`, booking resources |
| Drives/files | `GET /me/drive/root/children`, SharePoint sites & lists |
| Teams/chats | `GET /me/chats`, `POST /chats/{id}/messages` |
| Devices (Intune) | `GET /deviceManagement/managedDevices` |

The `$select`, `$filter`, `$expand`, `$top`, `$orderby`, and `$skip` **OData query options** are the whole game: they control what comes back, so you fetch exactly what you need instead of dumping a whole entity.

## Delegated vs application (app-only) auth

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  T[Which token?] --> Q{Signed-in user involved?}
  Q -->|yes - act on the user's behalf| D[Delegated]
  Q -->|no - background service| A[Application-only]
  D --> D1[Scopes: Mail.Read, Files.ReadWrite<br/>user consented, acts as the user]
  A --> A1[Roles: app with granted permissions<br/>tenant-admin consent, acts as the app]
```

- **Delegated**: the app acts *as* a signed-in user, with **scopes** (like `Mail.Read`) that the user (or tenant admin) consents to. The token represents user + app.
- **Application-only**: no user — the app has tenant-level **roles** (e.g. admin-granted `Mail.Read` for the whole org). This is the dangerous one: an app-only token reaches *everything* its app-level permissions allow, no individual user in the loop.

The recurring incident: an app registered with **Application permissions** when it only needed **Delegated** — one bad consent and a background job can read every mailbox. Least privilege means choosing the *narrowest* model that does the job.

## The API mechanics: tokens, throttling, pagination

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  A[Request] --> T{Limit hit?}
  T -->|yes, 429 + Retry-After| R[Backoff + retry]
  T -->|no| P{Nothing more?}
  P -->|partial| C[Follow @odata.nextLink]
  P -->|complete| D[Done - respect Retry-After]
```

- **Auth**: always a **Bearer token from MSAL/OAuth 2.0** — never the user's password. Tokens are short-lived; refresh before expiry.
- **Pagination**: Graph returns **`@odata.nextLink`** — follow it for the next page. Don't re-run the same query; don't assume a single response is the whole set.
- **Throttling**: Graph has rate limits (hit `429` with a `Retry-After`). The contract: **fetch less, cache more, and honor backoff** — a hammering batch app gets slowed by design, and retrying too fast makes it worse.
- **Batch**: `$batch` lets you combine up to 20 requests into one — the answered prayer for chatty apps that would otherwise pad a round-trip per call.

## The mistakes repeatedly remembered

- Using app-only permissions for a workflow that acts on a single user's behalf — every mailbox becomes readable.
- Ignoring pagination and paginating *by hand* — a 10k-row migration that assumed one response is a classic.
- No throttling handling: re-firing instantly on `429` triggers the very backoff the API is enforcing.
- Storing a user's password to "log in and sync mail" instead of using delegated auth properly.
- Treating `GET /me` as "any user": with app-only auth, `/me` doesn't exist as a personal context — you must target `/users/{id}`.

## Tester checklist

- Confirm delegated vs app-only: a background job must *not* have scopes it can't use, and an app-only token must not be able to read `GET /me`.
- Hit a paginated endpoint and verify you follow `@odata.nextLink` end-to-end (all pages collected, none skipped).
- Hammer a throttled endpoint and verify your client honors `Retry-After` instead of spinning.
- Test least privilege: a `Mail.Read`-only app cannot write or read other data types; add the minimal scope and confirm a forbidden call returns the expected error.
- Batch-test: combine 20 small calls into one `$batch` and confirm it saves round-trips without breaking errors.

## Related systems

- [OAuth 2.0 and OpenID Connect](../oauth2-openid-connect) — the token flow Graph's Bearer header rides on
- [Single sign-on (SSO)](../single-sign-on-sso) — Entra ID identity is the base of `/me`
- [RBAC and authorization](../rbac-and-authorization) — the app-only roles and scopes in action
- [Scim identity provisioning](../scim-identity-provisioning) — how this graph feeds user lifecycle79:Tdcf,
## What this system is

A "mobile framework" answers one question: **who draws the UI?** Native code targets each OS's own toolkit. React Native runs JS that talks to *native widgets*. Flutter ships a custom engine that *paints every pixel* in Dart. Hybrid web apps run a website inside a browser view. The framework you pick decides performance, store review, and what your team must know.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  A[Build a mobile app?] --> B{One or both platforms?}
  B -->|single platform now| C[Native - SwiftUI / Compose]
  B -->|both, shared team| D{How much native feel}
  D -->|pixel control + perf| E[Flutter - own engine]
  D -->|reuse JS skills| F[React Native - native widgets]
  D -->|ship web-like fast| G[Hybrid - Ionic / capacitor]
```

## How each renders, compared

| | Native (SwiftUI / Compose) | React Native | Flutter | Hybrid (Ionic) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Renders with | OS widget toolkit | Native widgets via JS bridge | Its own engine (Skia) | WebView / the web |
| UI parity with iOS/Android | Exact | High | Near-exact (may diverge slightly) | Web look and feel |
| Performance | Best | Great (bridge cost on hot paths) | Great, consistent | Depends on the device browser |
| Your team needs | Swift + Kotlin | JS/TS + React | Dart (new) | Web/JS as-is |
| Code sharing | None | One RN codebase | One Dart codebase | One web app reused |

The decision is really **"which constraint do I hold cheapest"**: store UX parity, raw performance, or team skills. There is no universal answer — there is a list of things each choice surrenders.

## The runtime model (why Flutter and RN behave differently)

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  App[Your app code] -->|React Native path| JS[RN: JS virtual tree]
  App -->|Flutter path| Dart[Flutter: Dart UI model]
  JS -->|complex scroll + gestures| Native[RN bridge to native widgets]
  Dart -->|60fps rendering| Engine[Flutter engine paints layers]
  Native -->|one codebase| Stores[Release to stores]
  Engine -->|one codebase| Stores
```

## The non-rendering true costs

- **Native platform code leaks in**: push notifications, biometrics, deep links, BLE, camera — all eventually need platform-specific code/plugins; that barrier decides whether sharing still pays.
- **Store review is the CI/CD** you cannot pre-trigger: an app package is an artifact you release through a human gate (App Store review, Play policy).
- **Offline, backgrounds, permissions** behave per-platform and per-framework — the classic "works in dev on iOS, dies on Android doze."

## Tester checklist

- Run the same tour on iOS and Android with real devices (or a coverage farm): gestures, back-button, keyboard, dark mode.
- Force offline, then background the app (Android Doze / iOS suspend) and verify state and notifications survive.
- Perf budget on the emulator: list scroll at 60fps, cold start under a threshold — compare RN vs Flutter honesty on your own app, not the marketing.
- Test store-readiness early: review guidelines around payments, permissions, and data collection — failing late is expensive.

## Related systems

- [Frontend frameworks and rendering](../frontend-frameworks-and-rendering) — the web skills these frameworks reuse
- [iOS and macOS in the enterprise](../ios-macos-in-the-enterprise) — where iOS corporate apps get deployed (MDM, VPP)
- [Android in the enterprise](../android-in-the-enterprise) — work-profile and Play EMM constraints
- [API request lifecycle](../api-request-lifecycle) — the API these apps actually talk to7a:T1f36,
## What this system is

Mobile Device Management (MDM) is the control plane for the **phones and tablets** employees carry: it enrolls the device, pushes configuration profiles, delivers apps, enforces passcodes and encryption, and reports compliance. In the [endpoint security](../endpoint-security-architecture) stack it plays the enrollment + policy role for mobile, and in the [zero trust](../zero-trust-architecture) model it is what a phone must prove to get access to corporate data. The promise is a phrase every admin has repeated: **"corporate data on the device, managed."**

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  subgraph Platform["MDM platform - the control plane"]
    ENR[Enrollment -<br/>ID + token + attestation]
    PROF[Profiles -<br/>Wi-Fi, VPN, certs, passcode]
    APP[App delivery -<br/>VPP / managed apps]
    POL[Compliance policies]
  end
  subgraph Devices["The fleet"]
    P1[Corporate phone]
    P2[BYOD phone]
    P3[Tablet]
  end
  ENR --> P1
  ENR --> P2
  ENR --> P3
  PROF --> P1
  APP --> P2
  POL --> P3
```

## The enrollment split that changes everything

Before anything else, MDM decisions hinge on one question: **who owns the device?**

| Mode | Device ownership | What MDM can do | What MDM won't touch |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Corporate-owned (COPE / fully managed)** | The org | Full profile control, app forcing, remote wipe of whole device | Nothing — entirely company-controlled |
| **BYOD (personally enabled)** | The employee | A managed-work-profile container | Personal apps, photos, SMS, location — the company enforces policy only *inside* its container |
| **Dedicated (Kiosk)** | The org, single-app | Lock the device to one app | Everything else — it's a purpose-built appliance |

The platform boundary is the entire governance story: **in a BYOD world the company manages the work container (profiles, apps, compliance inside it) but must not reach into the personal side.** Android does this via **work profiles**, iOS/macOS via **User Enrollment / managed app installation**. The architecture failure is treating BYOD as if it were corporate-owned — pushing broad "all-out" commands (full wipe) onto personal devices, which is both a legal problem and a user revolt.

## The device lifecycle

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant U as User
  participant D as Device
  participant M as MDM platform
  participant P as Policy engine

  U->>M: enroll (email + registration)
  M->>D: issue enrollment profile + identity token
  D->>M: attest device + report capabilities
  M->>D: push profiles, certs, managed apps
  D->>P: request data access (with posture)
  P->>P: check compliance (passcode, OS version, container health)
  alt compliant
    P-->>D: allow app + data access
  else device compromised / non-compliant
    P-->>D: block or restrict to remediation
  end
```

Each stage is a step in a continuous loop, not a one-time setup: enroll → configure → deliver apps → check compliance → remediate → repeat. The management plane owns the "current state" truth and the phone spends its life proving it.

## What MDM actually pushes

| Capability | What it looks like in practice | The failure mode |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Configuration profiles** | Wi-Fi, VPN, email, certs, passcode policy pushed silently | A profile that quietly weakens settings (long passcode, no timeout) |
| **Managed app delivery** | Business apps pushed/updated from VPP/app stores, app config XML | Apps in the personal store that nobody can force-update or configure |
| **Compliance policies** | Require passcode, current OS, no jailbreak/root, container health | Compliance checks that pass on install date, not current state |
| **Remote actions** | Wipe, lock, remove work data, retire device | Wipe fired on a BYOD device (legally + reputationally toxic) |
| **Cert/identity issuance** | A device identity/cert so Conditional Access trusts it | Expired certs silently kill access with no remediation path |
| **Telemetry & inventory** | OS, model, apps, compliance status in one pane | Gaps in enrollment make the dashboard a fiction |

## MDM vs the alternatives you'll confuse it with

| Thing | What it actually is | Versus MDM |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **EMM** (enterprise mobility management) | The superset: MDM + MAM (app-level) + app config + identity | MDM is the device branch; EMM is the umbrella |
| **MAM** (mobile app management) | Manage *apps and data inside them* rather than the device | Only makes sense for BYOD — doesn't need to control the OS |
| **UEM** (unified endpoint management) | One pane for PCs *and* mobile (Intune, Workspace ONE) | The convergence trend: mobile isn't a separate silo anymore |
| **App wrapping / SDK** | Embed app-level controls (VPN in app, DLP) | Heavier than MDM config; used when you must govern app-level behavior |

The strategic read: pure MDM is increasingly folded into **UEM** (one platform for Windows/macOS/iOS/Android — precisely the [endpoint security](../endpoint-security-architecture) management plane). A modern mobile strategy asks "does the same pane manage the laptop and the phone?" rather than running a separate mobility team per OS.

## The failure modes an architect owns

- **BYOD treated as corporate-owned** — broad remote-wipe commands and deep profiles on personal devices = legal exposure and user revolt.
- **Container leak** — the managed work profile and the personal side share storage/backups, so "managed" data escapes the container into personal iCloud/Drive.
- **Compliance theater** — checks confirm *a* passcode/OS but not *the enforced current* state; jailbroken devices pass because the check looks at install-time evidence.
- **Enrollment coverage gaps** — legacy devices or shadow phones were never enrolled, so the posture and inventory story has holes that nobody tracks.
- **Expired identities** — device certs/identity tokens expire and access dies without a remediation loop, so every renewal window is an outage.
- **Human-centered drift** — pushing overly aggressive lockouts in the name of "now," until genuine users are locked out of work and find their own way around ("just use personal Gmail").

## Tester checklist

- **Enrollment round trip**: enroll a fresh device and confirm it receives the profile, certificates, and managed apps without a user manually configuring anything.
- **BYOD boundary**: on a BYOD device, confirm the company can wipe/restrict the *work container only*, that personal data survives a work wipe, and that MDM cannot see personal photos/messages.
- **Compliance realness**: set a short passcode policy; instrument the device to bypass it; confirm the policy engine actually gates access rather than trusting install-time metadata.
- **Jailbreak/root detection**: root/jailbreak a test device and confirm posture flips to non-compliant and access degrades to remediation.
- **Remote wipe drill**: wipe a corporate device and confirm the data (not just the apps) is gone and the device is unenrolled cleanly.
- **Expiry drill**: expire a device identity/cert in test and confirm the user hits a clear remediation path, not a silent black-hole of access.

## Related systems

- [Endpoint security architecture](../endpoint-security-architecture) — the stack MDM's enrollment and policy branch runs inside
- [Zero trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — the model where a phone must prove posture on every request
- [Identity and access management](../identity-and-access-management) — the user identity MDM binds the device to
- [Conditional access and policy engines](../conditional-access-and-policy-engines) — where the phone's compliance actually gates access
- [Android in the enterprise](../android-in-the-enterprise) and [iOS/macOS in the enterprise](../ios-macos-in-the-enterprise) — the per-OS enrollment and profile models MDM drives
- [Windows in the enterprise](../windows-in-the-enterprise) — when MDM grows into UEM and manages laptops too7b:Tdf0,
## What this system is

Monitoring answers *"is it broken?"* Observability answers *"why?"* The toolkit is the **three pillars** — **metrics** (numbers over time), **logs** (events with context), and **traces** (how one request traveled across services). Alerting converts them into pages; SLOs convert pages into numbers your product understands.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  S[Your services] -->|OpenTelemetry SDK| I[Instrumentation]
  I --> M[Metrics - counters, gauges]
  I --> L[Logs - structured events]
  I --> T[Traces - distributed spans]
  M --> Store[(Time-series store<br/>Prometheus / CloudWatch / Azure Monitor)]
  L --> Store
  T --> Store
  Store --> Alert[Alert rules / SLO burn]
  Alert --> Page[On-call pages you actually act on]
  Store --> Dash[Dashboards - human eyes]
```

## The three pillars in practice

| Pillar | What it answers | Cloud home | Star resource when |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Metrics** | *How many / how fast / how full* | CloudWatch, Azure Monitor, Prometheus | Alerting, capacity, SLOs |
| **Logs** | *What exactly happened* | CloudWatch Logs, Log Analytics, Loki | Debugging, audit, forensics |
| **Traces** | *Where the time went* | X-Ray, App Insights, Jaeger/Tempo, OTEL | Slow calls across services |

**OpenTelemetry (OTEL)** is the standard instrumentation SDK — your app emits spans/metrics once, exporters send them anywhere. Instrument once, vendor later.

## The flow from service to page to runbook

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Service[Your service] -->|instrument| OTel[OTel SDK - emit]
  OTel -->|counters| Metrics[Metrics]
  OTel -->|structured| Logs[Logs]
  OTel -->|spans| Traces[Traces]
  Metrics -->|push/pull| Store[Monitoring store]
  Logs -->|ingest| Store
  Traces -->|correlate| Store
  Store -->|alert rules| Alert[Alerts + runbooks]
  Store -->|aggregate| Dash[Dashboards + SLO]
```

## SLOs and the alert triage that matters

- **SLO / SLI / SLI burn**: an SLO ("99.9% of checkout requests under 300ms") is built on SLIs. Alert on **error-budget burn** (how fast you're spending it) rather than raw thresholds — that catches "slow but not dead" pain without paging on every blip.
- **Alert → runbook → owner**: an alert with no runbook is noise. Every alert should name its owning ticket and the play to try first.
- **The warning signs**: alerts that never fire (guard-dog not barking), and alert fatigue from ones that always fire. Both are bugs in the alerting system itself.

## Tester checklist

- Sink a background bad deploy: keep requests failing 5% and confirm an SLO-burn alert pages before users complain.
- Trace drill: pick one slow API call and walk its trace spans to the actual bottleneck service.
- Verify log structure: a structured-log query finds a UUID across all three pillars (metrics named + logs + trace ids).
- Measure the alert pipeline latency: from metric breach to on-call page — fix the chain if that's minutes long.

## Related systems

- [DevOps delivery loop](../devops-delivery-loop) — observability is the observe stage of the loop
- [Disaster recovery and backup](../disaster-recovery-and-backup) — failover is meaningless without telemetry to confirm it
- [Security operations center (SOC)](../security-operations-center-soc) — the same telemetry, viewed for attacks instead of outages
- [ITIL incident and problem management](../itil-incident-and-problem-management) — where alerts become incidents, priorities, and SLA clocks
- [Message queue delivery](../message-queue-delivery) — queue depth is a metric; worker behavior is a trace7c:T1c72,
## What this system is

MuleSoft (Anypoint Platform) is the flagship **iPaaS**: it provides the runtime (Mule), the design tooling (Anypoint Studio), a catalog of pre-built connectors, and API management around the same flows. Its positioning is "any integration, any end" — the flagship use is stitching SaaS (Salesforce, SAP, Workday, NetSuite) to each other and to on-prem systems through a single governed integration layer instead of point-to-point spaghetti.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph Sources
    S1[SaaS - Salesforce, SAP, NetSuite]
    S2[On-prem - DB, files, mainframe]
    S3[Custom APIs + events]
  end
  Sources --> Runtime[Mule runtime<br/>- cloud + on-prem]
  Runtime --> D[(API Manager - policies, keys, governing)]
  D --> T[Downstream target APIs]
  Studio[Anypoint Studio - design] --> Runtime
```

## The pieces that matter

| Piece | What it is | The mental model |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Mule runtime engine** | The execution runtime: an API, a flow, its own OSGi-ish app | "A Mule app = a deployable integration unit" |
| **Anypoint Studio** | The Eclipse-based design IDE (drag-and-drop flow editor) | "Draw the flow, deploy it" |
| **Connectors** | Pre-built adapters: Salesforce, SAP, JDBC, HTTP, messaging | "Tiles, not coding the wire protocol" |
| **API Manager** | Policy + key + version management for exposed APIs | "The governance face your consumers touch" |
| **CloudHub** | MuleSoft's managed hosting for Mule apps | "Run it without running your own runtime fleet" |
| **Anypoint Exchange** | The shared hub of connectors, templates, and APIs | "The org-wide library" |

The mental model: **a flow is a chain of message processors** — a listener receives an event, transformers map the payload, connectors call endpoints, and the result flows onward. Every flow is inside a Mule app, and the app is what gets deployed to CloudHub or to your own runtime.

## Anatomy of a Mule flow

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant L as Listener - HTTP / queue / timer
  participant T as Transform (DataWeave)
  participant C as Connector call - Salesforce / SAP
  participant E as Error handling

  L->>T: incoming payload (XML / JSON / CSV)
  T->>C: map shape + fields (DataWeave)
  C->>C: perform the API call
  alt success
    C-->>L: respond / write to queue
  else failure
    C-->>E: retry / DLQ / log + alert
  end
```

- **Listeners** trigger the flow: HTTP/HTTPS, JMS, file polling, timers, or message queues.
- **DataWeave** is MuleSoft's expression + transformation language — the thing you spend most design time in, mapping an XML invoice into a Salesforce JSON opportunity.
- **Error handling** is scoped per flow: on-error-propagate, retries, and a dead-letter pattern keep a bad message from killing the pipeline.
- **Every flow tracks**: MuleSoft has baked-in logging, metrics, and runtime health per app — "why did it fail" gets a traceable answer without you building observability.

## API-led connectivity: the MuleSoft doctrine

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  subgraph Exp["Experience APIs"]
    EX1[Mobile / portal API]
    EX2[Partner API]
  end
  subgraph Proc["Process APIs"]
    PR1[Order orchestration]
    PR2[Customer sync]
  end
  subgraph Sys["System APIs"]
    SYS1[Salesforce canonical]
    SYS2[SAP canonical]
  end
  Ex[Experience layer] --> Pr[Process layer] --> Sy[System layer]
```

MuleSoft's flagship architecture is **API-led connectivity**: integration APIs are organized in three layers — *system* (wraps a backend with a stable contract), *process* (orchestrates business workflows across systems), *experience* (shapes data for a specific consumer). The value proposition: a consumer changes once instead of touching every wiring. The pragmatic caution: three layers for tiny estates is enterprise theater — run the layers you actually have consumers for.

## MuleSoft vs the alternatives

| Decision | When MuleSoft wins | When it doesn't |
