| --- | --- | --- |
| Estate | Many SaaS + on-prem, "any to any", big-enterprise | Small team, few integrations — a lighter iPaaS or webhooks suffice |
| Governance | You need API policies/catalog org-wide | You need nothing but point connections |
| Cloud | Hybrid-focused, heavy connector catalog | Single-cloud-only shop — Azure integration / OIC may be cheaper and native |
| Developer fit | GUI-first, "integration without deep coding" | Strong backend engineers who'd rather code with strong SDKs |

The honest framing: MuleSoft is expensive and enterprise-flavoured, and it earns that cost in **governance and breadth** — one governed layer for Salesforce, SAP, mainframe, and cloud. If your estate is smaller or cloud-native-uniform, [Azure integration services](../azure-integration-services) or [Oracle Integration Cloud](../oracle-integration-cloud) (for Oracle-centric) fit better. MuleSoft's user mains in a Salesforce-heavy world because of the deep Salesforce connector and the Exchange templates.

## The failure modes that plague iPaaS projects

- **Spaghetti reborn**: a MuleSoft integration per point-connection instead of per business capability — the hub grows but so does the tangle.
- **Re-inventing the backend**: flows that embed business rules that belong in the service behind the API.
- **Untested connectors**: assuming the "Salesforce upsert" connector handles fields you actually send — connectors still need real payload tests.
- **Runtime sprawl**: every developer shipping their own CloudHub environment until cost and governance explode.
- **Ignoring idempotency and ordering**: at-least-once over JMS or retries means duplicate delivery silently creates duplicate orders.

## Tester checklist

- **Flow trigger drill**: call the listener endpoint(s) — HTTP, queue, file-drop — and confirm each path tracks and completes.
- **Transformation fidelity**: run an XML→JSON DataWeave map with dates, decimals, nulls, and arrays; confirm no drift and no silent loss.
- **Retry/DLQ behavior**: force the downstream connector to fail, let it retry with backoff, then confirm the failing message dead-letters and is visible with its trace ID.
- **Policy gate**: expose an API through API Manager and confirm an unauthenticated / over-rate call is rejected by the policy before the flow runs.
- **Idempotency**: deliver the same message twice and confirm no duplicate order is created downstream.
- **Runtime health**: check the app's logs/metrics for a failed run — the answer "why" must be findable without scraping.

## Related systems

- [Integration patterns](../integration-patterns) — the five families every Mule flow implements
- [Azure integration services](../azure-integration-services) — the comparable managed stack when your estate is Azure-centric
- [Oracle Integration Cloud (OIC)](../oracle-integration-cloud) — the comparable platform for an Oracle-centric estate
- [Webhook integrations](../webhook-integrations) — the lighter, no-iPaaS alternative for point connections
- [Salesforce CRM](../salesforce-crm) — the flagship SaaS MuleSoft integrates with
- [API gateway caching](../api-gateway-caching) — the caching/API policy concerns that API Manager also owns
- [Mainframe computing](../mainframe-computing) — the legacy endpoint MuleSoft's "any to any" badge is about7d:T1dc7,
## What this system is

Network Access Control (NAC) is the **gate at the edge of the network**: before a device is allowed onto the LAN, Wi-Fi, or VPN, NAC checks *what it is* (identity) and *how healthy it is* (posture), then grants access, restricts it, or quarantines it. It's the network-layer enforcement arm of [endpoint security](../endpoint-security-architecture) and [zero trust](../zero-trust-architecture): the old "it's plugged into our switch, so it's trusted" assumption is replaced with "prove identity and posture first." Think of NAC as **802.1X on the wire, plus a health check, plus a quarantine loop.**

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  D[Device] -->|connect request| S[Switch / AP / VPN gateway]
  S --> Q{Is the device known?}
  Q -->|no - 802.1X fails| QZ[Guest / quarantine VLAN]
  Q -->|yes - identity proven| P{Posture OK?}
  P -->|no - out of date / missing AV| QZ
  P -->|yes| A[Full / employee VLAN]
  QZ -->|remediation - patch, update| P
```

## The three layers of NAC

| Layer | What it does | The failure if it's missing |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **802.1X authentication** | Port-based auth via RADIUS before the port/AP gives access | Any physical jack is a trusted port; unmanaged devices get straight in |
| **Posture assessment** | Checks AV present/current, patches, policy compliance after auth | A valid identity (even stolen) with a compromised device passes |
| **Enforcement / quarantine** | Drops non-compliant devices onto a remediation VLAN until fixed | Non-compliant devices sit on the trusted LAN indefinitely |

The mental model: **identity gets you onto the wire; posture gets you into the trusted zone.** NAC is the mechanism that makes "prove it before you're inside" literal at layer 2 — a sick or unknown device is contained to a guest/remediation network, not just "flagged" on a dashboard.

## The request path: device connects to the network

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant D as Device
  participant AP as Switch / AP / VPN GW
  participant R as RADIUS server
  participant P as Policy / posture server
  participant Q as Quarantine VLAN

  D->>AP: connect - EAP (802.1X) request
  AP->>R: forward auth request
  R->>R: validate device + user identity
  alt identity denied / unknown
    R-->>Q: access denied - quarantine or guest VLAN
  else identity accepted
    R-->>P: check posture (AV, patches, policy)
    P-->>AP: access-accept - trusted VLAN
    Note over D,Q: compliance lost later
    P-->>Q: downgrade to quarantine on posture change
  end
```

The key modern shift: **identity is the primary key, posture is the secondary check.** NAC used to be "can this MAC address in?" — now it's "is this a real, enrolled, attested device with a current posture?" That's why NAC is tightly coupled to [MDM](../mobile-device-management-mdm) and endpoint enrollment: the posture data has to come from a trusted source on the device, not from what the device *claims* about itself.

## NAC vs the things it gets confused with

| Thing | What it actually is | Versus NAC |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **802.1X** | The protocol/port-auth mechanism (EAP + RADIUS) | NAC's authentication layer, not the whole thing |
| **Firewall / microsegmentation** | Layer 3–7 filtering *between* zones | NAC gates the *entry* edge; firewalls gate traffic *after* entry |
| **MAC filtering** | Allow-list of MAC addresses | Trivial to spoof; NAC adds real identity + posture on top |
| **SASE / ZTNA** | Cloud edge access control for remote/cloud | The same idea, moved off-LAN; NAC is the on-prem LAN version |

The strategic read: **NAC secures the physical LAN edge; [SASE](../sase) / ZTNA secures the remote-and-cloud edge.** Modern estates don't choose one — they get NAC for the office floor and SASE for the rest of the world, with the same identity/posture logic underneath.

## Why NAC projects disappoint (and what actually works)

| Disappointment | The real cause | The fix |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **"We bought NAC and nothing happened"** | Enforcement never turned on; it was deployed observe-only | Decide the quarantine policy *before* rollout; enforce on a pilot VLAN first |
| **Posture checks trusted the device's claim** | Agent/device report not validated against a trusted source | Require an enrolled [endpoint](../endpoint-security-architecture) / MDM attestation, not a self-report |
| **Real devices got quarantined** | Stale posture source (old AV definition list, expired certs) | Revalidate posture data, not assumptions; allow remediation to be fast and self-service |
| **Guests / printers / IoT broke** | Network assumed all devices are managed | Non-managed categories get explicit guest/IoT policies, not a silent default-deny |
| **Quarantine loop was a dead end** | Devices stuck in quarantine with no path back | A remediation portal the user actually sees, with one-click patch/update flows |

The pattern that works: **NAC as a hygiene gate with a fast remediation loop, not as an access-control barricade.** Enforce on a small-but-real VLAN, measure how often genuine users hit quarantine, and make "fix it and come back" a 5-minute path rather than a ticket.

## The failure modes an architect owns

- **Enforcement never enabled** — the classic: NAC deployed in monitor-only mode forever, so it reports but never blocks.
- **Trusting the device's own claim** — no enrolled endpoint identity, so a compromised-but-"healthy"-claiming device walks straight through.
- **Stale posture sources** — checking against an AV definition list nobody updates; posture checks confirm yesterday's truth.
- **One-size quarantine** — printers, HVAC, and unmanaged guests all dumped into a "quarantine" with no path back or wrong per-category policy.
- **No BYOD story** — personal devices with no agent get default-deny or default-trust, instead of a designed guest/remediation posture.
- **NAC for the LAN only** — remote/cloud devices are invisible to the gate, so the office is protected and everything else isn't (hence [SASE](../sase)).

## Tester checklist

- **Identity gate**: connect a device with no/unknown identity and confirm it lands on guest/quarantine, not the trusted VLAN.
- **Posture gate**: connect a known identity with a deliberately stale AV/OS and confirm access is downgraded to quarantine with a visible remediation path.
- **Enforcement reality**: confirm quarantine is *enforced* (traffic actually blocked), not merely logged — run a probe from a quarantined port.
- **Remediation loop**: take a quarantined device, apply the fix, and confirm it re-authenticates into the trusted VLAN within the target time, self-service.
- **Posture-source truth**: break the posture source (expire the AV definition list, kill the MDM attestation) and confirm devices are *not* auto-granted on stale data.
- **Guest/IoT segregation**: connect a printer/guest and confirm they get the designed IoT/guest policy, never the trusted employee VLAN.

## Related systems

- [Endpoint security architecture](../endpoint-security-architecture) — the posture NAC gates on comes from this stack
- [Zero trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — the model where the LAN edge stops being trusted by default
- [SASE](../sase) — the same identity-posture gate moved to the remote/cloud edge
- [Identity and access management](../identity-and-access-management) — the identity NAC authenticates against
- [MDM](../mobile-device-management-mdm) — the enrolled device state NAC's posture checks draw from
- [Threat modeling (STRIDE)](../threat-modeling-stride) — where the "what gets in at the edge" scenarios are analyzed7e:Tf35,
## What this system is

Routing is how a byte for `8.8.8.8` leaves your server, crosses several companies' networks, and arrives at Google's. Every router keeps a **routing table**; on arrival it looks up the destination, matches the **longest prefix**, and forwards on. The difficult part is *populating* those tables at internet scale — that is BGP's job.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  SV[Your server] -->|forward| ED[Your edge router - routing table]
  ED -->|longest prefix match| TR[ISP / transit AS]
  TR -->|BGP best path - AS path| DA[Destination AS]
  DA -->|local delivery| TG[8.8.8.8]
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  S[Your server] --> R1[Your core router]
  R1 --> R2[ISP A router]
  R2 --> R3[Transit network]
  R3 --> R4[ISP B router]
  R4 --> D[8.8.8.8 — Google]
```

## Longest prefix match

Routers don't know the whole path — only the next hop. A table entry `10.0.0.0/16 → via 10.0.0.1` and `10.0.2.0/24 → via 10.0.0.9` both match `10.0.2.55`; the **/24 (longest) wins**. This is how a default route (`0.0.0.0/0`) coexists with precise prefixes.

| Routing type | Scope | Who builds the table | Convergence speed |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Static routes | Your edge, small DCs | You, by hand | Instant (but manual to fix) |
| OSPF (IGP) | Inside one AS / enterprise | Routers exchange link state | Fast (seconds), scales to ~hundreds |
| EIGRP/RIP | Cisco shops / tiny nets | Router metrics | Medium |
| BGP (EGP) | Between autonomous systems | Peers advertise prefixes | Minutes — engineered, not eager |

## BGP: the internet's glue

The internet is thousands of **autonomous systems (ASes)** — companies with an AS number and their own routing domain. BGP lets each AS tell its neighbors "I can reach `1.2.3.0/24` (via me)". Every router independently picks the best path by AS-path length, then policy, then tie-breakers.

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant A as AS64512 (your edge router)
  participant B as ISP peer AS15169

  A->>B: TCP handshake + OPEN (hold timer)
  B-->>A: UPDATE: network 8.8.8.0/24, ASPATH [15169]
  A->>A: install route, pick best path (AS path + policy)
  loop every 60s
    A->>B: KEEPALIVE
  end
  Note over A,B: session dies → route withdrawn → slow world until convergence
```

## Why routing breaks (skills worth keeping)

- **Blackholes**: a route advertised but silently dropping traffic — the classic failure when a path dies but BGP doesn't know.
- **Slow convergence**: BGP is *policy-first, speed-later*; a session flap at a tier-1 ISP can take minutes, and every dependency waits.
- **Prefix hijacking**: mis-advertised prefixes route traffic to the wrong AS (BGP has no built-in auth; [RPKI / MANRS](../zero-trust-architecture) tries to fix that).
- **AS-path prepending** trick: adding your own AS twice just to *downgrade* a path in someone's selection — traffic engineering by lying politely.

## Tester checklist

- Walk a real path with `traceroute`/`mtr` when users report latency — identify which hops add the ms.
- Verify failover: disable the primary uplink and confirm traffic shifts to the backup within your expected SLAs (seconds? minutes?).
- Use a looking glass (e.g. RouteViews, your upstream's LG) to confirm your prefixes are announced and not being suppressed.
- Confirm your static routes fall back correctly when an interface flaps — a static route to a dead next-hop *forwards into the void* unless you track reachability.

## Related systems

- [TCP/IP and TLS](../tcp-ip-and-tls) — the packet and segment this routing carries
- [Zero trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — routing is reachability; authorization still needs ZT layers
- [SD-WAN and edge connectivity](../sd-wan-and-edge-connectivity) — how enterprise branches steer over these paths
- [DNS and load balancing](../dns-and-load-balancing) — the address resolution that precedes the first hop7f:Ta96,
## What this system is

Logging in with “Sign in with Google” is easier to describe as a chat between three actors than as a sequence of claims. OAuth 2.0 hands out **tokens** for accessing resources; OpenID Connect (OIDC) adds the `id_token` that proves *who* you are.

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant B as Browser / SPA
  participant A as Your app
  participant P as Identity provider
  participant API as Resource API

  B->>A: user wants access
  A->>P: redirect + authorize request
  P-->>A: authorization code
  A->>A: exchange code for tokens - id_token + access
  A->>API: call with Bearer token
```

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant U as Browser / SPA
  participant A as Your App
  participant P as Identity Provider (Auth Server)
  participant R as Resource Server (API)

  U->>A: Click "Sign in"
  A->>P: Redirect to /authorize?code_challenge=...
  P->>U: Login + consent
  U->>P: Approve scopes
  P-->>A: Authorization code (short-lived, one-time)
  A->>P: POST /token code + code_verifier
  P-->>A: access_token + id_token
  A->>R: GET /me  Authorization: Bearer access_token
  R-->>A: user profile
  A-->>U: Render signed-in UI
```

## The three actors

| Actor | Talks to | Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Resource owner | The user who approves access | You |
| Client | Your application, wants access | The SPA / backend |
| Authorization server | Issues tokens, owns identities | Auth0, Keycloak, Okta |

## Key design decisions

- **Authorization code flow** is the default for web apps: the code never shows to the browser, only the backend exchanges it for tokens, so refresh tokens stay server-side.
- **PKCE** (proof key for code exchange) works even on public clients: the browser sends a one-time *challenge* of a secret it holds.
- **The `id_token` is signed JSON** (a JWT). Verify the signature and audience before trusting it — anyone can mint an unsigned JWT.
- **Scopes** (e.g. `openid`, `email`, `profile`) describe permission boundaries; grant the minimum set.

## What a tester watches for

1. **Token leakage** — does the access token ever appear in a URL query string or in browser storage for a public client?
2. **State / PKCE validation** — can an attacker replay or swap authorization codes?
3. **Refresh token rotation** — is a reused refresh token rotated or revoked?
4. **Expired tokens** — does the client silently refresh or hard-fail? Does the resource server actually check expiry?

## Related systems

- [API request lifecycle](../api-request-lifecycle) — how the bearer token flows through the chain on every request
- [API gateway caching](../api-gateway-caching) — caching authenticated responses without leaking private data80:T184a,
## What this system is

OAuth 2.0 issues **tokens**, but not all tokens are obtained the same way. A **grant type** is the recipe for *how a client proves it may ask for a token* — and picking the right one (or the wrong one) is the single most consequential decision in your auth design. There are five that matter; the rest are legacy footnotes.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  G[Grant type - how the client asks] -->|decide| P{Who is the client?}
  P -->|no secret| PUB[Browser / SPA / mobile - no secret to keep]
  P -->|secret-safe| CONF[Backend server - can keep a secret]
  P -->|no browser| DEV[TV / CLI - no browser]
  PUB -->|auth code + PKCE| T[access_token]
  CONF -->|client credentials| T
  DEV -->|device flow| T
```

## The five grants that matter

| Grant | Who it's for | How the token is obtained | Secret? |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Authorization code + PKCE** | Web apps, SPAs, mobile — anything with a user | Browser redirects to IdP, user consents, code exchanged for tokens | PKCE instead of a secret |
| **Client credentials** | Server-to-server, no user | Client signs with its own credentials, gets a token *for itself* | Yes — the client secret |
| **Device flow** | TVs, CLIs, IoT — no browser | User opens a URL on any device, enters a code | No |
| **Refresh token** | Long-lived sessions | Exchanged (server-side) for new access tokens | Where the access token came from |
| **Resource-owner password** | Legacy-only, discouraged | User's username+password sent to the IdP | Never do this in new code |

## The authorization code flow (with PKCE)

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant U as User (browser)
  participant A as SPA / mobile app
  participant P as Authorization server
  participant R as Resource API

  A->>A: generate code_verifier (random secret)
  A->>P: /authorize?code_challenge=SHA256(verifier)
  P->>U: login + consent
  U->>P: approve
  P-->>A: authorization code (via redirect)
  A->>P: POST /token code + code_verifier
  P->>P: verify verifier matches challenge
  P-->>A: access_token (+ refresh_token)
  A->>R: Authorization: Bearer access_token
  R-->>A: data
```

Why **PKCE** (proof key for code exchange) even when there's a client secret: the browser redirect carries the *code*, and a public client can't protect a secret the browser holds. The `code_verifier` proves that whoever is exchanging the code is the same party that started the flow — it kills a whole class of code-interception attacks. In modern OAuth (2.1), **PKCE is mandatory for everything**.

## Client credentials: the machine-to-machine grant

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  S[Backend service] -->|client_id + client_secret| P[Authorization server]
  P -->|access_token for the service itself| S
  S -->|Bearer token, no user involved| API[Another API]
```

When **no user** is in the conversation, the client authenticates *as itself*: it sends its `client_id` and `client_secret` to `/token`, and receives a token representing the **service**, not a person. Classic uses: a cron job calling an admin API, a backend calling a payment provider. The consequence to internalize: the token's authority is whatever that service is allowed to do — if you hand `scope: admin` to a client-credentials client, you've given a background job admin rights with no human in the middle.

## Device flow: when there's no browser at all

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant D as TV / CLI device
  participant P as Authorization server
  participant U as User's phone/browser

  D->>P: POST /device_authorization → device_code + user_code
  P-->>D: user_code: ABC-123, verification_uri
  D->>U: "go to URL, enter ABC-123"
  U->>P: login + approve
  loop polling
    D->>P: POST /token with device_code
    P-->>D: pending (poll again) / success
  end
  P-->>D: access_token
```

The device gets a `user_code` and a verification URL; the user approves on *any* device; the device **polls** until the token is ready. This is how "sign in with the code on your TV" works — and the tester-relevant detail is that the device must keep polling at a sane interval and stop once authorized or expired.

## Refresh tokens: extending a session safely

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  A[access_token expires] --> R{Have refresh_token?}
  R -->|yes - keep it server-side| B[POST /token grant_type=refresh_token]
  B --> C[new access_token + rotated refresh_token]
  R -->|no| D[Full re-auth]
```

Access tokens are short-lived; a **refresh token** lets a server-side client get a new one without making the user re-login. The discipline: the refresh token lives **server-side** (never in browser storage), is **rotated on each use** (a reused one is a sign of theft and gets revoked), and is itself revocable on logout. "Refresh token rotation" is the behavior your tester should poke at hardest.

## Picking and testing the right grant

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  Q{Is a user in the loop?} -->|no| M2M[Client credentials]
  Q -->|yes| B{Has a browser?}
  B -->|no| DEV[Device flow]
  B -->|yes| W{Can you keep a secret?}
  W -->|yes| CODE[Auth code + secret + PKCE]
  W -->|no| PKCE[Auth code + PKCE]
```

Tester checklist:

- **PKCE**: replay a stolen authorization code without the verifier — it must be rejected; reuse the verifier on two codes — second must fail.
- **Client credentials**: confirm the token's scopes are the *minimum* the service needs (an admin scope on a background job is a finding).
- **Refresh rotation**: use a refresh token twice — the second use must be denied (rotation + reuse detection).
- **Device flow**: confirm polling stops after expiry, the code expires, and the user's approval grants only the requested scopes.
- **No password grant**: if you see `grant_type=password` in logs or docs, that's legacy tech debt to flag, not copy.

## Related systems

- [OAuth 2.0 and OpenID Connect](../oauth2-openid-connect) — the authorization-code flow in end-to-end detail
- [HTTP authentication and Basic auth](../http-authentication-basic-auth) — the header these tokens ride in
- [JWT tokens](../jwt-tokens) — what the access token usually is, and how to verify it
- [Single sign-on (SSO)](../single-sign-on-sso) — the flow behind "log in once everywhere"81:T1b73,
## What this system is

Oracle Cloud Infrastructure (OCI) is Oracle's public cloud — its reason to exist is running **Oracle database**. Where AWS and Azure are generic clouds, OCI is the only one where a managed Oracle database (Autonomous Database / Exadata) is a first-class native service rather than a port. The rest of OCI (VCNs, compute, storage, IAM) exists to support that core use case — and increasingly to host generic workloads too.

Everything in OCI hangs off **tenancy → compartment** organization. You don't deploy "into a region" so much as "into a compartment inside your tenancy," with IAM policies deciding who can touch which compartment tree.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph Pub["Public layer"]
    In[Internet] -- edge --> IGW[Internet gateway]
    IGW -- public route --> LB[Load balancer<br/>public subnet]
  end
  subgraph App["App layer - private subnets"]
    C1[Compute - AZ a<br/>private subnet]
    C2[Compute - AZ b<br/>private subnet]
  end
  subgraph Data["Data layer"]
    ADB[(Autonomous DB<br/>or RAC/Exadata)]
    OST[(Object Storage<br/>bucket)]
  end
  LB -- route --> C1
  LB -- route --> C2
  C1 -- "SQL - private line" --> ADB
  C2 -- "SQL - private line" --> ADB
  C1 -- "backups / assets" --> OST
  C2 -- "backups / assets" --> OST
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  In[Internet] --> IGW[Internet gateway]
  IGW --> LB[Load balancer - public subnet]
  LB --> C1[Compute - AZ a private subnet]
  LB --> C2[Compute - AZ b private subnet]
  C1 --> ADB[(Autonomous Database / RAC)]
  C2 --> ADB
  C1 --> OS[(Object Storage bucket)]
  C2 --> OS
```

## The OCI mental model

| Concept | What it is | The closest AWS/Azure idea |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Tenancy** | Your root isolation boundary in OCI | AWS account / Azure subscription root |
| **Compartment** | Logical container for resources; IAM grants access to compartments | Resource group / organizational unit |
| **Region + AD** | Region = data center geography; availability domains inside | Region / availability zone |
| **VCN** | Your virtual network, sliced into subnets | VPC / VNet |
| **OCI IAM** | Policies (`allow group X to manage ... in compartment Y`) that are *statement-based*, not role-based | IAM policies (permission-based) / Azure RBAC |
| **OCI Object Storage** | Buckets with namespaces; `oci s3`-compatible API | S3 / Blob storage |

The big OCI difference: **IAM is statement-based, written in plain language.** You write `Allow group app_team to manage all-resources in compartment app-prod` — it reads like a sentence and composes from the root. The failure mode of this is overly-permissive sentences written once and copied forever.

## Compartments and IAM: the tree you govern

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  Root[Tenancy root] --> C1[Compartment: prod]
  C1 --> C1a[Compartment: app]
  C1 --> C1b[Compartment: data - Autonomous DB pool]
  Root --> C2[Compartment: non-prod]
  C2 --> C2a[Compartment: sandbox]
  Policy[allow group app_dev manage all-resources<br/>in compartment app] --> C1a
```

Policies attach to compartments; resources inherit placement inside a compartment's tree. Rules of thumb: **one compartment per cost owner**, never put prod and non-prod under the same grant, and put the database pool in its own compartment so "manage app" never means "manage the DB."

## The real OCI: running Oracle database natively

| Offering | What it is | When you reach for it |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Autonomous Database (ADB-S)** | Fully managed Oracle DB: auto-patching, auto-backup, auto-scaling, tooling for DBAs is gone | "We want Oracle without hiring a DBA team" — the flagship |
| **DB Systems (RAC)** | Classic Exadata/DB on managed VMs, full RAC, upgrade windows you control | Lift-and-shift of an existing Oracle estate |
| **Exadata Cloud Service** | Oracle's engineered hardware as a service (RAC + flash + storage servers) | Heavy OLTP that needs the engineered stack |
| **Autonomous JSON / NoSQL** | Different data models on the same infra | Document or key-value needs |

The architectural point: **Autonomous DB removes the DBA doing the same boring tasks** — patching, backup, tuning knobs are serverless behaviors, not jobs. But what it doesn't remove is *design*: schema, indexing, partitioning, RAC topology, and capacity still live in the same [Oracle database architecture](../oracle-database-ecosystem) conversation. "Autonomous" controls the machinery, not your data model.

## Networking: VCNs, subnets, and getting Oracle DB privacy

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  VCN[VCN 10.0.0.0/16] --> SubPub[Public subnet - LB]
  VCN --> SubPriv[Private subnet - compute]
  VCN --> SubData[Private subnet - DB subnet]
  SubPub --> RT[Route table: IGW for public only]
  SubPriv --> NSG[Network security groups / SL]
  SubData --> P[Peering / IPSec to on-prem]
```

- **Public vs private subnets** behave like AWS: public route table with an internet gateway; private subnets with no internet route, egress via NAT.
- **Security lists** (stateless-ish rules per subnet) and **NSGs** (stateful, per-NIC) both exist; the newer NSG model is the one to build on.
- **DB should be in a private subnet** reached over private IPs — the same lesson as every other cloud. The private line to Autonomous DB or the on-prem-to-OCI tunnel is where your database traffic should live.

## The mistake repeatedly remembered

- Exposing the database subnet to the internet because "it's only control plane." (It's not.)
- Writing one enormous `allow group engineers manage all-resources in tenancy` policy and calling it simpler.
- Cross-cloud hybrid: Oracle DB landing in OCI while its direct consumers live in AWS/Azure with no private interconnect — the latency and egress bill write the memo.
- Treating Autonomous DB as "no design needed" — it's fully managed, not self-organizing.

## Tester checklist

- Validate compartment isolation: an app-role principal attempting to `create database` in the `data` compartment must be denied by policy, not by a friendly guard.
- On Autonomous DB: run a restore to a point-in-time (flashback) and time it; confirm backups are automatic and the restore target is a fresh instance.
- Confirm DB traffic never crosses a public endpoint: check the app's connection string and route table for a private path only.
- Expose an NSG mis-rule deliberately and verify the security list / NSG actually blocks it (rules are evaluated, don't assume).
- Scale a compute shape and an ADB service level and watch cost change — know what auto-scaling is and is not doing.

## Related systems

- [Oracle database ecosystem](../oracle-database-ecosystem) — the engine OCI is built to run
- [Cloud computing fundamentals](../cloud-computing-fundamentals) — regions, AZs, shared responsibility in one view
- [Cloud landing zones](../cloud-landing-zones) — the compartment/policy structure is your landing zone
- [Cloud networking and hybrid connectivity](../cloud-networking-and-hybrid-connectivity) — peering, transit, and private links into OCI82:T18e2,
## What this system is

The Oracle database is the workhorse of enterprises that predate the cloud era — and a surprising number that don't. It's a relational database, but the real Oracle is not the SQL you write; it's the **architecture** underneath it. When a DBA says "redo," "undo," "RAC," or "Data Guard," they're describing the machinery that makes a single database a *system* you can run, recover, scale, and fail over.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  APP[Application<br/>SQL via JDBC / OCI] -->|SQL| SGA[SGA: shared pool<br/>buffer cache]
  SGA -->|parse + execute| BG[Background procs<br/>LGWR, DBWR, CKPT]
  BG -->|dirty blocks| DATA[Datafiles]
  BG -->|LGWR: log force| REDO[Redo log groups]
  SGA -->|read-consistency + rollback| UNDO[Undo tablespace]
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  App[App - JDBC / OCI / SQL*Plus] --> SGA[SGA<br/>shared pool + buffer cache]
  SGA --> LGWR[LGWR - log writer]
  SGA --> DBWR[DBWR - database writer]
  LGWR --> REDO[(Redo log groups<br/>ARCH goes to archive logs)]
  DBWR --> DATA[(Datafiles + undo tablespace)]
  DATA --> CKPT[Checkpoint - sync points]
```

## The architecture that makes Oracle Oracle

| Moving part | What it does | Why you'll hear it in the room |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **SGA** | Shared memory: parse cache, buffer cache, redo buffer | "Tune the buffer cache" = most of Oracle performance talk |
| **Redo log** | Append-only journal of every change, written *by LGWR before commit returns* | "Redo too small" / "log switch storm" — commit latency is redo latency |
| **Undo** | Old copies of rows for read-consistency + rollback | "ORA-01555 snapshot too old" kills long-running readers |
| **Checkpoint** | Progress marker tying disk writes to redo | Crash recovery replays redo from the last checkpoint |
| **Datafiles** | The actual on-disk tables/indices (in tablespaces) | "Tablespace full" — a full datafile fails every insert |
| **PGA** | Per-session memory for sorts/hashes | Sort-to-disk = where big queries slow down |

**Commits are redo-bound, not datafile-bound.** When you `COMMIT`, Oracle guarantees the redo (not the data) reached disk — the datafile write happens later, batched by DBWR. That's the single idea that explains both Oracle's speed and its recovery behavior: it can always *replay* what redo recorded.

## Read consistency: undo is the time machine

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant U as Session A (updater)
  participant S as Session B (long reader)
  participant DB as Oracle

  U->>DB: UPDATE row X (new value)
  DB->>DB: keep old value in undo
  S->>DB: SELECT row X
  DB->>DB: use undo to serve pre-update version
  DB->>S: consistent snapshot, no locks needed
```

A long-running `SELECT` sees a **consistent snapshot**, not "whatever's on disk right now." Oracle builds that snapshot from current data + undo. This is why a big report doesn't block writers, and why "snapshot too old" appears when undo gets overwritten (usually an undo tablespace or retention setting).

## RAC: one database, many instances

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Client[App / connection pool] --> L1[Instance 1 - node A]
  Client --> L2[Instance 2 - node B]
  Client --> L3[Instance 3 - node C]
  L1 --> CF[Cluster - shared cache / interconnect]
  L2 --> CF
  L3 --> CF
  CF --> ST[(Shared storage - ASM / SAN)]
```

Oracle Real Application Clusters (RAC) runs **multiple instances against one shared database** on shared storage. The payoff is high availability (a node dies, the others keep serving) and scale (more instances = more CPU/memory). The catch: it's shared-storage architecture, so everything funnels through the shared disk — and performance depends on the interconnect and on avoiding cross-instance traffic on hot blocks.

**Standby is a different animal —** that's **Data Guard**:

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  P[Primary database] -->|redo shipped live| DB[Data Guard:<br/>physical / logical standby]
  DB -->|apply, near real-time| S[(Standby database)]
  FAIL[Primary outage] -->|switchover / failover| DB
```

Data Guard keeps a **standby database** current by shipping redo and applying it. Physical standby = identical block-for-block copy (good for recovery + read-only reporting); logical standby = re-applied SQL (read/write ready but coverage varies). RAC and Data Guard are complementary: RAC covers instance/node loss inside one cluster; Data Guard covers database *site* loss and is half of every DR story.

## The parts people forget

- **Tablespaces ≠ schemas.** A tablespace is physical (datafiles on disk); a schema/user is logical. "Put the table in a big tablespace" and "give the app its own schema" are different actions.
- **PL/SQL is compiled, not interpreted.** Stored procedures run in the DB; calling one from the app avoids shipping SQL text but couples your logic to Oracle.
- **NLS/Oracle dates.** `TO_DATE`/`TO_CHAR` with explicit format masks; date arithmetic is in days. The classic "dates off by one" is an NLS format mismatch, not a timezone bug.
- **Licensing is the real constraint.** Enterprise Edition features (RAC, Data Guard, partitioning, compression) are separately licensed. Architecture decisions are often *licensing* conversations first.

## Tester checklist

- Force a kill (-9) on a session mid-transaction and confirm other readers are unaffected (relying on undo, not locks).
- Watch a `COMMIT` return while LGWR is deliberately delayed — understand why commit is redo-bound, and what `COMMIT NOWAIT` changes.
- Trigger a checkpoint and confirm recovery replays only from the last checkpoint, not from the beginning.
- Create a runaway long-running `SELECT` and update rows underneath it; confirm the reader sees a consistent snapshot and -- if undo overrites -- you get ORA-01555.
- For RAC: kill one instance and confirm the service stays up; for Data Guard: do a switchover and a real failover and time it.

- [Database indexing](../database-indexing) — the B-tree and table scans that run inside these datafiles
- [Cloud computing fundamentals](../cloud-computing-fundamentals) — where Oracle database lives in the shared-responsibility world
- [Oracle Cloud Infrastructure](../oracle-cloud-infrastructure) — running and managing Oracle database with the rest of the stack
- [Change data capture](../change-data-capture) — how the redo/archive log becomes a streaming source83:T1de4,
## What this system is

Oracle Integration Cloud (OIC) is Oracle's managed **integration service**: the tool that stitches Oracle SaaS (Fusion ERP, HCM, CX) to each other, to OCI, and to the outside world (REST, SOAP, FILE, email, on-prem). It is most often deployed because **Oracle SaaS must talk to SAP or a CRM or a warehouse**, and someone has to own the plumbing. Its reason to exist mirrors Oracle Cloud Infrastructure's: native, managed connectivity into the Oracle estate where a generic iPaaS is a worse fit.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph OIC["Oracle Integration Cloud"]
    I[Integrations -<br/>REST / SOAP / DB adapters]
    F[FBDI / file flows -<br/>bulk load]
    P[Process Automation -<br/>human workflow]
    M[Monitoring -<br/>dashboards, DLQ, logs]
  end
  subgraph Sources
    S1[Oracle Fusion ERP / HCM / CX]
    S2[OCI services]
    S3[On-prem - via remote agent]
  end
  subgraph Targets
    T1[SAP / legacy]
    T2[Files - SFTP / object storage]
    T3[CRM / custom REST]
    T4[Databases - DB adapter]
  end
  Sources --> OIC
  OIC --> Targets
```

## The layers that matter

| Layer | What it is | The mental model |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Integrations** | The workhorse unit: a flow of connections + expressions + mapping | OIC is "an integration per business process" |
| **Connections** | Pre-built adapters and endpoints (Fusion, REST, SOAP, DB, FILE, FTP, Kafka...) | Each is a configured tile you wire into flows |
| **FBDI / file uploads** | Bulk data exchange into Fusion via Oracle's own file batch templates | Load data *in bulk*, not via per-record APIs |
| **Process Automation** | Human-in-the-loop workflows (approvals, forms) — the old OIC Process Cloud | The "Logic Apps-style" orchestration arm |
| **Remote agent** | On-premise connector agent for reaching internal systems securely | Bridges OIC in the cloud to your network |
| **Monitoring / dashboards** | Run state, errors, dead-letter messages, metrics | OIC owns an ops surface, not just a flow editor |

The one-line mental model: **an integration is a named flow with a trigger** — maybe REST or SOAP, maybe a file drop or an on-prem DB query — that calls one or more downstream connections and maps data between them.

## Anatomy of an integration flow

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant T as Trigger (REST / SOAP / FILE / SCHEDULE)
  participant E as Expression & mapping (XSLT)
  participant S as Stage - call connection
  participant M as Monitoring / error handler

  T->>E: payload arrives
  E->>S: map + enrich data
  S-->>S: invoke Fusion SOAP / REST adapter
  alt success
    S-->>M: log success, close tracking
  else error
    S-->>M: dead-letter / retry policy
  end
  M-->>T: response to caller
```

- **Triggers** define *how* the integration starts: a REST endpoint you expose, a scheduled batch (poll an FBDI or DB), or a file-arrival watcher.
- **Stages** hold the orchestration: invoke adapters, run expressions, add conditions, branch, transform with the built-in XSLT mapper.
- **Every run is trackable**: OIC tracks each instance with a business identifier, status, and error detail — that's the "why did it fail?" answer you get without writing observability.
- **Error handling** is a first-class stage: catch, retry with backoff, and write to a dead-letter location so a bad payload never silently disappears.

## FBDI: how bulk data actually moves

| Mechanism | When used | Gotcha |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **REST/SOAP APIs** | Point-to-point, low-volume, event-driven | Per-record; slow for thousands of rows |
| **FBDI file load** (`File-Based Data Import`) | Bulk: GL journals, invoices, employees | Schema is Oracle's; validate before upload |
| **BIP/BICC export** | Getting reports/data *out* of Fusion | Bulk extracts land as files, not events |
| **DB adapter / polling** | On-prem or OCI database integration | Needs the remote agent, polling latency |

The architectural lesson inside OIC is the same as every integration stack: **low volume = API; high volume = bulk file flows.** OIC's file support is its strength for Oracle estates because Fusion has first-class bulk interfaces (FBDI) that a generic iPaaS doesn't know about — OIC surfaces them as a native file-upload flow with error reporting to an output file channel.

## OIC vs the alternatives

| Decision | When you choose OIC | When you don't |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cloud | Mostly Oracle SaaS/Fusion in the picture | Pure multi-cloud iPaaS, cloud-agnostic team |
| iPaaS | OIC has managed Fusion adapters + bulk | Strong developer, wants Kubernetes/high-throughput |
| Logic Apps / Service Bus | ...your estate is Azure | ...estate is Oracle |
| Webhook / hand-rolled | Only 1-2 point integrations | Many integrations to govern |

The honest take: OIC is the right tool when the *center of gravity is Oracle*. It has managed adapters for Fusion that nothing else ships with, FBDI handling built in, and a license that typically comes bundled with Oracle SaaS entitlements. If your estate is cloud-native, polyglot, or high-throughput, a general iPaaS or Azure integration services will serve you better — drag in [Azure integration services](../azure-integration-services) if that's your main cloud.

## Getting data out of Oracle into the rest of the world

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Fusion[Oracle Fusion ERP / HCM] -->|"SOAP/REST - OIC adapters"| OIC[OIC]
  OIC -->|"FBDI bulk - file"| Files[File / SFTP sharing]
  OIC -->|"webhook / REST"| AB[Anything that speaks HTTP]
  OIC -->|"ambassador - outbound"| SAP[SAP / legacy adapters]
  OnPrem -->|"remote agent"| OIC
```

OIC doesn't just expose Oracle to HTTP — it reverses: with the **remote agent** installed on-prem, OIC reaches back into SAP's BAPI/JCo, legacy files, and databases that live inside your network. That's the classic Oracle-adjacent integration: Fusion talks to SAP because OIC owns the bridge in both directions.

## Tester checklist

- **Trigger matrix**: hit the integration via REST, by file drop, and by schedule — confirm each trigger path lands and tracks an instance.
- **Failure trail**: make the downstream adapter fail (bad SOAP response), confirm retries with backoff, then confirm the error dead-letters and is visible in Monitoring with the business identifier.
- **Bulk import**: run an FBDI file with one bad row in the middle — confirm the partial-vs-failed semantics, the output error file, and that bad row didn't silently drop.
- **Idempotency**: deliver the same REST payload twice and confirm the integration doesn't create duplicates (validate its business-logic dedup).
- **Agent reach**: from OIC in the cloud, confirm the on-prem remote agent round-trip returns an accurate latency and a reliable connection status.
- **Data fidelity**: a mapping of a date, a decimal, and a null from Fusion → target must survive the XSLT map without drift.

- [Oracle Cloud Infrastructure (OCI)](../oracle-cloud-infrastructure) — where OIC runs and where its OCI-adjacent services live
- [Oracle database ecosystem](../oracle-database-ecosystem) — the data standing behind OCI and Oracle SaaS
- [Enterprise resource planning](../enterprise-resource-planning) and [ERP finance and record-to-report](../erp-finance-and-record-to-report) — the Fusion workloads OIC mostly connects
- [Azure integration services](../azure-integration-services) — the comparable managed stack when your estate isn't Oracle-centric
- [Webhook integrations](../webhook-integrations) — the lower-ceremony alternative for point connections84:T14f1,
## What this system is

The OSI (Open Systems Interconnection) model is a **reference model**: seven named layers that describe what must happen for data to leave one application and arrive at another. It is not a protocol — no packet carries an "OSI layer 5" header. Its job is to give engineers a shared vocabulary and a way to reason about *where* a problem lives before you go digging.

The internet does not run on OSI — it runs on the five-layer TCP/IP model. OSI is the lens you use to talk about it: HTTP is layer 7, TCP is layer 4, IP is layer 3, Ethernet is layer 2. When someone says "that's a layer 2 problem" or "the switch only works at L2," they are speaking OSI.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  L7["L7 Application<br/>HTTP, SMTP, DNS"] -- down --> L6["L6 Presentation<br/>encoding, encryption"]
  L6 -- down --> L5["L5 Session<br/>dialogs, checkpoints"]
  L5 -- down --> L4["L4 Transport<br/>TCP / UDP"]
  L4 -- segments --> L3["L3 Network<br/>IP routing"]
  L3 -- packets --> L2["L2 Data link<br/>Ethernet / Wi-Fi"]
  L2 -- frames --> L1["L1 Physical<br/>signal on wire"]
  L1 -- bits --> O[Other host / network]
```

## The seven layers

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  L7[7 Application<br/>human-facing protocols: HTTP, SMTP, DNS, TLS sits above it] --> L6[6 Presentation<br/>syntax: encoding, compression, encryption]
  L6 --> L5[5 Session<br/>dialog control: who talks, checkpoints]
  L5 --> L4[4 Transport<br/>end-to-end delivery: ports, reliability, ordering]
  L4 --> L3[3 Network<br/>routing: logical addressing, hop-by-hop]
  L3 --> L2[2 Data link<br/>local delivery: MAC addressing, frames, error detection]
  L2 --> L1[1 Physical<br/>raw bits: voltage, light, radio, timing]
```

| Layer | Unit | Your cast of characters | Typical gear |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 7 Application | Message | HTTP, SMTP, DNS, SSH, RDP | Apps, browsers, mail clients |
| 6 Presentation | — | TLS's crypto+encoding, JPEG/PDF decoding | Libraries, codecs |
| 5 Session | — | Sockets, RPC dialog state, checkpoint/resume | OS session layer, SMB sessions |
| 4 Transport | Segment / datagram | TCP, UDP, QUIC | OS kernels, load balancers |
| 3 Network | Packet | IP, ICMP, BGP, OSPF | Routers, L3 switches, firewalls |
| 2 Data link | Frame | Ethernet, Wi-Fi (802.11), ARP | Switches, bridges, NICs |
| 1 Physical | Bits | Copper, fibre, radio, signal encodings | Cables, optics, repeaters, AP radios |

**Mnemonic** (top-down): *All People Seem To Need Data Processing*; bottom-up: *Please Do Not Throw Sausage Pizza Away*.

## Encapsulation: each layer wraps the one above

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  A[L7 message: GET /users] --> B[L4: TCP header + message]
  B --> C[L3: IP header + TCP + message]
  C --> D[L2: Ethernet header + IP + TCP + message + FCS]
  D --> E[L1: bits on the wire]
```

The receiving host peels headers in reverse: the NIC checks the frame (L2), the kernel routes the packet (L3), demuxes the port (L4), and hands the message to the process (L7). **Middle boxes** live off to the side: a router reads L3 to forward (it does not care about your TCP or HTTP), a switch reads L2, a load balancer reads L4 (or L7 if it terminates TLS).

## Where OSI differs from TCP/IP

| | OSI (7 layers) | TCP/IP (5 layers, what's real) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Application | 7, 6, 5 split three ways | One Application layer |
| Transport | 4 | TCP / UDP |
| Internet | 3 | IP (plus routing protocols) |
| Network access | 2 + 1 | Link + Physical |
| Status | Reference model | The actual internet |

The classic trap: "SSL/TLS is layer 6." TLS is *usually* described as layer 6 because it encrypts payloads, but it rides inside TCP (layer 4) and outside the app protocol. For debugging, what matters is **where** it sits in the byte stream, not the layer number.

## Using the model to debug

- **"Layer 2 issue"** — cables, switches, VLANs, MAC, ARP, duplicate IP on the same segment. Symptoms: link flaps, broadcast storms.
- **"Layer 3 issue"** — routing, addressing, subnets, next-hops, ACLs. Symptoms: `destination host unreachable`, asymmetric routes.
- **"Layer 4 issue"** — ports, firewalls, NAT, TCP state. Symptoms: timeouts instead of refused, connections hang after handshake.
- **"Layer 7 issue"** — app behavior: 500s, TLS handshake errors, slow queries. Everything below it worked; the problem is in your code.

Practical debugging always runs the stack top-to-bottom or bottom-to-top: `curl` (L7) → `ss -tnp` (L4) → `traceroute` (L3) → `ping` (L3) → link lights (L1).

## Tester checklist

- Reproduce a failure and classify it by layer: an HTTP 500 is layer 7 even if the server's CPU is at 100% — don't call the network team for a database timeout.
- Verify a firewall rule change at L4 (port open) still blocks nothing at L7: confirm with a real request, not just `nc -zv`.
- Check ARP (L2) when a machine "can't reach the gateway": `arp -a` shows whether the MAC for the default gateway resolved.
- Confirm TLS termination point (L6/L7) matches your threat model — if the LB decrypts, it sees plaintext.

## Related systems

- [TCP/IP and TLS](../tcp-ip-and-tls) — the five-layer reality the OSI model describes
- [Network routing and BGP](../network-routing-and-bgp) — how layer 3 finds its way
- [DNS and load balancing](../dns-and-load-balancing) — layer 7 resolution in front of layer 4/7 balancing

## What this system is

Never store passwords — store **irreversible, salted, slow hashes**. A leaked database of hashes must be useless to attackers. This page walks the two moments that matter: **signup** (hash and save) and **login** (recompute and compare).

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  subgraph Signup["Signup"]
    P1[password] --> R1[argon2/bcrypt with random salt]
    R1 --> D1[(store hash + salt)]
  end

  subgraph Login["Login attempt"]
    P2[password] --> C1[recompute hash<br/>using stored salt]
    D1 -.-> C1
    C1 --> M{Match?}
    M -->|same| OK[grant access]
    M -->|different| NO[reject]
  end
```

## The three rules

1. **Hash, don't encrypt.** Encryption is reversible (with a key) — an attacker who steals the key reads every password. Hashing is a one-way trip.
2. **Salt with a random value per user.** Otherwise two identical passwords hash identically → rainbow tables crack them all at once.
3. **Use a deliberately slow KDF** (key-derivation function). Speed is the enemy:

| Algorithm | Built to be | Verdict for passwords |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `SHA-256`, `MD5` | fast, for integrity | ❌ too fast — billions of guesses/sec on a GPU |
| `bcrypt` | memory-light, iteration-scalable with cost factor | ✅ standard choice |
| `Argon2id` | memory-hard, resists GPUs | ✅ modern winner (won Password Hashing Competition) |
| `scrypt` | memory-hard | ✅ good alternative |
| plain text | — | ❌ catastrophic |

## Verify without killing the app

- **Constant-time comparison** (`crypto.timingSafeEqual`): if comparison timing reveals how many bytes matched, a remote attacker extracts the hash character by character.
- **Cost factors drift**: as hardware improves, raise the bcrypt *cost* / Argon2 *memory* on each login, and re-hash the user's password when you get a chance.
- **Pepper is optional**: a secret appended server-side (not in DB) turns DB-only theft into useless data; but it complicates rotation, so treat it as defense-in-depth only.

## Legends and traps

- **"We MD5 it"** — instant rainbow-table material.
- **"We base64 encode it"** — that's obfuscation, not security.
- **"We encrypt passwords with AES"** — see rule 1: the whole DB often ships with the key in config.
- **"bcrypt is slow"** — reduce *iterations* for the same user only via salted re-hash after login, never by disabling hashing.

## Tester checklist

- Dump the user table: confirm zero reversible values and per-row distinct salt/hash.
- Log in with a wrong password and confirm failure timing is indistinguishable from success timing.
- Rotate the cost factor and confirm existing users can still log in (hash upgrade path).
- Confirm no password ever appears in app logs, metrics, or error messages.

## Related systems

- [Authentication vs authorization](../authentication-vs-authorization) — where this fits in the authn stage
- [Database indexing](../database-indexing) — a user row *should* be found by a unique index, so the hash lookup stays fast86:Tead,
## What this system is

**PIM** (Azure PIM is the canonical product; the pattern exists everywhere) replaces *"you're an admin all day"* with *"you can ask to be an admin for 30 minutes."* Standing privileged access is the single most valuable compromise target in an estate. PIM makes privilege **temporary, approved, recorded, and auto-expiring**.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    AD[Admin user] -- ask for role --> REQ[PIM request - role + window]
    REQ -- MFA required --> APP[Approver - MFA + reason]
    REQ -- approved --> GR[Time-boxed grant - 30 minutes]
    GR -- every action recorded --> AU[Audit log - activation id]
    GR -- expiry clock is enforced --> EX[Auto-expired]
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  U[Admin user] -->|request elevation to X role| A[Approver flow]
  A -->|approve + MFA + just reason| G[JIT grant - time-boxed 30 min]
  G -->|user performs task| L[Audit log + session record]
  L -->|expiry clock| E[Grant auto-expires]
  E --> R[Re-evaluate next time - per request]
```

## Elevation, step by step

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant A as Admin
  participant P as PIM system
  participant App as Target app / cloud
  participant Log as Audit store

  A->>P: request role "Global Admin" for 30 min
  Note over P: approved - MFA required, reason logged
  P->>App: activate role with activation notice
  App->>A: elevated access to scope
  A->>App: perform task
  App->>Log: every action attributed to A + activation ID
  Note over P: timer ends - role deactivated automatically
```

The properties that make PIM meaningful: **MFA on the request**, a **reason** that reviewers can challenge, a **scope** (which resource, not "everything"), an **expiry** that is enforced not remembered, and **audit logs** that tie each elevated action to its activation.

## Standing access vs just-in-time

| | Standing access | Just-in-time (PIM) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| State by default | Always elevated | User only (least privilege) |
| Window | Permanent | Minutes-hours, enforced |
| When attacks happen | Credential steal = instant admin | Steal still needs approval + MFA + reason |
| Review burden | "Why is everyone still admin?" | Per-activation, auditable |
| Risk profile | Fat target | Sparse, recorded |

PIM is **zero trust for humans**: just like short-lived workload credentials ([workload identity](../workload-identity-and-mtls)), the value is in the *living* time window.

## Break-glass access

PIM must never become a lockout. **Break-glass** is a separate, rarely-used, heavily-audited path (paper-sealed credentials, dormant emergency accounts, a runbook) for when normal processes are down. Govern it: credentials in a sealed vault, alerts on any use, quarterly verification that it still works — and that its use is treated as an incident until proven benign.

## Tester checklist

- Activate a role and confirm **auto-expiry** revokes access on schedule even if the user forgets to end it.
- Verify the approval gate cannot be bypassed: no MFA, no reason, no role — and no one can self-approve.
- Audit drill: pick a completed activation and trace every elevated action back to that activation ID and reason.
- Test break-glass end-to-end: sign in with the emergency creds, confirm the incident alert fired, and that creds still work after six months of no use.

## Related systems

- [Zero Trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — the umbrella PIM serves
- [Identity and access management](../identity-and-access-management) — the parent IAM layer
- [Conditional access and policy engines](../conditional-access-and-policy-engines) — the signals that gate elevation requests
- [Joiner-mover-leaver](../joiner-mover-leaver) — where standing privileges get cleaned up for humans who leave87:Te0e,
## What this system is

Procurement (source-to-pay) is the **buying chain**: from finding a supplier, through contracting and ordering, to paying the invoice. The ERP owns the financial tail; procurement systems own the process spine. The goal is *controlled spend* — every purchase approved, captured, and matched before money moves.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  SR[Supplier and sourcing] --> CT[Contract]
  CT --> RQ[Purchase requisition]
  RQ --> PO[Purchase order]
  PO --> GR[Goods receipt]
  GR --> IV[Invoice]
  IV --> MT{3-way match}
  MT -->|match| PY[Payment]
  MT -->|mismatch| EX[Exception - hold]
```

## The core documents

| Document | Who creates it | What it captures | The control it provides |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Purchase requisition | Requester | What + why + budget | Approval routing before any commitment |
| Purchase order | Buyer | Supplier, items, price, terms | Legal commitment to buy |
| Goods receipt | Warehouse | What actually arrived | Physical truth of delivery |
| Invoice | Supplier / AP | What they want paid | The ask for money |

## The 3-way match

The 3-way match is the beating heart of source-to-pay: **PO price vs invoice price vs receipt quantity**. If all three agree, the invoice clears. If not, it sits on a hold until a person resolves it. This one control prevents most overpayment and duplicate-payment fraud — and it is exactly the behavior to verify in testing.

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant Buyer
  participant AP as Accounts payable
  participant Sys as ERP/Procurement
  Buyer->>Sys: PO created and approved
  Sys->>Warehouse: receive goods, post receipt
  Warehouse->>Sys: goods receipt (qty + quality ok)
  Supplier->>AP: invoice submitted
  AP->>Sys: invoice → 3-way match
  alt PO = invoice = receipt
    Sys->>Pay: auto-clears for payment
  else price or qty mismatch
    Sys->>Hold: invoice on hold, workflow routed
  end
```

The failure modes to care about: **partial or over shipments** (receipt qty vs PO qty), **price drift** (invoice deviates from PO), and **duplicate invoices** (same invoice number posted twice across a supplier portal and EDI).

## The request → happy path

```mermaid
stateDiagram-v2
  [*] --> Req: create
  Req: Requisition
  Req --> PO: approve + commit
  PO: Purchase order
  PO --> GR: receive
  GR: Goods receipt
  GR --> Inv: 3-way match
  Inv: Invoice
  Inv --> Pay: cleared
  Pay: Payment
  Pay --> [*]
```

## Tester checklist

- 3-way match: over-shipment, under-shipment, price drift, and duplicate invoice each must land the right hold state and route to the right resolver.
- Requisition approval: an approver chain must be enforced by budget and spend category, with no bypass path.
- GR/IR (goods receipt / invoice receipt) reconciliation: unmatched receipts and unmatched invoices must each surface in a clean exception report.
- VAT/tax: line tax must be derived from the right tax code and jurisdiction on both invoice and payment.
- Integration: an invoice arriving via supplier portal vs EDI must behave identically.
