- [ERP systems](../enterprise-resource-planning) — source-to-pay is one of the five ERP chains
- [Warehouse management systems](../warehouse-management-systems) — the goods-receipt end of the chain
- [API request lifecycle](../api-request-lifecycle) — the request path behind supplier portals and EDI
- [Message queue delivery](../message-queue-delivery) — decoupling receipt posting from invoice arrival
- [Data governance and catalog](../data-governance-and-catalog) — supplier master and spend data governance88:Te7b,
## What this system is

A **topic** in a pub/sub broker (Kafka, GCP Pub/Sub, SNS, RabbitMQ topic exchanges) decouples the thing that *happens* from everything that *reacts*. The moment an event lands, every subscriber that cares gets a copy — checkout service listeners, analytics pipelines, email senders, AI feature stores.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  P[Checkout service] -->|publish order.placed| B[Broker: order.placed topic]
  B -->|fan-out copy| EM[Consumer: emailer]
  B -->|fan-out copy| AN[Consumer: analytics]
  B -->|fan-out copy| RE[Consumer: recommender]
  EM -->|at-least-once → dedupe| C[Persist id + commit offset]
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  P[Publisher<br/>checkout service] -->|event: order.placed| T{Order.placed<br/>topic}
  T --> C1[Email service]
  T --> C2[Inventory update]
  T --> C3[Analytics sink]
  T --> C4[AI recommender]
```

## Queue vs pub/sub

| | Queue (SQS, Rabbit work queue) | Pub/Sub (Kafka topics, SNS) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Semantics | One consumer takes each message (competing consumers) | Every subscribed consumer group gets its own copy |
| Suits | Work distribution — jobs, tasks | Fan-out — events many systems must react to |
| State | Message deleted on ack | Retained in the topic (by retention/compaction) |

## Partitions and consumer groups

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  subgraph Topic["Topic: order.placed · 3 partitions"]
    P1[partition 0]
    P2[partition 1]
    P3[partition 2]
  end

  subgraph Group["Consumer group: recommender"]
    W1[consumer 1]
    W2[consumer 2]
  end

  P1 --> W1
  P2 --> W1
  P3 --> W2
```

Brokers scale a topic by splitting it into **partitions** (segments). Within a partition, order is preserved; across partitions it is not. A **consumer group** splits partitions among its members for parallel processing — but if you read in groups, you trade global order for throughput.

## The full journey of one event

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant P as Checkout service
  participant B as Broker
  participant C as Email consumer
  participant S as Email API

  P->>B: publish order.placed (key=order-42)
  B->>B: append to partition by key
  C->>B: poll (groupId=emailer)
  B-->>C: event + offset 481
  C->>S: send confirmation email
  C->>C: persist processed id order-42
  C->>B: commit offset 481
```

Note the guard: the consumer **commits the offset only after the side effect and its idempotency marker are persisted**. Crash between send and commit → redelivery → the `order-42` dedupe marker prevents the second email.

## Design questions that decide everything

| Question | Trade-off it exposes |
| --- | --- |
| One consumer or many consumers per topic? | Queue vs pub/sub semantics |
| Required ordering across all events or per key? | Partitioning strategy |
| Read-again or read-once? | Retention, replay, compaction |
| Deadline when a consumer stalls? | Rebalance, dead-letter, lag alerting |
| What happens to a poison event? | When it retries infinitely |

## Tester checklist

- Publish one event and confirm *all* subscribed groups receive it (fan-out), not just one.
- Crash a consumer mid-processing and confirm redelivery plus no duplicate side effect.
- Verify ordering guarantees you actually promised: same-partition ordered, cross-partition not.
- Check consumer lag is visible and alerting exists before queues grow unbounded.

## Related systems

- [Message queue delivery](../message-queue-delivery) — the competing-consumer counterpart to publish/subscribe
- [Change data capture](../change-data-capture) — how databases publish events without application changes
- [Webhook integrations](../webhook-integrations) — the pull-based cousin of pushing events89:T172c,
## What this system is

REST (Representational State Transfer) is a **style**, not a standard: the idea that your API is a set of **resources** (nouns) manipulated with a small set of **verbs** (HTTP methods), where each response carries enough to know what's next, and caching is an explicit part of the protocol. A well-designed REST API is boring in the best way — predictable, consistent, and learnable in an afternoon.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  C[Client] -->|HTTP verb + path| API[API<br/>resource model]
  API -->|read| R1["GET /users/{id}"]
  API -->|create| R2[POST /users]
  API -->|partial update| R3["PATCH /users/{id}"]
  R1 -->|200| Out[JSON + status code]
  R2 -->|201| Out
  R3 -->|200| Out
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  C[Client] -->|POST /users| S[Server]
  S -->|201 + Location header| C
  C -->|GET /users/42| S
  S -->|200 JSON| C
  C -->|PUT vs PATCH /users/42| S
  S -->|200 / 204| C
```

## Resources and verbs: the core contract

| Method | Semantics | Idempotent? | Typical status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `GET` | Read a resource or collection | Yes | 200 |
| `POST` | Create a new resource (or trigger an action) | No | 201 + `Location` |
| `PUT` | Replace a resource fully | Yes | 200 / 204 |
| `PATCH` | Partial update — send only changed fields | No | 200 / 204 |
| `DELETE` | Remove a resource | Yes | 204 |

Rules that prevent most design drift:

- **URLs are nouns, never verbs.** `POST /users/{id}/send` is a smell; a verb usually hides a missing resource (maybe `POST /users/{id}/email-verifications` is what you meant).
- **Plural collections, singular items**: `GET /users`, `GET /users/{id}`. Consistency beats cleverness.
- **Nested only for true sub-resources**: `GET /users/{id}/orders` — but a *link* (`/orders?userId=42`) is often more flexible and avoids deep nesting.
- **Idempotency matters for retries**: POST isn't idempotent, so either make creation keyed by the client (`Idempotency-Key` header) or document that double-POST double-creates.
- **PUT vs PATCH**: PUT replaces the whole entity (missing fields = cleared); PATCH merges only what you send. Confusing them is a data-loss bug.

## Status codes: the shared vocabulary

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Req[Request] --> A{2xx?}
  A -->|2xx| Ok[Success - proceed]
  A -->|3xx| R[Redirect - follow Location]
  A -->|4xx| C[Client error - fix the request]
  A -->|5xx| S[Server error - retry with backoff]
```

| Range | Meaning | Examples you'll actually use |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 200-299 | Success | 200 OK, 201 Created, 204 No Content, 202 Accepted |
| 300-399 | Redirect | 304 Not Modified (caching!), 301/308 |
| 400-499 | Client fault | 400 Bad Request, 401 Unauthorized, 403 Forbidden, 404, 409 Conflict, 422 Unprocessable |
| 500-599 | Server fault | 500, 502/504 behind a gateway |

The distinctions that win arguments: **401 vs 403** (not authenticated vs not allowed), **404 vs 403** (not found vs hidden — hide resource existence from unauthorized users), **409** (state conflict: version mismatch, duplicate) vs **422** (validation failed but the resource *could* be valid with different input).

## Versioning, pagination, and errors: decisions you make once

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Q[Versioning choices] --> A1[URL: /v2/users]
  Q --> A2[Header: Accept: application/vnd.api+json;version=2]
  Q --> A3[Query param: ?api-version=2]
  A1 --> R1[Clear, cacheable, copy-and-paste friendly]
  A2 --> R2[Cleaner URLs, invisible in logs]
  A3 --> R3[Simple but muddies your cache key]
```

- **Version your URL** (`/v2/...`) unless you have a strong reason not to — it's the only option where the version is visible everywhere, including CDN cache keys. **Deprecate, don't break**: a 410 Gone with a `Deprecation` header beats silently changing semantics.
- **Pagination**: cursor-based (`?cursor=...`) is the modern default for big lists (stable across inserts); page-based (`?page=2&limit=50`) is simpler but shifts as new rows arrive. Always return metadata about the page in the response, and a stable order.
- **Errors are data, not strings**: a consistent shape (`{ "error": { "code", "message", "details", "request_id" } }`) so clients can switch on `code`, show the `message`, and give you the `request_id` to debug.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  P[GET /users?limit=50] --> P1[page 1 + next cursor]
  P1 --> P2[page 2 + next cursor]
  P2 --> P3[page 3 - no next cursor]
```

## Caching: REST's forgotten superpower

- `GET` responses are **cacheable by design** — set `Cache-Control` and `ETag`, and let the [edge cache](../api-gateway-caching) do its job.
- Never cache `POST`/`PUT`/`DELETE` bodies, and mark user-specific data `Cache-Control: private`.
- **`304 Not Modified`** with `If-None-Match` is how you turn "recheck every 5 minutes" into almost-free polling.

## Tester checklist

- Verify verbs do what they say: `PUT` clears absent fields, `PATCH` doesn't; double-check idempotency by replaying a `PUT`/`DELETE` with the same payload.
- Confirm the error contract: every 4xx has the same shape, a machine-readable `code`, and a correlating `request_id`.
- Test status-code intent: 401 when unauthenticated, 403 when unauthorized, 404 for a nonexistent id, and — importantly — that unauthorized access to an existing id looks identical to a 404.
- Validate pagination stability: insert rows between page fetches and confirm cursor pagination doesn't skip or duplicate (page-based will).
- Confirm caching: `GET` responses carry `Cache-Control`/`ETag`, and a conditional re-request returns `304`.

## Related systems

- [API request lifecycle](../api-request-lifecycle) — the chain a REST call travels
- [GraphQL APIs](../graphql-apis) — the same job with a different query model
- [API gateway caching](../api-gateway-caching) — the cache layer REST is designed for
- [Authentication vs authorization](../authentication-vs-authorization) — who the 401/403 actually talk about8a:Tf14,
## What this system is

RBAC is the most common authorization model because it matches how organizations already think: **people hold roles**, roles hold permissions, and roles are assigned based on job function. Instead of a thousand Allow rules per user, you define a handful of roles and grant them at scale.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    U[User A] -- member of --> G[Group - Developers]
    G -- assigned --> R[Role - dev-read-logs]
    R -- bundles --> P[Permission - s3:GetObject]
    P -- effect allow --> RS[Resource - logs at scope]
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  U1[User A] --> G1[Group: Developers]
  U2[User B] --> G1
  G1 --> R1[Role: code-writer]
  G1 --> R2[Role: read-prod-logs]
  R1 --> P1[Push to repo]
  R2 --> P2[Read log bucket]
```

## The three pieces

| Piece | What it is | Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Permission** | The smallest actionable right | `s3:GetObject` on `logs/` |
| **Role** | A named bundle of permissions | `dev-read-logs` |
| **Assignment** | A user/group linked to a role, often with a scope | User A ← `dev-read-logs` *on prod* |

Effective access = **the union of all assignments** (both direct and via groups). Never let that fractal fact surprise you: nested groups multiply. "Why can they see it?" is usually answered by a cluster of groups, not a single row.

## How authority should flow

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant M as Hiring manager
  participant L as Identity team / system
  participant U as User

  M->>L: "Hire Alice as Senior Dev" (HR-driven)
  L->>L: auto-assign: Developers + dev-read-logs(on dev)
  L->>L: no prod write by default
  Note over L: removal is automatic on offboard - not a someday
```

The healthy pattern: **roles come from HR/manager input, not engineer discretion**. If a developer can mint themselves `prod-admin`, RBAC is decoration. Which brings us to the rule that hurts:

## Separation of duties and the access review

- **Separation of duties (SoD)**: the person who *writes* a payment amount must not be the one who *approves* it. RBAC encodes this as incompatible roles — assigning both is an alert.
- **Access reviews**: because roles drift (people move, gain rights "for a task", never lose them), reviews are the periodic audit: *"does this role still describe this person?"* Automate the invitation; make "no answer" become removal, never retention.

## RBAC vs ABAC (and the other letters)

| | RBAC | ABAC / conditional access |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Decides by | What role the user holds | Context attributes (device, time, location, risk) |
| Setup | Roles + assignments | Policies over attributes |
| The fail mode | Role explosion, stale grants | Policy sprawl, opaque denials |
| Where they meet | Coarse roles | Narrowing *when* the role works |

Prod: RBAC says "you may", conditional access ([policy engines](../conditional-access-and-policy-engines)) says "but only from a healthy device this hour."

## Tester checklist

- Recreate a user in a role and enumerate *effective* access including nested groups — the union must match intent.
- Break SoD: assign a user two incompatible roles and confirm the system flags the assignment.
- Run an access review as "no answer = remove" and confirm removals actually revoke, not just disappear from the review list.
- Check that *no one* can self-grant a role with prod-write; even admins go through an approval (see [PIM](../privileged-identity-management)).

## Related systems

- [Identity and access management](../identity-and-access-management) — the parent of this model
- [Privileged identity management](../privileged-identity-management) — the high-value roles inherit these rules
- [Single sign-on (SSO)](../single-sign-on-sso) — authn via SSO, authz via the roles here
- [SCIM provisioning](../scim-identity-provisioning) — how roles actually get there when someone joins8b:T1f36,
## What this system is

Salesforce is the dominant **CRM platform** — but the phrase hides a bigger fact: "Salesforce" is a development platform wearing a CRM hat. The mental model that makes everything click is **metadata, not code**: almost every customization is configuration stored as metadata (objects, fields, page layouts, flows, permission sets), and a tiny amount is real code (Apex, a Java-like language). "Building in Salesforce" means pushing metadata to an org — which is why it integrates, reviews, and *releases* differently from a codebase.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph Org["Sales org"]
    LF[Leads] --> O[Opportunities]
    O --> C[Contracts - via CPQ]
    A[Accounts - the company view]
    CT[Contacts - people within accounts]
    CSE[Service cloud - cases]
    MK[Marketing cloud - journeys]
  end
  subgraph Platform["The platform"]
    MET[Metadata - objects, fields, flows]
    FL[Flows - automation]
    AP[Apex - the edge cases]
    UI[Lightning UI + app builder]
  end
  Org --> Platform
```

## The data model: Salesforce's five core objects, minimally

| Object | The row meaning | The question it answers |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `Account` | A company (or household/org) | "Which companies do we touch?" |
| `Contact` | A person **inside** an account | "Who is the human on the account?" |
| `Lead` | An unqualified prospect not yet an account/contact | "Who might become a customer?" |
| `Opportunity` | A potential sale with stage, amount, close date | "What is the pipeline?" |
| `Case` | A support record (Service Cloud) | "What are customers asking?" |

Every enterprise adds its **own objects** (custom objects + custom fields on those five) — `InsurancePolicy__c`, `LoanApplication__c` — which is what makes Salesforce universal: it is a CRM core with an extensible schema. The `__c` suffix is the tell: names ending `__c` are custom, names without it are platform objects. That distinction governs every "whose data is this?" argument.

## Config vs code: the two-speed model

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph Config["Configuration (no deploy - switch on in the org)"]
    OBJ[Create objects + fields]
    LAY[Page layouts + Lightning app builder]
    FL[Flows - automation]
    PERM[Permission sets - who sees what]
  end
  subgraph Code["Code (a real release pipeline)"]
    AP[Apex]
    TR[Triggers]
    VF[LWC / Lightning Web Components]
    SOAP[API integrations]
  end
  Trust{Where does the change live?} --> Config
  Trust --> Code
```

The classic governance failure: **an admin clicks a field into existence, but a developer needed it, and now a production page breaks for a segment.** Salesforce's answer exists in the platform — change sets, sandboxes, and a proper metadata release via SFDX/adopting their DevOps tooling — but the *culture* of "config is change" has to be adopted too, because a config change is a production change even when it has no deployment pipeline.

## Flows and Apex: automation decisions

| Approach | What it is | Reach for when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Flow** | Point-and-click declarative automation (no-code) | Classic business rules: field updates, email, record creation, approvals, screen steps |
| **Formula fields** | On-the-fly calculated values | Simple derived data, no side effects |
| **Apex** | The Java-like language | Real computation, integration to external systems, Batch/DML-heavy jobs, anything flows can't express |
| **Triggers** | Apex fire on record save | Side effects beyond what `after update` flows cover — with governor limits in mind |

The default posture that survives contact with production: **flows first, Apex only when flows hit their limits.** Flows are inspectable, admin-maintainable, and safer; Apex is faster and more powerful but becomes technical debt when overused and is subject to **governor limits** (how many SOQL queries, DML statements, and heap per request) that force batch-aware design at scale.

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant U as User / system
  participant F as Flow (declarative)
  participant A as Apex (when needed)
  participant DB as Salesforce DB

  U->>F: record saved / button clicked
  F->>F: update fields, send email, route
  alt complex or external
    F->>A: invoke Apex (bulk-safe)
    A->>DB: SOQL + DML within governor limits
  end
```

## Integration: how the rest of the world reaches in

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  ERP[ERP / finance - invoices, orders] -->|REST / SOAP API| SF[Salesforce]
  SF -->|outbound webhook| M[Middleware - OIC / Azure integration / Mule]
  M --> O[Other SaaS + data warehouse]
  BI[BI tools] -->|Analytics API| SF
  PY[Other code] --> R[REST - consumer key + secret]
  R --> SF
```

- **REST/SOAP APIs** are first-class: every object + custom object is exposable, authenticated with a connected app (consumer key + secret). This is how an ERP pushes orders and how a warehouse pulls the pipeline.
- **Outbound integrations** usually travel through middleware (OIC, Azure integration services, MuleSoft) rather than letting every tool talk directly — the same front-door lesson as API Management.
- **The special Salesforce gotcha is data fallout**: because the schema is deeply configurable, an integration that "just syncs opportunities" must handle custom fields, picklist values, and record types or it mis-maps data to the wrong picklist — test with a real shape.

## Salesforce vs ServiceNow vs CRM

| Judgment | Salesforce | ServiceNow | When you choose the other |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Core purpose | Customer-facing (sales, marketing, service to customers) | Employee-facing service management | Not customer revenue — ServiceNow for internal ops |
| Data model | Sales + your objects | IT/ESM tables + CMDB | You're tracking assets, not pipeline |
| Automation | Flows + Apex | Flow designer + platform forms | Either works; pick the vendor's ecosystem |
| Fit signal | Pre-sales → revenue → support | Incident → change → asset | Division of labor: you may run both, integrated |

They overlap more than vendors admit — both are low-code record platforms with workflow engines and a "native, buy-vendors-call-it" integration story. The practical split is **customers vs employees**: [CRM systems](../crm-systems) is customer real; [ServiceNow ESM](../servicenow-esm) is employee-service real. Enterprises commonly run *both*, connected through the middleware of their choice.

## Tester checklist

- **Object drill**: create an Account → Contact → Opportunity and verify the relationship links and required-field rules actually fire.
- **Flow behavior**: a Flow-triggered field update must run on create *and* update, with the right order, and must not fire twice when the record is re-saved.
- **Governor limits**: a bulk Apex job inserting 500 records must complete within SOQL/DML limits or throw a catchable exception — not an empty silent skip.
- **Picklist fidelity**: an integration posting a picklist value must fail loudly (or map) on a mismatched value, not silently save blank.
- **API auth**: a connected-app REST call with the wrong secret must 401 cleanly, and a valid one must respect the org's permission sets (not just "API enabled").
- **Deploy parity**: a sandbox-tested configuration change must behave identically when released to production via the metadata release path.

## Related systems

- [CRM systems](../crm-systems) — the category and decision framework Salesforce sits inside
- [ServiceNow ESM](../servicenow-esm) — the employee-facing sibling platform it gets integrated with
- [Oracle Integration Cloud (OIC)](../oracle-integration-cloud) and [Azure integration services](../azure-integration-services) — the middleware that connects Salesforce to ERP
- [Enterprise resource planning (ERP)](../enterprise-resource-planning) — the back-office systems Salesforce syncs orders and invoices with
- [Webhook integrations](../webhook-integrations) — the push pattern for outbound Salesforce events8c:Teec,
## What this system is

SSO answers *"may this user log in?"*; **SCIM** answers *"does this user exist in the app at all?"* (System for Cross-domain Identity Management, SCIM 2.0). It's a **REST + JSON** standard: the identity store (the source of truth) pushes users, groups, and roles into each connected app over a standard `/Users` and `/Groups` API — so onboarding and offboarding become automation, not tickets.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  HR[(HR system - source of truth)] -->|join / move / leave event| ST[Identity store / IdP]
  ST -->|changes to sync| CL[SCIM client]
  CL -->|create user| US[App A - POST /Users]
  CL -->|assign groups| GR[App B - PUT /Groups]
  CL -->|active true| RD[Account ready - before first login]
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  HR[(HR system - source of truth)] --> IdP[Identity store / IdP]
  IdP -->|SCIM push| App1[App A - /Users]
  IdP -->|SCIM push| App2[App B - /Groups]
  IdP -->|SCIM push| App3[App C - roles mapping]
  IdP -->|SCIM delete on leave| App1
  IdP -->|SCIM delete on leave| App2
```

## The standard in one picture

| SCIM resource | What it represents | Key fields |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `/Users` | One person's account | `userName`, `emails`, `active`, `groups` |
| `/Groups` | A collection that becomes app roles | `displayName`, `members` |
| `/ServiceProviderConfig` | What this app supports | schemes, patch, bulk |

Operations are plain HTTP semantics: `GET` read, `POST` create, `PUT`/`PATCH` update, `DELETE` remove. A `bearer token` in the client authenticates the pushes; TLS + that bearer token is the whole security model.

## The typical sync flow

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant IdP as Identity provider
  participant App as SaaS app / directory
  participant User as New hire

  IdP->>IdP: HR event: user.department = engineering
  IdP->>App: POST /Users { userName, email, active:true }
  App-->>IdP: 201 created with app user id
  IdP->>App: PUT /Groups/dev-group members + new user
  App->>User: app account exists before first login
  Note over IdP: on termination - PATCH active:false and group removal
```

The result worth bragging about: a new person's accounts **exist before they need them**, and a leaver's accounts die the day they leave — because provisioning is driven by the [joiner-mover-leaver](../joiner-mover-leaver) events, not by someone's 4pm Friday ticket.

## SCIM vs SSO (the pair everyone conflates)

| | SSO | SCIM |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Asks | *Who are you?* (authentication) | *Should this account exist?* (provisioning) |
| Carry | Assertions/tokens | User/group JSON objects |
| Lifecycle | Sessions, logout | Create, update, suspend, delete |
| Wrong without | (still works, one by one) | you get shadow/ghost accounts |

An app with SSO *and* no SCIM users "logs in" but never gets accounts created, updated, or removed — the classic ghost-account factory.

## Tester checklist

- Trigger a join event end-to-end: SCIM creates the user *before* first sign-on, in the same groups as their role.
- Test the leaver path: `active:false` + group removal fire promptly — verify the app can't be logged into afterwards.
- Replay resilience: SCIM is idempotent-by-design; a retried `POST` must not duplicate the user (check `externalId`/`userName`).
- Check deprovisioning really deletes or suspends in the *app*, and that SCIM bearer tokens are scoped per-app, not shared.

## Related systems

- [Identity and access management](../identity-and-access-management) — the IAM layer SCIM wires together
- [Joiner-mover-leaver](../joiner-mover-leaver) — the lifecycle events SCIM executes
- [Single sign-on (SSO)](../single-sign-on-sso) — the login half that pairs with provisioning
- [Integration patterns](../integration-patterns) — SCIM is an integration pattern (API + events) in identity form8d:Tf54,
## What this system is

Wide-area networking decides how your offices, data centers, and cloud VPCs reach each other. **SD-WAN** is the modern answer to the old trade-off between MPLS (guaranteed but expensive) and VPN-over-the-internet (cheap but best-effort): overlay tunnels across *multiple* ordinary internet links, centrally orchestrated, steering traffic per-application.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  subgraph Branch["Branch"]
    APP["Branch app traffic"] -- "classify app" --> EDGE["SD-WAN edge device"]
  end
  subgraph Control["Orchestration"]
    CTL["Controller:<br/>policy engine"]
  end
  subgraph Underlays["Underlay links"]
    L1["Low-jitter fiber"]
    L2["High-throughput 5G"]
  end
  EDGE -- "best path?" --> CTL
  CTL -- "voice - low jitter" --> L1
  CTL -- "bulk sync" --> L2
  L1 -- "IPsec tunnel" --> HUB["Cloud / HQ gateway"]
  L2 -- "IPsec tunnel" --> HUB
```

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  subgraph Branch["Branch office: Madrid"]
    E1[Local LAN]
    R1[SD-WAN edge device]
  end

  subgraph Underlay["Underlay links"]
    L1[Fiber ISP]
    L2[5G / LTE]
  end

  subgraph HQ["HQ + cloud"]
    R2[SD-WAN controller / hub]
    V1[VPC / SaaS]
  end

  E1 --> R1
  R1 --> L1
  R1 --> L2
  L1 --> R2
  L2 --> R2
  R2 --> V1
```

## WAN evolution

| | MPLS | VPN / IPsec over internet | SD-WAN |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| What you buy | Carrier-managed QoS between sites | Ordinary broadband links | Broadband + software overlay |
| Reliability | Guaranteed SLA | Single best-effort link | Multiple links, seamless failover |
| Cost | High per-site | Low | Low, plus edge hardware |
| Steering | Static/carrier policy | Per-device config | Application/cloud-aware, centrally managed |
| Today's role | Legacy gold standard | Simple point-to-point | Standard for multi-site + cloud |

## How the overlay works

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  A[App traffic: MS Teams / API / SAP] --> B[Policy engine<br/>classifies traffic]
  B --> C{Best path for this app?}
  C -->|real-time voice| D[Lowest jitter link]
  C -->|batch ingest| E[Highest throughput link]
  D --> F[IPsec tunnel over link A]
  E --> G[IPsec tunnel over link B]
  F --> H[Hub / cloud gateway]
  G --> H
```

The three pieces you should be able to explain:

1. **Edge device** per site — terminates multiple underlay links (fiber, cable, 5G) and builds encrypted tunnels.
2. **Controller** — central brain that pushes policies and monitors link health; without it you have fancy VPNs, not SD-WAN.
3. **Application-aware policy** — MS Teams goes on the low-latency path, bulk sync on the cheap one. When a link dies, sessions re-route over the survivor in seconds.

## The cloud edge

SD-WAN reaches cloud the same way it reaches HQ — a tunnel into the cloud provider's gateway (Direct Connect / ExpressRoute) or a cloud-hosted SD-WAN instance. Consistent policy across office, data center, VPC. This is why enterprises retire remote-office VPN concentrators: the branch device *is* the VPN endpoint, covered by [zero trust](../zero-trust-architecture) as a bonus.

## Tester checklist

- Drill a failover: kill the primary link mid-session with a live VoIP/streaming call and confirm no drop (or a sub-3s re-route, per your SLA).
- Verify path steering really diverges per app — a bulk job and a voice call must take different underlays *simultaneously*.
- Check that cloud-tunneled traffic follows the same policies as branch traffic (one policy engine, not two).
- Confirm underlay link quality metrics (jitter/loss/throughput) are visible and alert on degradation — not just on outages.

## Related systems

- [Network routing and BGP](../network-routing-and-bgp) — how the underlying paths and BGP policies behave beneath the overlay
- [TCP/IP and TLS](../tcp-ip-and-tls) — the transport the tunnels encapsulate
- [Zero trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — overlay access enforced regardless of network position8e:Te2d,
## What this system is

Every identity in the enterprise — user tokens, TLS, mTLS, code signing, SSH — leans on two trust primitives: **certificates** (who can prove identity; backed by a CA hierarchy = PKI) and **secrets** (keys/tokens/passwords that unlock access). Zero Trust turns both into the same idea: *short-lived, automatically rotated, central-managed* — never hand-written, never hard-coded, never long-lived.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  Root[Root CA - offline, highest trust] --> Inter[Intermediate CA]
  Inter --> Leaf1[Site certs - *.example.com]
  Inter --> Leaf2[mTLS / workload SVIDs]
  Inter --> Leaf3[Code-signing / SSH signed keys]
  Root --> HSM[HSM stores CA keys - cannot be copied]
  Leaf1 --> Chain[Browser / client validates chain]
```

## The certificate lifecycle

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Issue[Cerify via ACME / RA] --> Deploy[Deploy to endpoints]
  Deploy --> Monitor[Monitor expiry + usage]
  Monitor -->|~90% lifetime| Renew[Automated renewal]
  Renew --> Issue
  Monitor -->|compromise detected| Revoke[Revoke + CRL / OCSP]
```

The modern rules are boring on purpose:
1. **Short lifetimes** (90 days is legacy-long; hours-to-days for workload identities) shrink the blast radius of a stolen cert.
2. **Automation everywhere** — ACME/SPIRE-style issuance means nothing is issued by hand, signed by hand, renewed by hand.
3. **Revocation actually propagates** — CRL/OCSP when CA is compromised; for short-lived certs you often *wait out* expiry instead of relying on revocation.

## Vaults: static vs dynamic secrets

| | Static secret | Dynamic / short-lived |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Example | Long-lived API key | Per-task DB credentials, one-time tokens |
| Lifetime | Years by accident | Seconds-to-minutes by construction |
| Rotation | Sometimes, painful | Automatic, constant |
| Leakage cost | Full access until rotated | Expires before it matters |
| Vault role | Store + access control | Generate on demand, lease with TTL |

**Vault-style systems** (HashiCorp Vault, cloud secret managers) do both: central storage + a **lease model** where services pull credentials for a window and they die after. The hard part is teaching every app to *fetch, not hard-code*.

## The failure modes that still hurt

- **Hard-coded secrets** shipped in images/CI logs — the leak that outlives two "rotations."
- **Long-lived root material** used for work (it should be offline behind an HSM).
- **Certificate expiry** on the one internal endpoint the outage-call answered with "it expired" — solved by monitoring + automation, not reminders.
- **A single trust root in software**: a CA key inside a binary the server ship accidentally contains. HSM containment or a strict split is non-negotiable.

## Tester checklist

- Pull a dynamic DB credential from the vault and confirm it expires on its lease even if the app forgets to release it.
- Rotate a TLS leaf cert mid-traffic and confirm zero-downtime drop-in (automated path, not manual).
- Compromise drill: a stolen short-lived credential must grant *bounded* time, and an HSM-bound CA key must be uncopyable.
- Audit for hard-coded secrets with a repo scan and prove the CI gate blocks them.

## Related systems

- [Zero Trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — why identities are short-lived machines, not names in a file
- [Workload identity and mTLS](../workload-identity-and-mtls) — certificates *as* workload identity
- [Identity and access management](../identity-and-access-management) — the humans this crypto validates
- [TCP/IP and TLS](../tcp-ip-and-tls) — the transport every cert ultimately protects8f:Tf91,
## What this system is

The **SOC** is the team and tooling that watches your estate for attacks, not outages. Its pipeline: collect security telemetry (cloud audit logs, endpoint, network), correlate it in a **SIEM** to surface detections, triage and investigate, then respond through **SOAR** playbooks — containment, remediation, and a post-incident review that makes the next one cheaper.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Tele[Telemetry: CloudTrail / Activity Log, GuardDuty / Defender, EDR, flow logs] --> SIEM[SIEM: ingest + correlation]
  SIEM --> De[Detection rules / detection engineering]
  De --> Triage[Triage - real or noise?]
  Triage --> SOAR[SOAR playbook: isolate, revoke, snapshot]
  SOAR --> IR[Incident response process]
  IR --> Retro[Post-incident review feeds the loop]
  Retro --> De
```

## The threat-detection funnel

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  SRC[Telemetry sources] -->|stream| SIEM[SIEM - correlation]
  SIEM -->|alert on rule hit| TRI[Triage + investigate]
  SIEM -->|auto-playbook for known case| SOAR[SOAR playbooks]
  SIEM -->|confirmed incident| CON[Contain + remediate]
  TRI -->|escalate| CON
  SOAR -->|automated steps| CON
  CON -->|learn| LESS[Post-incident review]
```

## The core components and cloud-native mapping

| Layer | Job | AWS native | Azure native |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Audit trail | Who did what, immutable | CloudTrail + S3 | Activity Log + log retention |
| Automated detection | Known attack patterns | GuardDuty, Security Hub | Microsoft Defender for Cloud, Sentinel |
| SIEM | Ingest + correlate + alert | OpenSearch / Elastic | Microsoft Sentinel |
| SOAR | Playbook-ify response | Step Functions (custom) | Sentinel automation rules |
| EDR / posture | Endpoint + cloud posture | Systems Manager, Inspector | Defender for Endpoint |

The pattern to teach: **cloud audit logs are the base truth** — if you keep nothing else, keep CloudTrail/Activity Log immutable and searchable, because every DR and SOC answer starts there.

## The SOC's five responsibilities

1. **Detect** — detection rules must be tested, tuned, and updated; an alert that never fires (or always fires) is a failed detection.
2. **Triage** — fast "is this real + how bad?" — the funnel where alert fatigue is defeated (or wins).
3. **Respond** — playbooks with *documented automated* steps: isolate the instance, revoke the key, snapshot the volume before cleanup.
4. **Hunt** — proactive looking for what detections miss (assume-breach hunting, baseline anomalies).
5. **Improve** — every incident ends in a review; the output is improved detections, playbooks, and controls.

## The detection-playbook loop

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  A[New attack / suspicion] --> B[Write detection - SIEM rule]
  B --> C[Wire to playbook - SOAR]
  C --> D[Test with positive + negative cases]
  D --> E[Deploy to monitoring]
  E -->|fires on real case| F[Incident - run the loop again]
  F --> A
```

## Tester checklist

- Feed a known-bad pattern (an exfiltration-shaped download, a failed-admin logon storm) and confirm SIEM correlation → alert → playbook fires within your SLA.
- Filter the noise: a legitimate admin task must not trip the detection (false-positive test on both sides).
- Tabletop a pivot: attacker has an IAM key and a compute box — does the playbook isolate the box *and* revoke the key *and* snapshot before evidence is lost?
- Audit-drift check: can a compromised identity delete the audit log store? If yes, fix the trust boundary (immutable, separate account).

- [Cloud landing zones](../cloud-landing-zones) — where the security accounts and guardrails live
- [Monitoring and observability](../monitoring-and-observability) — the sibling pipeline for outages, sharing telemetry
- [Zero Trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — the posture SOC telemetry enforces
- [Identity and access management](../identity-and-access-management) — who-question every detection answers first90:Te0b,
## What this system is

Security standards get conflated because they answer *different* questions. The fastest way to use them well is to know the axis: **design** vs **implementation** vs **operation** vs **compliance**.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  subgraph Design["Design: how should access be shaped?"]
    ZT[Zero Trust - NIST SP 800-207<br/>never trust, verify every request]
    STRIDE[STRIDE threat modeling<br/>find design flaws early]
  end

  subgraph Implementation["Implementation: how should it be coded?"]
    OWASP[OWASP Top 10 + ASVS<br/>the weaknesses and their fixes]
    CIS[OWASP SAMM / CIS Benchmarks<br/>process + config hardening]
  end

  subgraph Operation["Operation: are we actually protected?"]
    CSF[NIST Cybersecurity Framework<br/>govern, identify, protect, detect, respond]
    MITRE[MITRE ATT&CK<br/>adversary playbooks for detection]
  end

  subgraph Compliance["Compliance: can we prove it to auditors?"]
    ISO[ISO 27001<br/>ISMS, audit cycle]
    SOC[SOC 2<br/>trust-services attestation]
  end

  Design --> Implementation --> Operation --> Compliance
```

## One standard per question

| Standard | Answers | When you reach for it |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Zero Trust (NIST 800-207)** | "How is access architected?" | Greenfield design / perimeter redesign |
| **STRIDE** | "Where are the design flaws?" | Every system before heavy implementation |
| **OWASP Top 10 / ASVS** | "Which implementation bugs matter?" | Coding, code review, pentest prep, CI gates |
| **NIST CSF** | "How mature is our whole program?" | Annual maturity assessment, board reporting |
| **MITRE ATT&CK** | "What attacks should we detect?" | SOC playbooks, red-team tests, detection engineering |
| **ISO 27001** | "Does an ISMS exist and get audited?" | When customers/venue require certification |
| **SOC 2** | "Can we attest the security of your service/vendor?" | Vendor due diligence, SaaS exporting |

## The triangle nobody draws

These are **not alternatives**; they compound. A mature org runs all four layers:

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  A[Architecture: Zero Trust + STRIDE] -->|decides| B[Build: OWASP + SAMM]
  B -->|runs on| C[Run: NIST CSF + ATT&CK]
  C -->|certifies| D[Prove: ISO 27001 + SOC 2]
  D -->|feeds back lessons| A
```

The classic mistakes:

- **Marrying one layer.** "We have SOC 2" says nothing about whether your code is safe (nor does Zero Trust); "we passed OWASP scan" says nothing about governance.
- **Reading the wrong axis.** Engineering trusts to Compliance; auditors get asked to design architecture; nobody owns Operation.
- **Standard as checklist art.** ISO 27001 can be fully attested while the product is exploitable — certification measures process, not product.

## So which one should *I* start with?

| You are | Start here |
| --- | --- |
| A solution architect designing a system | Zero Trust + STRIDE → OWASP when building |
| A developer writing the code | OWASP Top 10, then ASVS as a rubric |
| A security architect setting direction | Zero Trust + NIST CSF maturity plan |
| A tester or reviewer | OWASP Top 10 as your review/walkthrough framework |
| An enterprise architect running a program | ISO 27001/SOC 2 for the org, NIST CSF for risk |

## Related systems

- [Zero Trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — the design-layer deep dive
- [Threat modeling with STRIDE](../threat-modeling-stride) — design-time flaw hunting
- [The OWASP Top 10](../owasp-top-10) — the implementation-layer deep dive
- [TOGAF and the ADM](../togaf-framework) — where security architecture plugs into the ADM cycles91:T198c,
## What this system is

Server-side rendering is not a framework checkbox — it is a **pipeline**. A browser request is a query into a machine that resolves the route, loads data, walks the component tree, and emits HTML as a byte stream; the client then layers interactivity on top through **hydration**. Understand where the pipeline is slow and you understand every SSR performance story. Understand what it does *not* do — there is still a JS bundle to fetch — and you understand why SSR is not "the end of SPAs".

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Req[Request] --> Resolve[Resolve route]
  Resolve --> Load[Load data on server]
  Load --> Render[Render component tree to HTML]
  Render --> Stream[Stream HTML + data]
  Stream --> Client[Browser parses HTML]
  Client --> Hydrate[Hydration - attach handlers and state]
  Hydrate --> Done[Interactive page]
```

## The pipeline, step by step

| Stage | What happens | Cost you pay |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Route resolve | Server maps the URL to a component/preload entry | Library dispatch, negligible |
| Data load | Server-side fetching (DB, APIs) *before* HTML exists | **The dominant latency for dynamic pages** |
| Render | Component tree executes to a static HTML string | CPU work on the server |
| Stream | HTML flushes progressively; shell paints first | Chunked transfer, edge buffering |
| Hydrate | JS re-attaches state and event handlers to existing DOM | Bundle download + parse on the client |
| Settle | Event handlers active; page truly interactive | Framework reconciliation |

The trap: "$ quickly because it is server-rendered". Server HTML is **inert** — it has no state and no event handlers until shipped JS hydrates it. First byte can be fast while time-to-interactive still waits on the client bundle. SSR moved work from the browser to the server; hydration keeps some of it right where it was.

## SPA shell vs server HTML

| | SPA / CSR | SSR |
| --- | --- | --- |
| First request returns | Empty `<div id="root">` + many `<script>` tags | Full HTML with content, plus scripts |
| Data for the page | Fetched client-side after mount (spinner) | In the HTML payload, already rendered |
| No-JS / crawler | Blank shell | Content visible |
| Route changes | Client router swaps views, no full reload | Full round trip (unless client router is layered on) |
| Worst latency | Bundle parse + fetch waterfall | Server data load + bundle parse |

The mix — **client-side transitions on top of server-rendered HTML** — is literally the modern full-stack framework play: SSR the first paint, then replace the document with client-routed views so navigation never reloads.

## Hydration and its failure modes

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  Server[Server HTML] --> Match{Client-side render matches?}
  Match -->|Yes - reuse DOM nodes| OK[Hydrate in place - fast]
  Match -->|No - hydration mismatch| Warn(Console mismatch warning) --> Rerender[Framework discards and re-renders on client]
  Rerender --> Flicker[Flash of wrong content + wasted work]
```

A hydration mismatch is a debug canary: dates in the server's timezone, random values, `localStorage` reads, browser-width-dependent UI. Every mismatch means the framework throws away server work and re-renders on the client — the opposite of what SSR was for. Alternatives to full hydration: **partial hydration** (hydrate only islands), **resumability** (serialize the full app state, skip the replay of render work), and **streaming with selective hydration**.

## Streaming: the shell-vs-data split

Streaming SSR flushes the static frame of the page the instant it renders, while slow data-loading sections stream in on placeholder plates (React Suspense-style). TTFB drops to near-static speed; TTI only waits on the data the user actually needs. The cost: the response is a **chunked stream**, so downstream caches must flush headers correctly and you cannot simply store the whole response.

## Caching and the CDN edge

Per-request SSR is the expensive mode — the origin re-renders on every hit. Caching turns a dynamic page into a static asset:

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Origin[Origin server renders] --> H{Cache headers}
  H -->|Cache-Control: max-age, stale-while-revalidate| Edge[CDN edge cache near user]
  Edge --> Blade[Cached HTML to every user]
  Edge -->|Expired, background refresh| Origin
  H -->|no-store / private| Direct[Per-user response, no caching]
```

- **Public/short TTL** — pages that change rarely (blog posts, marketing) become edge-served static assets.
- **stale-while-revalidate** — serve the cached copy instantly, refill in the background; users never see a cache stampede.
- **no-store** — anything personalized (dashboards, checkout) must reach the origin every time; this is where SSR and SPA costs converge.
- **Edge rendering** — move the render itself to a CDN function (edge runtime) so the origin only supplies data; cuts round-trips for global audiences.

## The request → happy path

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Req[Request] -->|GET + route| Load[Server<br/>resolve + load data]
  Load -->|data ready| Render[Server render HTML]
  Load -->|streaming partial| Stream[Stream HTML + data]
  Render -->|full HTML| Paint[First paint<br/>inert HTML]
  Stream -->|script tags| Hydrate[Hydration JS bundle]
  Paint -->|TTI after parse| Ready[Interactive page]
  Hydrate -->|attach handlers| Ready
```

## Tester checklist

- Measure TTFB and TTI **separately**; SSR pages with a heavy bundle look fast on TTFB and lie on TTI.
- Render in a no-JS/crawler profile: content must be in the initial HTML, not injected after a fetch.
- Grep the browser console for hydration mismatch warnings — every one is discarded server work.
- Verify streaming reaches first paint before the slowest data plate resolves, and that the response is a proper chunked stream.
- Check cache headers on a personalized route: `no-store` must prevent an edge cache from serving another user's dashboard.
- Load-test the origin with a cold cache to confirm `stale-while-revalidate` avoids a wall-of-render stampede.

## Related systems

- [Frontend frameworks and rendering](../frontend-frameworks-and-rendering) — where the rendering models sit in the bigger picture
- [Full-stack frameworks](../fullstack-frameworks) — the frameworks that ship this pipeline out of the box
- [Backend web frameworks](../backend-web-frameworks) — the server machinery doing the render work
- [API gateway caching](../api-gateway-caching) — the caching layer that turns SSR pages into edge assets92:T1951,
## What this system is

Serverless means you never book capacity: you define a function and the platform runs it when an event arrives, scales it to zero when idle, and bills you per invocation and per millisecond. The flagship products are **AWS Lambda** and **Azure Functions** — the model is the same on both.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Client[Client / event source] -->|invoke| GW[API Gateway / Event Grid]
  GW -->|1 event in| F1[Function instance]
  GW -->|many events| F2[Function instance 2]
  GW -->|“scale up”| F3[Function instance 3]
  F1 -->|result| Client
```

The thing people miss: a function is **one unit of work**, not an application. An API with five endpoints is five functions (or one function with a router) plus the glue — the gateway, the queues, the state store — that connects them. The platform owns the server; you own the wiring.

## The execution model

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant C as Client
  participant G as Gateway
  participant P as Platform
  participant F as Function
  C->>G: HTTP request
  G->>P: route to function
  P->>P: warm or cold start?
  P->>F: execute handler
  F-->>G: response
  G-->>C: HTTP response
```

- **Invocation model**: you never start or stop anything — you define a trigger (HTTP, queue, timer), and the platform invokes your function for each event.
- **Per-invocation billing**: pay for requests, GB-seconds of memory, and duration. Idle time costs nothing.
- **Concurrency**: the platform spins up as many function instances as the event flow needs, up to your (safety) reserved concurrency limit.

## Cold starts and warm pools

The one failure mode every serverless app feels: a **cold start**. When no instance exists, the platform must bootstrap a runtime before your code runs — adding latency to that invocation (the first in a burst, or after idle expiry).

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  subgraph Cold["Cold start (the slow path)"]
    A1[Platform provisions container] --> A2[Runtime boots + deps loaded] --> A3[Your handler runs first time]
  end
  subgraph Warm["Warm start (the fast path)"]
    B1[Instance already running] --> B2[Handler runs immediately]
  end
  Event[Invocation arrives] --> Choice{Instance warm?}
  Choice -- no --> Cold
  Choice -- yes --> Warm
```

Mitigations that actually move the number:

- **Provisioned concurrency / always-on plans** (Lambda reserved concurrency with provisioned instances, Azure Functions Premium plan) keep N instances warm — you pay for them while idle, in exchange for consistent latency.
- **Smaller deployment packages** and minimal runtime overhead reduce the cold-to-ready time.
- **Static assets & config reading** should be done once and cached (outside the handler), not per invocation.
- The **first call from a warm edge** (e.g. a cold API call after a period of no traffic) is the one that stings — design timeouts around the p99 *cold* latency, not the warm p50.

## Event-driven composition

Serverless earns its keep when events chain together. The canonical pattern is **fan-out + orchestration**:

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  Upload[File uploaded] --> F1[Fan-out: split & validate]
  F1 --> Q1[(Queue / topic A)]
  F1 --> Q2[(Queue / topic B)]
  Q1 --> W1[Worker: format]
  W1 --> S1[(Job status store)]
  Q2 --> W2[Worker: enrich]
  W2 --> S1
  S1 --> W3[Step: notify on completion]
  W3 --> Done[Result notified]
```

Weak points are where the "serverless is easy" sales pitch breaks:

- **No built-in ordering or exactly-once.** Queues are at-least-once: your function may receive the same event twice. Handlers must be **idempotent** (dedupe by event id, or write-then-commit).
- **Multiple functions each invent their own retry logic** produce chaos. Put retries, dead-letter queues (DLQ), and tracing in one place.
- **Hours-long workflows** (a report, a batch) shouldn't be one big function — the platform times out individual invocations (minutes, not hours). Split into steps, persist state, and let a stateful orchestrator (Step Functions / Durable Functions / Logic Apps) resume where the previous step ended.

## Idempotency: the rule that saves you

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  E[Event arrives] --> D{Dedup token seen?}
  D -- yes --> Ignore[Already processed - ack]
  D -- no --> W[Process + record token]
  W --> Ack[Ack to queue]
```

Because at-least-once delivery is guaranteed and exactly-once is not, every side-effecting function must dedupe. Store the dedupe key (from the event) with a TTL, and make the write path itself idempotent — e.g. `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` before adding the key, or store the dedupe key in the same transaction as the side effect.

## Platform names, same ideas

| Concern | AWS Lambda | Azure Functions |
| --- | --- | --- |
| HTTP entry | API Gateway, Function URL | API Management, static web apps |
| Queue/stream trigger | SQS, SNS, Kinesis, DynamoDB streams | Queue Storage, Event Grid, Event Hubs |
| Orchestration | Step Functions | Durable Functions |
| State / table | DynamoDB | Cosmos DB / Table storage |
| Long-running code | keep it short; orchestrate | keep it short; durable orchestrate |
| Warm instances | provisioned concurrency | Premium plan |

Pick by ecosystem, not religion — an AWS shop uses Lambda because the service grid (SQS/Kinesis/Cognito) is the point, not the name.

## Tester checklist

- Trigger a burst after a period of idle and assert the **cold-start p99** is within your SLO — don't only test under warm load.
- Replay the same event twice (DLQ redrive, manual publish) and confirm the side effect runs **once**, not twice.
- Kill the DLQ consumer or point it at a bad endpoint and confirm poison messages land in the dead-letter queue, not forever in a retry loop.
- Test `reserved concurrency` throttling: when the limit is hit, verify the right error arrives and queued events are not silently dropped.
- Check **timeouts and memory**: a handler near its duration limit should fail loudly with a bounded response, and memory-constrained runs shouldn't crash the platform.

- [AWS core services](../aws-core-services) — Lambda in the context of the whole AWS grid
- [Azure core services](../azure-core-services) — Functions inside the Azure ecosystem
- [Cloud computing fundamentals](../cloud-computing-fundamentals) — service models, regions, and when serverless wins on cost
