## What this system is

ServiceNow is the market's dominant **enterprise service management (ESM) platform**: it hosts ITIL modules (incident, problem, change, request), a CMDB, a service catalog, and — via its low-code platform — any workflow you can model (HR, finance, facilities, security). The mental model is a **platform, not an app**: a database of tables (with a hidden `sys_id` per record), a workflow engine, a notification engine, and a UI layer. "Building in ServiceNow" means defining tables, fields, flows, and service portal pages — not writing services.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph ITSM["ITSM / IT operations"]
    IN[Incident management]
    PR[Problem management]
    CH[Change management]
    RQ[Request + catalog]
  end
  subgraph Platform["The platform"]
    T[(Tables - sys_id per record)]
    F[Flow designer - workflows]
    N[Notifications + SLAs]
    E[Integration Hub - spokes]
  end
  subgraph Enterprise["Enterprise service management"]
    HR[HR service delivery]
    FP[Finance & procurement]
    SEC[Security operations]
  end
  ITSM --> Platform
  Enterprise --> Platform
  CH --> CMDB[(CMDB - in the same system)]
```

## The platform logic: tables, sys_id, CMDB

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  A[Incident table] --> S[Common schema<br/>- sys_id, created, state]
  B[Change table] --> S
  C[Request table] --> S
  D[CMDB CI table] --> S
  S --> UI[Service portal]
  S --> API[ServiceNow API - REST]
  S --> IM[Integration Hub -<br/>OAuth spokes to other tools]
```

- **Tables are the schema**: incidents, problems, changes, requests, and the CMDB all live as tables in one database, each record carrying a globally unique `sys_id`.
- **The CMDB is native** — ServiceNow's does not sit adjacent to the platform; CI relationships are tables, which is why incident-to-impact lookups are one query, not an export.
- **Business rules, notifications, SLA definitions** are platform primitives attached to tables, so "re-open the P1 incident to its owner when SLA breaches" is configuration, not code.
- **The API+Integration Hub** makes ServiceNow a spoke in *other* systems too — it both receives webhooks and pushes to ticketing, chat, and clouds.

The strategy trap is forgetting it's a platform: ServiceNow *can* do almost anything, so teams model every process and ship an over-engineered, hard-to-run system. The production discipline is to configure the **minimum tables and flows that satisfy the process**, then govern "can this be a simple notification instead of a flow?"

## ESM as a term

ServiceNow calls ITSM's backyard "enterprise service management" because the same records-and-workflow engine serves every department. The architectural identity of ESM: **one platform, many siled tables, shared plumbing** — shared notification, shared SLA clock, shared service portal, shared CMDB that incident and security both consult. The shared plumbing is the value: HR's leave request and IT's password reset run the same engine, so governance is uniform.

## The governance patterns that make it work

| Pattern | Why it matters | The failure mode |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Keep it configured, not coded** | Platform stability & upgrade path | Custom scripts multiply until an upgrade breaks the instance |
| **CI ownership + discovery** | CMDB stays trustworthy like a [CMDB](../cmdb-and-configuration-management) must | Stale records poison incident triage |
| **SLA via definitions, not scripts** | Timers live in the platform | Teams hand-roll SLA logic and it drifts |
| **Minimal tables** | Every table is an ops surface | A field-per-whim = ungoverned sprawl |
| **API-first integration** | Other tools consume the platform by contract | Point-to-point PowerShell/scripts replaced wholesale with no contract |

## ServiceNow vs the alternatives

| Need | ServiceNow | Excel / homegrown | Jira ITSM | A generic CMS |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Expressive CMDB relationships | ✓ native | ✗ | partial | ✗ |
| Multi-department platform | ✓ ESM | ✗ | ~ dev/IT only | ✗ |
| Cost & ops burden | high | low | medium | depends |
| Fit when "it's already bought" | strong | — | strong if dev-led | — |

ServiceNow wins decisively where a **managed, auditable, multi-department service backbone** is the requirement (typical mid/large enterprise). It is expensive and has a learning curve; a lightweight team gets comparable value from Jira plus a spreadsheet-plus-docs CMDB — the choice is portfolio-level, not feature-per-feature.

## Tester checklist

- **Record lifecycle**: create an incident, run it through state (new → in progress → resolved → closed) and confirm the state-history and SLA timers behave.
- **CI impact drill**: attach a CI to an incident and confirm the CMDB shows affected services; break the relationship and confirm the lookup no longer misleads.
- **SLA breach**: set a short SLA, let it breach, and verify a notification + business rule fire as defined (not as a script).
- **Portal fidelity**: a catalog request created in the Service Portal must produce the correct request record with the right workflow.
- **API contract**: push an event via Integration Hub OAuth and confirm it lands as the right table record; verify outbound webhooks carry the expected payload shape.

## Related systems

- [CMDB and configuration management](../cmdb-and-configuration-management) — the model ServiceNow ships natively
- [ITIL incident and problem management](../itil-incident-and-problem-management) — the incident/problem track ServiceNow automates
- [ITIL change and release management](../itil-change-and-release-management) — the change workflow living alongside them
- [Monitoring and observability](../monitoring-and-observability) — alerts feed incident records via the API/Integration Hub
- [CRM systems](../crm-systems) — the sibling departmental platform for customer-side workflows94:Tc27,
## What this system is

Cookies are the web's default "remember me". The browser stores a **session id** in a cookie and sends it on every request to your origin. That is powerful — and it opens the door to **CSRF**: a *different* site (or an HTML email) that makes the *same-origin-trusted* browser send a forged request on your victim's behalf.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  A[User logs in] --> B[Server stores session<br/>id  →  user_id]
  B -->|Set-Cookie: session_id<br/>HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite| C[Browser cookie jar]
  C -->|every request carries cookie| D[Your API]
```

## The cookie flags that matter

| Flag | Effect | If you skip it |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `HttpOnly` | JS can't read the cookie | Any XSS reads the session cookie and hijacks accounts |
| `Secure` | Only sent over HTTPS | Cookie travels in cleartext over HTTP |
| `SameSite=Lax` | Blocks cross-site sends except top-level navigation | CSRF succeeds silently |
| `SameSite=Strict` | Blocks even top-level cross-site sends | Slightly worse UX (broken external links while logged-in) |
| `Path`/`Domain` | Restricts where it's sent | Cookie sent to unintended host → leak |

## The CSRF attack, step by step

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant V as Victim's browser
  participant A as Attacker's page
  participant B as Victim's banking app

  V->>B: login (gets session cookie)
  V->>A: visits evil.example (cookie NOT shared — but that doesn't matter!)
  A->>V: <img src="https://bank.com/transfer?to=attacker&amount=9999">
  V->>B: GET transfer... with session_id cookie (auto-sent!)
  B-->>V: transfers money
```

The mean trick: the attacker's site never *reads* your cookie (and doesn't need to). The **browser** attaches the cookie to the request because it's going to `bank.com` — the victim is logged in, so the forged request is "authenticated".

## Defenses, in the order to apply them

1. **`SameSite=Lax`** (default in modern browsers) — kills the common cross-site POST form/image CSRF.
2. **CSRF tokens** — a per-session unpredictable token the server requires in forms / `X-CSRF-Token` fetch headers; the attacker's page can't read or forge it.
3. **`Origin` / `Host` header checks** on state-changing endpoints — cheap extra layer.
4. **Double-submit cookie** — server expects the CSRF value both in a cookie and a header; they must match, so a foreign page can't supply both.

## Tester checklist

- Confirm `HttpOnly` + `Secure` + `SameSite=Lax` are set on session cookies.
- From a *second* origin, attempt POST / transfer-type endpoints — they must fail without a valid CSRF token.
- Confirm state-changing endpoints (not just GET) are CSRF-protected.
- Check logout actually invalidates the session server-side (cookie delete is not enough).

## Related systems

- [Authentication vs authorization](../authentication-vs-authorization) — session = authn result
- [JWT access tokens](../jwt-tokens) — the stateless alternative to cookie sessions
- [OAuth 2.0 and OpenID Connect](../oauth2-openid-connect) — modern login-with-IDP flows and the login-related CSRF (`state`) protections95:Tee9,
## What this system is

SSO means the **identity provider (IdP)** authenticates you once, and every connected app trusts its assertion — no more N passwords, no more shadow accounts, and revoke-at-the-IdP ends access everywhere. Two protocols pull this off: **SAML** (older, XML, enterprise/SaaS legacy) and **OIDC** (modern, JSON, web/native).

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant B as Browser / user
  participant A as App - relying party
  participant P as Identity provider - holds the session

  B->>A: open protected page
  A->>P: redirect auth request
  P-->>A: authorization code
  A->>A: exchange code for tokens - id_token + access
  Note over A: app session minted
  Note over A,P: SLO kills both sessions
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  User[User] -->|one login| IdP[Corporate IdP - holds the session]
  IdP -->|assertions/tokens| A1[App A - SAML]
  IdP -->|assertions/tokens| A2[App B - OIDC]
  IdP -->|assertions/tokens| A3[App C - OIDC]
  IdP -->|kill session - everywhere at once| Revoke[Logout / deprovision]
```

## The flow your browser lives in (SP-initiated OIDC)

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant B as Browser
  participant A as App (RP)
  participant I as Identity provider

  B->>A: open protected page
  A->>B: redirect + auth request to IdP
  B->>I: already has IdP session - no prompt
  I->>B: authorization code back to App
  A->>I: exchange code + client secret for tokens
  I-->>A: ID token + access token
  A->>A: validate signature, lifetime, audience
  A-->>B: session granted on the app side too
```

Two sessions actually exist: the **IdP session** (your "logged in" at the IdP) and each **app's local session**. SSO saved you the IdP login; the app still mints its own cookie/token per login via the IdP.

## OIDC vs SAML — which road are you on

| | OIDC | SAML |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Format | JSON / JWT-friendly | XML assertions |
| Modern fit | Web, mobile, APIs, SPAs | SaaS platforms, legacy enterprise |
| Sits on | OAuth 2.0 | Standalone |
| Nit | IdP roles (authorize) and scopes | Assertion conditions, federation |

Two different protocols solving the same job; if you're building greenfield, OIDC wins. If your SaaS buyer only speaks SAML, you integrate SAML.

## What everyone forgets: logout

SSO that can't log out everywhere is a myth. **Single Logout (SLO)** tells every app's session to die when the IdP session dies. OIDC adds **RP-initiated logout** (app tells IdP, IdP tells the rest). Test it, because the default state of most stacks is: *the IdP session ends, the apps' sessions quietly survive* — a real exposure after a shared-computer session.

## SSO is authentication, not authorization

SSO proves *who*; it says nothing about *what you may do*. The ID token's scopes/claims seed the app's session, and the app's own [RBAC](../rbac-and-authorization)/policy decides access. "Trusting employee email" ≠ "employee may see the payroll ledger."

## Tester checklist

- Round-trip a full login and logout through every app: after SLO, hitting a protected page must bounce to the IdP login.
- Validate a token beyond its signature: expiry, audience, issuer, `nonce` — a well-signed spoof is still a forgery.
- Confirm no app keeps its own credential store (shadow identity = SSO divot).
- Kill the IdP session mid-flight and confirm each connected app loses it within expected bounds (session + refresh lifetimes).

## Related systems

- [OAuth 2.0 and OpenID Connect](../oauth2-openid-connect) — the protocol detail behind modern SSO
- [Role-based access control](../rbac-and-authorization) — the authorization layer SSO feeds
- [SCIM provisioning](../scim-identity-provisioning) — SSO handles login, SCIM handles accounts
- [Identity and access management](../identity-and-access-management) — the IAM whole this is half of96:T1826,
## What this system is

SMTP (Simple Mail Transfer Protocol) is the **store-and-forward** protocol that moves email between servers. It is 40+ years old, still goes over TCP/25 between mail servers, and it never, ever blocks waiting for a recipient to be online: mail is accepted, queued, and retried — that's the "store and forward" that makes it different from a chat protocol.

An email has two halves: the **envelope** (who to deliver to, used by SMTP: `MAIL FROM` and `RCPT TO`) and the **message** (headers + body, including `From:`/`To:` which are just text). The envelope is the contract between mail servers; the headers are what your mail client displays. Forgers abuse the gap between them, which is why SPF, DKIM, and DMARC exist.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  MU[Sender MUA - Outlook / Gmail app] -->|submit mail| SU[Submission server - port 587 STARTTLS]
  SU -->|queue| MT[Sender MTA - queue + retry]
  MT -->|MX lookup, SMTP on port 25| MX[Recipient MX - accepted by MX]
  MX -->|deliver to mailbox| LD[LDA to mailbox - IMAP / POP serves it]
```

## The path an email takes

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant U as Sender (MUA)
  participant S as Submission :587
  participant M as Sender MTA (queue)
  participant X as Recipient MX :25
  participant L as Mailbox (IMAP)

  U->>S: SMTP AUTH + STARTTLS, MAIL FROM / RCPT TO / DATA
  S->>M: accept, add Received + headers
  M->>X: DNS MX lookup → connect :25
  M->>X: EHLO, MAIL FROM, RCPT TO, DATA
  X->>M: 250 OK, queue it
  X->>L: deliver to user's mailbox
  L->>U: client pulls via IMAP
```

Every **MTA→MTA** hop re-writes the envelope path in `Received:` headers — that chain is your forensic trail (the "Received: by ... from ..." block you can read in any spam).

## The SMTP conversation

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  A[Client] -->|EHLO example.com| B[Server]
  B -->|250-hello| A
  A -->|MAIL FROM: bob@example.com| B
  B -->|250 2.1.0 Ok| A
  A -->|RCPT TO: alice@corp.net| B
  B -->|250 2.1.5 Ok| A
  A -->|DATA| B
  B -->|354 End data with CRLF-CRLF| A
  A -->|headers + body| B
  B -->|250 2.0.0 Ok queued| A
  A -->|QUIT| B
```

Key facts to internalize:

- **Ports**: `:25` is MTA→MTA relay; `:587` is client submission (with STARTTLS); `:465` is implicit-TLS submission (legacy). Port 25 has no encryption by default — that's why submission uses 587.
- **MAIL FROM ≠ From:**. One is the bounce/return path (envelope), the other is what your client shows. Spam scores both.
- **Retry, don't fail**: a temporary failure (`4xx`) triggers queue + retry with exponential backoff (minutes → hours, typically up to 2 days). A permanent failure (`5xx`, e.g. unknown user) means **bounce** (a delivery-status notification sent back to the envelope `MAIL FROM`).
- **Received headers** get prepended at each hop; the *lowest* `Received:` on your received email is where it entered your org.

## MX records: finding where to send

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  S[Your MTA] -->|A? / AAAA? mx1.corp.net| D[DNS]
  D -->|priority 10 → mx1, 20 → mx2| S
  S -->|try mx1 :25, fail → retry queue| S
  S -->|connect mx1| E[Recipient MX]
```

The MX record lists mail servers **by preference number** (lower = higher priority). Mail is tried on the lowest-preference MX; if that fails, the next. MX records for the same domain may point to different networks — that's your redundancy. An SMTP server is not required to accept mail for a domain just because it has an A record; only MX (or an implicit A if no MX) says "this host receives mail for me."

## SPF, DKIM, DMARC — the email armor

Email has no built-in proof of who sent it, so the industry layered DNS records on top:

| Mechanism | What it verifies | DNS record |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **SPF** | That the sending IP is *authorized* by the domain's policy | `TXT v=spf1 ip4:... include:_spf.google.com ~all` |
| **DKIM** | That the message body + selected headers were *signed* by a key the domain publishes | `TXT` at `selector._domainkey.example.com` with a public key |
| **DMARC** | A policy telling receivers what to do when SPF/DKIM fail | `TXT _dmarc.example.com v=DMARC1; p=reject; rua=mailto:...` |

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  A[Sender signs with private DKIM key] --> B[Message: DKIM-Signature header]
  B --> C[Recipient queries domain key via DNS]
  C --> D[Verify signature + SPF source + DMARC policy]
  D -->|align From header with envelope domain| E[Quarantine or reject if unaligned]
```

DMARC is the piece that actually changes behavior: SPF/DKIM prove *something* about the sender, but DMARC's `p=reject` is what makes receivers drop messages where the visible `From:` domain doesn't align with the authenticated sender. That's also why DMARC is the fence for **domain spoofing** — and why you should check `_dmarc` before you trust a lookalike domain.

## Bounces, and why you never configure bounce loops

- **5xx** = permanent → bounce immediately (mailbox full is actually 4xx temporary). 
- **4xx** = temporary → queue and retry with backoff.
- If a server's `MAIL FROM` is empty (`<>`), it's a **delivery-status notification** (a bounce); mail servers *must not* bounce a bounce, or you get a loop.

## Tester checklist

- Inspect the full header chain of a suspicious email (`Show original` in Gmail): are the `Received:` hops plausible, and does DMARC pass?
- Send to a test mailbox and confirm the `Received:` `from` header records the real sending IP — then check that IP against SPF.
- Force a `4xx` (e.g., pause the MX) and verify your MTA queues and retries with growing delays — a flapping MX must not drop mail.
- Verify TLS in transit: submission to your provider uses STARTTLS (see the `220` banner), and MTA-to-MTA supports it where the peer does.
- Test DMARC alignment: send with `From:` different from the envelope domain and confirm the policy (`p=none` should be your *monitor first* mode).

## Related systems

- [DNS and load balancing](../dns-and-load-balancing) — MX/TXT/DMARC live in the same DNS you resolve
- [OSI seven-layer model](../osi-seven-layer-model) — SMTP is a layer-7 protocol riding TCP/IP
- [TCP/IP and TLS](../tcp-ip-and-tls) — the transport SMTP depends on
97:T1c6e,
## What this system is

Snowflake is a **managed cloud data warehouse** whose defining trick is *separation of compute from storage*. Storage (your data in object storage) and compute (virtual warehouses that actually run queries) are independent: you can have many compute clusters reading the same storage, scale each up and down on its own, and pay only for the compute you run. That one architectural decision drives everything else about Snowflake — instant scale, no DataOps lock-in, and a "concurrency without contention" story.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  subgraph Store["Storage layer - object store (shared)"]
    D1[(Tables + micro-partitions)]
  end
  subgraph Compute["Compute layer - 'virtual warehouses'"]
    VW1[VW - BI / ad-hoc]
    VW2[VW - ETL / load]
    VW3[VW - ML / heavy scans]
  end
  C[Cloud account] --> VW1
  C --> VW2
  C --> VW3
  VW1 --> D1
  VW2 --> D1
  VW3 --> D1
```

## The three layers

| Layer | What it is | The mental model |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Storage** | Columnar files, micro-partitioned, compressed, in object storage | "Your data in a cheap, shared bucket" |
| **Compute (virtual warehouses)** | Independent clusters you size, pause, auto-scale | "Rent CPU as you query; nothing is 'your' server" |
| **Services** | Metadata, catalog, security, transactions, cloning | "The brain that coordinates the other two" |

The consequence worth internalizing: a warehouse is **not** a thing you size like an old RDBMS. You (or the BI tool) spin transient compute up and down per workload, using `XS→4XL` warehouses, and Snowflake separately charges for storage *and* compute. Oversizing a warehouse that idles = money burned; under-sizing = slow queries. Right-sizing is a live, iterative budget exercise.

## Micro-partitions, cloning, time travel

- **Micro-partitions** are small immutable columnar chunks (50–500 MB) with embedded metadata. Queries prune to only the partitions their filters need — which is why Snowflake scans less than a naive "read the whole table" warehouse. There is **no manual indexing or tuning**: the engine decides partitioning from the data. The result is the *absence* of the DBA's partitioning job — one of Snowflake's core appeals.
- **Zero-copy cloning** creates a copy of a table/schema/database *instantly*, sharing the underlying storage until you write — the classic "clone prod to QA without copying gigabytes" move, and the foundation of safe dev/test.
- **Time travel** lets you query a table *as of* minutes to days ago (CTAS backup, point-in-time recovery from a bad update) independent of the storage bill — standard retention is configurable, and it's the safety net behind "we can always undo."

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  S[Tables - micro-partitions] -->|clone| S2[Clone - shares storage, cheap, instant]
  S -->|time travel| T1[Query as of N minutes ago]
  S -->|query| VW{Your virtual warehouse}
  VW -->|prune by metadata| S
```

## Concurrency and the "no lock contention" design

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant U1 as Warehouse (ETL load)
  participant U2 as Warehouse (BI query)
  participant S as Same table

  U1->>S: INSERT - new micro-partitions
  U2->>S: SELECT - old snapshot (time-travel consistent)
  U1->>S: commit transaction
  U2-->>S: next query sees the new data
```

Because the data is immutable and micro-partitions are versioned, a long-running load and a query *don't block each other* — readers keep reading consistent snapshots while writers append new partitions. The locking noise that dominates classic warehouses ("kill the query, the table is locked") mostly disappears. The catch that remains: **cost concurrency** — many concurrent heavy warehouses are possible but bill concurrently, so auto-suspend/auto-resume and scaling policies are your spend control.

## Snowflake vs the warehouse/lakehouse field

| Decision | Snowflake is strong | Prefer instead |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Who wants it | "Warehouse but cloud-native and low-ops; SQL as the interface" | A data lake / open table formats (Delta/Iceberg) for lake-first raw exploration |
| Storage economics | Managed columnar, pay per scan | Object storage cost for *everything* raw |
| Openness | Proprietary engine; fine if you accept managed | Open-file (lakehouse) if you need engine-agnostic control |
| Fit | Enterprise warehouse, BI, governed SQL | Cheesy minimal ops, a generic cloud stack |

The practical note: Snowflake is a **managed warehouse**, not a lake — it competes with the "warehouse" side of [Data warehouse and lakehouse architecture](../data-warehouse-lakehouse-architecture). Organizations that want lake storage cost with warehouse governance often run Snowflake *alongside* a lake (landing in Parquet/Delta, curated into Snowflake) rather than choosing one. Snowflake's modern offering (Iceberg tables) narrows that gap, but the mental model difference stands: managed-engine vs open-format.

## The failure modes a data architect owns

- **Un-governed auto-scale**: virtual warehouses left running 24/7 with no auto-suspend become a surprise compute bill.
- **Paying for storage to do nothing**: no clustering or purge policy; huge account drift (Snowflake storage is cheap *to* a point — dead copies accumulate).
- **Treating it like a full lake**: trying to store every raw form of every thing in Snowflake when a lake is the cheaper landing zone.
- **Trusting "no tuning" too far**: micro-partitions prune well for naturally-sorted/filtered workloads; genuinely orderless heavy scans still scan.
- **Security slip**: inadequate masking/roles — Snowflake's governance is powerful but is only as strong as the roles you define.

## Tester checklist

- **Clone drill**: `CREATE TABLE ... CLONE` a large table and verify instant availability + shared storage cost; write to the clone and confirm the original is untouched.
- **Time travel**: run a destructive `UPDATE`/`DELETE`, then `UNDROP`/`AT(TIMESTAMP)` and confirm the old data is recoverable within the configured retention.
- **Compute isolation**: run a load on VW-A while querying the same table on VW-B — confirm no lock and no cross-warehouse contention.
- **Pruning**: put a WHERE on a selective column and confirm the query *acts* on fewer micro-partitions (see query profile), not a full table scan.
- **Auto-suspend**: set low auto-suspend, run a query, idle, and confirm the warehouse suspends and the compute bill stops ticking.
- **Role/masking**: a nongranted role cannot read a masked column; verify access control is enforced, not cosmetic.

## Related systems

- [Data warehouse and lakehouse architecture](../data-warehouse-lakehouse-architecture) — where the warehouse-vs-lakehouse decision lives
- [Data pipelines (ETL/ELT)](../data-pipelines-etl-elt) — how data reaches (and leaves) Snowflake
- [Data governance and catalog](../data-governance-and-catalog) — the roles, lineage, and masking Snowflake enforces
- [Data modeling and star schemas](../data-modeling-star-schemas) — the shapes curated Snowflake tables should take
- [Change data capture](../change-data-capture) — the streaming path into a warehouse like Snowflake
- [Oracle database ecosystem](../oracle-database-ecosystem) — the managed-Oracle contrast for a database-platform decision98:T13fc,
## What this system is

The **software development lifecycle** (SDLC) is the spine that turns business intent into a working, supported system. Its strength is not the phases — every team has an argument about those — it is the **gates**: each phase must meet its *exit criteria* and hand over a *usable artifact* before the next phase may begin. The lifecycle only works when the artifacts, not the meetings, carry the state forward.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  R[Requirements] --> A[Analysis]
  A --> D[Design]
  D --> B[Build]
  B --> T[Test]
  T --> P[Deploy]
  P --> O[Operate]
  O -.->|change request / defect| R
```

Whether you run it as a waterfall, a portfolio of sprints, or a continuous pipeline, the **phase logic** is the same — the delivery model changes how fast you loop, not which questions must be answered.

## The phases, the artifacts, and the exit tests

The lifecycle is a chain of hand-offs where *the artifact is the contract*. If you can't name the artifact a phase produces, you can't verify the phase finished.

| Phase | Input | Artifact produced | Exit test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **1 · Requirements** | Business need, problem statement | Prioritized requirements, success criteria | Every requirement is testable and has an owner |
| **2 · Analysis** | Requirements | Domain model, data flows, rules, edge cases | Ambiguities resolved; "what if" questions answered |
| **3 · Design** | Analysis artifacts | Architecture, API contracts, schema, UI specs | Designers + testers agree it is buildable and testable |
| **4 · Build** | Design artifacts | Code, migrations, config | Everything merges, CI is green, feature toggles in |
| **5 · Test** | Build outputs | Test evidence, defect list, go/no-go report | Coverage meets the bar; no known critical defects |
| **6 · Deploy** | Approved build | Released environment, runbook, rollback plan | Smoke tests pass in prod; rollback practiced |
| **7 · Operate** | Live system | Alerting, SLAs, incident records | Team owns the fix/change loop going forward |

The most common lifecycle failures are not phase-specific — they are **exit-test failures**: a phase was declared done on a schedule, not on evidence of completeness.

## The requirements-to-design bridge

```mermaid
stateDiagram-v2
  [*] --> Requirements
  Requirements: Requirements + success criteria
  Requirements --> Analysis: clarified
  Analysis: Analysis - domain model + rules
  Analysis --> Design: model + rules
  Design: Design - contracts + schema
  Design --> Build: contracts approved
  Build: Build - code + migrations
  Build --> Test: artifacts handed over
  Test: Test - evidence + go/no-go
  Test --> [*]
```

The fatal gap in most lifecycles sits between **Analysis** and **Design**: the requirements say *what*, the analysis unpacks *what if* (rules, edge cases, exceptions), and the design only *then* freezes contracts. Jumping straight from requirements to a schema is how the "weird one-off" becomes a production surprise.

## Where the phase gates hurt (and why they exist)

- **Waterfall** — the strict form: each phase completes before the next starts. Cheap to govern, brutal when a late discovery invalidates earlier work.
- **Agile / sprints** — the compressed form: requirements, design, and test happen *every iteration*, so the gates become lighter but never disappear.
- **Continuous delivery** — the pipeline form: build→test→deploy are automated stages with gates embedded as checks, approvals, and environments.

The discipline in every model is the same: **a gate is a question, not a ceremony**. "Does the artifact meet the exit criteria?" asked *by the person who depends on it next*. When the tester doesn't approve the design spec, the lifecycle is working.

## Tester checklist

- Traceability: take one requirement and follow it through analysis, design, build, and its passing test — any dead end is a gap.
- Gate audit: pick a recent delivery and confirm each phase actually met its exit test on evidence, not on a date.
- Defect routing: a bug found in production must map back to the phase whose exit test missed it — that is where the lifecycle should improve.
- Rollback readiness: every deploy record names its rollback play and proves it was rehearsed.
- Artifact survival: a new engineer should be able to reconstruct a feature's *why* from the artifacts alone (not from memory).

## Related systems

- [Business process modeling and management](../business-process-modeling-and-management) — the upstream source of the requirements the lifecycle processes
- [System acquisition lifecycle](../system-acquisition-lifecycle) — the build-vs-buy decision that picks SDLC or procurement in the first place
- [Jira and agile delivery](../jira-and-agile-delivery) — the issue tracking that carries artifacts through the lifecycle
- [ITIL change and release management](../itil-change-and-release-management) — the governance gate the deploy phase hands off to
- [DevOps delivery loop](../devops-delivery-loop) — the operate→improve loop that feeds grievances back into requirements99:Tf81,
## What this system is

Supply chain management (SCM) is the **whole journey a product takes** from raw material to the customer's hands. It is not one system — it is a chain of systems (ERP, procurement, warehouse, transport, and planning tools) coordinated by a common set of numbers. The classic framing is the SCOR reference model: **Plan, Source, Make, Deliver, Return**.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  PL[Plan] --> SO[Source]
  SO --> MK[Make]
  MK --> DE[Deliver]
  DE --> RE[Return]
  RE --> PL
```

## The two halves: planning vs execution

| | Planning (SCP) | Execution (SCE) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Asks | *What should happen?* | *What is happening?* |
| Horizon | Weeks to months | Minutes to days |
| Key systems | Demand planning, S&OP, inventory optimization, supply planning | ERP, WMS, TMS, MES, OMS |
| Output | Forecasts, plans, purchase/delivery signals | POs, picks, shipments, production orders |
| Failure mode | Plan and reality drift apart | Firefighting without a plan |

The recurring architecture problem in SCM is **plan-execution gap**: the plan says X, the execution says Y, and nobody reconciles. The systems that close that gap — demand sensing, control towers, exception alerts — are where the real SCM value is being built.

## The demand → fulfillment chain

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  FC[Demand forecast] --> PL[Supply / inventory plan]
  PL --> PO[Purchase or production order]
  PO --> RCV[Receive or build]
  RCV --> ST[Stock position]
  ST --> ORD[Customer order]
  ORD --> OOS[Ship and deliver]
  OOS --> LF[Demand feedback - refresh forecast]
```

## The planning loop

Demand planning is a **closed feedback loop**, not a one-shot forecast: historical demand, sales inputs, and market signals feed a forecast; that forecast becomes a supply/inventory plan; execution produces actuals; actuals are compared to forecast; the plan is re-forecast. The metric that shows you a healthy chain is *forecast accuracy* — a single number that every downstream sub-system inherits.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Sig["Demand signals"] -->|model| FC["Forecast"]
  FC -->|net requirements| Plan["Supply + inventory plan"]
  Plan -->|signals| Exec["Execute - buy / make"]
  Exec -->|results| Act["Actuals + stock"]
  Act -->|actuals| Recon["Reconcile + re-forecast"]
  Recon -->|re-forecast| FC
```

## The bullwhip effect

Small demand changes amplify upstream: **order variability grows with distance from the customer**. A 5 % dip in retail demand can become a 40 % swing in factory orders if every tier over-reacts. The architectural cure is visibility and sharing: demand data propagating across tiers (not just order messages) and planning horizons that dampen reaction. When you test a "supply chain", this is the dynamics you are really testing.

## Tester checklist

- Forecast accuracy: given actuals, re-run the forecast and assert the accuracy metric and its tolerance are met/exceeded.
- Plan-execution reconciliation: inject a planned PO that the execution side fails to honor — a real exception must surface, not vanish.
- Netting logic: stock on hand + on order − demand must produce a defensible net requirement (no double-count, no missed safety stock).
- Data freshness: the plan must use the latest actuals; stale data must trip an alert, not silently plan from old numbers.
- End-to-end: a demand spike must cascade through forecast → plan → PO → receive → ship in a coherent trace.

## Related systems

- [ERP systems](../enterprise-resource-planning) — the execution backbone of the chain
- [Procurement (source-to-pay)](../procurement-source-to-pay) — the source (buy) leg
- [Warehouse management systems](../warehouse-management-systems) — the warehouse (store) leg
- [Data pipelines (ETL/ELT)](../data-pipelines-etl-elt) — how demand and inventory data feeds the plans
- [Monitoring and observability](../monitoring-and-observability) — the control-tower telemetry that surfaces exceptions9a:T1c05,
## What this system is

Adding a new system to an organization is a **lifecycle with decision gates**, not a procurement event. It runs from *"we have a business need and no system for it"* through selection, implementation, and years of operation, to an eventual retirement that must not orphan the data. The whole chain is a single question repeated at each gate: **what must be true before we spend the next level of money and commitment?**

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  R[Analyze requirements] --> S[Select option - build vs buy]
  S --> C[Choose software / solution]
  C --> I[Implement - deploy + migrate]
  I --> O[Operate - maintain + evolve]
  O --> T[Retire - extract + decommission]
  T -.->|lessons| R
```

Each arrow is a gate. The lifecycle succeeds when every gate is crossed on *evidence* — a documented requirement, a scored option, a signed contract, a successful go-live, a clean exit — and fails when a phase is skipped on momentum.

## The five phases and their gates

| Phase | What you do | Artifact / decision | Exit gate |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **1 · Analyze requirements** | Understand the real need, not the assumed solution | Requirements spec, success criteria, stakeholders | Every requirement is testable and has an owner |
| **2 · Select option** | Decide build vs buy vs extend vs outsource | Options analysis, recommendation | The rejected options are documented *and why* |
| **3 · Choose software** | Evaluate vendors/solutions against the spec | Shortlist, scored RFP, contract | Selection is scored against the spec, not the demo |
| **4 · Implement** | Configure, integrate, migrate data, train | Cutover plan, go-live record | Data migrated + verified; users trained; rollback defined |
| **5 · Operate** | Run, support, upgrade, evolve | SLAs, release cadence, roadmap | The system is owned, measured, and improveable |
| **6 · Retire** | Extract data, stop spend, decommission | Data extract, decommission plan | Nothing still depends on it; data is preserved or disposed |

The lifecycle's dirty secret: **phase 6 is usually the one nobody planned**, so data gets stranded, integrations keep pointing at dead systems, and the "new system" spend never actually ends.

## Analyze requirements — the phase that decides everything

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Need[Business need] -->|baseline| Current[As-is - what exists today]
  Need -->|who + why| Stake[Stakeholders + pain]
  Current -->|gaps| Spec[Requirements spec]
  Stake -->|wants + constraints| Spec
  Spec -->|prioritize| Mapped[Must / should / could]
  Mapped -->|agree scope + budget| Criteria[Success criteria + cost budget]
```

The trap here is **skipping to the solution**. Requirements analysis must describe the *need* ("orders take 3 days to approve") before anyone names a product. And every requirement needs a **must / should / could** weight and a **cost ceiling** — because the selection phase will otherwise be decided by the shiniest demo, not the spec.

## Select option: build vs buy vs extend

| Option | Pick when | Hidden cost to budget |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Buy / SaaS** | Need is common, market is mature | Integration, data migration, vendor lock-in |
| **Build custom** | Need is a differentiator, no fit | Long lifecycle, you own the bugs and roadmap |
| **Extend existing** | Adjacent system already holds the data | Technical debt, coupling to the legacy core |
| **Outsource / BPO** | Execution is not a core capability | Governance, hand-off quality, exit penalties |

The honest rule: **buy when the process is commodity, build when it is strategy.** And an option "analysis" that never seriously scored the other three options is a memo, not a gate.

## Choose software: the scored selection

A defensible selection scores candidates against the **requirements spec** — functional fit, integration effort, total cost of ownership, vendor viability, security posture — before any demo impressions. The pattern that saves organizations:

1. **Shortlist** from the spec, not from brand awareness.
2. **Scored RFP** — every candidate answers the same questions, weighted to must/should/could.
3. **Proof of concept** — the top candidate proves the riskiest integration against *your* data, not a scripted demo.
4. **Negotiate + contract** — lock the scored commitments (uptime, support SLA, migration help, exit data rights) into the contract.

**Demo theater** is the number-one selection killer: a polished demo hides the gaps the scored spec would have caught.

## Implement: configure, migrate, cut over

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  C[Configure + integrate] --> M[Migrate data]
  M --> V[Verify - reconcile + smoke test]
  V --> T[Train + communications]
  T --> G[Go-live cutover]
  G --> R[Rollback - rehearsed, not hoped]
  G --> L[Live support + hypercare]
```

Implementation converts the signed contract into a running system. The three things that break it:

- **Data migration treated as a copy job** — it is a reconciliation project; the old system and the new must agree on counts and totals before go-live.
- **Training as an afterthought** — a system the users don't trust gets bypassed by shadow spreadsheets.
- **Rollback as a hope** — the cutover plan must name the exact condition under which you *will* roll back, and prove it was rehearsed.

## Operate, then retire

- **Operate** — the system lives inside [ITIL change and release management](../itil-change-and-release-management) and [DevOps delivery loop](../devops-delivery-loop): owned, measured, upgraded, and improved on a roadmap. A system with no owner is already retired in spirit.
- **Retire** — the end game: extract all data in a usable, documented format, confirm nothing still depends on the system (integrations, reports, people), decommission the infrastructure, and stop the spend. A good exit is part of the contract from day one — **you cannot extract data the vendor won't release** unless it was negotiated.

## Tester checklist

- Requirements traceability: follow one must-have requirement from spec → selection score → contract → acceptance test → live.
- Selection evidence: audit the RFP scores and confirm the winning candidate actually outscored, not just out-demoed.
- Migration reconcile: run the parallel count/total check and find the discrepancies *before* cutover, not after.
- Rollback drill: execute the rehearsed rollback and confirm the old system and its data are actually intact.
- Exit drill: produce the data-extract and dependency inventory as if retiring today — if it can't be done, the contract is failing.

## Related systems

- [Software development lifecycle (SDLC)](../software-development-lifecycle) — the build-side lifecycle this wraps around
- [Business process modeling and management](../business-process-modeling-and-management) — the process requirements the new system must carry
- [ITIL change and release management](../itil-change-and-release-management) — the operate-phase governance every cutover lands in
- [Enterprise resource planning](../enterprise-resource-planning) — the typical big acquisition, with its data-migration and retirement weight9b:Te82,
## What this system is

TCP/IP is the plumbing under everything you ship. Every HTTP request, database connection, stream, and SSH session is a TCP socket riding over IP. Understanding the layers is how you read a `traceroute`, diagnose `connection refused`, and reason about why that packet arrived late.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  C[Client app] -->|GET /users| A[Application layer]
  A -->|wrap with TCP header| T[Transport - TCP segment]
  T -->|SYN to SYN-ACK to ACK| I[Internet - IP packet]
  I -->|route hop-by-hop| L[Link - Ethernet frame]
  L -->|deliver frame| S[Server socket on 443]
```

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  A[Application layer] -->|HTTP / TLS / DNS| B[Transport layer]
  B -->|TCP segments or UDP datagrams| C[Internet layer]
  C -->|IP packets with source/dest address| D[Link layer]
  D -->|Ethernet frames on the wire| E[Physical medium]
```

## The stack, top to bottom

| Layer | Unit | What carries | Your cast of characters |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Application | Message | Protocols like HTTP, DNS, TLS | NGINX, curl, browsers |
| Transport | Segment / datagram | Ports, reliability, ordering | TCP, UDP |
| Internet | Packet | IP addressing, hop-by-hop routing | IP, ICMP, routing tables |
| Link | Frame | MAC addressing on a local net | Ethernet, Wi-Fi |
| Physical | Bits | Signal on copper/fibre/radio | NICs, switches |

Each layer **wraps** the one above — the classic encapsulation dance:

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  App[GET /users] --> Transport[TCP header + GET]
  Transport --> Internet[IP header + TCP header + GET]
  Internet --> Link[Ethernet header + IP + TCP + GET + CRC]
```

## The three-way handshake

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant C as Client (port 44300)
  participant S as Server (:443)

  C->>S: SYN (seq=x)
  S->>C: SYN-ACK (seq=y, ack=x+1)
  C->>S: ACK (ack=y+1) — connection established
  C->>S: request bytes…
```

Once open, TCP gives you **ordered, reliable, retransmitted** delivery — but each byte is burdened with ACKs, congestion control, and head-of-line blocking.

## TCP vs UDP

| | TCP | UDP |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Reliability | Guaranteed, retransmitted | Fire-and-forget |
| Ordering | Ordered stream | None |
| Connection | Static handshake, connection state | Connectionless |
| Latency tax | Handshake + ACK flow control | Minimal — no state |
| Use cases | HTTP, SQL, SSH, file transfer | DNS, VoIP, video, game position, QUIC's base |

## TLS on top of TCP

TLS sits *between* transport and application. Same TCP socket, but the bytes are sealed: the handshake exchanges keys, then every record is encrypted + authenticated. That handshake is why your first request to a cold TLS endpoint feels slow — two TCP round-trips of handshake, then 2+ TLS round-trips, before the first HTTP byte.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  TLS[Application + TLS<br/>records encrypted/authenticated] --> TCP[TCP segments] --> IP[IP packets]
```

## Tester checklist

- Reproduce a slow response: is it app latency or TCP? Packet loss is the cause of retransmits — see it in `tcpdump`.
- Confirm `TIME_WAIT`/`CLOSE_WAIT` states: a leak of `CLOSE_WAIT` connections is a server that isn't finishing sockets.
- Verify every service you expose only listens on the ports+interfaces you intend (`ss -ltnp`).
- Check TLS: certificate validity, supported ciphers, and that the private key stays server-side.

## Related systems

- [API request lifecycle](../api-request-lifecycle) — what runs *above* this stack for a frontend
- [Authentication vs authorization](../authentication-vs-authorization) — TLS encrypts; it is not an access control
- [Zero trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — micro-segments that layer on top of IP/L49c:T110a,
## What this system is

A tenancy management system runs the **contract-and-cash side** of renting: who rents which unit, under what lease, at what rent, with which deposit — and what happens on move-in, renewal, and vacate. Where the [housing management system](../housing-management-systems) owns the *building*, the tenancy system owns the *stream of income*. The two meet at the unit, and the tenancy is what makes a unit an asset rather than a set of rooms.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  U[Unit] --> M[Marketing + screening]
  M --> L[Lease / tenancy agreement]
  L --> IN[Move-in + deposit]
  IN --> RC[Rent cycle - invoices and arrears]
  RC --> RN[Renewal / rent review]
  RN --> OU[Move-out - deposit return]
  OU --> RE[Re-let the unit]
```

## The core objects

| Object | Holds | Owned by | Failure mode |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Tenancy / lease | Tenant, unit, term, rent, clauses | Tenancy system | Term ends but system keeps charging |
| Rent schedule | Due dates, amounts, payment splits | Tenancy system | Payment never allocates to the schedule |
| Deposit | Amount, protected?, refund state | Tenancy system | Refund paid but protection not closed out |
| Arrears / credit | What the tenant owes | Tenancy system | Doubled by an unfiled payment |
| Notice | Type, dates, expiry | Tenancy system | Expired notice auto-rolls the tenancy |

The tenancy is the **unit of billing**: everything financial hangs off the tenancy record, not the tenant alone (a tenant with two units gets two tenancies and two rent schedules). Mistakes here are money mistakes — doubled invoices, unfiled payments, deposits paid back without protection closure.

## The rental lifecycle

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  AV[Unit available] --> L1[Market + screen tenant]
  L1 --> L2[Sign lease - term + rent]
  L2 --> M1[Move-in - keys, inventory, deposit]
  M1 --> R1[Monthly rent cycle]
  R1 --> R2[Renewal or notice]
  R2 -->|renew| R1
  R2 -->|vacate| MO[Move-out - inspection]
  MO --> DR[Deposit return + final account]
  DR --> RE[Unit back to available]
```

## The rent cycle, close up

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant System
  participant Tenant
  participant Bank
  System->>Tenant: invoice for the month
  Tenant->>Bank: payment arrives
  Bank->>System: payment notification
  System->>System: allocate payment to rent schedule
  alt full and on time
    System-->>Tenant: receipt, ledger clean
  else partial or late
    System->>System: split + arrears accrues
    System-->>Tenant: warning + late fee per lease
  end
```

Rent accounting is a **cash-allocation problem**: a payment rarely matches exactly one invoice. The system must split it across schedule lines, apply late fees *in lease order*, and keep the running balance legible — because every tenant disputes the math at some point.

## The let → rent → vacate path

```mermaid
stateDiagram-v2
  [*] --> Lease: sign
  Lease: Lease signed
  Lease --> MoveIn: handover
  MoveIn: Move-in + deposit
  MoveIn --> Rent: schedule starts
  Rent: Rent cycle
  Rent --> Renew: term end
  Renew: Renewal / notice
  Renew --> Vacate: move-out
  Vacate: Vacate + refund
  Vacate --> [*]
```

## Tester checklist

- Term end: a non-renewed tenancy must stop generating invoices on the exact end date and return the unit to available in the housing system.
- Arrears math: a partial payment must allocate to the oldest-falling rent line first and apply the late fee per the lease order — verify the running balance line by line.
- Deposit lifecycle: deposit protected on move-in must be closed out (protected → refunded/kept with reasons) on vacate — no orphan deposits.
- Notice logic: an expired notice must not auto-roll into a renewal without an explicit action.
- Re-let linking: vacating one tenancy must free the unit for the next while preserving history for both.

## Related systems

- [Housing management systems](../housing-management-systems) — the asset half joined at the unit
- [ERP systems](../enterprise-resource-planning) — where rent revenue posts to finance
- [Change data capture](../change-data-capture) — payments and status events fanning out to analytics
- [API request lifecycle](../api-request-lifecycle) — the payment-bank integration path
- [Message queue delivery](../message-queue-delivery) — decoupling payment notifications from rent processing9d:Te81,
## What this framework is

The Zachman Framework is not a methodology — it is a **classification schema**: a 6×6 matrix whose columns are the six interrogatives and whose rows are six levels of reification. Every artefact in an enterprise (a requirements list, a class diagram, an API spec) has a canonical home in one cell. Knowing the matrix tells you **what is missing** before it bites you.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  R1[Row 1 · Scope · Executive<br/>context: business terms, budgets]
  R2[Row 2 · Business Model · Owner<br/>semantic models, bylaws]
  R3[Row 3 · System Model · Architect<br/>logical models, use cases]
  R4[Row 4 · Technology Model · Engineer<br/>physical designs, APIs, schemas]
  R5[Row 5 · Detailed Representations · Technician<br/>contracts, configs, data definitions]
  R6[Row 6 · Functioning Enterprise<br/>the running system itself]

  R1 --> R2
  R2 --> R3
  R3 --> R4
  R4 --> R5
  R5 --> R6
```

## The six columns

| Column | Interrogative | The question it answers |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Data | **What** | What things exist and their relationships? |
| Function | **How** | How are things transformed and processed? |
| Network | **Where** | Where do the actors and data physically sit? |
| People | **Who** | Who performs each part of the work? |
| Time | **When** | When do events and schedules occur? |
| Motivation | **Why** | Why does the enterprise do this at all (goals, rules)? |

## The full matrix (abbreviated)

| | What | How | Where | Who | When | Why |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **1 · Scope** | things important to the business | business processes | business locations | roles & org units | business events | goals & strategy |
| **2 · Business** | semantic data model | business process model | logistics network | work products & flows | master schedule | business plan |
| **3 · System** | logical data model | application architecture | logical distribution | system / user roles | processing structure | business rule model |
| **4 · Technology** | physical data model | system design | network architecture | human/tech interface | timing / control | rule design |
| **5 · Technical detail** | data definitions | program / module design | network protocols | security & audit | time slicing | rule specifications |
| **6 · Functioning** | deployed data | functioning processes | working network | working organization | actual events | strategy in operation |

## How an architect actually uses it

1. **Start a new initiative in Row 1** — agree the scope cells before any design work.
2. **Refine left-to-right, top-to-bottom**: pick a column (say *What* = data), then reify each row: business terms → logical model → physical schema → deployed database.
3. **Audit for gaps**: for any system, ask "do all 6 columns at Rows 3–4 have artefacts?" Missing *Why/Motivation* artefacts are the classic cause of architecture sign-off fights.
4. **Avoid the trap**: Zachman structures *what exists, slot by slot*. It does not tell you *how to build* — sequencing and method live in TOGAF (see below).

## A caution for testers and reviewers

- The matrix is **system-agnostic**: it describes a space, not a flow. Don’t look for an execution path inside it.
- Two different teams will label the *same* artefact differently — every cell needs an owner and a name.
- The framework does not mention technology stacks, vendors, or tools; those live in row 5, not row 1.

## Related systems

- [TOGAF ADM](../togaf-framework) — the *method* that produces the artefacts Zachman classifies
- [API request lifecycle](../api-request-lifecycle) — trace one cell (e.g. *How · Row 3* = the logical flow) down to code9e:T2303,
## What this system is

Michael Porter's **value chain** is a way of seeing a company as a chain of activities, each of which either *adds value* the customer will pay for or *doesn't*. The whole is compared to competitors via **Porter's five forces** (rivalry, threat of entrants, buyer power, supplier power, substitute threat), and the strategy that follows is "win by doing a set of activities *differently* than rivals, not by doing the same activities marginally better." Where [supply chain management](../supply-chain-management) is the operational chain of *things*, the value chain is the analytical chain of *activities and margins*.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph Primary["Primary activities"]
    IL[Inbound logistics] --> OP[Operations]
    OP --> OL[Outbound logistics]
    OL --> MS[Marketing & sales]
    MS --> SV[Service]
  end
  subgraph Support["Support activities"]
    FI[Firm infrastructure]
    HR[Human resources]
    TD[Technology development]
    PR[Procurement]
  end
```

## Primary vs support activities

| Activity | What it is | Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Inbound logistics** | Receiving, storing, handling inputs | Raw material receiving, inventory control |
| **Operations** | Transforming inputs into products | Assembly, fabrication, testing, a banking platform |
| **Outbound logistics** | Distributing the finished product | Warehousing, order delivery, fulfillment |
| **Marketing & sales** | Making customers want and buy | Brand, pricing, channels, selling |
| **Service** | Supporting the product after sale | Support desk, maintenance, returns |
| **Firm infrastructure** | General management, finance, legal | Executive leadership, planning, compliance |
| **Human resources** | Hiring, training, compensation | Recruiting, people systems |
| **Technology development** | Know-how and technology across the chain | R&D, IT systems, engineering |
| **Procurement** | Sourcing inputs (not the inputs themselves) | Purchasing function across activities |

The analytical habit: for **every** activity ask *does this add value the customer pays for, or is it support?* Activities that do neither are cost with no return — the candidates for outsourcing, automation, or removal.

## The margin view

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  C[Costs across all activities] --> M[Margin = price - costs]
  V[Value / differentiation the customer perceives] --> M
```

The chain's whole point is **margin**: every activity adds cost, and only some add value the customer perceives. Two strategies flow from this:

- **Cost leadership** — optimize the chain so your total cost beats rivals at the same price. Cost is decided across the *whole chain*, not one activity: a company with a slightly more expensive factory but dramatically cheaper logistics can still lead on cost.
- **Differentiation** — make specific activities (design, service, brand, the platform's developer experience) better than rivals so customers pay a premium. Differentiation lives in specific activities, not "being good overall."

The architecture translation: when you map value to systems, **cost-leadership estates are built to minimize operational cost** (consolidate, standardize, outsource the commodity, automate support); **differentiation estates invest heaviest in the differentiated activities' systems** (a best-in-class e-commerce platform, a proprietary analytics capability), even if the surrounding support systems stay generic.

## Five forces: what shapes the value you can keep

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  NE[New entrants] -->|threat| RA[Industry rivalry]
  BU[Buyer power] -->|pressure| RA
  SU[Supplier power] -->|pressure| RA
  SU[Substitutes] -->|threat| RA
  RA --> P[Profit pool in the industry]
```

Porter's companion idea: your ability to *keep* margin is set by the structure around the chain, not just your own activities. Strong supplier power (few suppliers, switching costs) squeezes your cost side; strong buyer power squeezes your price side; entrants and substitutes cap what you can charge. The five forces explain why two identical chains in different industries have very different margins — the airline chain is structurally thin, the chip chain structurally fat.

## Value chain vs supply chain vs business model

| Frame | Unit | Asks | Answers |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Value chain** (Porter) | Activities | Which activities create value and margin? | Where to differentiate, outsource, invest |
| **Supply chain** (SCM) | Materials & flow | How does stuff flow plan→source→make→deliver? | How to run the flow with accuracy and speed |
| **Business model** | Economic logic | How is money made? | What's the revenue/cost engine and who pays |
| **Business process** (BPM) | Process steps | Who does what in what order? | How to model, automate, and govern work |

The value chain is the **strategy layer**: it says *which activities we must be best at*. The [supply chain](../supply-chain-management) and the [business processes](../business-process-modeling-and-management) are the *operation layers* that then implement those activities. When a value chain says "service is our differentiator," the estate responds with a service platform, not a cheaper factory system.

## Outsourcing and make-vs-buy, by activity

```mermaid
flowchart TD
  Act{Which activity?} --> Core{Core / differentiated?}
  Core -->|yes - strategic| Keep[Own and invest]
  Core -->|no - support or commodity| Out[Outsource / buy / automate]
  Core -->|margin check| M{Adds value customer pays for?}
  M -->|yes| Keep
  M -->|no| Out
```

The value chain turns make-vs-buy from a hunch into a checklist: an activity is a candidate for outsourcing when it is (a) **support rather than primary**, (b) **a commodity anyone can do** (IT support, payroll, logistics for many firms), or (c) **a support activity where an external specialist is better and cheaper**. It is a candidate for in-house investment when it is core to differentiation — because outsourcing a differentiator outsources the reason customers choose you.

| Activity | Typical treatment | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Payroll, IT helpdesk | Outsource | Support, commodity, scale external specialists |
| Manufacturing (many brands) | Outsource (contract manufacturing) | Not the differentiator; capital-light wins |
| Customer experience / brand | Own | Directly differentiates |
| Core platform / data | Own | The proprietary advantage |

## The architectural questions

- **Where is the profit pool in your chain?** Map the estate's investment budget against the activities that earn margin — the mismatch is where technology spend is being wasted.
- **Which activities differentiate?** Those systems get the best-in-class treatment; everything else can be commodity, cloud, or off-the-shelf.
- **What do you actually outsource?** Every outsourced activity becomes an integration problem: data crosses a company boundary, so the [integration patterns](../integration-patterns) apply, and a contract (SLA, API, data share) replaces an internal handshake.
- **Is your support stack cheap?** Cost-leadership firms keep support systems lean and standardized; gold-plating support activities is the quiet margin leak.

## Tester checklist

- Map a real business to the nine activities and score each: primary vs support, core vs commodity, value-added vs pure cost — the map should identify at least one outsourcing candidate and one must-own activity.
- For an outsourcing candidate, enumerate the estate impact: which data crosses the boundary, what integration (patterns, SLAs, APIs) must be built to replace the internal handshake.
- Verify the differentiator is funded: is the *differentiated* activity's system budget larger than a *support* activity's, or has gold-plating crept into the commodity side?
- Price the chain: sum the activity costs and confirm the margin claim — an activity that costs more than the value it adds is a candidate to cut, automate, or outsource.
- Stress the five forces: pick one force (e.g., supplier power) and identify what the estate must build (multi-source tooling, switching options) to reduce that pressure.

## Related systems

- [Supply chain management](../supply-chain-management) — the operational chain the value chain's strategy layer directs
- [Vertical vs horizontal integration](../vertical-vs-horizontal-integration) — the growth moves that change which activities the company owns
- [Business process modeling and management](../business-process-modeling-and-management) — how the chain's activities become modeled, governed processes
- [Enterprise resource planning (ERP)](../enterprise-resource-planning) — the backbone running most primary activities
- [Procurement (source-to-pay)](../procurement-source-to-pay) — the purchasing activity that appears across the chain
- [Integration patterns](../integration-patterns) — how outsourced and adjacent activities connect back in9f:T251a,
## What this system is

"Integration" in the business sense is different from the [integration patterns](../integration-patterns) that connect systems. Here it answers the strategy question: **which pieces of the value chain does the company own?** **Vertical integration** grows by owning more stages of a single chain — a carmaker also smelting its own steel, or manufacturing its own batteries. **Horizontal integration** grows by owning more of the *same stage* — a carmaker buying other carmakers, or an airline buying competitors. Both are growth theses about where value (and margin) sits, not software.

```mermaid
flowchart TD
  subgraph Chain["The value chain - raw material to customer"]
    RAW[Raw materials]
    MK[Manufacture]
    DIST[Distribution / logistics]
    RTL[Retail / customer]
  end
  subgraph V["Vertical integration = own more of the chain"]
    RAW --> MK
    MK --> DIST
    DIST --> RTL
  end
  subgraph H["Horizontal integration = own more of one step"]
    C1[Competitor A]
    C2[Competitor B]
    C3[Competitor C]
  end
  V ~~~ H
```

## The value chain, as architecture sees it

| Stage | Example companies operating there | What consolidating here buys |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Raw materials | Miners, growers, chemical suppliers | Price/cost certainty, supply security |
| Components & manufacturing | Assembly, fabrication, OEMs | Control of cost, quality, production timing |
| Distribution & logistics | Freight, warehousing, 3PLs | Delivery control, margin capture, speed |
| Retail & customer | Storefronts, e-commerce, marketplaces | Direct customer relationships, data, margin |

The architect's job is to ask "where does the value chain run through *our* systems, and which adjacent stages should it run through too?" — because every integration move lands on the enterprise estate as a new supply chain, a new ERP/SCM footprint, or a new sales/platform system to graft on.

## Vertical integration: backward and forward

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph Backward["Backward - upstream"]
    SUPP[Supplier<br/>- we acquire this]
  end
  subgraph Firm["The firm"]
    MK[Manufacture<br/>our existing stage]
  end
  subgraph Forward["Forward - downstream"]
    RTL[Retail channel<br/>- we acquire this]
  end
  SUPP --> MK
  MK --> RTL
```

- **Backward integration** buys the *upstream* stage — the inputs. Why: raw-material cost swings, supplier bottlenecks, quality control, or a supplier earning too much margin.
- **Forward integration** buys the *downstream* stage — distribution or the customer. Why: capture the retail margin, control the customer experience, own the data and the relationship.
- **Full vertical integration** owns stages both directions (a retailer that also makes its products, a manufacturer that also runs its own logistics and stores).

The decisive trade: vertical integration converts an **arms-length market transaction into an internal one**. That removes a negotiation and a margin, but it also removes *price signal* and *specialist competition* — the internal mine is never benchmarked against outside mines, and inefficiency quietly moves up the chain. The value-chain economist's view: you want to own the stage where the **profit pool** is deepest, and outsource the stages where everybody can do it.

## Horizontal integration: scale at one stage

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  A[Airline A] --> Merge[One larger airline]
  B[Airline B] --> Merge
  C[Airline C] --> Merge
  Merge --> Scale[Bigger network, fewer rivals, pricing power]
```

Horizontal integration is the classic **M&A growth play**: buy a competitor (or merge) to gain scale *at your own stage*. The logic:

- **Economies of scale** — fixed costs (factories, fleets, R&D, a banking platform) spread over more volume, driving unit cost down.
- **Market power** — fewer competitors means greater pricing power; anti-trust regulators are precisely the check here.
- **Network effects** — a bigger network/service/base is worth more per user (delivery coverage, flight routes, banking customers).
- **Cost synergy** — overlapping functions (two HQs, two ERPs, two IT estates) collapse into one.

The horizontal failure mode is the amalgamation problem: merging two companies into one **platform** is brutally harder than merging two balance sheets. Two different ERPs, two customer systems, two IT towers must become one — and the "synergies" evaporate if integration of systems costs more and takes longer than the savings.

## Make vs buy: the decision that generates both

```mermaid
flowchart TD
  Q[Should we own this stage?] --> A{Core to our differentiation?}
  A -->|yes - strategic| OWN[Integrate - vertically or via capability build]
  A -->|no - commodity| OUT[Buy / outsource - keep it arms-length]
  A -->|uncertain| TEST[Enough volume + margin to justify?]
  TEST -->|yes| OWN
  TEST -->|no| OUT
```

| Consideration | Favors owning (integrate) | Favors buying (outsource) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Where is the margin? | The deepest profit pool, consistent quality | A thin-margin commodity, many suppliers |
| Strategic importance | Core capability, differentiation, IP | Table stakes anyone can do |
| Dependency risk | Supplier can bottleneck you | You can always switch, benchmark, or 3-bid |
| Capital & focus | You have capital and attention to spare | Capital is better deployed at your own stage |
| Tech intensity | Proprietary advantage (manufacturing tech, data) | Rapidly changing niche owned by specialists |

## The enterprise estate angle

Whatever the strategy, integration lands as **integration work** your estate must absorb:

- **Backward integration** extends the [supply chain](../supply-chain-management): new suppliers feed new procurement flows, inventory moves up the chain, and your ERP's purchasing/master-data footprint grows.
- **Forward integration** extends the customer surface: new sales channels, a new [CRM](../salesforce-crm) or POS to onboard, order-to-cash flows to wire.
- **Horizontal integration** is the hardest systems case: **two estates become one** — two ERPs, two customer systems, two payrolls have to run as one. That is a migration program of its own (the classic "all synergies are on the balance sheet, all costs are in the join"), and the enterprise-architecture work that keeps both sides running through it is where deals typically win or lose.

The architect's honest summary for leadership: the *strategy* is the easy 10%. The 90% is proving that two organizations' systems, data models, and processes can actually be joined — and that's the [enterprise architecture](../business-process-modeling-and-management) conversation.

## The classic mistakes

- **Integrating for margin that isn't there** — buying the mine *because* the supplier's margin looked fat, then discovering the margin was the supplier's reward for competence the owner can't replicate (governed by the make-vs-buy test).
- **Confusing the two axes** — "horizontal integration for synergy" when the real upside was vertical position; or buying volume at a stage where scale buys nothing (costs are already mostly variable).
- **Ignoring the amalgamation cost** — the deal banks synergy, but two different ERPs, tax systems, and payrolls take years and enormous spend to unify; the synergy often lands as one massive program of [integration patterns](../integration-patterns) work, not a margin line.
- **Anti-trust blindness** — horizontal moves reach regulator scrutiny; vertical moves can too when they foreclose competitors (e.g., owning both the platform and the stores who rival your merchants).
- **Doing strategy without the estate** — signing a vertical acquisition, then discovering the company can't run a supplier relationship across three SCM systems; the strategy must be bought *with* the roadmap to absorb it.

## Tester checklist

- Given a strategy announcement, lay out the concrete estate impact: which new systems, which [ERP](../enterprise-resource-planning) scope, which new [processes](../business-process-modeling-and-management) — the strategy is only real where it shows up in the roadmap.
- Map the new value chain onto the existing [supply chain](../supply-chain-management) model: which stage is now internal, what data crosses the new boundary.
- For a horizontal deal: inventory the system estates on each side and find the overlapping capabilities (two ERPs, two CRMs) — those are the amalgamation work list.
- Verify the make-vs-buy decision holds for a real component: does the "own" case still hold once capital, tech intensity, and switching cost are priced in?
- Check the profit-pool logic: the stage you integrate must be where margin actually accumulates; test the assumption against the numbers, not the narrative.

## Related systems

- [Supply chain management](../supply-chain-management) — the chain that vertical integration lengthens
- [Enterprise resource planning (ERP)](../enterprise-resource-planning) — the backbone that must absorb both directions of integration
- [Procurement (source-to-pay)](../procurement-source-to-pay) — the flows that backward integration multiplies
- [Salesforce CRM](../salesforce-crm) — the customer surface forward integration adds
- [Business process modeling and management](../business-process-modeling-and-management) — how integrated processes get mapped and governed
- [Integration patterns](../integration-patterns) — the systems-level machinery that makes integration reala0:T181c,
## What this system is

Virtualization lets one physical server run many isolated operating systems by interposing a layer — the **hypervisor** — that shares and protects the hardware. Cloud computing runs on it: every EC2/Azure VM you rent is a guest OS inside a hypervisor on a machine someone else owns. The idea is three layers: **hardware → hypervisor → guest OSes**, each believing it owns the machine.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  H[Physical server - CPU / RAM / disk / NIC]
  subgraph HV["Hypervisor (Type 1)"]
    V1[VM 1 - guest OS + app]
    V2[VM 2 - guest OS + app]
    V3["VM 3 - guest OS + app (…)"]
  end
  H --> HV
  V1 -.- I1[Isolated: own kernel, own IPs, own filesystem]
  V2 -.- I2[Isolated even from sibling VMs]
```

The crucial property is **isolation**: a guest can't read another guest's memory or disk, can crash and be restarted without affecting its neighbors, and each carries its own full OS — a boundary that's stronger than containers' but heavier to run.

## Type 1 vs Type 2: where the hypervisor lives

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  subgraph T1["Type 1 - bare metal (the cloud)"]
    A[Hypervisor - KVM / ESXi / Hyper-V<br/>runs directly on the hardware]
  end
  subgraph T2["Type 2 - hosted (developer laptops)"]
    B[Host OS] --> C[Hypervisor app - VirtualBox / VMware Workstation / Parallels]
  end
  HW[Physical hardware] --> T1
  HW --> T2
```

- **Type 1** runs directly on the silicon with no host OS beneath it — minuscule overhead, near-native performance, and it's what AWS, Azure, and VMware vSphere use. Think of it as the OS of the machine's *management* while guests are the *workloads*.
- **Type 2** runs as an application on top of a regular OS (macOS → Parallels, Windows → Hyper-V/VMware Workstation). Everything the guest does goes through the host OS first — slower, but free to install and perfect for local dev.

The rule: **Type 1 for anything that must be fast or served, Type 2 for your laptop.**

## CPU, memory, and the magic term: overcommit

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  P[Physical host - 64 cores / 256 GiB RAM] --> N[16 guest VMs]
  N --> U1[vCPU per VM × cores used &lt; physical cores?]
  N --> U2[Scheduled - not dedicated]
```

- Every guest exposes **vCPUs** — most allocate MORE vCPU and RAM than the host physically has. That's **overcommit**, and it works because guests rarely all peak at once; the hypervisor schedules vCPUs onto physical cores just like an OS schedules threads.
- RAM overcommit is riskier than CPU: the hypervisor typically needs a memory balloon or dedup to reclaim pages, and sudden global peaks cause the cloud's "insufficient capacity" or performance collapse. Cap total guest RAM below physical RAM and you buy safety at the cost of density.
- **NUMA** (which RAM is attached to which CPU socket) still leaks through at scale — good hypervisors pin vCPUs to the right socket, or guest performance mysteriously halves.

## Storage and networking for guests

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph Net["Virtual networking"]
    N1["A virtual NIC per guest"]
    N2["vSwitch - bridges / NAT / isolated VLANs"]
    N3["Same L2 semantics as physical"]
  end
  subgraph Disk["Virtual disks"]
    D1[Thin vs thick provisioning]
    D2[Snapshots - fast rollback]
    D3[Host RAID / storage pool underneath]
  end
```

- Guests see a **virtual NIC** plugged into a **virtual switch**; whether it's bridged (like a real NIC), NATted (host forwards), or on an isolated VLAN is invisible to the guest — and exactly how cloud VPCs present "your own" network.
- Virtual disks are just big files: **thick** preallocates (predictable, slower to create), **thin** grows on demand (compact, but can surprise you when a full host runs out). **Snapshots** let you roll back a guest — the killer feature for upgrades and patch testing.
- The high-level insight for architects: a hypervisor adds up-front overhead (cores, RAM, I/O) but buys tremendous *manageability* — clone, snapshot, migrate, rebuild in minutes instead of days.

## VM vs container: two kinds of isolation

| | Virtual machine | Container |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Isolation boundary | Full kernel per guest | Shared host kernel, namespaces |
| Startup | Minutes (boot a whole OS) | Milliseconds |
| Footprint | GBs (full OS) | MBs (one app + libs) |
| Overhead | ~small but real CPU/RAM per guest | Near-zero per container |
| Security boundary | Strong (kernel boundary) | Weaker (same kernel) — needs hardening |
| Portability | Same hypervisor family | Anywhere with a container runtime |

They're complementary, not rival: Kubernetes runs *on* VMs in every major cloud. The **VM is the unit of frontier security and ownership; the container is the unit of application packaging and scale.**

## Tester checklist

- **Isolation drill**: on one host, cause memory or I/O pressure in a guest and verify siblings stay stable — that's the hypervisor's headline promise.
- **Overcommit test**: prove your peak-load profile is genuinely below the host's physical capacity; don't discover it in an outage.
- **Snapshot discipline**: snapshot before patching, roll back, and verify the restored guest is exactly the pre-patch state (clock, stateful apps).
- **Pin/placement**: confirm vCPUs stay on the same NUMA node for latency-sensitive guests, or the perf graph will betray you.
- **Networking semantics**: verify guest MACs/IPs don't collide across vSwitches and that isolation (bridged vs VLAN) actually blocks what you think it blocks.
