- [Cloud computing fundamentals](../cloud-computing-fundamentals) — where VMs become IaaS (EC2/Azure VMs) and regions/AZs make them resilient
- [Dockerizing applications](../dockerizing-applications) — containers: the lighter-weight sibling and when to choose it
- [Kubernetes and container orchestration](../kubernetes-and-container-orchestration) — how container clusters sit *on top of* a VM fleet
- [CPU and GPU, ARM and x86](../cpu-gpu-and-silicon-choices) — the silicon constraints the hypervisor has to schedule
- [Mainframe computing](../mainframe-computing) — the pre-hypervisor ancestor that invented logical partitioninga1:Td34,
## What this system is

A WMS (warehouse management system) is the **physical execution layer** of the supply chain: it says where things are, where to put them, and what to pick. It sits below — and often now replaces — the ERP's inventory module, because "inventory on paper" and "inventory on a pallet" are never the same number. The WMS bridges that gap by telling workers exactly what to do and recording what actually happened.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  RC[1 Receive] --> PA[2 Putaway]
  PA --> ST[3 Store - location logic]
  ST --> PK[4 Pick - by order or wave]
  PK --> PKG[5 Pack]
  PKG --> SH[6 Ship]
  SH --> CY[7 Cycle count / reconcile]
```

## The core flows

| Flow | What it does | Failure mode to test |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Receive** | ASN (advance shipment notice) sets the expected; you record the actual | Over/under-receipt vs ASN |
| **Putaway** | Decide *where* each receipt goes (velocity, size, zone) | Wrong location = lost inventory |
| **Store / restock** | Slotting keeps fast movers near packing; restock feeds picks | Starvation stops picking |
| **Pick** | Batch, wave, zone, or pick-to-carton assemble orders | Picked but never staged/loaded |
| **Pack** | Box, verify, label, assign carrier | Label vs order mismatch |
| **Ship** | Outbound shipment + manifest confirmation | Shipped but not confirmed = lost in transit |
| **Cycle count** | Reconcile physical vs system stock continuously | Counts never close the gap |

## Why a WMS instead of the ERP inventory module

The ERP's inventory tab answers "what does the system think we have?" A WMS answers "where exactly is it, and is it still there?" The difference shows up as **location-level control** and **labor efficiency**: instead of a warehouse worker typing what went in and out, the WMS commands each move by RF scanner and verifies it in real time.

## The receive → ship pipeline

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Recv["Receive / ASN"] -->|directed| Put["Putaway"]
  Recv -->|credit on hand| Inv["Inventory<br/>location"]
  Put -->|confirm location| Inv
  Inv -->|debit on hand| Pick["Pick - order / wave"]
  Pick -->|handoff| Ship["Pack + ship"]
  Ship -->|stock transaction| ERP["ERP stock sync"]
```

## Tester checklist

- **Location truth**: putaway must land in the directed slot; a wrong slot logged must not reconcile silently.
- **Stock movement**: every receive/put/pick/ship must produce an audit-trail transaction the ERP can replay — no phantom writes.
- **Wave/batch logic**: a wave of orders must pick, pack, and ship atomically, with partial picks handled (short-pick then backorder).
- **Cycle count variance**: a physical/system mismatch must surface an adjustment workflow, not vanish.
- **ASN vs receipt**: over-receipt beyond a tolerance must be rejected or flagged, not silently accepted.

## Related systems

- [Procurement (source-to-pay)](../procurement-source-to-pay) — the goods receipt that starts this pipeline
- [ERP systems](../enterprise-resource-planning) — the inventory truth this layer keeps honest
- [Supply chain management](../supply-chain-management) — the planning layer that decides what the warehouse executes
- [File transfer and object storage](../file-transfer-and-object-storage) — how ASNs and EDI messages arrive
- [Change data capture](../change-data-capture) — how stock movements fan out to analytics and partnersa2:Te34,
## What this system is

A webhook is the opposite of polling: instead of your service asking "anything new?", the source *pushes* an HTTP request to your URL when an event happens. Payments, CI, GitHub, Slack, and CRMs all deliver webhooks — and the failure modes are all on the delivery side.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Event[Event happens] -->|webhook triggered| S[Source]
  S -->|POST + signature| R[Receiver<br/>verify HMAC + timestamp]
  R -->|verify first| Q[Dedupe by event-id + persist]
  Q -->|consume| W[Process async]
  W -->|reply fast| OK[200 after persist]
  S -->|timeout / 5xx| Retry[Retry with backoff]
  Retry -->|resend same event-id| S
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  A[Event happens] --> B[Source delivers POST to your URL]
  B -->|signature header| C[Verify HMAC + timestamp]
  C -->|valid| D[idempotency check]
  D -->|has seen - skipped| E[Already processed]
  D -->|new - process + ack fast| F[Reply 200]
  B -->|timeout / 5xx| G[Retry with backoff]
  G -->|exhausted| H[Dead-letter for manual replay]
```

## Pull vs push

| | Pull (polling) | Push (webhook) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Latency | Bounded by poll interval | Near-real-time |
| Complexity on source | Low (a GET endpoint) | Signature + retry infra |
| Receiver outage | Safe — stays in queue | Source must retry |
| Firewalling | Easy | Needs an inbound endpoint exposed |
| Real-time fit | Batch-friendly | Event-driven systems |

## Delivery semantics your handler must survive

- **At-least-once**: sources retry on timeouts and 5xx (and sometimes on 2xx that arrive too late). Your handler *will* see duplicates.
- **Retries with backoff**: typical schedulings are exponential (e.g. 1m, 5m, 10m…) over hours; a delayed retry can arrive after the requirement changed.
- **Ordering** is not guaranteed across retries, and often not across events. Do not build on "the newer event always arrives after".

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant S as Source
  participant R as Your receiver
  participant Q as Your queue / store

  S->>R: POST /webhooks/orders (X-Webhook-Signature, X-Event-Id)
  R->>R: verify signature (HMAC over raw body)
  alt signature invalid
    R-->>S: 400 reject (no retry storm)
  else valid
    R->>Q: dedupe by X-Event-Id + persist
    R-->>S: 200 OK (accept after persist, not just after parse)
  end
  alt no 200 in time
    S->>R: retry POST (same X-Event-Id)
  end
```

## Security checklist for the receiver

1. **Verify the signature** over the *raw request body* using a shared secret (`HMAC-SHA256`), timing-safe compare.
2. **Check timestamp** to reject replay windows.
3. **Don't block** the callback on slow downstream work — accept → persist → process async.
4. **Return 2xx fast and only after persisting** the dedupe key; a fast 200 *before* state is saved is how events get lost.

## Tester checklist

- Replay a previously-sent webhook (same `X-Event-Id`) — must be a no-op that still returns 200.
- Corrupt the signature and confirm the receiver returns 4xx and logs without processing.
- Time the receiver: force slow processing and confirm the source's retry does not cause a duplicate side effect.
- Verify the dead-letter/monitoring path exists — retries should be visible, not silent.

## Related systems

- [Message queue delivery](../message-queue-delivery) — the durable queue behind many webhook receivers
- [Pub/Sub and event streaming](../pub-sub-event-streaming) — push to a broker instead of an HTTP endpoint when delivery guarantees matter
- [Authentication vs authorization](../authentication-vs-authorization) — HMAC is authentication of the *source*, not the usera3:T13ef,
## What this system is

Wi-Fi 6 (802.11ax) is the first standard written for **dense, crowded networks** rather than fatter megabytes. Wi-Fi 5 was "make one client fast." Wi-Fi 6 is "make 50 clients tolerable" — which is why the headline features are OFDMA, MU-MIMO, and Target Wake Time, not raw Mbps.

Wi-Fi 6E adds the **6 GHz band**, an entirely new, uncrowded set of channels that only 6E-and-newer devices can use. It's the same protocol on new spectrum — the win is *exclusivity*, not just the protocol.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  AP["Wi-Fi 6 AP<br/>OFDMA + MU-MIMO"] -- "MU-MIMO spatial" --> C1["Client 1<br/>low packet"]
  AP -- "OFDMA RU" --> C2["Client 2<br/>streaming"]
  AP -- "small RU" --> C3["Client 3<br/>sensor"]
  C1 -- uplink --> CH["One frame, shared by<br/>resource units (OFDMA)"]
  C2 -- uplink --> CH
  C3 -- uplink --> CH
  CH -- "fewer waits" --> OK["All served in parallel"]
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  AP[Wi-Fi 6 AP] -->|OFDMA: one frame split into resource units| C1[Client 1 small packet]
  AP -->|OFDMA: resource unit| C2[Client 2 streaming]
  AP -->|OFDMA: tiny resource unit| C3[Client 3 sensor]
  AP -->|MU-MIMO: 8 spatial streams| C4[Multiple antennas at once]
```

## What actually changed with Wi-Fi 6

| Feature | What it does | The pain it fixes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **OFDMA** | Splits one frame into resource units, serving several clients simultaneously | "low-data clients hog the medium" — back-to-back small packets become one parallel burst |
| **MU-MIMO (8 streams)** | Transmits to several clients on separate spatial streams at once | Wi-Fi 5 did downlink MU-MIMO poorly; Wi-Fi 6 makes it real and adds uplink |
| **Target Wake Time (TWT)** | Devices tell the AP when they'll wake; AP schedules sleep | **Battery life** — IoT and low-power devices stop polling endlessly |
| **BSS coloring** | Frames from crowding APs get a "color" and are ignored instead of causing backoff | The "dense apartment Wi-Fi" slowdown — repeaters among neighbors stop screaming |
| **1024-QAM** | More bits per symbol at high SNR | Bling for fast, close, clean links |

The counterintuitive truth: Wi-Fi 6 doesn't make a single laptop feel 2x faster on a quiet network. It makes 30 laptops not-crawl on a busy one. If you're alone on a channel, Wi-Fi 5 with wide channel is still very fast; Wi-Fi 6 pays off where the network is *populated*.

## Why 6 GHz (6E) is the real story

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  OLD[2.4 + 5 GHz<br/>crowded, shared with legacy] --> NOW[6 GHz
  ]
  NOW --> A[Only 6E+ devices can join]
  NOW --> B[Many wide, clean 80/160 MHz channels]
  NOW --> C[No DFS radar weathership timeouts]
  NOW --> D[Same Wi-Fi 6 protocol, new spectrum]
  NOW --> Q[The highway is empty again]
```

The decisive changes: **new clients only** (the band forces capability), **no DFS** (no radar-detection pauses), wide 80/160 MHz channels without fighting neighbors for them, and 6E mandates WPA3 — no legacy-weakness mode. If you're upgrading a fleet, 6E gives the *same* Wi-Fi 6 efficiency plus a private highway. Wi-Fi 7 (works on the same 6 GHz) pushes throughput higher with even wider channels and multi-link, but 6E is where the practical leap lives in 2026.

## Wi-Fi 6 realities in deployment

- **Backhaul still matters**: an AP fed by a 100 Mb/s Ethernet port delivering 1 Gb/s wireless is just a bottleneck on a stick. Plan port speed = peak wireless, not wireless speed.
- **Clients must be Wi-Fi 6 capable to benefit**: mixed fleets get only the improvement that the *oldest* client can use in that exchange (per-BSS-wide effects like BSS coloring and OFDMA help everyone, per-client features require new clients).
- **RF design is still RF design**: no protocol fixes a dead spot. Coverage survey, antenna placement, and channel planning are still the prerequisites — 6E just removes the neighbors-your-neighbors problem.
- **Monitor with the right metric**: look at *airtime utilization* and *retry rate*, not RSSI. A noisy airtime-90% AP is worse than a "slower" clean one.

## Tester checklist

- Compare AP throughput with a single Wi-Fi 6 client vs a device on Wi-Fi 5 mixed and explain the delta (expected: modest for one, big for many).
- Load the AP: hit it with 30-50 clients doing small IoT-style frames and confirm OFDMA keeps latency/airtime low (that's the design goal, not peak Mbps).
- Enable TWT on a low-power device and confirm battery drain drops measurably.
- If you have a 6 GHz-capable client, move it to 6 GHz and verify it leaves 2.4/5 GHz devices (and legacy 5 GHz DFS waits) alone.
- Verify BSS coloring actually kicks in on a dense apartment test: two APs on the same channel with clients should hurt less than pre-6.

## Related systems

- [Wi-Fi radio fundamentals](../wi-fi-radio-fundamentals) — the shared-medium physics this builds on
- [Wi-Fi mesh networking](../wifi-mesh-networking) — how Wi-Fi 6 scales to many APs
- [Network routing and BGP](../network-routing-and-bgp) — the wired path these APs ride on top of
- [TCP/IP and TLS](../tcp-ip-and-tls) — the transport the wireless frames carrya4:T16f8,
## What this system is

A Wi-Fi mesh is a single logical network made of **many access points (APs)** that coordinate: one SSID, one shared config, and seamless roaming between them. The "mesh" word specifically means APs talk to each other — wired **or** wirelessly — to relay traffic and hand clients off. It's the fix for "big house / thick walls / one router in the corner."

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  GW["Gateway / router"] -- Ethernet --> R["Root AP<br/>wired to gateway"]
  R -- "wireless backhaul" --> S1["Satellite AP<br/>living"]
  R -- "wireless backhaul" --> S2["Satellite AP<br/>office"]
  S1 -- "relay hop" --> S3["Satellite AP<br/>upstairs"]
  S2 -- "client connect" --> DEV["Roaming client"]
```

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  GW[Gateway] -->|wired| R[Root AP]
  R -->|backhaul radio| S1[Satellite AP 1]
  R -->|backhaul radio| S2[Satellite AP 2]
  S1 --> S3[Satellite AP 3 - daisy chain]
  S1 --> C1[Client]
  S2 --> C2[Client]
  S3 --> C3[Client]
```

## Mesh topology: the tree

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  G[Gateway] --> R[Root]
  R --> S1[Sat 1] --> S4[Sat 4]
  R --> S2[Sat 2] --> S5[Sat 5]
  R --> S3[Sat 3] --> S6[Sat 6]
  C[Client] -.-> S4
```

A mesh is a **tree** rooted at the gateway. Each satellite connects to exactly one parent (the root or another satellite) and relays. The critical trade-off: every **wireless hop halves (or more) your throughput** — a satellite two hops away is roughly a third to a half the speed of the root AP. This is why "mesh is slow" usually means "someone put a satellite two wireless hops out instead of wiring one of them."

## Backhaul: wired is the cheat code

| Backhaul | Speed reality | Best for |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Wired / Ethernet** | Every AP runs at full line rate — no hop tax | Enterprise, offices, anywhere you can pull cable |
| **Dedicated radio** | A tri-band mesh uses a separate radio just for backhaul | Home/small office: good compromise |
| **Shared radio (dual-band)** | Backhaul steals airtime from clients on the same band | Avoid if you can; daisy-chaining two hops is worst |

The rule: **wire every AP you possibly can.** Ethernet backhaul isn't "cheating" — it's the same architecture as enterprise Wi-Fi, and it removes the halving tax entirely. A mesh is only worth the wireless-hop tax where cabling is impossible.

## Roaming: how a client moves between APs

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant C as Client
  participant AP1 as AP 1
  participant AP2 as AP 2
  participant Ctl as Controller (or distributed)

  C->>AP1: connected, RSSI fading
  C->>AP1: RSSI drops below roam threshold
  AP1->>Ctl: report weak signal
  Ctl->>AP2: candidate AP ready
  C->>AP2: 802.11r fast transition (FT) key exchange
  C->>AP2: reassociate - short handoff
  AP2->>C: served, session preserved
```

Three standards make roaming smooth:

- **802.11r (FT)** — fast BSS transition: pre-shared keys so handoff skips the full re-authentication round-trip (the difference between a "blip" and a dropped call).
- **802.11k (RM)** — radio resource measurement: APs give the client a list of nearby APs to scan, so it doesn't probe blindly.
- **802.11v (BSS)** — AP-guided roaming: the network *tells* a sticky client "move to AP 2" instead of waiting for it to decide.

**One SSID everywhere is non-negotiable.** Splitting "attic Wi-Fi" and "office Wi-Fi" into two SSIDs *forces* devices to reconnect and interrupt when moving. One SSID + the three standards above = a client walks through the house without dropping a video call.

## Mesh vs wired APs: which one?

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  Q[Can you pull a cable to each location?] -->|yes| W[Wired APs - better speed, simpler]
  Q -->|no| M[Wireless mesh - fewer wires, hop tax]
  M --> D{Multiple hops needed?}
  D -->|1 hop, few APs| H[Great - home mesh]
  D -->|2+ hops, dense| E[Consider powerline / MoCA / more root APs]
```

| | Wired APs (enterprise) | Wireless mesh |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Peak speed per AP | Full line rate | Halved per wireless hop |
| Cost/complexity | Cabling + switch + controller | Self-organizing, plug-in |
| Roaming | Controller-assisted (802.11k/v/r) | Often the same, built-in |
| When to choose | Offices, anyone who can run cable | Homes, spaces cable can't reach |

## The mistakes repeatedly remembered

- Treating mesh as "more speed": adding satellites *without* wiring them adds coverage but can **reduce** per-client throughput once the wireless backhaul is the bottleneck.
- Two SSIDs ("Upstairs" / "Downstairs") killing roaming: one network, one name, always.
- Daisy-chaining three satellites and wondering why the far room is slow — backhaul hops compound.
- Placing satellites adjacent instead of spaced across the coverage area, so the mesh keeps relaying between neighbors and never actually covers the dead zone.

## Tester checklist

- Roam while streaming a video call: confirm the call holds (802.11r/k/v active) and note the handoff gap.
- Measure throughput at a satellite one hop away vs the root: expect roughly half — and use that number to decide whether wiring that AP is worth it.
- Verify one-SSID behavior: move around the home and confirm the device stays on the same network name while switching APs.
- Stress the backhaul: saturate a satellite's client with traffic and confirm the root AP isn't also stuck (if it is, backhaul is sharing airtime).
- Check roaming stickiness: a client parked under one AP while a better AP is adjacent should get 802.11v guidance to move.

## Related systems

- [Wi-Fi radio fundamentals](../wi-fi-radio-fundamentals) — the physics every hop inherits
- [Wi-Fi 6 and 6E](../wifi-6-and-6e) — the protocol that makes crowded mesh tolerable
- [Network routing and BGP](../network-routing-and-bgp) — the wired routing the mesh plugs into
- [TCP/IP and TLS](../tcp-ip-and-tls) — the transport sessions that must survive roaminga5:T168e,
## What this system is

Wi-Fi (IEEE 802.11) is a **shared radio medium**, which is the whole story: unlike a switch, only one client can transmit on a channel at a time, they all hear each other, and the medium is half-duplex. Every Wi-Fi problem — slow LAN nights, dropped video calls, "the microwave kills my connection" — is a symptom of radio physics, not (only) bad firmware.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  CL[Client device - Wi-Fi radio] -->|radio frame 2.4 / 5 / 6 GHz| AP[Access point - SSID on a channel]
  AP -->|802.1Q VLAN| SW[Switch / VLAN]
  SW -->|routed| RT[Router / gateway]
  RT -->|WAN| IN[Internet]
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  C[Client] -->|half-duplex radio frames| AP[Access point - one channel, shared]
  AP --> SW[Switch - full duplex] --> RT[Router] --> IN[Internet]
```

## Bands, channels, and overlap

| Band | Typical channels | Reality | Good for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 2.4 GHz | 1-13, but really 1/6/11 non-overlapping | 3 usable channels; everyone+microwave+Bluetooth; long range, low throughput | Range, IoT, legacy |
| 5 GHz | 36+ (UNII-1/2, DFS) | Many non-overlapping channels, higher data rates | Modern clients, less crowded |
| 6 GHz (Wi-Fi 6E/7) | Lots, clean | Exclusive to 6E+ gear, no legacy interference | The empty highway |

Adjacent channels in the **same band interfere** because of wavelength skirts — 2.4 GHz's 20 MHz channel spacing means channels 1, 6, and 11 are the only truly non-overlapping set. A neighbor AP on channel 2 chews your channel 1. This is why enterprise surveys put APs on overlapping channels: neighbors on **different channels** to minimize their mutual interference.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  A[AP on ch 1] -->|20 MHz| CH1[ch 1]
  B[AP on ch 6] -->|20 MHz| CH6[ch 6]
  C[AP on ch 11] -->|20 MHz| CH11[ch 11]
  CH1 --> N1[neighbors on 2,3,4 interfere]
  CH6 --> N2[neighbors on 5,7 interfere]
  CH11 --> N3[neighbors on 10,12 interfere]
```

## CSMA/CA: why Wi-Fi is half-duplex and "polite"

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant A as Client A
  participant W as Wireless medium (shared)
  participant B as Client B

  A->>W: listen (carrier sense) - clear?
  A->>W: send frame → W
  A->>W: wait for ACK
  B->>W: listen - medium busy, back off
  B->>W: after DIFS + random backoff, try again
  W->>B: ACK received - successful
```

Ethernet is cabled and collision-free per port; Wi-Fi must **listen before talking** (carrier sense) and **collision-avoid** (CSMA/CA) with random backoff, then wait for an **ACK**. Only one transmission per channel at a time means: more clients = more waiting = the "it's slow at dinner" effect, all else equal.

## The signals: RSSI, SNR, and how you tell "bad" from "bad area"

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  AP -->|signal + noise| C[Client]
  C --> M[Measure RSSI and noise]
  M --> S[SNR = signal - noise]
  S --> V{Decision}
  V -->|low RSSI -150 dBm| D[Move closer / more APs]
  V -->|high RSSI but low SNR| E[Interference - change channel / band]
  V -->|good SNR -25 dB| F[Data rate ramps up]
```

- **RSSI** (dBm): how strong the received signal is. -40 dBm = excellent, -85 dBm = borderline, less = roaming time.
- **SNR** (dB): signal strength *relative to noise* — this is what actually determines usable data rate, not raw RSSI. High RSSI next to a loud noise source is still bad Wi-Fi.
- **Data rate** falls off the cliff as SNR drops: one bad neighbor on your channel (or a per-user 40 MHz channel that needs more headroom) drops you from 400 Mb/s to 12.

## SSID, bands, and security (WPA for short)

- **One SSID, many bands**: modern APs broadcast one SSID across 2.4/5/6 GHz; clients band-steer where possible. SSID is a name, *not* a security boundary — there's no isolation by name unless you configure it.
- **WPA2** (AES-CCMP) is the floor. **WPA3** (SAE + forward secrecy, modern handshake, easy with WPA2-compatible transition mode) is the default for anything new. WPA2-PSK shared passwords are only as strong as the password and age.
- **Enterprise (802.1X/EAP)**: each user gets a certificate or credential exchange — the default for corporate networks precisely because shared-PSK weakens the moment someone leaks or loses a device.
- **Hidden SSIDs do nothing for security** (clients boilerplate-broadcast for the hidden name) and slow roaming: leave broadcast on.

## The mistake repeatedly remembered

- Two APs on the same channel stepping on each other = "AP 2 has no internet" when it's really interference.
- Turning the 2.4 GHz radio off for "speed" and then wondering why IoT devices can't join.
- Buying a "WPA" router for a post-2019 device, then warning staff about "the easy Wi-Fi."
- Placing the AP behind a metal shelf, in a cupboard, or on the floor, and treating the poor result as a config bug.

## Tester checklist

- Walk the floor with a client tool (or `wi-fi inspector`-style survey): confirm RSSI stays better than -70 dBm in usable spaces.
- Check for rogue/duplicate SSIDs or an AP on an overlapping channel (wigle/netspot-style scan) — you may be your own interference.
- Force a client to the 5 GHz band and confirm band-steering moves it off 2.4 when capable.
- Verify WPA3/enterprise: the signing happens at the AP; confirm WPA2-only devices still join (transition mode) while WPA3 clients get SAE.
- Watch `ACK` behavior under load: a busy-open client list (many slow ACKs) is a capacity, not speed, problem.

- [TCP/IP and TLS](../tcp-ip-and-tls) — the frames on the wire carry IP/TCP; Wi-Fi is the link layer
- [Wi-Fi 6 and 6E](../wifi-6-and-6e) — everything here, made parallel-efficient
- [Wi-Fi mesh networking](../wifi-mesh-networking) — multiple APs, one network, roaming
- [OSI seven-layer model](../osi-seven-layer-model) — Wi-Fi lives at layers 1-2a6:Te08,
## What this system is

Corporate Windows is not the retail OS. Every device is **identity-bound** (joined to a domain / Entra ID), **policy-managed** (GPO or Intune), and **monitored** (inventory, compliance, telemetry). The goal: a user opens a laptop, it is corporate-configured, compliant, patched, and remotely wipeable — with nobody touching it manually.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    D[New laptop] -- boot + call home --> AU[Autopilot - device hash]
    AU -- auto-join --> E[Entra ID / AD]
    E -- enroll + report --> I[Intune - policies + apps]
    I -- apply config --> D
    I -- BitLocker + Defender --> C[Compliance check]
    C -- allow or quarantine --> H[Healthy - access]
```

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  D[New laptop] -->|Autopilot profile| A[Entra ID / AD] -->|identity + policy| I[Intune]
  I -->|push app + config| D
  D -->|enroll + report inventory| I
  I --> C{Compliant?}
  C -->|yes| E[Healthy device → allow access]
  C -->|no| F[Block / quarantine until fixed]
```

## The enrollment lifecycle

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant D as Device (out-of-box)
  participant ABM as Vendor / Autopilot
  participant Entra as Entra ID / AD
  participant Intune as Intune MDM
  participant App as Corporate portal

  D->>ABM: boot, calls home with serial
  ABM-->>Entra: known hardware hash → auto-join
  D->>Entra: enroll device identity
  Entra->>Intune: MDM enrollment token
  Intune->>D: push policies + apps
  D->>App: user signs in, device now managed
  Note over Intune,D: device stays enrolled: inventory, patch, compliance, remote wipe
```

## GPO vs Intune policy

| | Group Policy (GPO) | Intune policy (MSP) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Scope | On-prem AD domain members | Cloud (Entra ID-joined) devices |
| Where policy lives | DC-based | Cloud-delivered, device-specific |
| Best for | Legacy/desktop workflows | Modern endpoint management |
| Venue | Apply at boot / re-eval every ~90 min | Check-in every few minutes |

Both can collide; the modern estate converges on Intune (or SCCM co-management) as the single source of truth. **Autopatch** handles the drumbeat: rings where updates roll out in waves (rings = pilot → broad → canary) so a bad patch poisons only a fraction of devices.

## What "compliant" means (and why it gates access)

Compliance is a *claim check*, not a promise: BitLocker on, Defender on and updated, OS ≥ version, password policy met, no jailbreak-type mutations, hardware healthy. The **conditional access** layer (part of [zero trust](../zero-trust-architecture)) releases access only on a *healthy* device — this is the modern "device is an identity" idea.

## Tester checklist

- Enroll a fresh device via Autopilot/automated provisioning and verify zero manual steps (guy affirms: no local admin fiddling).
- Validate policy application **timing**: GPO drift vs Intune check-in cadence — then audit one device and see both outputs.
- Test the compliance loop: disable BitLocker or strip Defender and confirm access is blocked/quarantined, then restored on fix.
- Do a real patch drill: bad update in a ring must quarantine in that ring, not spread to all.

## Related systems

- [Zero trust architecture](../zero-trust-architecture) — device posture as a condition of access
- [Authentication vs authorization](../authentication-vs-authorization) — what the device identity actually proves
- [iOS and macOS in the enterprise](../ios-macos-in-the-enterprise) — the sibling OS under the same MDM umbrella
- [Linux servers in the enterprise](../linux-servers-in-the-enterprise) — the server-side cousina7:Tf63,
## What this system is

Users have identities; so do workloads. **Workload identity** gives every service, container, and pod a cryptographic identity, and **mTLS** uses it to encrypt *and* authenticate calls between services — closing the east-west traffic hole that firewalls never saw.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  A["Service A<br/>spiffe://corp/catalog/a"] -->|sidecar| X1[Sidecar proxy]
  X1 -->|mTLS + SPIFFE trust| X2[Sidecar proxy]
  X2 -->|labeled untrusted unless verified| B["Service B<br/>spiffe://corp/orders/b"]
```

## User identity vs workload identity

| | User identity | Workload identity |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Proves | A human logged in | A workload *is* what it claims (its SPIFFE ID) |
| Carried in | Tokens / cookies | Short-lived X.509 certs presented on the connection |
| For you | SSO, OIDC, MFA | SPIFFE/SPIRE, service mesh, cloud SA (Workload Identity) |
| Expiry | Minutes-to-hours sessions | Hours-to-minutes certs, rotated continuously |

The mental model: **mTLS proves the *service*, tokens prove the *request*.** Both must hold — "known service" alone must never equate to "allowed to read everything."

## SPIFFE and SPIRE

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant W as Workload (pod)
  participant A as SPIRE agent
  participant S as SPIFFE server

  W->>A: request identity for spiffe://corp/catalog/a (attested by k8s metadata)
  A->>S: attestation check (agent valid, node trusted)
  S-->>A: short-lived identity (SVID cert + key)
  A-->>W: hand to workload, burned into sidecar memory
  Note over W,S: SVIDs rotate automatically - no human, no static keys
```

**SPIRE** is the reference implementation of producing short-lived service identities from the platform's own attestation (pod labels, node trust, instance metadata). The identity is whatever the platform says, per policy — no stored secrets, auto-rotating certs.

## The sequence of a secure call

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
  participant SA as Service A (orders)
  participant SX as Sidecar X
  participant SY as Sidecar Y
  participant SB as Service B (payments)

  SA->>SX: local connection
  SX->>SY: TLS with client cert (spiffe://corp/orders/a), server cert (spiffe://corp/payments/b)
  SY->>SX: certs validated - both X.509 chain to the SPIFFE trust root
  SX->>SY: allow (or policy: only orders may call payments with v2 header)
  SY->>SB: plaintext into the pod
  SB-->>SA: response returns
```

Service meshes (Istio, Linkerd) implement this as zero-code: the sidecar proxies intercept all traffic, present/validate the identities, and apply L7 policy (who may call whom, with what).

## Why not just encrypt everything at the wall

A firewall/LB terminates TLS at the edge — inside the cluster it's usually plaintext. mTLS between every pair matters because **attackers are already inside** (assume breach): they land in one pod, and without workload identity they can impersonate any caller to any service. mTLS turns a one-command pivot into "attackers would need to steal a live rotated key from a trusted pod."

## Tester checklist

- Call service B impersonating service C's SPIFFE ID and confirm the handshake/client-cert validation fails.
- Kill a pod; its SVID must be invalidated quickly (short expiry, tight rotation, no golden access).
- Check that a *known* workload still cannot call endpoints policy forbids (identity grants authenticity, not authority).
- Rotate the trust bundle and verify all running workloads re-trust without downtime.
